# Nava-vraja-mahima. Vol. 5

## Dedication

Although I have written Nava-vraja-mahima for the pleasure of all Vaisnavas who either physically or mentally reside in Vrndavana, I dedicate this book to those whose hearts are devoted to Radha-Syamasundara and New Vraja-dhama. It is my hope that they will pass their time in this way:

*iha vatsan samacarayad*  
*iha nah svami jagau vamsim*  
*iti sasram gadato me*  
*yamuna-tire dinam yayat*

**“Here our Lord herded the calves, and here He played the flute. I pray that I may pass my days shedding tears as I speak these words on the Yamuna’s shore.” (Sri Raghupati Upadhyaya)**

## Preface

The title of the hook you are now reading, Nava-vraja- mahima, when rendered in English becomes “The Glories of New Vraja-dhama.” I originally conceived of it as a booklet, meant to guide devotees visiting the holy places of New Vraja-dhama, iskcon’s rural community in Hungary.

As you must have noticed, the book has grown in size since its inception and would now likely require a small handcart for eager pilgrims to be able to transport it from one pastime place to another! Nonetheless, it still serves its original purpose of extolling New Vraja-dhama by praising its presiding Deities, Radha-Syamasundara, and by narrating the Supreme Lord Krsna’s pastimes in His eternal abode, Sri Vrndavana-dhama.

Texts that glorify the holy abodes (dhamas) of the Lord are called dhama-mahatmyas. Two prime examples of this genre of devotional literature are Rupa Gosvami’s Sri Mathura-mahatmya and Bhaktivinoda Thakura’s Sri Navadvipa-dhama-mahatmya. Both of these books establish the sanctity of the dhamas they depict — Vrndavana and Navadvipa respectively — by citing Puranic praise of the dhamas themselves as well as the pastime places within their boundaries. There is, however, an interesting difference between the two. Sri Mathura-mahatmya is an unordered compendium of scriptural references to the pastime places it describes, while Sri Navadvipa-dhama-mahatmya chronicles the Lord’s pastimes and the places at which they occurred by following the sequence in which pilgrims visit those places as they circumambulate the dhama. Thakura Bhaktivinoda also recounts the personal spiritual reflections he gleans from circumamhulating Navadvipa in an appendix to Sri Navadvipa-dhama-mahatmya that he calls Sri Navadvipa-bhava-taranga.

Despite their stylistic differences, the transcendental benefit readers derive from these two types of mahatmyas is the same. They act as practical guidebooks to the dhama, and at the same time they aid pilgrims in immersing their thoughts in the Lord’s glories. For that reason, devotees who do not travel to the dhama can also derive transcendental benefit by circumambulating the holy places in their minds as they meditate on both the Lord’s pastimes and the dhama’s glories as they are recounted in sacred texts of this nature.

Nava-vraja-mahima is a mahatmya in the style of Sri Navadvipa-dhama-mahatmya in that it includes an account of my own personal circumambulation, or Parikrama, of New Vraja-dhama and the realisations that came to me as a result. In fact, as I visit the pastime places in New Vraja-dhama — more commonly known to the people of Hungary as Krsna Valley — I sometimes reflect on similar parikramas I have undertaken in Bhauma Vrndavana, recount histories of how New Vraja-dhama was established and developed, or express my own devotional aspirations. This personal approach is primarily an attempt to please the devotees of New Vraja-dhama, who requested me to describe the Parikrama not as a passive observer but as a participant who shares his adventure with the reader.

Amongst many possible readers, this personal adventure is especially intended to be shared with the residents of and visitors to New Vraja-dhama, for whom this book will naturally be of greatest interest. Those readers who are not a part of that particular audience may rightly question what New Vraja-dhama is and how its glorification can be relevant to persons who live outside Hungary or who may never visit.

As its name suggests, New Vraja-dhama is modelled after the original Vraja-dhama, Bhauma Vrndavana, Krsna’s eternal home as it appears on earth. The inspiration to establish New Vraja-dhama came from my spiritual master, His Divine Grace A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupada, the founder-acarya of the International Society for Krishna Consciousness. In the 1960s in America, Srila Prabhupada established New Vrndavana and envisioned it to be a self-sufficient community, dependent upon agriculture and cow protection, as well as a place of pilgrimage in the West. New Vraja-dhama is therefore our humble attempt to fulfil the desire of His Divine Grace that such places of pilgrimage be established throughout the world.

In the introduction to this book and in the essay entitled “The Truths of the Dhama,” the reader will find a detailed history of how and why New Vraja-dhama came to be, along with a philosophical study of the spiritual principles that make it a holy place. I cannot overstate how important it is for the reader to carefully go through these parts of the book in order to clearly understand the spiritual foundations upon which New Vraja-dhama and Nava-vraja-mahima rest.

What benefit will those who do not visit New Vraja-dhama gain from reading this book? The answer is threefold.

Nava-vraja-mahima contains extensive and very detailed narrations of Krsna’s pastimes at all the major places in Vrndavana, as well as at many lesser-known places. Extensive research went into compiling — from the Vedas, Upanisads, Puranas, Samhitas, and other Vedic histories, as well as from the commentaries of our previous acaryas and other sources — the information it contains. Altogether, the text and the accompanying references offer the reader a unique opportunity to delve deeply into Krsna’s pastimes in Bhauma and Goloka Vrndavana and better get to know the Supreme Lord and His loving associates.

Second, although the topographical orientation, distances, and occasionally the sequence of New Vraja-dhama's Parikrama differ from the same in Bhauma Vrndavana, the book also serves as an in-depth guide for pilgrims to use in visiting the pastime places of Sri Krsna’s original earthly abode.

Third, through the glorification of New Vraja-dhama, this book testifies to the greatness of Srila Prabhupada’s achievement in spreading the potency of Vrndavana throughout the world.

Now, allow me to clarify a few points about certain expressions and literary devices used in the text, about the structure of the volumes, and about the actual parikrama route of New Vraja-dhama.

The expression “Krsnas pastimes in New Vraja-dhama” is not meant to imply that Krsna manifests His eternal pastimes here in the way that He does, creation after creation, in Bhauma Vrndavana. Rather it means that the pastime places in New Vraja-dhama correspond to the original places in Bhauma Vrndavana or Goloka Vrndavana. Largely due to the uncommon format of the text (which I will discuss elsewhere in this preface) and because of the correspondence between the pastime places in both dhamas, it was sometimes necessary to refer to the pastimes associated with those places in a way that may make it sound as if they happened in New Vraja-dhama.

It I say, for example, “At this place, called Aghavana, Krsna killed Aghasura,” it means “Krsna killed Aghasura in the Aghavana of Bhauma Vrndavana, which is commemorated by the place of the same name here in New Vraja-dhama.” Similarly, phrases such as “At this place Radha and Krsna enact Their rasa dance” should be taken to mean “At the Rasa-sthali of Goloka Vrndavana, the divine couple enact Their eternal rasa dance. The place called Rasa-sthali in New Vraja-dhama commemorates that original place.”

The reason I use such expressions, aside from literary necessity, is to emphasise the importance of New Vraja-dhama and its stature as a place of pilgrimage. Again, the reader will find the philosophical and spiritual reasoning and supporting scriptural evidence for employing this convention in the introduction and the essay on dhama-tattva that follow this preface.

Another literary device found in this book — as well as in other books describing the holy dhama— is that I sometimes speak about New Vraja-dhama as if I were directly seeing the unmanifest (aprakata) abode and pastimes of the Lord. These expressions are not declarations of my existential position; rather, they arc poetic expressions of my deep feelings for the subject matter and the places themselves. I qualify such statements here to avoid causing misunderstandings that might blur the line between the manifest and unmanifest features of the dhama, merging them in a way that could be taken as contrary to authorised dhama-tattva.

When, in describing the dhama, authors weave between manifest and unmanifest realities in this way, it often causes the features of the aprakata realm or the pastimes Krsna is relishing there to seem as if they are part of the world visible to conditioned souls. But devotees well versed in the principles of dhama-tattva will have no difficulty following these transitions between the manifest and unmanifest realms, whereas others may either not notice them or find them confusing.

Our great acaryas may express themselves in such ways due to the overwhelming bliss they feel at seeing the aprakata-dhama and the eternal pastimes going on there. Or, in the case of those who are not on the hilly realised platform of our great acaryas, authors may be making a conscious endeavour to emphasise the divinity that underlies the features of the dhama that conditioned souls are able to perceive.

In writing Nava-vraja-mahima, poetic inspiration would sometimes impel me to describe the features of the dhama not in terms of what I was seeing with my eyes — for I cannot see that which is unmanifest — but in terms of the aprakata counter-whole as I have heard about it from scripture and as I contemplate it in relation to New Vraja-dhama. Readers who notice such expressions in the pages of this book should take them in this light, not as my personal realisation of the unmanifest dhama in the way of the great acaryas.

**\* \* \* \***

Parikrama is the traditional way of visiting Vrndavana. When Krsna sent Uddhava to Nandagrama with messages for the Vraja-vasis, He directed His cousin along a specific route and pointed out places to note along the journey. Thus Uddhava relished Parikrama by travelling from Mathura to Nandagrama, as did Krsna by describing the journey to him.

On His only visit to Vrndavana, Caitanya Mahaprabhu spent the first month of His two-month stay touring through Vraja’s twelve forests. And even though Lord Caitanya is Krsna Himself, He requested the help of a guide and followed the established route of His time.

With the rediscovery of Krsna’s pastime places, the Vraja-mandala Parikrama has evolved into what is currently called caurasi-krosa Parikrama, or circumambulation of the one hundred and sixty-eight- mile dhama, the longest version of which takes almost two months to complete. Today, often during the holy month of Karttika, hundreds of devotees relish walking barefoot from place to place on iskcon’s Vraja-mandala Parikrama, living in the meadows or fields of Vraja, and absorbed in hearing about Krsna and chanting His names.

For Hungarian devotees who cannot go to Bhauma Vrndavana, who cannot stay there for thirty days or more, or who would like to go on Parikrama every year but cannot afford to go to India so often, the Nava-vraja-mandala Parikrama is for them. Annually, members of the Hungarian yatra visit selected places of New Vraja-dhama over the course of several days, and as the dhama is further developed and Parikrama paths established, the guided tours will become more extensive. In addition, by using the separate book of maps provided with Nava-vraja-mahima, devotees may go on Parikrama at any time they want and thus relish the nectar of hearing Krsna’s pastimes and serving His dhama.

In this way, Nava-vraja-mahima seeks to serve those devotees keen to visit Krsna’s abode, both in Hungary and in India. And for devotees who arc neither residents of nor pilgrims to one dhama or the other, this work gives them the chance to visit Krsna’s holy dhama in their minds and thus bring the atmosphere of Vrndavana to wherever they reside.

Circumambulating the dhama is a means of offering respect. It includes visiting pastimes places, remembering pastimes, bowing down, taking the dust from pastime places on one’s head, bathing in sacred waters, and performing nama-sankirtana. Many major places within the dhama — Govardhana Hill, Vrndavana town, and Varsana amongst them — have their own Parikrama routes. As far as possible, these places have been or will be represented in New Vraja-dhama. There is never a dearth of transcendental diversion for the devout pilgrim!

At the time of writing this book, the main pastime places of New Vraja-dhama have been established, whereas many Parikrama paths and other secondary pastime places have been mapped out but not yet developed. These places — like the construction of a magnificent new temple tor Radha-Syamasundara — remain part of future plans to develop New Vraja-dhama.

Let me now describe the book’s structure.

As already mentioned, this book is written in the form of my own Parikrama, which is actually a compilation of multiple parikramas I made of New Vraja-dhama, some encompassing the entirety of the dhama, others only of places especially dear to me.

In the course of describing my Parikrama, there were times at which deep philosophical topics or lengthy pastimes came up that required explanations so extensive that to describe them properly would have broken the flow of the narration. These explanations became essays that are now appendices at the end of each volume. I request my reader not to neglect these appendices — nor the endnotes that accompany each chapter, for that matter — as readers are sometimes wont to do. They comprise roughly one third of the book’s total content and are instrumental in bringing the reader to a balanced understanding of its message. Designated stanzas in the running text direct the reader to these appendices at the proper times.

In addition to the Parikrama and appendices, the ten final chapters of Nava-vraja-mahima are dedicated to directly glorifying Radha- Syamasundara, the presiding Deities of New Vraja-dhama. In these chapters I exalt Their Lordships through the meditations that arise as I chant the Gayatri mantras and the maha-mantra and from my direct service to Them on the altar. This last section of the book therefore contains in-depth analyses of the Gaudiya Vaisnava diksa-mantras as well as the Hare Krsna maha-mantra. In this way the book has three parts: Parikrama, appendices, and direct glorification of Radha-Syamasundara.

The running text that forms the bulk of the book, comprised of the Parikrama and the praises of Radha-Syama, is written in three-line prose stanzas. It is pleasant to look at and easy to read, but it was a challenge to write, edit, and lay out on the page. This challenge led us to adopt certain conventions, such as mixing Sanskrit verses with English translations within a single stanza, or having longer verses straddle more than one stanza. However, we feel confident that the hook’s format will enable you to relish the topic all the more.

From the outset I wanted Nava-vraja-mahima to be well illustrated to give windows into Krsna’s pastimes and to contribute to the overall purpose of the work — to increase the reader’s attachment to the Lord. I hope this has been achieved to your satisfaction. In Sri Krsna-samhita (9.16), Bhaktivinoda Thakura writes,

**“By seeing pictures of Krsna or by repeatedly hearing descriptions of Krsna from persons who have seen Him, one’s greed for attaining Krsna increases unlimitedly.”**

To assist you in navigating through the unfamiliar names of pastime places and the variances in the locations of these places in New Vraja-dhama and Bhauma Vrndavana, each chapter also includes a map or maps of the area it describes.

Altogether there are eight volumes of text. The set is complete with a ninth volume solely dedicated to maps of the various holy places of both New Vraja-dhama and Bhauma Vrndavana, along with short descriptions of each pastime place. Devotees can take this map book with them to follow the parikrama routes in both dhamas and have a reminder of Krsna’s activities there.

Nava-vraja-mahima is meant to serve as a companion for devotees who want to take full advantage of the presence of Vrndavana in Hungary and to develop their relationship with its presiding Deities, Radha-Syamasundara. Furthermore, it is meant to encourage devotees anywhere in the world to worship their local Deities with devotion and recognise the Vaikuntha nature of those Deities’ abodes. It is my hope as the author that this work will serve these purposes in some small way and thus bring pleasure to devotees, for my own access to the dhama will be possible only by the good wishes of the Vaisnavas.

In conclusion, I request the reader, wherever he or she may reside, to consider that in its essence this book praises the same dhama and the same Krsna that are there in their own hearts, and which are thus very near and dear to them. It is in this way that our journey around New Vraja-dhama through the pages of this book is synonymous with our own inner journey back to Godhead.

The journey through Nava-vraja-mahima is a long one. The book spans over five thousand pages and took five years to write. Therein I have tried my best to portray the truths of Vrndavana as they are recorded in scriptures and narrated by perfect devotees like Srila Prabhupada. Sadly, I remain a conditioned soul and thus am prone to make mistakes. If my readers find mistakes within the pages of this book, I ask them to tolerate such faults in the way they do the pebbles or thorns that prick their feet while on Parikrama of the dhama.

Hare Krsna.  
Sivarama Swami  
New Vraja-dhama  
Gopastami, 2 November 2011

*Vṛndāvana is Kṛṣṇa’s original abode and home to the original forms of all sacred places  — sixty billion in all — that are, in their expanded forms, found throughout the three strata of the universe.*

## Chapter 18 - Adi-badri to Srngara

All glories to the two foremost connoisseurs of sublime amorous mellows, Radha and Syama, who enjoy swinging on an ornate jewelled throne at the centre of a golden lotus grove.

That wonderful grove on the bank of Radha’s lake is replete with all the sweetness and beauty of Vrndavana, but it is made all the more delightful by the presence of the eternally youthful divine couple.

Let us always meditate upon Radha and Syamasundara, one gold and one blue lotus that sprout from the reservoir of sweetness. Beelike servants, humming joyfully, eagerly attend to Them with love.

After completing my parikrama of the Yoga-pitha and Radha-kunda, I stopped to relish the beauty of Radha and Syama, and to peacefully offer prayers at Their lotus feet, the very goal of my life.

Just as the petals of New Vraja — dhama's pastime places blossom from the corolla of the Yoga-pitha, the chapters of this book unfold from a central theme: the glories of Radha and Syamasundara.

I entered the temple room and paid obeisances to Srila Prabhupada, who is krsnananda (“the form of Krsna,s bliss,” “the giver of bliss to Krsna,” or “the one who tastes the bliss of service to Krsna”).

The hearts of the world’s people had formed a global desert made from the dry sands of impersonalism, scorched by the relentless sun of hedonism, and populated by the cacti of debt-driven consumerism.

Srila Prabhupada created oases in that desert by showering the rain of Sri Caitanya’s mercy in the form of Krsnas holy names, the words of Srimad-Bhagavatam, the company of saints, and Krsnas many forms.

Grateful Vaisnavas gather at these oases — be they homes, temples, restaurants, or farm communities — all of which are sacred places, embassies of terrestrial Mayapur and Vrndavana.

To Srila Prabhupada, who gave us these holy places in which to live, a reason to stay alive, and a way to survive in the desert of Kali-yuga, I offered my obeisances, my heart overflowing with gratitude and love.

I then offered my obeisances to Lord Nrsimha, whose rolling tongue, pointed ears, blood-stained claws, gnashing teeth, and majestic mane are pleasing to devotees, yet fearful to demons.

By killing Hiranyakasipu, the lion-like Lord vanquished the demons of pride and destroyed for devotees like Prahlada the suffering caused by repeated birth and death. All glories unto Lord Nrsimha!

The Lord’s presence in New Vraja-dhama frees His worshippers from the greatest sins and fulfils all of their desires. Through His cakra He nullifies the power of time and the force of astrological influences.

Taking permission from Lord Nrsimhadeva and His consort, I went to fill my eyes with the vision they hanker for day and night. Before Radha-Syamasundara’s altar, I offered prostrated obeisances.

With joined palms I prayed to Syamasundara, “All glories to You, who enjoy pastimes in the forests and groves of New Vraja-dhama. All glories to You whose complexion shines like polished sapphires.

“All glories to You, upon whose broad chest precious necklaces hang, around whose form silken garments are artfully draped, and upon whose head a colourful turban topped with peacock feathers resides.

“O Lord! The foremost of demigods worship You, and the soft hands of Srimati Radharani serve You; still, this insignificant person offers You a humble prayer in the hope that You will hear.

“All glories to Syamasundara, who constantly drinks the nectar of Radha’s face, whose smile steals the composure of the other gopis, and whose sidelong glance is sought by great devotees.

“All glories to a handsome dark cowherd who breathes the intoxicate ing elixir of His breath into His flute, a flute from which pour many hypnotic melodies that exalt the festival of amorous love.

“You dance to the sound of the milkmaids’ singing, and skilfully clap Your hands to their dancing. At these times Your flute, even though tucked into Your belt, sings of its own accord.

“Jaya Sri Syama! You forbade Your kinsmen to offer sacrifice to Indra and instead had them worship Govardhana Hill. You then effortlessly lifted Giriraja to protect the Vraja-vasis and to humble Indra.

“You long to wander through the groves of Vraja — Your knee-length garland swinging this way and that — but You stay here, tied by the ropes of Radha’s stunning sidelong glances. All glories to You!

“O Syama! It is well known to those of pure faith that You are madly in love with Sri Radha, that She is madly in love with You, and that Your love for one another is the pinnacle of divine loving exchange.

“O beloved of Radha! Your sweet smile bewilders the wives of the demigods, impels the gopis to abandon their household duties, and gives me hope that one day I might earn the right to serve You.

“Great poets, pure devotees, the Vraja-vasis, even the parrots of Vraja praise Your form, qualities, and pastimes. Compared to their artful and love-filled tributes, my words are like the cawing of a crow.

“Nevertheless, I make the following plea at Your lotus feet: O beloved of Sri Radha! May You endow me with the wealth of those transcendental virtues that mature into direct service to You both!

“O Syamasundara! All glories to You! Whether my request is appropriate or not, allow me to keep serving You and Your beloved in the divine playground of New Vraja-dhama. Please grant me that!”

After offering prostrated obeisances to Syamasundara, I turned to Sri Radha and prayed, “All glories to You, whose sweetly smiling face can only be compared to Syama’s own face, reflected in polished gold.

“All glories to You, whose form is like an ocean of beauty constantly flooding its shores with waves of sweet charm because of the enticing force of the black moon that constantly hangs above its waters.

“O Goddess! Your complexion is like kunkuma crushed with a golden pestle. Delicate flowers decorate Your braided hair. You dress in the finest silks. And Your restless eyes dance in all directions.

“Jaya Sri Radha! Your sweet youthfulness and playful contrary spirit make You dearer to Krsna than all other gopis. And Krsnas toenails are millions of times more valuable to You than Your own life.

“O Vrndavanesvari, crowned queen of Vraja! ? Karttika-devi, presiding Deity of the month of Karttika! ? Damodara-prestha, most dearly beloved of Krsna. This lowly soul calls out Your divine names.

“Jaya Radhe! You are a storehouse of transcendental virtues, and Your name is the steady heat in Krsnas heart. Your fame spreads the glories of Your father’s dynasty and removes the sins of the world.

“O beautiful princess! It is well known that Your heart is a treasure trove of priceless gems of pure romantic love for Krsna, hut Your affection for Your girlfriends and maidservants is less widely known.

“All glories to You whose heart overflows with love for Lalita-devi, who is always absorbed in friendly dealings with Visakha, and who tutors the beautiful gopis of Vraja in the sixty-four divine arts.

“Jaya Radhe! Srila Prabhupada told us that if You are pleased with a devotee then You will recommend that devotee to Krsna, saying, ‘Please accept him.’ He called this the ‘Radharani spirit.’

“O supreme Goddess! My body is a product of many sins. My heart is full of unwanted desires. My mind is besieged by terrible thoughts. But I have unflagging faith in that ‘Radharani spirit.’

“Jaya Sri Radhe! My service is insignificant. But since Your affectionate heart is so magnanimous, I beseech You! My one request is this: Please ask Krsna to accept me as one of His servants.

“O Sri Radha! Your divine presence inspires me to attain the sort of selfless service to Krsna You exemplify. While You deliberate on what to do, please allow me to continue my present service to You.”

After offering obeisances to Sri Radha, I opened my heart to Gaura Nataraja and Govardhana Lala, the two forms Syamasundara assumes to act as His own devotee and plumb the depths of loving service.

To Gaura Nataraja I prayed, “O Lord! You are more handsome than millions of Cupids, more soothing than millions of moons, kinder than millions of mothers, more generous than millions of desire trees.

“Furthermore, You are deeper than millions of oceans, sweeter than millions of nectarean drinks. All glories to You who display millions of amazing sentiments of the most exalted mellow of love for Krsna.”

To Sri Lala I prayed,

*nirupadhi-karunena sri-sacinandanena tvayi kapati- satho ’pi tvat-priyenarpito 'smi*  
*iti khalu mama yogyayogyatam mam agrhnan nija-nikata-nivasam dehi govardhana tvam*

**“I am a cheater and a liar, hut merciful Lord Sacinandana, who is dear to You, has given me to You. ? Govardhana! Without considering whether I am acceptable or not, grant me residence near You.”’**

Taking permission from Their Lordships to continue my Parikrama, I bowed before Them and then returned to the Parikrama path, now heading cast, keeping Radha’s and Syama’s smiling faces in my heart.

**\* \* \* \***

I walked along the Vrndavana Parikrama path past Nikunjavana, leaving the path at Damodaravana and climbing the hill, from the summit of which I could see the eastern petals of New Vraja-dhama.

Just below the forest summit of Kamaravana, I stopped at Phakarapura, the place where Krsna, simply hy playing His flute, made a small lake in order to quench the thirst of His cowherd friends.

The cowherd boys often play there. On that occasion, exhilarated hy carefree youth, they lost track of time and forgot bodily demands like eating or drinking, until one gopa finally remarked, “I’m thirsty!”

That boy’s comment acted like sparks on dry tinder. Within moments all the gopas were complaining about being thirsty. Some boys opened their eyes wide with alarm and others held their throats in distress.

As they do in all circumstances, trivial or grave, the boys all turned to Krsna for shelter, saying, “O Gopala! We know You have many powers. Please find some cool, clear water for us to drink.”

Krsna would never let His friends suffer, not even for a moment. He placed the flute to His lips and played a special melody. Suddenly, water bubbled out of the ground and formed a small lake.

“Krsna has done it again,” the cowherd boys cheered, excitedly clapping their hands. And in the bliss of embracing and applauding their mystical friend, they forgot all about being thirsty.

Indeed, Krsna had to remind them, teasing, “Friends! Test the water to see if you like it.” The boys crowded around the lake and either drank from their cupped hands or directly from the water as cows do.

Because the gopas — raving all the while about the sparkling, cool taste of the water — quenched their thirst (phakara) from the kunda created by Krsna, this place is known as Phakarapura.

A short distance above Phakarapura, I came to Kamaravana, which was named as much for the passionate mood (kama) in which Krsna waited here for Sri Radha, as for the blanket (kamara) He once lost.

“O Sri Radha! Your divine presence inspires me to attain the sort of selfless service to Krsna You exemplify. While You deliberate on what to do, please allow me to continue my present service to You.”

After offering obeisances to Sri Radha, I opened my heart to Gaura Nataraja and Govardhana Lala, the two forms Syamasundara assumes to act as His own devotee and plumb the depths of loving service.

To Gaura Nataraja I prayed, “O Lord! You are more handsome than millions of Cupids, more soothing than millions of moons, kinder than millions of mothers, more generous than millions of desire trees.

“Furthermore, You are deeper than millions of oceans, sweeter than millions of nectarean drinks. All glories to You who display millions of amazing sentiments of the most exalted mellow of love for Krsna.”

To Sri Lala I prayed,

*nirupadhi-karunena sri-sacinandanena tvayi kapati-satho ’pi tvat-priyenarpito ’smi*  
*iti khalu mama yogyayogyatam mam agrhnan nija-nikata-nivasam dehi govardhana tvam*

**“I am a cheater and a liar, hut merciful Lord Sacinandana, who is dear to You, has given me to You. ? Govardhana! Without considering whether I am acceptable or not, grant me residence near You.”’**

Taking permission from Their Lordships to continue my Parikrama, I bowed before Them and then returned to the Parikrama path, now heading east, keeping Radha’s and Syama’s smiling faces in my heart.

**\* \* \* \***

I walked along the Vrndavana Parikrama path past Nikunjavana, leaving the path at Damodaravana and climbing the hill, from the summit of which I could see the eastern petals of New Vraja-dhama.

Just below the forest summit of Kamaravana, I stopped at Phakarapura, the place where Krsna, simply by playing His flute, made a small lake in order to quench the thirst of His cowherd friends.

The cowherd boys often play there. On that occasion, exhilarated by carefree youth, they lost track of time and forgot bodily demands like eating or drinking, until one gopa finally remarked, “I’m thirsty!”

That boy’s comment acted like sparks on dry tinder. Within moments all the gopas were complaining about being thirsty. Some boys opened their eyes wide with alarm and others held their throats in distress.

As they do in all circumstances, trivial or grave, the boys all turned to Krsna for shelter, saying, “O Gopala! We know You have many powers. Please find some cool, clear water for us to drink.”

Krsna would never let His friends suffer, not even for a moment. He placed the flute to His lips and played a special melody. Suddenly, water bubbled out of the ground and formed a small lake.

“Krsna has done it again,” the cowherd boys cheered, excitedly clapping their hands. And in the bliss of embracing and applauding their mystical friend, they forgot all about being thirsty.

Indeed, Krsna had to remind them, teasing, “Friends! Test the water to see if you like it.” The boys crowded around the lake and either drank from their cupped hands or directly from the water as cows do.

Because the gopas — raving all the while about the sparkling, cool taste of the water—quenched their thirst (phakara) from the kunda created by Krsna, this place is known as Phakarapura.

A short distance above Phakarapura, I came to Kamaravana, which was named as much for the passionate mood (kama) in which Krsna waited here tor Sri Radha, as for the blanket (kamara) He once lost.

These pastimes took place a short distance south at a place called Kamara (Kamera). Kamara is close enough to be seen from one of Durvasa Muni’s Vrndavana asramas at nearby Durvasa-kunda.

One winter day, because it was such a secluded place far from the common road, Krsna chose Kamara as the place where He would meet Radharani. A gopi messenger relayed this plan to His beloved.

The winter air is chilly in the morning, so both gopas and gopis carry thick shawls or blankets with them. Sometimes cows that are sensitive to the cold are also covered with thick decorative blankets.

As the cowherd boys rested and the cows grazed in nearby Nipavana, Krsna took His blanket and, telling His friends He wanted to see the beauty of the forest, eagerly made His way to Kamaravana.

There He placed His folded blanket on a large rock, sat on it, and surveyed the beautiful landscape before Him. But before long, restless for Radha’s company, He started to worry, “Why is She late?”

Indeed, Radha was late that morning. Jatila was keeping a close eye on her daughter-in-law; for some reason, she was even more reluctant than usual to let Radha leave for Her daily worship at Surya-kunda.

But Krsna felt unhappy being alone. Without His companions He could not taste rasa, the mellows of loving exchange, for which He had expanded Himself into so many forms.

Scriptures like the Taittiriya Upanisad confirm that Krsna does not like solitude, so ’kamayata bahu syam prajayeya: “The Supreme Lord desired, ‘Let Me become many. Let Me father many living entities.’”

The Vedanta-sutra summarises this principle: Krsna wants the company of others because He is full of transcendental desires that require the help of others to fulfill (*kamac ca nanumanapeksa*).

Krsna desires to be served and to taste loving exchanges from that service, and so He creates servants. He desires intimate friendship and the taste of friendly loving exchange, and so He creates friends.

Krsna also desires to he maintained and to taste the loving exchange of being maintained, and so He creates parents. And to fulfil His desire for amorous exchange and its many tastes, He creates lovers.

Surrounded hy and interacting with all these eternal companions in Vrndavana, Krsna is able to fully satisfy His very nature. Krsna is the supreme enjoyer, and so His nature is to enjoy supreme pleasure.

That Krsnas very essence is enjoyment is confirmed hy the constitution of His body: Krsnas head is pleasure, His right side is joy, His left side is delight, and His identity is bliss.

By this description the Vedic literature indirectly refers to Krsna as the aniandamaya-purusa. It also declares that liberated souls — like Krsnas associates — can become like Him and enjoy His company.

Krsna Himself says to Arjuna, idam jnanam upasritya mama sadharmyam agatah: “By becoming fixed in this knowledge, one can attain to the transcendental nature like My own.”

Thus, at Kamaravana Krsna was not anxious only for His own enjoyment, He was also anxious for the enjoyment of Sri Radha and Her gopi friends, whose enjoyment depends entirely upon His.

Selfless spiritual exchange is called love (prema), and amorous feelings in prema are called kama. Krsnas kama was so intense at that place that it can still be felt today. Thus that area is called Kamara.

Repeatedly rising from His seat, walking down the path, and then returning to His seat again, Krsnas eagerness to meet Radha built up minute by minute until He was on the verge of fainting.

To prevent Himself from actually losing consciousness and to convey to Radha the severity of His condition, Krsna lifted His flute and with a trembling voice said, “Friend! You are My final resort!

“Call Radha. Tell Her that I stand on the edge of a precipice from which only Her touch can pull Me back.” Krsna placed the flute to His lips, but He was practically too lightheaded to breathe.

Luckily the flute did not need Krsna to transmit the message to Radharani. It was competent enough to achieve its master’s purpose on its own, as it had done in similar situations many times before.

Only Radha and a few of Her intimate friends could decipher the flute’s message. Fortunately, when She heard it She was already close enough to save Krsna before He fell lifeless to the ground.

As soon as Radha and the gopis were close enough for Krsna to see, She stepped on a dry branch, making a cracking noise to herald Her arrival and to draw Krsna back from the edge of unconsciousness.

Krsna heard the noise and raised His head. At once His eyes drank in Radharani’s golden effulgence, a comforting sun-like brilliance more than able to drive away the wintry fog of His sorrow.

In His eagerness to meet Radha, Krsna left His blanket where it was. The warmth of Her love and Their pastimes made Him completely forget about it until long after She had left for Surya-kunda.

Later Krsna thought, “Where is My blanket? Did I bring it or did I leave it with the gopas?" Still bewildered by the effects of Radha’s company — and even more so by Her departure — He could not recall.

Addressing a group of gopis who were busily clearing away the remnants of His pastimes with Radharani, Krsna enquired, “Friends! Have you seen My winter blanket somewhere in this garden?”

The girls at once exchanged glances, understanding that fate had given them a chance to talk with Krsna. One gopi said, “A strong gust of wind blew it into the Yamuna. I saw it float away.”

Krsna was startled! Then another gopi chimed in, “I don’t think that was Krsnas blanket. On our way here I saw some cows near the path chewing on something. That must have been Krsnas blanket.”

As both cowherd girls smiled widely, a third girl teased, “Krsna! Don’t worry. For the small price of a flute-song and a dance, we will give You a new blanket, much better than the old one.”

In no mood to be ridiculed, and eager to find His blanket, Krsna ignored the gopis. As soon as they had left for Surya-kunda, He went back to where He had been waiting for Radha to look for it Himself.

When Krsna arrived at the rock He found Durvasa Muni standing there, covered in matted locks but wearing only a loincloth. Durvasa was holding the blanket fondly, as if he had just discovered a treasure.

Krsna offered His respects to the brahmana, who offered his blessings in return. The sage then said, “O son of Nanda! Is this Your blanket?” Krsna replied, “O Muni! It was Mine until it came to you.

“My duty is to distribute wealth to the brahmanas daily. But I can never accept a gift from a brahmana, other than the gift of transcendental knowledge. Therefore, what you hold belongs to you.

“You know the principles of dharma, the knowledge that paves the way to liberation for conditioned souls. Please do with the blanket as you wish. My respectful obeisances unto you! All glories unto you.”

Pleased with Krsnas humility and kindness, Durvasa Muni accepted the Lord’s blanket (kamara) as maha-prasada. The sage knew that by wearing it he would be freed from the cycle of birth and death.

Uddhava teaches us about the importance of honouring Krsnas remnants,

*tvayopabhukta-srag-gandha vaso-’lankara-carcitah*  
*ucchista-bhojino dasas tava mayam jayema hi*

**“Simply by decorating —**

**“Ourselves with the garlands, fragrant oils, clothes, and ornaments that You have enjoyed, and by eating the remnants of Your meals, we, Your servants, will indeed conquer Your illusory energy.”**

Dear readers! With hearts full of love, worship the beautiful son of Nanda Maharaja, whose transcendental playground is unpredictable, inconceivable, and as full of wonder as He is.

**\* \* \* \***

Further south of Kamaravana and part of the district of Baithana is Carana-pahari, a stone outcrop (pahari) that contains the impressions of Krsnas feet (carana) as well as many other markings.

Five places in Vraja, all called Carana-pahari, have footprints in stone: Nandagrama, Chota-baithana, Govardhana, and in Kamyavana, one on and one below Candrasena-parvata.

Of the five, the Carana-pahari at Baithana is most prominent because it exhibits the greatest variety of markings, spans such a large area, and stands alone without a connection to any other holy place.

Once, as the cows grazed nearby and the gopas rested in the shade, Krsna, dressed like a dancer, climbed this hill. He was eager to absorb His friends in a pastime that would be preserved for posterity.

Standing beneath a tree, His body bent in three places, Krsna sounded His flute in such an attractive way that not only the boys and cows but also local villagers and forest animals flocked to Him.

The only ones who couldn’t go to Krsna were the gopis, who were confined to their homes by indaws and social norms, which prevented women from meeting with men other than their husbands.

So many people and animals crowded onto the stone outcrop of Carana-pahari that it resembled the ark of Maharaja Satyavrata. And Krsnas flute-song sounded like Lord Matsya reciting the Vedas.

Yet the flute-song differed from Vedic recitation, even the recitation of Lord Matsya. It created anomalies in nature, causing animate things to stand still and inanimate things to start moving.

Sukadeva Gosvami says, *aspandanam gati-matam pulakas tarunam*: “The sweet music causes the moving living entities to become stunned and the nonmoving trees to tremble with ecstasy.”

Stunned, the humans and animals that had come to Krsna appeared just like statues, and the trees and vines that could at first not come to Him now displayed human-like symptoms of ecstasy.

The water in nearby rivers and lakes, such as Durvasa-kunda, transformed into a pleasant kind of semi-solid substance, and rocks on the mountains like Carana-pahari turned soft like putty.

The sweet, drawn-out melody of the flute had another, invisible effect, known only to Krsna and a few others qualified to perceive it: it melted the hearts of everyone, even the cows, deer, and trees.

Melting of the heart is symptomatic of an extraordinary type of love Krsna invokes within His devotees to make them taste novel symptoms of ecstasy, symptoms beyond cither words or imagination.

Just by seeing Caitanya Mahaprabhu, the hearts of Ramananda Raya’s companions melted; they all unwittingly chanted Krsnas names and displayed diverse symptoms of ecstasy like crying.

If Krsna bestows such spiritual treasures upon non-devotees, who can say what gems of ecstatic love He awarded the Vraja-vasis on Carana-pahari? After all, they are unconditionally surrendered to Him.

Completely unaware of what was happening in the ecstasy of divine trance, all the living things surrounding Krsna sank into the softened stone, making imprints of their feet upon the mountainside.

Satisfied that His devotees had tasted the joy one feels from a melting heart, and seeing that their footprints had made deep impressions in the mountain, Krsna stopped playing His flute.

As soon as His flute-song stopped, the supernatural symptoms that had transformed Vrndavana dissipated, and everyone and everything regained their natural state.

The cowherd boys — delighted to see their own footprints alongside those of cows and deer and even an elephant imprinted on the mountainside — declared the marks a testimony to Krsnas prowess.

But Krsnas purpose was not just to leave historical evidence of His descent and pastimes cast in stone. Actually, He achieved many things at once by leaving those footprints behind.

By this mystical arrangement on Carana-pahari, Krsna made history, proclaimed the glories of His flute, hinted at the greatness of His devotees’ love for Him, and invited all devotees to join His pastimes.

As a final act of wonder Krsna called for the Ganges, which burst through stone and filled a large depression to form a pond so that Krsna and the gopas could cleanse their feet of stone particles.

That pond is now known as Carana-ganga because Krsna washed His feet in its waters, waters one thousand times more purifying than the Ganges River where it flows through Haridvara.

Offering my respects to Carana-pahari and to the impressions of the lotus feet of Krsna and His companions, I climbed to the top of the hill with the hope that the dhama would be kind to me.

The eastern portion of New Vraja-dhama is expansive. At the time of this writing, it is farmland extending to the western slope of a valley, through which flows a stream that represents many holy rivers.

The holy places here are not yet developed, but I have assigned them to particular locations and hope that some time in the future they, along with the entire outer Parikrama path, will be developed.

I begin this description of the eastern section of the dhama from those places furthest north, gradually working my way south. Readers may note that there are fewer pastime places in this area.

Krsna has favourite places, just as He has favourite foods, clothing, and music. As is only natural, in enacting His pastimes Krsna spends more time in His favourite places than in others.

And yet all these pastime places are transcendental. For the astute practitioner of Krsna consciousness, they give access to thoughts of Krsna, to absorption in chanting His names, and ultimately to Him.

The northernmost place is a town called Dig, which, though near the Parikrama path, actually lies outside of Vraja. Neither the scriptures nor our acaryas mention it as a pastime place.

The pastime place nearest to Dig is Didavali, the birthplace of Variyasi-devi — Krsnas grandmother (didima)— and a place sacred even to the Supreme Lord, who worships this best (variyasi) of gopis.

Variyasi-devi, Nanda Maharaja’s mother, is well respected in Vraja. She wears green garments and is short in stature. Her complexion is like an orange kusumbha flower, and her hair is the colour of milk.

The meaning of her name — variyasi (“the best”)—itself attests to why both the residents of Vrndavana and devotees in general acknowledge her special status amongst Krsnas kin.

The import of the word “best” is disclosed in this prayer to Radharani,

*maha-bhava-svarupa tvam krsna-priy-variyasi*  
*prema-bhakti-prade devi radhike tvam namamy aham*

**“O Srimati Radharani —**

**“You are the exalted form of great love, therefore You are most dear to Krsna. ? Goddess! You alone are able to bestow pure love for the Lord; therefore I offer my humble obeisances unto You.”**

Variyasi-devi is the best amongst Krsnas entourage because her maternal love for Krsna is so exalted, and she is able to bless devotees with sublime love in return for sincere service to the Lord.

During Caitanya Mahaprabhu’s advent Variyasi-devi appeared as Kamalavati-devi, of whom the Lord’s biographers do not write.But Raghunatha Dasa Gosvami teaches us to pray to her in this way:

**“I offer my obeisances to Variyasi-devi, Krsnas grandmother, who worships Krsna with joking words like, ‘moon of grandsons.’ She is so proud of Krsna that as she walks her feet never touch the ground.”**

**\* \* \* \***

To the south of Didavali is Maharaniya, a beautiful garden where the gopis decorated Sri Radha with profuse floral ornaments. Sometimes while chanting Hare Krsna I fondly think of this place.

One spring morning, in the days following the cooking episode at Sonkh, the gopis set out to explore fragrant and colourful Vrndavana, joyfully wandering from one forest garden to another.

Spring, the king of seasons, decorates every creeper, tree, garden, and meadow with its flowers. But until it can offer its floral bounty to its queen, it feels melancholy and incomplete.

When the gopis arrived at Maharaniya, all-pervasive spring — which, amongst seasons, represents Sri Krsna Himself — inspired the girls to offer all its wealth to its worshipable Deity, Srimati Radharani.

The gopis sat Radharani upon a golden throne. Collecting all kinds of flowers — whether budding, blossoming, or in full bloom — they set about making anklets, garlands, a crown, and other ornaments.

Lalita and Visakha directed the older girls, who in turn told their own assistants what kind of flower decorations to make. All the while the girls happily chatted about Srimati Radharanl’s natural beauty.

As the ornaments were handed to them one by one, a few especially clever girls like Rupa and Rati placed them on Sri Radha, as others danced around the throne, singing and playing musical instruments.

All the ornaments in place on Radharani’s limbs, She appeared like the queen of spring. The gopis called Her “Maharani” and named the place where She was adorned “Maharaniya.”

In Vraja, the title Maharani signifies more than just royal lineage; it is also awarded to gopis with sovereignty over pure devotion. Vaisnavas thus honour Yamuna-devi and Tulasi-devi with the title Maharani.

Candrasekhara Kavi prays to Tulasi MaharanI to hestow the gift of pure devotion in the prayer that begins, *namo namah tulasi maharani, vrnde maharani namo namah, namo re namo re meiya namo nardyani*.

Just south of Maharaniya is Gohana, the birthplace of Sudama-gopa. In childhood, Krsna and Balarama would herd Their cows there, and Sudama would happily join the two brothers, who were his very life.

Amongst Krsnas many confidential friends (priya-sakhas), who are the same age as He, four are most prominent: Sridama, Sudama, Vasudama, and Kinkini. Sridama is the leader of them all.

Sudama is Srimati Radharani’s and Sridama’s cousin. His mother is Susila and his father, Ratnabhanu, the younger brother of Maharaja Vrsabhanu. Sudama’s younger brother is Vidagdha.

Sudama has fair skin, a moonlike face, and large lotus eyes. His limbs are large, round, and strong, and he dresses in blue garments and wears golden ornaments, a pearl necklace, and a jasmine garland.

Sudama is an intimate friend of Krsna whose affection for the Lord is devoid of the slightest taint of awe and reverence. His service is to collect and prepare forest flowers for garlands and ornaments.

Rupa Gosvami describes Sudama as playful and expert in a variety of games. His deep faith in Krsna is such that in their pastimes he thinks of himself as Krsnas equal, or even His superior.

While Krsna held up Govardhana Hill, Sudama’s friends said to Krsna, “Dear friend! You have been standing for the last seven days and nights without any rest. This aggrieves us terribly.

“Why pur Yourself through so much trouble, standing and holding this hill? Just give it to Sudama. He will hold it. Or it You doubt he can, at least shift hands, so we can give Your left hand a massage.”

Sudama’s heart is one with Krsnas, and so he knows Krsnas thoughts and desires. For us, then, he is as worshipable as the Lord; indeed, Krsna Himself praises the character and devotion of His dear friend.

Sudama’s nature is heroic, and his dealings are simple and straight' forward. When overwhelmed with ecstasy he can be seen dancing in a wild, unrestrained way with his hair standing on end.

Born in Gohana, Sudama later moved with his family to Varsana where he kept a pet cow called Syamalika. Over all other forests, he favours Bhadravana as a place for frolicking with Krsna.

Jiva Gosvami tells us that when gopas like Sudama appear on earth, demigods appear within their bodies to serve the Supreme Lord, just as the limbs of the body serve the body as a whole.

But Sudama is not a demigod; he is Krsnas eternal companion. The fact that he touches and wrestles with Krsna, a privilege available only to the Lord’s dearest triends, testifies to this truth.

As mentioned earlier, Sudama appeared in Lord Caitanya’s pastimes as Sundara Thakura. All glories to the cowherd boy Sudama and his pure affection tor Sri Krsna and Krsnas brother Balarama!

Downhill from Gohana is Kalyana, a pasture with sweet grasses favoured by the cows. Krsna and Balarama once rested here. And the gopas, in a tunny, good-natured way, ottered the brothers dandavats.

“O Supreme Lords!” they chuckled. “Bless us with auspiciousness.” Krsna and Balarama caught the mood of Their friends. Raising Their right hands, They said, kalyana hauk: “Let there be auspiciousness.”

The cows, seeing the cowherd boys offer respects to the divine brothers, followed suit and also bowed down, creating a unique scene. This is a pastime worthy of meditating on with affection.

We may doubt whether cows can offer obeisances. But we should remember that these are not ordinary cows; scriptures say that they are capable of yielding oceans of milk and fulfilling all desires.

Therefore, what difficulty could there he for these surabhi cows to fulfil their own desire hy bowing down before Krsna and Balarama and praying to the divine brothers for the well-being of their kin?

Further east is Adi-badri, also called Setukandara, comprised of four hills that surround a beautifully forested valley called Tapovana where Nara-Narayana Rsi performs austerities even today.

In a time long past, King Indra, unaware of Nara-Narayana Rsi’s transcendental position, was anxious that the Lord would, by His austerities, he able to seize the heavenly kingdom.

The king of heaven sent Cupid to Tapovana — where the god of love evoked the sensuous atmosphere of spring — along with beautiful damsels whose alluring poses could have bewildered even Brahma.

But the Lord, rather than being tempted by the celestial enticements that surrounded Him, calmly and politely welcomed to His asrama Cupid and His temptresses, who now trembled in fear before Him.

Their heads hung low in shame, the celestials offered prayers to Nara-Narayana Rsi, who is always beyond the influence of lust, anger, and greed, as are those devotees under His protection.

As His guests were praising Him, the Lord suddenly generated from His body many women who were astonishingly gorgeous, finely dressed, opulently ornamented, and faithfully engaged in His service.

The fascinating beauty and enchanting fragrance of those women bewildered the minds of Cupid’s followers, both male and female, because their heavenly opulence paled in comparison.

How could the lesser courtesans of heaven command the attention of the Supreme Lord Narayana when women millions of times more attractive were already serving His lotus feet?

As the celestials bowed before Nara-Narayana Rsi, the Lord offered them any one of the women He had created. Accepting Urvasi as the best of the Apsaras, the demigods then returned with her to heaven.

Cupid and the other demigods reached the assembly of Indra and told him of the supreme power of Lord Narayana. The residents of heaven listened, and their king trembled in fear for his grievous offence.

Indra repented, but he was not resolute in his devotion to the Lord. As Krsna Himself explains,

*bhogaisvarya-prasaktanam taydpahrta-cetasam*  
*vyavasayatmika buddhih samadhau na vidhiyate*

**“In the minds of those who are too attached to sense enjoyment and material opulence, and who are bewildered by such things, the resolute determination for service to the Lord does not take place.”**

Again and again we see that because of his intense attachment to sense gratification, Indra, though a devotee, misunderstands the Lord’s pastimes and thus challenges His supremacy.

**\* \* \* \***

Four hills surroun Tapovana: Gandhamadana to the north, Kesava to the east, Nisadha to the south, and Sankhakuta to the west. These are the original forms of the mountains that surround Mount Meru.

Vrndavana is Krsnas original abode and home to the original forms of all sacred places — sixty billion in all — that are, in their expanded forms, found throughout the three strata of the universe.

For this reason the Badarinatha of Vraja is called Adi-badri, “the original Badarinatha.” It differs from its expansion, the Badarinatha of the Himalayas, mainly in scale: the former is smaller than the latter.

There is another difference, however, between the two: the benefit gained by visiting Adi-badri is a thousand times greater than that attained by visiting the Himalayan Badarinatha.

The following pastime corroborates this truth. The Vraja-vasis, being naturally religious, often think of visiting sacred places to purify themselves and to ensure their perpetual link with Krsna.

Once, the senior men and women of Vraja decided to go on pilgrimage to the Himalayas, specifically to Badarinatha, Kedaranatha, and the sacred places and rivers connected with them.

When the topic came up amongst the elders at the breakfast table, Krsna explained to His father that all holy places, including Badarinatha and Kedaranatha, are eternally present in Vrndavana.

“Not only that,” Krsna continued, “but by visiting Adi-badri you will get a thousand times the benefit of visiting the Himalayas.” At first neither Nanda Maharaja nor his brothers took Krsna seriously.

But when the other boys, including Balarama, supported Krsnas statement and enthusiastically testified that they too had been to Adi-badri and even seen divine ascetics there, the adults listened.

The Vraja-vasis had first-hand experience of Krsnas divine powers and self-acquired knowledge, so when He offered to take them to Badarinatha that very day, they put aside their chores to follow Him.

Singing songs of Krsna and riding in bullock carts, the Vraja-vasi caravan set out from Nandagrama. They passed Kaman and the open plains and then finally saw a ring of mountains in the distance.

Excited, Krsna tugged on His mother’s hand and declared, “There is Setukandara. Now you will all see, face to face, Lord Narayana.” As the carts stopped at the end of the road, Krsna leaped out.

Holding His father’s hand, Krsna said, “Let Me guide you to Lord Badarinatha.” Surrounded by His exuberant friends and followed hy the good-natured Vraja-vasis, Krsna started the ascent up the valley.

As they walked, Krsna pointed to the trees that stood on either side of them and announced, “This is Tapovana, the forest where many sages, including Nara and Narayana, perform severe austerities.”

Arriving at a small pond, He declared, “This is Gauri-kunda. Because Mother Durga lives here, we should bathe in her lake.” The pilgrims offered prayers and sprinkled water on their heads before going on.

Some distance above Gauri-kunda was another lake called Tapta-kunda. Though not hot, as its name suggests, hut air temperature, this Tapta-kunda is still more purifying than its Himalayan counterpart.

Ahove Tapta-kunda, on a large slab of black granite, sitting in the posture of a yogi rapt in meditation, was a large Deity of Lord Badarinatha, as black as the stone upon which He sat.

Krsna called out, “There! There is the Lord!” The Vraja-vasis looked up, amazed to see what did indeed appear to be the Deity of Badarinatha out in the open on the mountainside.

Palms joined, the Vraja-vasis offered prayers and then bowed down before Badarinaryana, who sat in the padmasana pose, His upraised palm of benediction a sign of good fortune for the pilgrims.

“Just see,” Krsna remarked, “the Lord has manifested Himself from the stone upon which He sits. By the strength of His divine austerities He protects all of Vrndavana, its residents, and our cows.”

For the rest of the day the Vraja-vasis cooked offerings for Badarinatha. They had brahmanas decorate Him with flowers, garlands, and some of the gopis own jewellery. They all felt great bliss.

Krsna took some of the Vraja-vasis to the surrounding mountain peaks and holy sites, such as Haridvara, Hrsikesa, Gandhamadana, Yamunotri, Gangotri, and famous Mana-sarovara.

In the meantime, Nanda Maharaja had a makeshift shelter built above the Deity of the Lord, and as the afternoon shadows fell over Tapovana, the Vraja-vasis reluctantly returned home.

On the way back to Nandagrama the gopas and gopis sang the glories of Vraja, where all holy places and all forms of the Lord reside, especially ladling praise on Krsna for having shown them Adi-badri.

As the sun set and the caravan continued home by torchlight, Krsna fell asleep in the lap of Mother Yasoda, who cradled Him and thought with pride, “Nowhere is there another child like my son.”

This had been the Vraja-vasis’ first trip to Adi-badri, but the gopis had already trekked to Adi-badri after being told by Krsna that Badarinatha and its presiding Deity could be found there.

The gopis great expectations were dashed, however, when they had failed to find the Lord; in fact, an argument broke out amongst them whether Nara-Narayana Rsi was really living there or not.

Suddenly Krsna appeared in their midst and boasted that it was He — as the four-armed brahmacari, Nara-Narayana Rsi — who was always performing severe penances for the welfare of all at Adi-badri.

“Hari,” said Lalita sarcastically, “Your claim to be Nara-Narayana Rsi is almost as credible as Your claim to be an austere bralunacdri” At this the cowherd girls glanced knowingly at one another.

Visakha-devi took her cue: “Considering that You cannot go half a muhurta without eating sweets or touching a gopi, Your claim to be that renounced rsi must be either dream or hallucination.”

Krsna flashed a wry, bemused smile at the gopis and murmured, “Om!" Then He slapped His flute to His left side and, to the gopis’ chastened amazement, transformed Himself into the brahmacari ascetic.

Four-armed Nara-Narayana Rsi, now standing before the gopis, had a blackish complexion, eyes resembling lotus petals, garments made of black deerskin, and an effulgent sacred thread of three strands.

He held a waterpot, bamboo staff, prayer beads, and a bunch of darbha grass in His four hands. His bearing tall, His effulgence the colour of radiant lightning, He appeared to be austerity personified.

Worshipped by the foremost of demigods, the Lord stood in front of the gopis, who humbly bowed down to Him and offered suitable prayers along with the milk products they had brought with them.

Then Nara-Narayana Rsi again transformed into Krsna, who stood with His hands on His hips, appearing quite satisfied with Himself. The gopis approached Him with joined palms and said:

“O son of Yasoda! We do nor know how You perform these wonders. But we request that for the benefit of others, please again manifest the form of that wonderful brahmacari we saw just a moment ago.”

Smiling, Krsna said, “So be it!” Suddenly a slab of black granite nearby began to transform. The form of Nara-Narayana Rsi then appeared, looking just like the form Krsna had displayed.

To dispel any doubt the gopis may have had that this Deity was the Lord Himself, Narayana Rsi moved and spoke transcendental truths to the gopis before again resuming His form in stone.

As mentioned earlier, the area surrounding Adi-badri is home to many sacred places, including the Sarasvati River, the Triveni confluence, and, at the bottom of the valley, the Alakananda Ganga.

This original form of Badarinatha allows us to see the forests and places where Pandu and Madri performed austerities, the Pandavas passed on their way to heaven, and Bhima met the mighty Hanuman.

Srila Vyasadeva lives on the bank of the Sarasvati. It was there that he compiled the Vedas, dictated the Mahabharata to Ganesa, and most important of all, wrote Srimad'Bhagavatam.

In the serene gardens of Tapovana we can also see the place to which Krsna sent Uddhava, having instructed him to teach the saintly pen sons who lived in and around Adi-badri the secrets of vraja-bhakti.

Adi-badri or Badarinatha is so named for its abundant badari trees. As the Lord was performing austerity, Laksmiji once assumed the form of a badari tree to shelter Him; hence His name, Badari-narayana.

On the next sub-petal of New Vraja-dhama is Kedaranatha, the monolithic temple of Sadasiva, where the Pandavas came to free themselves of sins incurred during the Battle of Kuruksetra.

Near the original temple of Kedaranatha constructed by the Pandavas, Sripada Sankaracarya, having completed his thankless mission, entered into samadhi at the age of thirty-two.

It is said that at the conclusion of the Mahabharata war, Maharaja Yudhisthira and his brothers desired to take shelter of Lord Siva to free themselves of the sin of having killed so many soldiers.

But Lord Siva, afraid of the sinful reactions, assumed the form of a bull and ran away from the Pandavas. Where the Kedaranatha temple now stands he dove into the ground, leaving only his hump exposed.

The Pandavas constructed a temple around that hump, which became the foremost of the twelve jyotir-lingas. Unlike other lingas, which are cylindrical, Kedaranatha linga is conical, like the hump of a hull.

In the eighth century, Sahkaracarya constructed another temple beside the original. There he left this world for his own abode after finishing his mission. His samadhi-mandira is also located there.

**\* \* \* \***

The pastime places between Adi-badri and Kedaranatha begin with Pahalabada, an exhilarating place that the cowherd boys would try to forget whenever Krsna used to brag of His achievement there.

It all started as the cows were resting peacefully in the early afternoon. The gopas, brimming over with energy, each began boasting to the others that he was the best wrestler there.

Gopas like Subala, Sridama, and Stoka-krsna inevitably compared themselves to Krsna. And Krsna, not one to be berated, vigorously defended His strength and agility by denouncing all His friends.

“You are all children,” Krsna chided. “You don’t know Me. I can defeat the best of Kamsa’s wrestlers, individually or all together.” At this the boys all rolled their eyes and laughed heartily.

But Krsna was adamant. “If any of you, or all of you, want to fight Me, then come on.” Krsna rose to His feet, walked a few steps into a clearing, bent forward, palms on knees, and called out, “I’m ready!”

Some boys like Subala and Dama rose to their feet, but they stayed with the other boys, uncertain what to do next. “Come on!” Krsna challenged. He was serious. “I’m ready. One at a time, or all at once.”

As more boys rose to their feet, one of them suggested, “Let’s surround Him and attack Him all together.” Another hoy, voice trembling, said, “O brother! You attack first (pahila bada)."

Because the boys could not decide who would lead the charge, in their list of playgrounds this field became known as Pahalabada. And what came of Krsna's challenge and the boys’ bravado?

Slowly all the cowherd boys surrounded Krsna in a large circle, sending up juvenile battle cries, speaking provocative words, slapping their arms, and rubbing dust between their palms.

Undaunted, Krsna hiked up His dhoti and tightened His belt then began whirling round and round, slowly at first and then faster, making noises like the bellowing of a bull or the roaring of a lion.

With assorted complexions and varied dress, the cowherd boys appeared like rings of multicoloured gems circling a sapphire Krsna, all set in the emerald pendant of the surrounding meadow.

Sometimes, as the other boys cheered, one gopa after another would run forward to provoke Krsna hut then return to the outer ring. Sometimes all the boys closed in on Krsna, only to retreat.

During all these sorties Krsna remained calm, facing His adversaries; indeed, as the boys later confirmed with one another, they had each been sure that Krsna was facing off with only him.

Finally, the boys’ taunting reached fever pitch. Seeing Krsna’s face flushed red with excitement, Subala called out, “Charge!” and the boys all ran towards their adversary, yelling, “Get Him! Get Him!”

Dear readers! It may be difficult to describe bow one Krsna expands into many for an orderly dance with the gopis, but I simply cannot explain how He expands to fight with each gopa in a wrestling match.

Just as the gopis could not grasp that Krsna had expanded Himself, thinking that He was moving so fast as to appear to dance with each of them, the boys could not understand that there were many Krsnas.

They honestly thought that Krsna was moving so fast that He threw Subala to the ground, grappled with Sudama, and tripped Kinkini all in the same second, only to move on to Dama and others in the next.

Meanwhile, the boys’ joyful shouts attracted the cows and forest creatures as spectators, and their scuffling raised such a dust cloud that nearby villagers thought Trnavarta had returned to Vraja.

Finally, after one danda had passed, the cowherd boys all lay with their shoulders pinned to the ground, laughing and gasping, “I give up!” The match was over, and Krsna was the winner.

As the boys all got up and brushed themselves and Krsna off, they could not help but garland their friend with countless words of praise, incredulous as to how He could have achieved such a feat.

They collected their cows and headed back to Nandagrama, all the while revelling in their chivalrous loving exchange with Krsna, fondly remembering each of His wrestling holds as loving embraces.

Now nearly home, Sridama still couldn’t help but tease Krsna. “Friend!” he ventured. “You may have defeated us, but I still don’t think You could defeat Kama's wrestlers, even one at a time.”

Krsna gave a knowing smile to Balarama before He turned to Radha’s brother and said, “O friend! One day I will take you to Mathura. Then you can see for yourself. I can surely defeat Kama's best.”

O desire trees of Pahalahada! Please bless all sincere Vaisnavas that they may one day see the brilliant sapphire of Vraja wrestling with His beloved friends beneath your ancient, all-knowing boughs.

Beyond Pahalahada are two places — Bhayari and Kayarika— with one pastime in common: the time the milkmaids of Vrndavana were once lost in the darkness with Krsna as their only beacon.

One day the young girls of Vraja wandered to distant Bhayari, where they decided to start a game of kho. Soon they were so absorbed in their play that they lost all track of time, as children tend to do.

Though still daylight when the girls set off on their way back to Varsana, all holding hands, they hadn’t gone far when darkness engulfed them. It was winter, so nightfall came early and quickly.

The gopis were simple village girls; as such, they were naturally fearfill of both the dark and the threatening sounds of the jungle. This place, then, came to be known as Bhayari, the place of fear (blaya).

To make matters worse, it was a moonless night. And in spite of following the path by which they had come, they were lost, without any form of lamplight or even a clear idea how to get home.

After some time they arrived at a fork in the road and were so perplexed about which direction to go that in desperation they called out for Krsna. Who else would they turn to for protection?

When they called “Krsna! Krsna!” the gopis heard only the echo of their voices reverberating in the trees. Feeling emboldened by that divine name, however, they continued to chant and clap their hands.

The gopis’ cries of distress soon turned into a joyous kirtana, which Sri Krsna heard. At once attracted, He thought, “What is this nocturnal celebration in such a far-flung corner of Vraja?”

Krsna appeared before the gopis like Cupid incarnate, and the girls, overjoyed at the sight of Him, continued to chant Krsna’s glories, holding hands as they danced around Him in a circle.

After some time the gopis regained their composure and explained their predicament. “Krsna! We are lost and have no way to light our way home. Please help us!” Krsna saw an opportunity for some fun.

“When I heard you calling I quickly ran here, following your voices. I know My way hack, hut I don’t have a lamp with Me either.” The gopis shook their heads, dismissing Krsna’s excuse.

“We know that You are the source of the Brahman effulgence,” they said. “The sun and moon are Your eyes. You don’t need a lamp. You arc self-luminous. You are our lamp in the darkness.”

Pleased hy the words of the gopis, Krsna showed His soft bodily effulgence and lit up the forest. He was a living lamp as He walked ahead, and the gopis followed Him continuing their kirtana.

The place where Krsna lit up the forest with the effulgence of His body (kaya) is called Kayarika. Devotees who desire that the Lord light their way out of the darkness of ignorance worship this place.

South of Bhayari is a forest now known as Pasupavana. Within it is a grove called Pasupa where Krsna and Balarama once hid from their friends, only to be discovered by Their clever cows.

As usual, the cowherd boys were absorbed in playing different games like ball and tag. Krsna and Balarama decided to play a trick on Their friends, hiding Themselves in a dense grove in the forest.

At first the gopas thought that the two brothers had gone to search for some stray cows, but when all the cows were accounted for the boys began to worry about the welfare of their friends.

Krsna and Balarama are the very life of the cowherd boys — They are like the air the cowherd boys breathe. In the absence of the brothers, the boys felt as if they were slowly suffocating.

The gopas repeatedly called out, “O Krsna! ? Rama!” They searched the forest, asking the trees, deer, and birds if they had seen Krsna and Balarama. But, try as they might, the gopas could not find Them.

Full of panic, the boys thought the worst. Surely some demon had captured their divine friends. But did a demon exist who could defeat the two heroes? So far every demon had failed.

Soon the boys’ concern spread to the cows, who rose to their feet, lowed yearningly for Krsna and Balarama, and began to search the forest as if they were searching for their own calves.

Some especially clever cows took the lead and followed the scent of Krsna’s and Balarama’s bodies. The gopas saw this and joyfully followed behind the cows, prodding them on.

The small herd of cows, trailed by cheering cowherd boys, snaked through Pasupavana until reaching Krsna’s and Balarama’s hideout, where the cows began lowing, digging the earth, and sniffing bushes.

“They’re in there!” shouted the cowherd boys as they ran into the grove en masse, only to find Krsna and Balarama jumping up and down gleefully at having played such a good trick on Their friends.

The cows (pasu) had found Krsna and Balarama by following the two brothers’ bodily aroma, and the cowherd boys had followed in the cows’ hoof-prints (pa); therefore, this place is called Pasupa.

Krsna is present everywhere, even in our hearts, but we cannot know Him unless — like the cowherd boys — we follow in the footsteps of great souls — like the cows — who already know how to find Krsna.

Quoting a verse of the Mahabharata—dharmasya tattvam nihitam guhayam maha-jano yena gatah sa panthah — Srila Prabhupada writes: “It is very difficult to understand the secret of Krsna consciousness —

“But one who advances by the instruction of the previous acaryas and follows in the footsteps of his predecessors in the line of disciplic succession will have success.” Of this there is no doubt.

On the way home later that afternoon, soft-hearted Devaprastha came up beside Krsna, wrapped his arm around his dear friend’s hack, and said, “O friend! I beg You, please do not try to hide from us again.

“We curse the creator because, during the time it takes our eyes to blink, we cannot see You. When You and Balarama disappear for such a long time our life airs rise to our throats, ready to leave our bodies.

“Don’t think that putting us in such danger is a game. Seeing You is our very life. When we cannot sec You, it is as if we are dead. Please keep this in mind the next time You feel inspired to hide from us.”

All glories to the cowherd boyfriends of Sri Krsna! All glories to their fraternal love, which one can attain only through spontaneous loving service, not by countless lifetimes of austerity or sacrifice.

**\* \* \* \***

Further south of Pasupa and due east of Kamyavana are two places connected to Krsna’s amorous pastimes with the cowherd damsels of Vrndavana: the gardens of Karamuka and Virara.

Karamuka is a place where Radha and Krsna enact Their pastime of meeting, a pastime of which the variety is unlimited — as we will soon see — and which, even when repeated, always appears fresh.

One type of meeting occurs when Radha and Krsna arrive from different places, eager to meet. Another type is when either of Them is late, which adds an element of exhilaration to Their union.

A third kind of meeting happens when Radha comes to the grove where Krsna is waiting hut is overcome by shyness upon seeing Him. Her friends, or Krsna, must then keep Her from leaving.

An overpoweringly emotional type of meeting takes place when Radha has been waiting faithfully all night, and Krsna, waylaid by another gopi, finally arrives, showing marks of infidelity on His body.

Spurred on by Lalita, Radha turns Him away, saying, hari han yahi madhavo, yahi kesava md vada kaitava vadam: “O Hari! Madhava! Kesava! Go away from here and spare Me Your lies and half-truths.”

Radha later regrets having treated Krsna so harshly. Consequently, the next time They meet She is apologetic and submissive. Eager to make up with Krsna, She is quick to requite His displays of affection.

In other unique kinds of meetings, the aforementioned situations could he combined in any number of ways or even take place all at one time, thus giving pleasure to Krsna through endless variety.

For example, Radha and Krsna may simultaneously embrace in one meeting, exchange jokes with the gopis in another, stroll by the Yamuna alone in yet another, and part ways in still yet another.

Next to Karamuka is Virata, which is known hy that name for being a resting place (visrama) of Sri Krsna, a resting place that was carefully prepared for Him hy the loving hands of the Vraja gopis.

Once, after Krsna had danced with a multitude of cowherd girls, He and the girls swam in a lake and ate a variety of forest fruits and nuts to refresh themselves, but Krsna still looked tired. The gopis thought that He needed more rest, and so within a quiet grove they made a comfortable bed tor Krsna with a thick mattress of aromatic flowers and covered it with a spotless white cotton sheet.

Krsna is the source of strength for all living things and certainly not in need of rest, hut He accepted the gopis’ offering of love. While a few girls fanned Him, Krsna entered into the trance of yoga-nidra.

The fatigue that Krsna or His eternal companions display in the course of their pastimes must not he confused with the material fault also called fatigue, one of eighteen faults conditioned souls possess.

Krsna’s fatigue is one of thirty-three symptoms of overwhelming ecstatic love. As is lamentation or humility, it may be caused by dancing, losing one’s way, or any number of other circumstances.

When Radha and Krsna first fall in love, for example, They become fatigued and listless because of Their mutual loving attraction, and only Their meeting can fully remove that fatigue.
