Moreover, by showing fatigue, Krsna, depending on the circumstances, gives a variety of opportunities for His servants, friends, mother, girlfriends, and even Sri Radha to serve Him.

For instance, Radha and Krsna, once tired from an afternoon of walking, swimming, and playing dice, had lain down within a beautiful pavilion overgrown with flowering vines.

At that time gopis massaged Their lotus feet, fanned Them, and provided refreshing drinks. The girls were thus able to show their love for Radha and Krsna through varieties of practical service.

Krsna is also pleased to see His eternal friends display the ecstasy of fatigue and its symptoms, thus showing their determination to serve Him in all circumstances and in spite of all impediments.

For example, in the particular rasa dance just mentioned, the gopis were also fatigued hy exertion. Their clothes became loose, their chests heaved, and their faces dripped with perspiration.

The sight of the gopis’ fatigue pleased Krsna even more than the dancing did, and so He brought the festival to a close in order to let them rest, even though they at once went about serving Him.

Adamant that His beloveds be refreshed, Krsna took them into the water, just as a male elephant does with his wives. Such are the exchanges of love between the Lord and His devotees.

By meditating on Krsna’s resting place in Virara — which is beautified by blossoming trees, unknown to the Upanisads, and worshipped by demigods — we can be relieved of the fatigue of material life.

One summer day, after the above incident at Pasupa, as they were tending their cows in the vicinity, Krsna and the cowherd boys came upon the forest orchard now known as Badali.

At midday the weather had turned unbearably hot. The boys and cows sought out the coolest shade they could find and waited for the heat to subside. But it didn’t. And it felt like it would last forever.

Krsna was lying down, His head in the lap of a cowherd boy. Two other boys fanned Him with a leafy tree branch rich with blossoming flowers while another two boys massaged His hands and feet.

From a hoy lying next to Krsna in the shade came a request that sounded almost like a moan: “Gopala! You have shown us Your mystic powers in the past. Can’t You do something about this heat?

“It’s so hot that we can hardly breathe. A refreshing rain or cooling breeze would be just the remedy for this distressing situation. Look! Just look at Your friends and cows and see how they are suffering.”

Krsna is omniscient, but until that moment He had seemed not to notice the discomfort of His friends. Now moved to tend to their welfare, He played on His flute, calling the clouds for their assistance.

No sooner had Krsna beckoned than a cloud appeared above Him, gradually expanding itself then rising high in the sky to form an umbrella that blocked out the powerful glare of the sun.

Then, as a cooling breeze began to blow from the north, tiny flowerlike droplets of water showered down upon the area around Krsna and His friends, who all embraced their saviour with grateful glances.

In a matter of moments the atmosphere around Badali felt just like spring. The gopas’ fatigue and discomfort flew far away, and the cows rose to their feet to graze while being bathed in the refreshing rain.

The gopas happily praised the clouds as Krsna’s true friends, citing the gopis, who often note the similarity between Krsna and the clouds — they both remove distress by a downpour of nectar.

They are both blackish in colour, and they both display lightning and thunder — for Krsna, His yellow dhoti and flute-song respectively. In this way the clouds give pleasure to their friend Krsna.

Overwhelmed by affection for Krsna, the cowherd boys embraced Him again and again, not because He had relieved their suffering, but because He had once more done something unexpectedly wonderful.

Krsna’s astonishing deeds increase His loved ones’ affection, which in turn inspires greater admiration for His smallest acts of wonder, not to speak of the true miracles He performs.

Therefore, because the clouds appeared here at Krsna’s beck and call to release a pleasant and cooling rainfall (badala), the cowherd boys named this place Badali, and they always remember it fondly.

**\* \* \* \***

Within the eastern petal of the lotus of New Vraja-dhama is the village Kanahadi, not to he confused with Kananbada near Kamyavana, where Queen Radha gave an audience to Krsna and the gopas.

It is said that one day the cowherd boys were playing and tending their cows when it came to everyone’s notice that one gopa had not come to join them that day. Naturally, everyone was concerned.

Was the boy ill? Perhaps he had been attacked by a demon on the way. Anxious for his welfare, Krsna pacified the others and came to this village with a few gopas, in search of His missing friend.

As soon as the boys arrived at the village Krsna turned to Subala and asked, “Where does he live?” Subala shrugged his shoulders and looked at some houses nearby as if to say, “Somewhere around here.”

Krsna at once went house to house, asking each family, “Is this the house of My friend? Is this the house of My friend?” (kena badi). For this, the village was renamed Kanabadi.

One of Krsna’s countless qualities — the one most praised by devotees— is that He is controlled hy love (prema-vasyah). His concern for the well-being of His friend in Kanabadi is clear proof.

In another example, Krsna received His classmate Sudama Vipra, who was so poor that all he could afford to bring the Lord was a small portion of stale chipped rice, practically unacceptable as an offering.

But Krsna was bound to the brahmana Sudama by affection, so much so that He accepted the chipped rice, completely ignoring its inferior quality, and blessed His friend with unlimited wealth.

Did Krsna find His friend in Kanabadi? He must have! Had he not, He would still he in search of Him right now. Such is Krsna’s regard for His devotees, especially for His eternal companions.

South of Kanabadi is the flowering forest of Papadivana, named for its central grove, Papadi, where the gopis received and decorated Radha and Krsna with an assortment of petal (papadi) ornaments.

Radha and Krsna are to he offered an abundance of flowers in Their temples, as They are in Their pastimes, for Their Lordships are forest dwellers and prefer the natural opulence of Vraja: fragrant flowers.

And while flower ornaments and garlands are essential in service, flower-petal ornaments and garlands may he said to he the essence of that essence, as they are the flowers’ softest and most colourful part.

Furthermore, making things from petals is more labour-intensive and time consuming than making them from whole flowers. The divine couple therefore see special affection and caring in such efforts.

This was precisely the gopis' purpose in making not only ornaments, hut also a throne, umbrella, and festoons from flower petals. They wanted to proclaim their unbounded love for the divine couple.

When Radha and Krsna saw these special arrangements They were deeply moved, and They responded to Their friends by submitting to whatever was requested of Them, like wearing flower-petal crowns.

People sometimes ask, “If Krsna consciousness is non-violent, then why do we take the lives of so many flowers for worship, as in the example of the pastime in Papadi? Why not let them live?”

First, let us consider whether plucking a flower from a plant is actually taking a life. Technically, when we pick a flower from a plant, or a fruit from a tree, the plant or tree doesn’t die.

But accepting, tor the sake of argument, that we do take a life when we pick a flower, we must first consider that everything in this world, although temporary, has its purpose in the service of the Lord.

Krsna is the source of everything and everyone. So the purpose of everything and everyone in this world is to serve Krsna, just as the purpose of the hand is to serve the whole body.

To separate something from this inherent service is unnatural, and therefore violent, even if that thing lives a longer life. Reconnecting everything to Krsna, then, is compassion and true non-violence.

The purpose of flowers is to decorate the bodies of Krsna and His devotees! The souls of flowers used in this way attain devotee births, or liberation, or both. And that is their perfection.

Also, as a result of this service, flowers and flower petals are eternally glorified by beautiful poetry, especially the works of Vaisnavas, who compare those fortunate blossoms to Krsna’s feet, navel, and eyes.

Such flowers are eternally connected to Krsna; indeed, Vaisnavas address Krsna as Vanamali, the “forest gardener,” or “He who wears forest flowers.” Consequently, such flowers are eternally blessed.

When people justify the daily slaughter of millions of animals so they can gratify their senses — poisoning their own bodies and accruing heaps of sin — to complain about offering flowers to Krsna is petty.

Next on the eastern petal of New Vraja-dhama is Satavasa. Due cast of Kamaravana, Satavasa is first to catch the rising sun, which is fitting since Srimati Radharani worships the sun here.

At Satavasa, on the bank of a lake known as Surya-kunda, Satrajit constructed a temple for the sun god. There he worshipped the sun to attain an immeasurable source of wealth and an incomparable child.

Satrajit was austere and determined but impure at heart, and so the goals he attained from his worship would later he his undoing. Ultimately they were the cause of his death.

Rupa Gosvami reveals that Radharani was so distraught when Krsna left Vraja that She drowned Herself, entering the Lord’s unmanifest pastimes and also the sun planet as an amsa expansion.

That amsa expansion was Satyabhama. She and the Syamantaka jewel, which Krsna had taken from Sankhacuda and given to Radha, would stay with the sun god until they were both awarded to Satrajit.

The same Radha who regularly worshipped and enacted pastimes with Krsna at Surya-kunda became a ward of the sun god, who felt most fortunate to render service to his worshipable Deity.

While residing in the sun, Radha felt acute separation from Krsna. Even though Samjna, the wife of the sun god, thought her husband was non-different from Krsna, Radha was not convinced.

Samjna said, "O Radha! ? lotus-faced girl who fills the worlds with bliss! Do not be unhappy. Your beloved Lord, who is the object of saintly devotees’ meditation, eternally resides in this sun planet.”

Laughing, the daughter of the sun god, Kalindi, replied to her mother, “O Mother Samjna! Let me give you an example of the gopis' exclusive feelings of love for Sri Krsna in ecstatic parama-rasa.

“Krsna once manifested Himself as Narayana, with tour hands and a very beautiful form. But when the gopis saw this exalted form their ecstatic feelings abated. Even a learned scholar —

“Cannot understand the gopis’ ecstasy, which is tirmly centred upon the original form of Sri Krsna as the son of Maharaja Nanda, and which constitutes the greatest mystery of spiritual life.”

The sun god could see into the future. He knew that Radha would be Krsna’s queen Satyabhama in Dvaraka, so he arranged that Satrajit would deliver Her into the hands of the Supreme Lord.

To facilitate Satyabhama’s Vraja mood and to ensure that the stream of Radha’s and Krsna’s pastimes could continue to flow at Dvaraka, Samjna requested her father Visvakarma to build Nava-Vrndavana.

Finally, in response to Satrajit’s worship, the lord of the sun affectionately gave his devotee two gifts: the jewel of Sankhacuda and Satyabhama-devi. Surya also instructed Satrajit:

“Follow Narada’s direction and give this beautiful girl in marriage to the most exalted person. By doing so you will become famous. And by worshipping this jewel you will gain eight bharas of gold daily.”

Satrajit couldn’t believe his good fortune. He began to worship the Syamantaka jewel, keeping Satyabhama within his palace. As foretold, the jewel produced eight bharas of gold every day.

But unlimited wealth blinded Satrajit. He forgot the goal of life and his spiritual duties along with it. Foolishly, he began wearing the Syamantaka jewel in public to impress the innocent masses.

In pursuance of Narada’s instruction to offer his daughter to the Supreme Person, Satrajit moved his family to Dvaraka, where he continued to worship and make a show of the Syamantaka gem.

Coming to know that Satrajit had constructed himself an opulent home where brahmanas had installed and were worshipping the jewel, Krsna decided it was time to bring Satrajit to his senses.

He advised Satrajit to give the jewel to Maharaja Ugrasena. But Satrajit was blinded by greed. He could neither heed the Lord’s advice nor understand what sort of offence he had committed by refusing.

**\* \* \* \***

After Satrajit had rejected Krsna’s request that the Syamantaka jewel be placed in the royal treasury, Narada visited Satrajit to warn him what would happen now that he had refused to follow the Lord.

The great sage said, “O sinful Satrajit! Due to your offence to the Lord, a lion will kill your brother Prasena. The Syamantaka jewel will then fall into the hands of Jambavan, the king of the Bhallukas.

“Eventually the Supreme Lord Krsna will retrieve your valuable jewel from Jambavan. But that will not be the end of your woes, which like the rip tide of an ocean will pull you into its depths and drown you.”

Satrajit replied, “A person burning in a fire can be saved by the intervention of the fire god, Agni; similarly, although I have offended Lord Krsna, I will be saved by taking shelter of that same Lord.”

These words of evil-minded Satrajit implied that he would offer Satyabhama’s hand to Krsna in marriage, and in that way win the Lord’s favour and counteract the offence of disobeying Him.

Satrajit was, in effect, promoting the tenth offence against the holy name. Narada was aware of Satyabhama’s suffering in separation from Krsna, but the time for the two to meet had not yet arrived.

Thus he replied, “O crooked-hearted Satrajit! The time has not yet come for you to offer your long-suffering daughter Satyabhama to the Lord. Until it does, keep Her safely in the palace.”

As the sage had predicted, Prasena was killed and the Syamantaka gem lost. Satrajit then compounded his offences by spreading the vicious rumour that Krsna had killed his brother to get the jewel.

To clear His name, the Lord reclaimed the jewel from His devotee Jambavan, gaining the hand of Jambavan’s daughter Jambavati in the process, and then returned to Dvaraka to put all rumours to rest.

Krsna summoned Satrajit and publicly returned the Syamantaka gem to him. Fearing a conflict with the Lord’s devotees, Satrajit offered both the gem and his beautiful daughter to the Lord.

Krsna accepted Satyabhama, bringing her quest for the Lord of her life to an end. But, knowing His new father-in-law's heart, the Lord encouraged Satrajit to continue worshipping the gem in his home.

Krsna’s devotees in Dvaraka, however, could not tolerate Satrajit’s offences to the Lord. Consequently Akrura and Krtavarma conspired against Satrajit and convinced Satadhanva to kill him.

Akrura, fearing that he would be punished for his intrigue, then fled Dvaraka and took up residence in Kasi; thus he had to live separated from Krsna for many years, just like the gopis who had cursed him.

Meanwhile, some of Dvaraka’s residents were accusing Krsna of having banished Akrura from the city. In response, Krsna asked Akrura to return and help clear the Lord’s name.

When the Dvaraka-vasis saw how intimate the Lord was with Akrura, and that Satyabhama — the legal heiress to Satrajit’s wealth —had no interest in the Syamantaka gem, they gave up criticising the Lord.

Sukadeva Gosvami concludes this pastime with the following benediction:

**“This narration, rich with descriptions of the prowess of Lord Visnu, removes sinful reactions and bestows all auspiciousness.**

**“Anyone who recites, hears, or remembers it will drive away his own infamy and sins and attain peace.”**

In other words, hearing this story will destroy one’s bad reputation and the sins that caused it.

Those whose reputations have been tarnished because of the sins they have committed may come to Satavasa, recall the pastime of the Syamantaka jewel, and be relieved of their miseries.

It’s fascinating that Krsna’s own relatives — the Dvaraka-vasis — could repeatedly malign He whom they knew to be the Supreme Lord to such an extent that the Lord had to personally clear His own name.

The story of the Syamantaka jewel shows that no matter how perfect one may be — and who can be more perfect than Lord Krsna — no one in this world is above criticism, especially when money is involved.

Moreover, if we disobey Krsna and then refuse to rectify ourselves even after receiving good counsel, our lives are doomed regardless of any material formula we may embrace for protection.

The Syamantaka jewel was so powerful that wherever it was worshipped, that place and its residents would be free of calamities like untimely death and the presence of deceitful persons.

But despite Satrajit’s careful worship of the gem, the effect was nullified by his offences to Krsna, from which nothing could save him. Thus he had to die an untimely death at the hands of a traitor.

While visiting Satavasa, devotees carefully consider the orders they have received from the Lord, avoid any gossip about others, stay far distant from excessive wealth, and recall the Syamantaka pastime.

On the tip of the eastern petal of New Vraja-dhama, just beside the outside parikrama-marga, is the garden called Sita-Rama, where Radha and Krsna played as the exiled king and queen of Ayodhya.

Lord Ramacandra and His wife, Sita, appeared more than two million years ago, and yet even today all of India knows Their story; in fact, children still joyfully act out Their pastimes in play.

And five thousand years ago such play was far more common. The cowherd boys would repeatedly enact the Ramayana, and the gopis would ask the divine couple to show Themselves as Sita and Rama.

Upon arriving in this charming forest, one gopi mentioned that the atmosphere was just like the forest of Pancavati where Sita and Rama happily lived together in exile along with Rama’s brother Laksmana.

The forest blossomed with flowers, birds sang everywhere, the waterways brimmed with pure nectar, and wild animals moved peacefully amongst each other and the cowherd girls.

The girls pointed out a small grove that resembled a hermit’s cottage nestled in the lap of a small hill. One gopi said, “Look! It’s a cottage just like the one Laksmana built for Sita and Rama. Let’s go inside.”

Radha and Krsna were always happy to accommodate Their friends and always ready to participate in a new diversion, especially one touched by the purity and renown of Lord Ramacandra’s pastimes.

And so when the gopis asked to decorate Radha and Krsna like Sita and Rama, the divine couple happily agreed. They sat upon a flower cot, placing Themselves completely at Their friends’ disposal.

First the gopis offered Radha and Krsna very simple outfits, like the clothes ascetics would wear, and tied Krsna’s hair in a topknot that resembled the crown of the king of sages.

As young gopis made flower ornaments, older ones brought them to Radha and Krsna and insisted, “Sita and Rama had no maidservants; They ornamented each other. Won’t You do the same?”

Radha and Krsna faced each other, and the gopis handed Them ornaments, one after the other. Allowed by the circumstances, the young lovers took great pleasure in the chance to touch each other.

Greatly pleased to see the divine couple’s loving exchanges and Their artistic skills at ornamenting each other, the gopis sang of the exploits of Lord Rama and His joyful life in Pancavati forest.

As the gopis’ singing submerged Radha and Krsna in the identities of Sita and Rama, They forgot They were play-acting. They truly thought of Themselves as the storied exiles from Ayodhya.

Seeing the divine couple transformed, the gopis placed a large bow and a quiver of arrows made from flowers beside Krsna, withdrew to a corner of the cottage, and continued with their songs.

The gopis’ mystic narration of Lord Ramacandra’s exploits transformed Radha and Krsna into Sita and Rama, for whom the gopis’ songs were not songs but the unveiling of actual pastimes.

And the more absorbed Radha and Krsna became in the pastimes of Pancavati forest, the more They began to resemble Sita and Rama; Syama’s cloud-blue complexion, for instance, turned grass-green.

**\* \* \* \***

The gopis sang, “Seeing Agastya Muni’s asrama, Laksmana approached a disciple of his and, after introducing Rama, Sita, and Himself, requested the privilege of an audience with the great sage.

“The disciple asked Laksmana to wait while he informed his guru. Agastya was displeased to hear that the Lord and His consort had been made to wait and demanded They be escorted in at once.

“The three divinities were brought before the sage, who, surrounded by many disciples, shone like the sun surrounded by planets. The two brothers reverentially touched Agastya’s feet.

“The great sage knew of Rama’s identity. He offered his divine guests water for washing Their hands and feet, enquired about Their health, offered Them sears, and fed Them whatever food was available.

“After his guests had finished Their meal, Agastya went into his cottage and returned with an armful of weapons that blazed with divine splendour and placed them before Rama one by one, saying:

‘“Here is Visnu’s how made by Visvakarma, studded with gold and diamonds; here is Brahma’s arrow, which never misses its mark; here are Siva’s two inexhaustible quivers; and here is Lord Visnu’s sword.

“‘Please accept these weapons in Your fight against evil. Employ them as Indra uses his thunderbolt.’ Thereafter Agastya sat with his guests. His eyes full of tears, voice trembling, the great sage said:

“‘O Rama! ? Laksmana! You have taken so much trouble to visit me. I see that You are perspiring, and the daughter of Janaka, being young and unaccustomed to the trials of jungle life, is clearly tired.

“‘Women are wont to abandon a man in his time of distress, but this wife of Yours is free from defects. She is like Arundhat! amongst the demigods. Wherever You three stay is a sacred place, without doubt.’”

The gopis continued, “Upon hearing such kind words, Rama stood and enquired, ‘O preceptor! Kindly direct Us to a region with sufficient water and trees in which to establish Our hermitage.’

“Agastya thought for awhile and then said, ‘Some distance from here is the beautiful forest of Pancavati, full of roots, fruits, water, and abundant with deer. Build Your cottage there and live peacefully.’

“After offering Their respects, Rama, Laksmana, and Sita followed the sage’s direction and in time reached Pancavati forest, which was frequented by many birds and always abloom with fragrant flowers.

“On the way to Pancavati forest, the threesome met a friend of King Dasaratha’s, the giant vulture Jatayu, who made a pact with the trio to always protect Sita in the absence of the two brothers.

“Rama wandered the forest and selected a beautiful level place surrounded hy flowering trees and also not far from the Godavari River. There He requested Laksmana to build a cottage.”

As the gopis sang, Krsna — now fully absorbed in His role as Rama — continued the song as if He were speaking directly to Laksmana. “O brother!” sang Krsna. “Not too close and not too far is the Godavari.

“It is teeming with waterfowl and frequented hy herds of deer. Over there are lofty mountains with flowering trees, and scattered about are flakes of gold, silver, and copper embellishing the soft ground.”

Krsna looked to the right of Radha, as if seeing Laksmana, and motioning with His hand, said, “Look! The mountains abound with an unlimited variety of vines, fruit trees, and flowering hushes.

“This place is pious and enjoyable, a most fitting abode for Us and Our friend Jatayu.” As Krsna spoke, the gopis were amazed to see before them all the things of which they and Krsna had sung.

Huddled in a corner of the grove, the gopis were no longer bards singing of a pastime, but observers of Radha’s and Krsna’s absorption in rama-lila, made visible to them by the mercy of the divine couple.

Now it was Krsna who sang the songs of Pancavati: “Laksmana quickly constructed a cottage with walls made of mud reinforced by log pillars and a roof with branches fastened to bamboo poles.

“After bathing in the Godavari, Laksmana returned and, with an offering of fruit and mantras, performed the rituals recommended in scripture for a new residence. Then He showed it to Sita and Rama.

“Sita was delighted with the cottage, and so Rama embraced His brother and said, ‘O Laksmana! When I have You hy My side, I feel that My father is with Me, and thus the world is at My feet.’”

In divine trance, Krsna took Radha’s hand, walked out of Their cottage — which now looked just like the one constructed by Laksmana — and began to stroll with Her through the forest.

The gopis followed Radha and Krsna, not quite believing that they were actually part of Sita’s and Rama’s pastimes, which they had repeatedly heard before but were now seeing with their own eyes.

And just as Radha and Krsna had transformed into Sita and Rama, Vrndavana had become Pancavati forest. No longer was this a garden that merely resembled Pancavati. It was Pancavati.

As the gopis lost themselves in the marvel of what they saw, felt, and heard, Krsna said, “Thus Rama lived happily in the forest. Served by Sita and Laksmana, He was like an immortal god in the heavens."

Krsna continued, “Autumn turned to winter as They resided happily at Pancavati. Then, early one morning, as Rama and Sita went to the Godavari to bathe, Laksmana came to Them and said, ‘O brother!

‘“Your favourite season has arrived! Crops blanket the land, milk is in surplus, grains overflow the granaries, and fire has become more gratifying tor its heat than water is for its coolness.

‘“Noontime will be good for strolling now because the sunshine will be pleasantly warm. But the nights will be too cold tor sleeping outside, and the dawn will be heavy, with fog and a chill in the air.

“‘In the mornings the grass will be wet with frost, the flowers and leaves covered in dew, the rivers discernible only by the sound of their waves, and the weak sun slow to clear the fog.

“‘Just think of Your younger brother Bharata, who out of devotion to You practises austerities outside the city. He has renounced all enjoyment, sleeping on the cold bare earth and eating only fruits.

“‘As we are going to the Godavari, He must be going to the Sarayu to take bath in the company of His ministers. Such a tender youth, raised in comforts, has accepted the hardships of an ascetic.”’

Krsna was emotional as He recited Laksmana’s song of Bharata, calling out, “O Bharata! ? bashful brother who speaks truthfully and sweetly, who is friendly to everyone and so dutiful.

“I can easily bear My own austere life. But thinking of the austerities You have embraced to share this kind of life with Me is too much for My heart to bear. ? Bharata! Why have We been separated like this?”

Krsna’s sudden sadness at being separated from Bharata silenced the singing birds and stilled the wind. The cowherd girls watched intently, anticipating what would happen next.

Radha, in the role of submissive Sita, stroked Her husband’s powerful arm reassuringly and said, “My Lord! At the end of the exile You can pamper Your Bharata with the opulences of Ayodhya.”

These words pacified Krsna, and the divine couple continued to walk through the forest, singing of Their own pastimes until, hy Yogamaya’s arrangement, Their rama-lila trance was broken.

The gopis rarely see the divine couple so absorbed in an identity other than Their own, and thus they could hardly do anything more than follow Radha and Krsna, watching and listening.

Even as Radha and Krsna and the gopis headed home, they were all silent due to being absorbed in the memory of the glorious pastimes of Sita and Rama in beautiful Pancavati forest.

Some days later, as the gopis exchanged accounts of what they had seen and heard, they concluded that the place where Radha and Krsna became Sita and Rama actually was Pancavati forest.

And the cottage in which the divine couple had decorated each other as forest exiles has become known as Sita-Rama, a place devotees may visit to enter deeply into the pastimes of Krsna as Lord Ramacandra.

**\* \* \* \***

South of Sita-Rama is a garden called Vanacariya, where, in Krsna’s absence, the cowherd boys once discussed His vama, wondering whether Krsna was actually a cowherd or a forest dweller.

The gopas had brought their cows to the rich pastures of this area, and Krsna, as usual, had left them with Madhu on the plea of seeing the forest, a plea some gopas knew to he an excuse to meet the gopis.

While Krsna was away a group of boys gathered within this garden. As they rested comfortably, they talked about their friend, and in the course of the conversation one boy asked, half-jokingly:

“I have a doubt! The duty of our caste is to protect cows. For this we are known as gopas. Is Krsna also a gopa like us, or is He a forestdweller who happens to take care of cows as well? I am not sure.”

Another boy answered, “What makes you think that Gopala, with whom we herd cows every day, could be anything other than a gopa?” “Hear! Hear!” some of the other boys chimed in.

“Dear friends!” the first boy replied. “Consider this: We live in villages where we house our cows, tend our gardens and orchards, and come to the pastures during the day. Then we go home.

“But Krsna’s life is different.” The gopas knew this boy to be levelheaded, and so they were interested to hear his explanation. “Krsna comes cowherding with us every morning. No argument there.

“Then, during the day—just like now — He goes off ‘to see the beauty of the forest,’ which we know includes seeing the beauty of our sisters, who are always loitering somewhere nearby to catch His attention.

“And what does Krsna do while with our sisters? He wears garlands of forest flowers, strolls with them in the forests beside the Yamuna, and rests with them in forest groves on flower cots.

“Finally, when Krsna returns to us, what does He look like?” The boy caught the eyes of his friends to emphasise his point. “Well! What does He look like?” The gopas blurted out, “A sylvan deity.”

“Precisely!” confirmed the boy. “He’s covered head to toe in flowers, flower decorations, and more. And He’s usually dressed in new clothes from one of Vrnda-devi’s storehouses.

“So not only does He prefer to look like a forest dweller, His wardrobe is also in the forest — ” “But Krsna doesn’t live in the forest,” another boy interrupted, “He lives in Nandagrama.” The first boy continued:

“For some time! But not long enough for you to call it His home.” “What do you mean?” the gopas chimed in. “Dear friends!” the boy continued. “Just consider Krsna’s daily schedule:

“He returns to Nandagrama with us, milks the cows, bathes, enjoys His supper and evening entertainment— ” “And then goes to sleep,” added another gopa, as if disproving the hoy’s argument.

But the first gopa continued undaunted. “Exactly! He goes to sleep. But then what does He do? He wakes up, climbs out His window, and runs off again to Vrndavana forest for the night.

“And only in the wee hours of the morning does He return, pretending that He has been sleeping in His bed all night long.” The boy clapped his hands to indicate he was coming to a point and said:

“So! Does Krsna really reside in Nandagrama? No! He spends His days and nights in the forest, and only comes home to bathe, to have two cooked meals, and to collect and drop off His father’s cows.

“Krsna uses Nandagrama like an actor uses his dressing room. Krsna lives in the forest. He just visits His family and kinsmen. That’s why I say He’s really a forest dweller that just happens to tend cows.

“Gopala is not a gopa like us, who live in villages and herd cows in the forest. This is my conclusion.” Having given his assessment, the wise gopa stopped speaking, inviting others to comment.

The cowherd boys were abuzz like a beehive poked with a stick, arguing back and forth in smaller groups and raising a din in which the word vana-cari danced on their lips and echoed through the trees.

Who knows where the gopas’ debate would have led. Perhaps even to a scuffle! But, just as things looked as if they might get out of hand, the sound of a flute cut through clamour — Krsna had arrived.

All of a sudden the boys were deathly silent. Krsna looked around suspiciously and asked, “What are you talking about?” The boys were baffled for a moment, but the instigator soon blurted out, “You!”

The other boys at once surrounded Krsna, all calling out, “You! You! You!" Krsna was still suspicious and uneasy, but as the boys embraced Him one by one, or many at a time, He could not help but smile.

And for the cowherd boys, the sight of Krsna was like a full moon causing tides of love to crash upon the shores of doubt and submerge all concern about His vama. Was He a gopa or a vana-cari?

Ultimately, whatever Krsna was, He was the only beloved of the gopas’ hearts. So intense and unconditional was their love for Him that it could accommodate His being a gopa, a vana-cari, even God.

Raghunatha Dasa Gosvami glorifies and worships these cowherd boyfriends of Krsna with these words: “Affectionately protected by Sri Krsna, their hearts rapt in meditation —

“On the particles of dust falling from the tips of His handsome lotus toenails, the gopas relish a happiness greater than the happiness of many millions of universes. I worship these exalted *gopas*.

Further south from Vanacariya, in a nook of the next sub-petal of New Vraja-dhama, lies the grove called Parehi, where Krsna told the gopis that He was fed up with their taunting Him.

The gopis and Krsna had agreed to meet at Parehi, and before Krsna’s arrival the girls had prepared the grove with floral decorations, aromatic incense, a soft sitting place, and nectarean fruit juices.

On His way through the forest Krsna had thought of a way to make new waves in the ocean of the gopis' love for Him. Therefore, upon arriving, He feigned indifference to the girls’ cheerful greeting.

The gopis were confused. Krsna did not even accept their invitation to enter the grove; instead, He stood outside the doorway and spoke to the gopis from there, “Young ladies! Please listen.

“When I arrived here I could tell from the smiles on your faces that you plan to, yet again, ridicule Me or perhaps set Me up for one of your tired village jokes. But I won’t have any more of that behaviour.”

The gopis were stunned at Krsna’s allegations. Speechless, they stood where they were as He wagged His forefinger at them. Krsna continued, now picking up speed. “You play innocent now —

“But how can you be? Time and again, you steal My flute and hide it in your braids, or accuse Me of being dull-witted for spending too much time with the cows!” Krsna was just getting started.

“You may appear to welcome Me, but where is that hospitality when I have walked through a thorn-filled jungle in the dead of night, and after My long sacrifice, you turn Me away at the door to some grove?

“And this silence! Where is your silence when, after dressing and ornamenting yourselves in ways that make demigods fall from the sky, I express appreciation for your beauty only to be called a debauchee?

“Some may think you kind, but that kindness is a ruse: You challenge Me — a prince who is duty-bound to accept any challenge —to a game of dice and then gang up on Me and call Me a cheat when you lose!

“I can see My words have had a sobering effect on you, but how sober can you be when you forest-maids tempt Me with honey-wine so that you can later accuse Me of being a drunkard and a libertine?”

Most of the gopis were reeling from Krsna’s scathing reproach, but a few were about to defend themselves when Krsna dropped one last bombshell. “Before you say anything,” He warned, “listen to this.

“If you pull any more of your tricks, if you berate or belittle Me in any way, then you won’t see Me any more. Is that clear?” And with that Krsna stopped speaking and simply stared at the gopis.

With the words “you won’t see Me,” the forest went deathly quiet. Even the more contrary gopis who were about to argue held their breath. What did He mean, “You won’t see Me any more?”

The gopis did not want to know. Such a threat was not to be questioned, and certainly not challenged. After several long moments the wind moved the leaves of the trees, breaking the silence.

Birds chirped, streams bubbled, and the other forest sounds resumed as soft background music to the gopis’ unified voice. “Syamasundara!” they said. “It will always be as You wish.”

Pleased by the gopis’ submissive attitude and exulting within for having achieved His purpose, Krsna entered the grove, where the gopis waited on Him hand and foot as maidservants wait on a king.

Because Krsna had threatened the gopis that if they joked with Him again they would not see Him afterwards (pure), the gopis called this grove Parehi. Although it is a beautiful place, they never returned.

**\* \* \* \***

Not far from Parehi, at the tip of the same sub-petal, are Pai and Calana-sila, two places connected together by the same pastime: a game of hide-and-seek in which Radha caught Krsna.

Childhood and boyhood precede — but also lend their qualities to — the divine couple’s adolescence; thus, in youth Radha and Krsna also engage in the sporting activities of these preceding ages.

Inspired by memories of boyhood and girlhood, Krsna and the gopis also play games like ball and hide-and-seek during their adolescence. Vrndavana is filled with places connected to such pastimes.

But the difference in the games of pure childhood and the same games played during adolescence is that in adolescence the amorous mood adds a dimension that makes such games even more relishable.

And because those games arc imbued with the amorous mellow, they nourish the divine couple with transitory and continuous ecstasies, ecstasies more intimate than those found in earlier ages.

In simple language, the enjoyment that Radha and Krsna derive from playing children’s games in Their adolescence is greater than when They played those very same games when They were children.

A resolution of the varying ways in which Krsna’s ages are described by scripture and acaryas is presented in the appendix entitled “Deliberation on the Ages of Krsna and His Associates.”

The word pai means “to get.” The game called pai is equivalent to the Western game “hide-and-seek.” Krsna and the gopis played this game in the dense bakula forest now known by the name Pai.

When it was Radha’s turn to be the seeker, She naturally felt like ignoring everyone else and only looking for Krsna. But She knew that Her friends expected Her to do just that, so She didn’t.

First Radha captured Citra, Rupa, and a few others. Sometimes She found gopis by their unique fragrance, sometimes by their effulgence, and sometimes by their giggles as She approached them.

Radha then set out to catch Krsna. She knew that He could hide Himself even within an atom, so He would he difficult to find. Finally She decided to lure Him into the open with Her feminine charms.

In pursuit of victory Radha employed Her bodily effulgence, Her natural beauty, Her tinkling ornaments, and above all — what some might call cheating — the sound of Her voice singing Hare Krsna.

Krsna had hidden Himself well. He had gone to the top of a nearby hill and crouched behind a bush. From there He could see Radha’s every move and easily shift locations when He saw Her getting closer.

And, indeed, He did see Her approaching. But the sight of Her beauty, Her soothing effulgence, and above all the sound of Her chanting His names, all combined to freeze Him to the spot.

Try as He might, Krsna was unable to move. He attempted to crawl away, but His hands and feet were frozen. And when He stood up hoping to run, His whole body went numb with ecstasy.

Only with great difficulty did Krsna manage to turn His eyes away from Sri Radha, but the spell cast by the mystical “Hare Krsna, Hare Rama” rolling off Her lips would not let Him move.

The gopis all watched in amusement — some laughing aloud, some holding their sides — to see Krsna frozen on top of the hill while Radha stealthily approached Him chanting His, and Her, names.

Krsna was like a deer mesmerised by a hunter’s flute-song. His ears twitched at the sound of Sri Radha’s voice, just as those of a deer twitch upon hearing a hunter’s flute playing.

Unable to move (calana), Krsna stood helplessly on Calana-sila as Radha came closer. She finally reached Him, placed Her hand on His arm, and announced Her victory, “You are caught!”

At first Krsna accused Radha of cheating, but since the rules of the game said nothing to forbid chanting mantras, Krsna had to admit that He had been well and truly defeated.

Still fixed to His spot on Calana-sila, Krsna pleaded, “O friend! I have but one request.” “What is that?” Radha asked innocently. “Please stop chanting,” Krsna replied, “so that I can move again.”

West of Parehi on the road to Sita-Rama is Patharali. The pathways of New Vraja-dhama generally demarcate the petals of the dhama, which in this case trace the southern curve of the eastern petal.

One day Krsna went with the gopis to a small hill at Patharali in order to help fill their pitchers with mineral water. A spring whose waters contained medicinal properties bubbled out of the ground there.

But before all the gopis had filled their pitchers, Krsna said, “Friends! Collect your water. I’ll walk a ways towards Sita-Rama and return soon. Wait for Me, and I’ll walk you home.”

The gopis continued to fill their pitchers one at a time after Krsna had left. When they had finished they sat down to rest and wait for Krsna to come back. “Do you see Him?” one gopi asked.

Another gopi replied, “No! I can’t see or hear anything.” After they had all drunk water from the spring with their cupped hands, another gopi asked again, “Is Krsna coming?” But again the answer was no.

At first the gopis rested. But they soon became restless, and one girl again said, “I think I see Him coming.” But another gopi replied, “That’s a branch hanging over the road, moving in the wind.”

A few minutes later one of the girls rose to her feet and said, “That’s Krsna for sure. I see Him coming.” But when the others looked, they answered, “Silly! That’s a peacock crossing the road.”

Time and again the gopis thought they saw their beloved, only to be disappointed. Gradually they sank deeper and deeper into a mire of dejection, though they still buzzed with anticipation.

But the only way hack along the road passed right in front of the gopis. And Krsna did say He would be hack. Maybe He decided to go another way. “I think I see our Krsna,” said another gopi.

Heightened anticipation and feelings of separation pushed the gopis into a state of ecstasy in which they all envisioned Krsna. Excitedly they called out, “I see Him!” “Yes, that’s Krsna!” “He’s coming.”

Their minds saturated with desire, their eyes anointed with love, the gopis had a vision of Krsna standing right there before them. They turned away from Him and covered their faces with their veils.

And what a beautiful scene it was: the gopis standing in a circle around their imaginary Krsna, backs turned to Him, necks slightly bent, their eyes shooting angry arrows at Him for being so late.

At that moment Krsna actually appeared on the road, but the gopis were too absorbed in their ecstatic vision of Him to notice. As Krsna took in the scene, He could at once understand what had happened.

Wanting to witness their ecstasy, Krsna stood a short distance away and listened as one gopi after another chastised the vision for keeping them waiting and putting them in anxiety.

The gopis’ every word was sweet nectar to Krsna’s ears. Sprinkled with the camphor of happiness He felt from having the chance to witness their ecstasy, the mixture was pure bliss.

When Krsna had drunk His fill of the cowherd girls’ ecstatic reprimands, He requested the flute to break their trance with a song and bring their attention to the real Krsna standing nearby.

As soon as the gopis heard the flute-song, their vision vanished and they saw the real Krsna a short distance away — just as they like to see Him the most — standing beneath a tree, body bent, playing the flute.

The gopis all exclaimed in unison, “There’s the beloved of my life, my Prana-vallabha!” The area surrounding this mineral spring, at first called Prana-vallabha, has come to be known as Patharali.

**\* \* \* \***

Some distance to the west, just off the road leading to Patharali, is Letari, where the cowherd boys, exhausted from herding cows and collecting strays, decided they could go no further.

The boys had herded their cows from one forest of Vrndavana to the next, along the way picking flowers to string garlands and collecting mineral dyes to use as paint for decorating their bodies.

Each cowherd hoy would control his own herd by playing his flute and horn, and at the same time stay as close to Krsna as possible hy entertaining Him with jokes, funny faces, or gifts of fruit.

The gopas were eternal companions of the Lord, with spiritual bodies, but in their roles as human boys they felt exhausted and hungry from playing all day, and so they appealed to Krsna.

One of the boys asked, “O friend! Our bodies have become lean from hunger and our throats parched from thirst. Why don’t we open our lunch packs and satisfy the urgent demands of our bellies?”

Feeling as though He also had an empty stomach and weary limbs, Krsna exclaimed, “Wonderful! A wonderful proposal! Let’s select a suitable place and see what our mothers packed for lunch.”

The boys voiced their agreement with an enthusiastic “Jaya!” They selected the level, shaded meadow of Letari and sat in concentric circles, with Krsna and Balarama at the centre.

Then the fun began, the kind of fun that comes when the senses are satisfied. The boys raided each other’s lunch packs, joked amongst themselves, and all begged for a morsel of the food Krsna had tasted.

One boy explained, “Dear Gopala! No doubt You have attained mystic powers as a result of Mother Yasoda’s cooking. Otherwise, what difference is there between You and us?

“So don’t be miserly! Share some of Your lunch with us so that we can also kill demons. That way we can save You the trouble.” Everyone applauded these words and reached out tor Krsna’s remnants.

After filling their bellies, the boys drank from a nearby pond and then rested in the shade while some boys fed Krsna betel, massaged His lotus feet, and cooled Him by waving a peacock fan.

All glories to Bhauma Vrndavana, the emperor that rules over all pilgrimage places in the three worlds. All glories to New Vraja-dhama, and its pastime places like Letari, which remind us of Krsna.

South of Letari is Rasoli. Rasoli is named after a rasa dance Krsna enacted there on the bank of Rasa-kunda beneath a large banyan tree whose boughs and roots spread out in all directions.

Rasoli is relatively close to Kosikala, but still secluded due to being far from Vraja’s roads or residential areas. It also appears to be of no agricultural or commercial interest to the Vraja-vasis.

The banyan tree there featured significantly in the rasa dance, for its branches not only provided cover but, in addition, its maze of hanging roots added entertaining variety to the dancers’ movements.

In a rasa dance the dancers generally move in one or many concentric circles, but at Rasoli they twisted and wove in a zigzag pattern around the tangled hanging roots of the gigantic tree.

But as different as the patterns of dancing were at Rasoli, the singing, the steps, the touching, and the music were the same as any rasa dance, as was the bliss felt by Krsna and His dance partners.

Indeed, the ecstasy of the participants occasionally reached such heights that the roots of the tree also appeared to be dancing and the trunk swaying; of course, this just increased the ecstasy even more.

The only ones to feel excluded from this unique festival were the demigods, who were unable to see through the tree’s canopy from their airplanes; they could only hear the sounds of this rasa dance.

After some time Krsna noticed that the gopis were fatigued from dancing, and so He encouraged them to refresh themselves in the cool waters of Rasa-kunda, where they played various water games.

At the conclusion of these water sports, Krsna sat upon a golden throne at the base of the tree, surrounded by gopis. Together they relished a picnic of luscious fruits catered by Vrnda-devi.

Then Radha and Krsna and the gopis rested in banyan cottages whose pillars were the roots of the tree and where garlands, curtains, and festoons served as walls and domes.

Most of this petal of New Vraja-dhama is taken up by places like Rasoli, where Krsna and the gopis meet, but there is also an area around Nai where Lord Balarama and His friends go to play.

Krsna’s daytime meetings with Srimati Radharani and Her friends usually, although not always, take place while Krsna’s friends and cows arc resting somewhere close by, waiting for Him to return.

For example, when He and Radha meet at Her lake, Radha-kunda, the gopas are nearby at Govardhana. When They meet at Lukluki-kunda in Kamyavana, the gopas are at Bhojana-sthali.

Only at night, while His friends and cows are asleep in their villages, does Krsna travel alone to places like Sanketa or the shores of the Yamuna. His only obligation then is to return home by dawn.

While Krsna meets with the gopis in this part of the dhama, Balarama and the cowherd boys tend their cows around Nai, playing and resting on the banks of beautiful Sankarsana-kunda.

Balarama comes to Nai with the gopas and takes a short nap on a jewelled platform beneath a tree whose arching branches sweep the ground. Meanwhile, Krsna is off on one of His midday excursions.

Some boys search the gardens nearby for fruits and then place them on plates along with sweets brought from home. When Balarama wakes up they offer these delicacies to Him and His friends.

As usual, after eating, the boys drink water and chew betel nuts. Fully refreshed, they play sports, wandering from grove to grove, where the supplies for the specific game they want to play are always available.

While the boys are in the heat of play, Krsna suddenly and nonchalantly appears — acting as if He had just been gone a few moments — and stirs the waves of love within His friends’ hearts.

After offering sweet words to His friends and touching them with His nectar-giving hand, Krsna embraces Balarama and sits beside His brother on the jewelled dais to listen to the boys’ loving complaints.

“O friend!” says Sridama. “To You a few moments away from us might not mean much, but to us, who love You so much, every moment away from You is like a thousand millennia of unbearable torture.”

With a loving smile Krsna pacifies His dear friends and then rewards them for their pain with a question: “O friend! Sridama! Are you going to let Me starve while you chastise Me?”

At this the boys at once collect the best foods available and arrange a forest picnic, feeding Krsna with their own hands and forgetting whatever complaints they had just lodged against Him.

One night, when the gopas were resting at home, Krsna met the gopis further south of Nai at a place named Vichora, a beautiful forest garden at whose centre was a sparkling clear lake.

As Krsna approached — like a mass of effulgence with restless eyes — He noted that numbers of graceful gopis with various moods had assembled. But He had wanted to be alone with Radha.

Still, as He is the shelter of all His devotees, He first fulfilled each of the gopis’ expectations, greeting them with kind words and loving gestures, reciprocating with each according to her desire.

Krsna knows the hearts of His beloveds, and so He moved amongst them like moonlight shining on different crystals: embracing one, kissing another, conversing with one, pacifying yet another.

To suit Radha’s submissive mood, Krsna rested beside Her and let Her place the world’s storehouse of beauty — His lotus feet — upon Her heart. Krsna assured His beloved that They would soon be alone.

Radha worried that Her request for Krsna’s company would not be fulfilled, but because the touch of the Lord’s feet has the power to give fearlessness to the surrendered, She felt pacified.

Bilvamangala Thakura says in this regard, “May Srimati Radharani’s heart carry the happiness of Sri Krsna’s lotus feet, which completely crush the pride of rows of lotus groves —

“Which are the eternal pleasure abodes for all the beauty in the world, and which are intensely worshipped for their power to give fearlessness to the surrendered gopis \[and to surrendered devotees.”

**\* \* \* \***

Still, Radha wondered, “How can We he alone with so many others present?” Reading Radha’s mind, Krsna answered Her question by glancing at a secluded grove in the distance.

Rising to His feet, Krsna then played His flute, inviting the other gopis to join Him in dancing, as Radha, still uncertain whether She had understood Him or not, melted into the shadows of the forest.

The dexterous gopis moved around Krsna in a circle — sometimes holding hands, sometimes performing synchronised pirouettes — as Krsna teased rhythmic melodies from His bamboo flute.

Within those flute-Songs, hut indiscernible to the other girls, Krsna had encoded a message to Radharani encouraging Her not to delay hut to at once take shelter in the distant lakeside cane grove.

Did Krsna cheat His girlfriends hy such subterfuge? Not at all! While they danced around Him, He flooded their hearts with bliss, heightened by His charming smile and youthful limbs.

The taste of this elixir’s abundant sweetness so intoxicated the gopis that they were oblivious to one another — including Radha. They simply drank more and more with an insatiable thirst.

Seeing Vraja’s cowherd maidens fully immersed in His qualities, Sri Krsna further increased their bliss with the radiance of His body, the sound of His flute, and His pleasing moon-like face.

These deeds succeeded in softening not only the hearts of the gopis but also the earth, and so the soil beneath their feet transformed into a kind of divine mud that sparkled with melted cintdmani stones.

The cumulative effect of these divine stimuli caused the gopis to lose direct contact with Krsna. All they could see, hear, and feel was the transcendental potion He had conjured up to enchant them.

Krsna seized this moment to slip away and follow the path Sri Radha had taken. His tinkling anklets might have alerted the gopis to His departure, but to them the sound was simply another draught of bliss.

Could Krsna’s footprints have led the gopis to the divine couple’s secret rendezvous? Hardly! Krsna’s footprints covered the area in zigzag patterns impossible to follow, especially at night.

And so while the gopis were totally immersed in the sankirtana of Krsna’s names and glories, He confidently snuck away to the grove in which Sri Radha was waiting, unsure if He would arrive.

But arrive He did. And from His lotus eyes, which constantly increase in beauty, He poured His love into Radha’s eyes. And those eyes, unable to contain His love, overflowed with tears.

But for once, tears would not keep Radha from seeing Krsna. It was shyness, rather, that tried to draw Her eyes away from His. Luckily, love thwarted the efforts of that shyness, and She wondered aloud:

“How Krsna’s gleaming eyes pour His very self into the heart of this insignificant sparrow, while increasing in sweetness, beauty, and compassion at every moment, I cannot say.

“It is said that Krsna’s glance brings good fortune because His eyes are the shelter of goddess Laksmi Herself. By receiving His powerful glance, am I assured of becoming fortunate tonight?”

Radha was like a mouse frozen in a cobra’s stare. She was unable to move until Krsna came to Her side and freed Her from His spell with the cooling touch of His hand and the moon rays of His smile.

Radha and Krsna entered into a deep, confidential exchange of love in which neither of Them were aware of anything outside the increasing feelings of joy that passed freely between Them.

Just as a philosophers’ stone transforms whatever it touches to gold, the philosophers’ stone of love for Krsna makes everything it touches — from most significant, to most insignificant — lovable.

Thus, Radha’s and Krsna’s limbs, gestures, movements, talks, and apparel — inherently of inestimable value — are transformed by the philosophers’ stone of prema into indispensable ingredients of its play.

Thus divine love increases Radha’s and Krsna’s desire to give and to receive everything that They have to each other. But even when They are able to do so, still They remain dissatisfied and lament.

What to say, then, about how They felt when the time came for Their pastimes to end? The divine couple gave up that lakeside grove and, parting ways (viccheda), left Vichora to return to Their own villages.

When Mount Mandara crushed the demigods and the demons, Visnu appeared as Ajita to lift it. Even Lord Visnu, however, cannot lift the intolerable burden Radha and Krsna feel when parting ways.

As Krsna was leaving Vichora, the other gopis awoke from the bliss of their sankirtana, adding their own pain of separation to that of Radha’s and Krsna’s, from which this area takes its distinctive name.

To the west of Vichora but still in the same forest is Vasosi, so named because it bears the fragrance (vasa) of Krsna’s body, which perfected Vaisnavas can still perceive today.

Although the scent of Krsna’s body still lingers at VasosI, at other places and times His’s fragrance served as a beacon by which His intimate associates were able to find Him.

Once, empowered by prema, the bee-like eyes of the gopis followed the fragrance of Krsna’s honey-like sweetness. Wandering around in the breezes of bashfulness, they finally found the lotus of His face.

Another time, Radha caught Krsna’s fragrance in the breeze, but despite looking in every direction, could not find its source. Trapping that fragrance within Her heart, like a bee She followed it.

Pushing on in spite of ecstatic symptoms, Radha followed Krsna’s fragrance through the forest until She found Him at last. Once within His sight, She pretended to pick flowers, to trip, and to fall.

The presence of Krsna’s fragrance at VasosI, like His footprints at Carana-pahari, sends His message to us, etam vibhutim yogam ca mama yo vetti tattvatah / so ’vikalpena yogena yujyate natra samsayah:

“One who is factually convinced of this opulence and mystic power of Mine engages in unalloyed devotional service; of this there is no doubt.” So these signs emphatically declare.

To what can we liken Krsna’s bodily fragrance? It can be compared to the pleasing aroma of musk, sandalwood, aguru (or saffron), and camphor, ground and mixed together into what is called catuhsama.

The aroma of catuhsama, characteristic of the eight parts of Lord Krsna’s body, is extremely attractive to women and can he best compared to the fragrance of lotuses mixed with camphor.

Still, it cannot he said that the fragrance of VasosI exactly resembles the scents of those material substances or the aroma of Krsna’s limbs. There is much more to that transcendental bouquet.

The fragrance at VasosI is that of catuhsama mixed with the added aromas of Krsna’s youth, smile, beauty, and lotus feet. The aggregate of these scents is called the “limit of fragrance” (saurabhya-sima).

The fragrance of Krsna’s youth is like the appearance of spring, the blossoming of flowers, the awakening of sensuality, the maturation of the limbs of the body. It is also the pinnacle of beauty and cleverness.

This aroma intoxicates Krsna, causing His wide eyes to become reddish. When combined with His sidelong glance, it chains the fickle minds of young girls, making them Krsna’s maidservants.

Krsna’s irresistible smile also contributes a special ingredient to His fragrance. That smile is a honey-like sweetness with two sources: His lotus face, and the honey-wine Krsna is accustomed to drink.

Krsna’s lotus face exudes that honey-like sweetness, and so it has a natural honey scent. And because Krsna often drinks varuni during His amorous sports with the gopis, His breath smells slightly of honey.

The third ingredient, the fragrance of beauty, is described by Bilvamangala Thakura,

*kamam santu sahasrasah katipaye sarasya-dhaurcyakah*  
*kamam va kamaniyata-parimala-svarajya baddha-vratah —*  
*Naivaivam vivadamahe na ca vayam deva priyam hrumahe*  
*yat satyam ramaniyata-parinatis tvayy eva param gata*

**“There may be thousands of charming persons, and among them a few —**

**“May be firmly fixed in the fragrance of beauty. We do not quarrel with that. And yet we do not falsely praise You. We speak the truth, because the perfection of beauty reaches its limit in Youalone.”**

In short, the fragrance of Krsna’s beauty derives its potency from the ultimate limit of the perfection of beauty — the original feature of the Absolute Truth, the Supreme Personality of Godhead.

And the last ingredient, perhaps the most agreeable due to its connection with Srimati Radharani, is the fragrance of His lotus feet, which crushes the pride of lotuses and gives the senses joy.

Krsna’s lotus feet have a natural fragrance that is unlimitedly enhanced when dusted by the kunkuma from Radha’s breasts, which has unrivalled fragrance, like the rest of Her transcendental form.

The fragrance of Krsna’s lotus feet has a unique feature: it grants fearlessness from Cupid’s torments to those who fully surrender to Krsna, as graphically shown by His pastimes with Kubja of Mathura.

We have heard that the perfumed atmosphere of Vasosi is an extraordinary blend of the fragrances of musk, aguru, sandalwood, saffron, camphor, and Krsna’s youth, smile, beauty, and lotus feet.

How can we imagine, let alone catch a whiff of, Vasosi's transcendental scent? Is there some way to get a hint of this aroma before we actually become qualified to smell it for ourselves?

The fragrance of Krsna’s pastimes —created as purified souls remember those pastimes and chant the maha-mantra — is the closest that practising devotees can come to smelling the aroma of Vasosi.

May the fragrance of such mellow-laden nama-sankirtana fill our sense of smell with its sweetness and empower us to completely abandon the pungent odour of sense gratification.

**\* \* \* \***

In the southeastern corner of New Vraja-dhama is the forest of Srngaravana. Within this forest are the last three pastime places of the dhamas eastern petal I will discuss in this chapter.

Because the eastern portion of New Vraja-dhama is so remote, my travels to it are infrequent. Like most residents of the dhama, I rarely visit the inspiring pastime places found here.

But when we do visit, recall, or describe them, we will naturally feel just as connected to Radha-Syamasundara’s abode as we do in more commonly visited places, like Nandagrama or Vrndavana village.

Let us worship New Vraja-dhama in the same mood as we do Radha-Syamasundara. The tiniest tip of Their fingernails is just as important and indispensable as Their lotus feet.

East of Vichora is Niganya, where the gopis once cross-examined Krsna. This pastime began one morning after the cowherd girls had convinced their husbands to let them visit Kosikala (Kusasthali).

The Vraja-vasis knew that the holy city of Kusasthali would bless those who bathed in her Gomati-kunda, and so, on the pretext of acquiring a wealth of cows and gold, the go pis set out on pilgrimage.

The beautiful wives of Abhimanyu’s clan left Yavata early. On their way through Vrndavana forest, near Seva-kunja, they came upon Krsna, who was standing by the road with only His flute for company.

The girls pretended not to notice Him and tried to walk past, but Krsna politely stepped in front of them and enquired, “O goddesses of Vraja! Where are you going this early, dressed in all your finery?”

One gopi challenged, “Since our destination is different than Yours, shouldn’t You be concerned with Your plans and not ours?” Unfazed at being dismissed, Krsna twirled the flute in His hand and replied:

“I am going to Kosikala to inspect My father’s store of grains. From what I have heard, you are going to bathe in nearby Gomati-kunda. So aren’t we going to the same place, My dear friend?”

Inwardly, the gopis were overjoyed, but externally they expressed unwillingness to travel with Krsna. Lalita said, “Chaste brides like us cannot be seen walking with a man other than our husbands.”

Krsna answered, “That may he! But chaste brides like you also don’t travel long distances without a chaperone.” Determined to be on their way, the gopis started to leave, but Krsna again stopped them.

He said, “If you take the path through the village you will be the talk of envious, small-minded people.” Pointing to the path through Nipavana, Krsna added, “Please. Take this peaceful forest path.”

The gopis thought it wise to avoid arguing with Krsna on the main road, and so they followed Him along the secluded path to Kosikala, circumventing forests like Biharivana and Chattravana.

Krsna, however — hoping to extend the time He spent with the gopis as long as possible — took a detour east to Patharali, and then south to Parehi, until the suspicious gopis forcibly stopped Him at Niganya.

It had been a pleasant early-morning walk, with Krsna politely carrying the gopis’ articles of worship and repeatedly praising their beauty by comparing their features to the beauty of the forest.

But the gopis were painfully aware that they had been unwittingly led on a scenic tour of Vrndavana. They should have reached Kosikala long ago; instead, they found themselves in the middle of nowhere.

Suddenly Radha stopped and declared, “This shortcut has proven to be the long way to our destination. Son of Nanda! Why have You done this?” Krsna replied, “I must have gotten lost.”

Hanging His head in a show of shame, Krsna said, “At the turn in the road we should have gone right instead of left. But I was so distracted by Your effulgent beauty that I couldn’t tell one from the other.”

The gopis shook their heads in disbelief. By now they should have been savvy to Krsna’s skill in manipulating words to get what He wanted. But no! Whatever He did, it was as if for the first time.

And what was His real plan? Was it only to accompany them to Kosikala? Surely they would find out. Visakha-devi said, “No doubt, our princess has blinded You with Her charm.

“And that blindness might also excuse You for visiting the women’s bathing ghata by the Yamuna yesterday.” Krsna objected, “How dare you accuse Me of a felony committed by some miscreant — ”

“O really?” Lalita interrupted. “So it wasn’t You in the shadows throwing pebbles at our dear friend while She was waist-deep in the Yamuna.” “Certainly not!” Krsna shot back.

“I was nowhere near the Yamuna. I was tending My cows by Govardhana Hill, to which hundreds of gopas and thousands of cows can surely testify.” The gopis all nodded as Tungavidya asked:

“Then who was standing beneath the tamala tree playing the flute so expertly?” Krsna said, looking innocent, “It wasn’t Me playing the flute. It must have been someone else (hama nahi gaya or bolaya)."

Laughing out loud, the girls all clapped their hands and repeated over and over, "hama nahi gaya or bolaya, hama nahi gaya or bolaya,” from which this part of the forest received the name Niganya.

The gopis found Krsna’s reply so hilarious that they could no longer restrain themselves. They joked, “Maybe Subala was playing the flute!” “Maybe it was Madhu!” “No, maybe it was a cow!”

The vision of a cow playing a flute by the Yamuna was the last straw. Krsna looked on incredulously as the girls covered their mouths or held their sides while their laughter filled the forest.

Krsna decided that enough was enough. Placing the gopis' things on a rock, He walked down the road until He could no longer hear laughter and, with no one else to talk to, He spoke to His flute:

“Friend! Learn this lesson well: Never put your faith in a woman. When the demons asked the Supreme Lord in the form of Mohini to distribute the nectar of immortality, She warned them:

‘“O demons! Why do you have so much faith in Me? A learned person never puts his faith in a woman (pandito jatu kaminisu na yati hi)., These are the infallible words of Lord Narayana.”

Polishing the flute with His upper cloth, Krsna continued, “Day and night these gopis tempt Me, pray for My company, and then — when I do appear to fulfil their desires — simply berate or belittle Me.”

While Krsna was voicing His frustration to the venu, Sri Radha decided to go after Him and appease Him. He had brought them into the thick of the jungle, after all, and He should now escort them out.

“Friends!” Radharani announced. “While you entertain one another and rest your weary feet, I will find our chaperone, who so rudely left us here alone and unprotected. I will he hack soon.”

Srimati Radharani’s words subdued the gopis laughter but did little to change their mood. Besides, the girls knew that She, like Krsna, was expert at concealing the real purpose behind Her words.

Unable to suppress their smiles, they replied, “By all means, please find our guide and convince Him to take us to Kosi.” A gopi wryly added, “Yes. Convince Him hy whatever means You can.”

As the gopis parodied the conversation they expected Radha and Krsna to have, Radha flashed a good-natured smile and headed south, accompanied hy a few maidservants, following Krsna’s footprints.

The paths in this part of Vrndavana were rarely used, making Krsna’s footprints, with their distinctive markings, clearly visible in the dust. Radha meditated upon Krsna’s lotus footprints as She walked along.

It didn’t take long for Radharani and Her companions to find Krsna sulking hy the roadside. He was obviously pleased that She had come in search of Him without Her boisterous girlfriends in tow.

Sri Radha saw Krsna as more handsome than a host of Cupids, His complexion more dazzling than a blossoming blue lotus, and His smile more enchanting than the essence of sweetness.

First She perspired and trembled, then Her bodily hairs stood on end. Her spontaneous attraction to Krsna was obvious, but She tried to hide all these symptoms by wrapping Herself in Her veil.

Krsna at once noticed these signs of Radha’s divine love and approached Her in a chivalrous mood. He offered Her His outstretched hand and said, “Would You like to rest for a moment?”

**\* \* \* \***

Krsna led Radha through Srngaravana to a pleasant grove surrounded by the forest on three sides. The fourth side overlooked the rolling countryside. Cows grazed in the far distance.

Srimati Radharani’s gopi maidservants silently busied themselves outside the forest grove, gathering and preparing paraphernalia soon to be needed in their services to Radha and Krsna.

Inside the grove Radha took a seat on a flower cot and, like a golden cuckoo in the garden of Krsna’s pastimes, enticed deer-like Krsna with the sound of Her voice to sit down beside Her.

Throughout the morning Krsna had displayed an independent spirit, but when Radha cast the spell of Her love over Him, Krsna became submissive and was now fully under Her control.

Radha’s and Krsna’s hearts were both full of desire to enjoy endless pastimes of love, but simply by sitting beside each other and basking in the rays of Their beauty and love that desire was satiated.

As the manjaris gazed through the vine-walls of the grove, some saw the divine couple as a golden vine climbing up a tamala tree, others saw Them as a flash of lightning beside a new monsoon cloud.

For the manjaris, Radha is life itself; service to Her is the purpose of their existence. On this occasion they could see that, for Krsna also, their goddess is everything, the sole source of His ultimate happiness.

In the heat of summer She is the camphor, sandalwood paste, and moonlight that cools His limbs. In winter She is the woollen yellow garment that protects His divine body from chilly winds.

In joyful spring She is the charming beauty that makes Him blossom with happiness. In the monsoon downpour She is the graceful mallara melody amidst torrential rains that gives delight to His heart.

In the autumn season She is the enchantment of the rasa dance, enjoying pastimes with the king of dancers. And in the fall, She is the supreme goddess of fortune who defeats Krsna in pastimes of love.

Through eyes completely dedicated to Srimati Radharani, the manjaris saw how in all seasons, in all places, in all ways, their mistress is the be-all and end-all of Krsna, just as He is to Her.

As the maidservants went about their services, Radha, being so near to Krsna, was transformed into a reservoir of divine nectar, brimming with different ecstasies, just as water overflows from a pitcher.

This wealth of unmatched love for Krsna, the ecstasies that love causes in Her, and the taste those ecstasies bestow, all distinguish Radharani from Her gopi rivals, even from Her confidential friends.

The beauty of one’s bodily features reflects the purity of one’s love; Radha’s love for Krsna is so pure that the beauty of Her body makes Her rivals hide in shame and keeps Krsna always under Her control.

As amorous love welled up in Radha, Krsna’s sidelong glances and striking features made Her forget Her marital vows and the lessons on chastity She had learned from the strict traditions of Vraja. Thus began an intense romantic exchange between the divine couple, free from the slightest (tilu) interruption or disturbance. For that reason the manjaris named the surrounding area Tiloyara.

Radha and Krsna began by exchanging joking words, with which They stoked the fire of Their mutual attraction, and just when Krsna thought the time was right to take Radharani’s hand —

The winds of caprice blew through the grove. Without reason, Radha stood up to leave. All too familiar with Radha’s unpredictable moods, Krsna instinctively grasped Her hand to bring Her back.

To entice Her, Krsna reminded Her of His blockade at Dana-ghati: “O Radhika! You and Your generals, headed by Lalita, possess difficult-to-obtain weapons intended for amorous battle.

“Intoxicated by pride, You disrespect Me, even though I am well known in Vraja as Cupid’s tax collector. Either pay Me voluntarily or I shall forcibly extract the proper taxes from You and Your party.”

Hearing Krsna’s arrogant jokes and remembering Their toll pastimes on Govardhana Hill, Radha was overpowered with amorous love. But to tease Him, She pretended that She was still leaving.

She used sidelong glances to make Krsna loosen His grip, and, with the graceful gait of a swan, She walked away from Him. At once He rose to His feet, desperately holding on to the edge of Her veil.

Radha continued walking. Krsna playfully tugged at Her sari, but She rebuked Him. He blocked the doorway to the grove, but She stared at Him with crooked, angry eyes. Krsna was desperate.

Radha could see now that Visakha had been right. Krsna’s plan in misguiding the gopis was to be alone with Her in just such a secluded place. But if She left for Niganya, His plan would be derailed.

Falling to His knees, His eyes tilled with tears, Krsna pleaded, “Devi! You once won My flute in a dice game and, laughing, broke it to pieces. Do You now want to break My heart to pieces as well?”

Krsna’s words pierced Radharani’s heart, a heart already fully dedicated to serving and pleasing Him. Reaching for His hand, She replied, “My only desire is to give happiness to You.”

Just as the fragrance of musk is not different from musk itself, or moonlight from the moon, or flower blossoms from a flower, so Srimati Radharani is not different from Sri Krsna.

As the potency of the supreme potent, Radharani is separately manifest from Krsna to enable Him to taste the pleasure of His own divine qualities in His own abode of Vrndavana.

It is difficult to say how long Radha’s and Krsna’s pastimes lasted on that day. During that time the manjaris came and went with items of service while singing songs in praise of the divine couple.

What songs did they sing? Songs like

*bhajami radham aravinda-netram smarami radham madhura-smitasyam*  
*vadami radham karunabharardratn tato mamanyasti gatir na kapi*

**“I worship lotus-eyed Srimati Radharani. I meditate on sweetly smiling Srimati Radharani. I glorify supremely merciful Srimati Radharani. She is the only goal of my life. I have no other goal.”**

**\* \* \* \***

The divine couple finally ended Their transcendental sports. At the same time, the gopis in Niganya decided that they had rested and waited enough, and were going out to find both Radha and Krsna.

Meanwhile, not wanting to he the brunt of the gopis’ jokes, Radha and Krsna hurriedly put Their clothing and ornaments in order so that They would look just as They had earlier.

Seated facing Her on the flower cor, Krsna decorated Radha with small jasmine flowers. When He had finished, He sat behind Her, combing and braiding Her loosened hair and then decorating it.

Krsna used flowers, jewels, peacock feathers, gunja, and other ornaments to decorate Radha’s hair. All the while, His touch ably succeeded in making the hairs of Her body stand up in ecstasy.

Krsna drew motifs on Her forehead and cheeks, applied lipstick to Her lips, reddened Her feet with lacquer, and, when finished, offered Her His chewed betel, which She refused.

Just then the rest of the gopis — heralded hy chatter and giggling — approached Tiloyara. The manjaris guided them to the grove, where Radha and Krsna were sitting at opposite ends of a table, playing dice.

Lalita and Visakha were first to enter, soon to he followed by Rangadevi and others. But when the senior gopis found the divine couple innocently playing dice, they were suspicious.

Radha looked as fresh and radiant as She had before She left them. “We have agreed to take the fastest route to Kosi,” She said nonchalantly. “We’ve been waiting. Are you ready?”

The gopis surveyed the grove for signs of a dalliance. Finding none, they exchanged suspicious glances. As she glared at the manjaris, Lalita said angrily, “Are we ready? We’ve been waiting for You!”

Determined to know what had really transpired over the last three dandas, Lalita cast threatening glances at one manjari, who revealed with a look of her eyes that Radha had indeed enjoyed with Krsna.

Srimati Radharani was quick to notice this exchange and angrily rebuked Her maidservant as the other cowherd girls exposed the charade the divine couple were staging tor them.

Internally Radha was overflowing with bliss, but externally She showed displeasure with Her tattletale maidservants, and also with Her friends, who seemed to relish mocking Her.
