The following day, the king again appeared before Syamananda and his chanting party, hut without soldiers or weapons. This time he bowed before Syamananda and begged forgiveness.

Sher Khan requested to he initiated into the chanting of the Hare Krsna maha-mantra. He received the spiritual name Caitanya Dasa and later became a prominent disciple of Syamananda Prabhu.

Eventually Syamananda Prabhu entered Radha’s and Krsna’s eternal abode as Kanaka-manjari in the town of Gopivallabha-pura, where his divine body was placed into samadhi and is worshipped today.

The area encircling Syamananda Prabhu’s puspa-samadhi and the Tilaka-sthana is called Kanaka-kunja because it marks the grove where Kanaka-manjari resides and serves the divine couple.

Let us repeatedly otter our obeisances to Syamananda Prabhu who taught everyone how to serve the spiritual masters, the Lord’s abode, and by his fearless preaching, the mission of Caitanya Mahaprabhu.

**\* \* \* \***

On the opposite side of Varsana Road from Syamananda’s puspa-samadhi is the guesthouse that represents the Krsna-Balarama Mandira at the heart of Krsna-Balarama’s Ramana-reti playground.

I named the garden forest of this area Prabhupadavana in honour of His Divine Grace’s samadhi, which forever reminds us of the person who made Krsna and Vrndavana known throughout the world.

The story of how Srila Prabhupada acquired the land and constructed the temple for the pleasure of Krsna and Balarama is well known. The shade of the plum trees here is an ideal place to hear and remember it.

I wasn’t present at the installation of Gaura-Nitai, Krsna-Balarama, and Radha-Syamasundara but heard about it from our temple president upon his return. I saw the Deities myself the following year.

At that time the vision of Srila Prabhupada seated on his vyasasana, flanked by two marble lions, speaking on Prahlada Maharaja’s prayers to Lord Nrsimha made an indelible impression upon my mind.

And as Srila Prabhupada sat in his room surrounded hy senior devotees, I would sit outside and watch through the window. I had the distinct impression that he felt quite at home in Vrndavana.

Many memorable events occurred in Krsna-Balarama Mandira during the few years Srila Prabhupada was with us and have occurred throughout the years following his departure to aprakata Vrndavana.

One event stands out: On 20 April 1975, Srila Prabhupada opened the temple, offered the first arati to Krsna-Balarama, and then, standing before his two Lords, addressed the assembled guests.

Nearly a thousand devotees had come from all over the world to rejoice with Srila Prabhupada and celebrate his having established his international society in Krsna’s own home. It was a grand victory.

Krsna and Balarama would play and tend cows in Ramana-reti, and so Srila Prabhupada established Them on the central altar, with Gaura-Nitai on the left, and Radha-Syamasundara on the right.

Later that evening, after the festivities had wound down and all the guests and dignitaries had been served prasada, Srila Prabhupada was relaxing in his room with various senior disciples. He told them:

**“Because temple construction is so important, we are willing to spend so much money for it. And yet as important as it is, book production and book distribution is even more important.”**

Those words have stayed with me; in fact, as we try to develop New Vraja-dhama, I know that our success will depend upon the continued and ever-increasing distribution of Srila Prabhupada's books.

To our glorious exemplar — who personally set the standard in how to preach Krsna consciousness, practise Krsna consciousness, and be Krsna conscious — to Srila Prabhupada I offer my undying respect.

In the spring of 1989, while staying at the Krsna-Balarama guesthouse, I dreamt of Srila Prabhupada. He was sitting and translating at his table in the downstairs reception room there in his house.

A single lamp illuminated the dark room and the table, at which Srila Prabhupada sat reading from a large book, giving his commentary. Aside from his voice and the dictaphone, everything was silent.

Certainly, the focus of the dream was Srila Prabhupada’s voice. I listened as he quoted Sanskrit verses, gave his translations, and composed his “Bhaktivedanta purports’’ with intense concentration.

As Srila Prabhupada spoke into the dictaphone, his deep, sonorous voice gradually changed, taking on the qualities of a golden-toned male voice I intuitively recognised as the voice of Rupa Gosvami.

Exhilarated by the encounter, and by the subsequent realisation that Rupa Gosvami was speaking directly through Srila Prabhupada, I felt waves of spiritual emotion wash over me. Then I awoke.

In this garden of New Vraja-dhama, a cherry laurel tree represents the tamala tree in the courtyard of Krsna-Balarama Mandira, which Srila Prabhupada said was a meeting place for Radha and Krsna.

Srila Prabhupada indicated that the luxuriant growth of that tamala tree—one of only a few still remaining in Vrndavana town — would indicate the spiritual health of his disciples.

In the corner of Prabhupadavana is Gajendra-moksana-kunda, which represents the lake in Bhauma Vrndavana’s Rangaji temple where the deliverance of Gajendra the elephant is enacted yearly.

An especially meaningful part of that pastime is when the Lord finally appears to answer the prayer of the king of elephants, who had been captured by a crocodile while bathing in a lake.

Gajendra was in great pain, but when he saw the Lord coming to his rescue, Sudarsana cakra in hand, he wanted to offer something in devotion and convey his respects, despite his awkward situation.

He reached out with his trunk and plucked a nearby white lotus. With great difficulty, because of his painful condition, the king of elephants raised the flower towards the Lord.

Teaching us to show respect to Krsna in every situation, Gajendra said, “O my Lord, Narayana! Master of the universe! ? Supreme Personality of Godhead! I offer my respectful obeisances unto You.”

We may easily serve and respect Radha and Syama when such practices fit our own plans for life, but then neglect them when Their Lordships’ own plans conflict with our ideas of personal comfort.

The pastime of gajendra-moksana instructs us to always place the service of the Supreme Lord before our own needs. Such an attitude of service will open the gate that leads to the Lord’s causeless mercy.

What is that causeless mercy? The Lord will personally appear to save us, as He saved Gajendra. And by the direct touch of His soft lotus hands we will at once be freed of all material ignorance and bondage.

Sri Suka says that those who daily recite or remember the Lord’s deliverance of Gajendra become recognised devotees, remain always unaffected by the influence of Kali, and never have bad dreams.

The area north of Prabhupadavana represents Ramana-reti, the pleasing sands of Vrndavana forest. Krsna described this place to His cowherd boyfriends when they came to this area.

Gopala said,

*aho 'tiramyam pulinam vayasyah sva-keli-sampan mrdulaccha-balukam*  
*sphutat-saro-gandha hrtali-patrika-dhvani pratidhvana-lasad-drumakulam*

**“My dear friends! Just see how this riverbank is extremely beautiful because of its pleasing atmosphere. And just see bow the blooming lotuses are attracting bees and birds by their aroma.**

**“The humming and chirping of the bees and birds echoes throughout the beautiful forest and the sands are clean and soft. Therefore, this must be considered the best place for our sporting and pastimes.’’**

When Krsna was living at Chatikara, the boys would bring their calves along the shore of the Yamuna until they arrived here to play, eat, and rest upon the cool sands in the shade of luxuriant trees.

And when Nanda Maharaja moved bis capital to Nandagrama, Ramana-reti became one of the cowherd boys’ favourite stops on their cow-herding tours throughout the forests of Vraja.

Krsna and Balarama would decorate the area of Ramana-reti with Their divine presence, Their friends, and Their cows, and fill the air with the sounds of Their songs, jokes, and cow-calls, like hai! hai!

The boys’ bodily complexions and clothes were of various colours, and so they appeared like a rainbow, descended upon the forest of Ramana-reti, with Krsna and Balarama as the pot of gold at its end.

Even the greatest of demigods could not hope to equal in beauty or opulence the least of the cowherd boys. To whom, then, could Krsna and Balarama he compared aside from Themselves?

Krsna’s and Balarama’s cowherd friends dressed and ornamented themselves with jewels, pearls, and gold, and carried flutes, bugles, and sticks in an effort to look like their two heroes.

The gopas also wore turbans of blue, yellow, white, and red silk. They wore Vaijayanti garlands made from forest flowers and carried walking sticks, fighting sticks, and sticks they used to herd the cows.

The facial features, bodily limbs, manner of dressing, and demeanour of these boys so resembled those of Krsna and Balarama that meditating on the gopas leads one to recall the divine brothers.

Just as the calves did when released from their corrals, the boys burst into the wide open spaces of Ramana-reti, dancing, singing, rolling on the ground, and bubbling over with boyish pride and bravado.

Meanwhile, the cows drank water from the Yamuna and rested beneath the trees, watching the commotion the boys made while enjoying various diversions like wrestling or catch.

Some servants busied themselves collecting fruit from the trees and setting these — mangoes, bananas, lotus stems, and other delights — in a festive array and calling the other boys when everything was ready.

It didn’t take much to draw the always-hungry boys to their forest picnic. They all brought out their own packed lunches and exchanged sweets and savouries with one another like market traders.

The boys took pleasure not in the fruit, but in Krsna and Balarama; the true taste of their meal was found in loving exchange — glancing at, joking with, and embracing the divine brothers.

In this way the boys made the treasure chest of fraternal love overflow. Their ecstasy spilled out here and there to nourish the trees, transform the lakes, and moisten the sands with that love.

**\* \* \* \***

Ramana-reti is saturated to this day with the mellows of fraternal love. It is the most suitable place for those devotees who wish to enter Krsna’s entourage in the ecstasy of a cowherd friend.

Govinda Maharaja and I had a friend, Dadu, who had left family life and gone to live in Radha-kunda to practise devotional service. After some time his guru asked him what mood of devotion he aspired for.

Dadu replied that he wanted to be a cowherd boy. His spiritual master said, “In that case, Radha-kunda is not suited to your mood. This place is for those who want to be gopis. ”

“Then where should I go?” Dadu enquired. His guru told him, “Go to Vrndavana, to the area of Ramana-reti where Krsna and Balarama give great pleasure to the cowherd boys. Try the ISKCON temple.”

Dadu came to Krsna-Balarama Mandira and became the temple postman, delivering devotees’ letters in and around the temple. He would often come to our residence with mail, or just to pay us a visit.

Govinda Maharaja had a close relationship with Dadu. Sometimes Maharaja would read Krsna’s cowherd pastimes to him, and Dadu would cry or show other symptoms of ecstasy.

Like a cowherd boy, Dadu was very fond of honouring prasada. He would glorify each preparation, eating much more than anyone else, and finishing his meal an hour or more after the others had finished.

At the age of eighty-seven, in Ramana-reti, Dadu left this world in his sleep. A year earlier he had been very ill. We had all thought that he would leave this world, but he miraculously recovered.

When asked why he didn’t die at that time, Dadu, who had been a teacher by profession, said, “Lord Gaitanya looked at my report card and saw that if I had died then, I would not have made it to Goloka.”

By that assessment, it seemed the extra year of sadhana and service in Vrndavana satisfied Caitanya Mahaprabhu. As other devotees slept, Dadu entered the eternal entourage of Krsna and Balarama.

Whatever our aspiration tor service, we must respect all of the Lord’s associates and pray to them for the fulfilment of our own mood of devotion. Even Rupa Gosvami would pray to the cowherd boys.

The great acarya writes, “O Subala! You are the intimate friend of the son and daughter of the best of the gopas in Vraja. Please be merciful and introduce this unhappy person to your two friends.”

In 1990 Govinda Maharaja and I had a small house built just a few hundred yards behind the Krsna-Balarama temple in Ramana-reti. Over the years I spent many happy days there.

Once I lived alone in the house for six months. During that time I received inspiration from Srila Prabhupada to write the Krsna in Vrndavana series, beginning with Venu-gita.

In Krsna-Balarama Mandira I once prayed to Radha and Syama, “Do You really want me to write these books?” At once the pujari took a garland from Radha’s hand and placed it around my neck.

In the lengthy process of composing this book, I have often turned to Radha-Syama in New Vraja-dhama tor support. Time and again They have reiterated Their desire that the glories of Their home be known.

In Ramana-reti resides the famous “Krsna-Balarama tree” — a white eucalyptus growing from the trunk of a black tamala — that symbolises the two divine brothers and is revered by pilgrims and residents alike.

The locals say that Radha and Krsna would meet here at night and then continue on together to Seva-kunja and the banks of the Yamuna, where They would freely enjoy each other’s company.

Here in New Vraja-dhama we have grafted a white Himalayan birch to a black Dahurian birch and planted it in the centre of Ramana-reti to represent the Krsna-Balarama tree in Bhauma Vrndavana.

The Krsna-Balarama tree is significant in many ways. The dark tamala tree, supporting the white eucalyptus, shows how Krsna is the basis and support of Balarama’s existence, indeed of all existence.

And since Balarama is the original guru, the embodiment of the guru'tattva principle, the Krsna-Balarama tree conveys that Sri Krsna and Sri Guru are the inseparable roots of the vraja-bhakti creeper.

Cakravarti Thakura glorifies Vraja’s trees:

**“The splendid trees of Vrajabhumi are perfectly spiritual in nature and full of all potencies. They are like incarnations of the Lord.**

**“Unfortunately, just as conditioned souls misunderstand the spiritual nature of the incarnations of the Supreme Lord Sri Krsna, they also misunderstand the glory of the trees in Vraja.”**

A short distance from the Krsna-Balarama tree is the Paksi-sthana, the bird sanctuary where Srimati Radharani once ordered a devotee to daily feed all the birds of Sri Vrndavana-dhama.

Many years ago a devotee named Krsna Dasa made a vow to remain absorbed in sankirtana of Krsna’s pastimes day and night, except for when he begged his daily quota of two capatis.

One night as Krsna Dasa was briefly dozing, Sri Radha, with a parrot on either shoulder, appeared and instructed him, “I want you to feed grains to the birds of Vrndavana every day. They are hungry.”

Radha smiled and then disappeared. Krsna Dasa awoke with a start and wondered how he would fulfil Radha’s order. “Where will I get enough food for the flocks of birds that live here?”

Krsna Dasa faithfully begged extra capatis from the Vraja-vasis. Putting aside two for himself, he tore up the others, spread them on the ground, and called the birds by saying, “Jaya Radhe!”

In a matter of minutes flocks of pigeons, parrots, and other birds came to consume the capati crumbs. And since many birds didn’t get anything, Krsna Dasa fed them bis two capatis and fasted.

This practice went on for some days, until Krsna Dasa was so weak from not having eaten that be could not go out to beg on behalf of bis feathered charges; be and the birds both went hungry that day.

That night Radha again appeared to Krsna Dasa and chastised him for neglecting Her order as well as bis health. But Krsna Dasa defended himself: “How can I feed so many birds?

“O Devi! Every day more and more birds come to eat here and I cannot collect enough capatis to feed them all. What should I do?” Radha replied, “I will arrange everything, don’t worry.”

Again Krsna Dasa awoke, this time wondering what Radha would arrange. How would She feed all those birds? At first light of dawn he found a large sack of seeds leaning against the wall of his cottage.

On top of the sack were two capatis rolled in a leal plate. Overcome with ecstasy at seeing this miracle, Krsna Dasa tainted. How had he been drawn into Srimati Radharani’s own pastime?

When Krsna Dasa returned to consciousness he reverently ate the capatis, but their celestial taste and divine influence stirred such spiritual emotions in him that he again entered into divine trance.

Finally, as afternoon approached, Krsna Dasa rose from his swoon and scattered the seeds on the ground while calling, “Jaya Radhe!” Soon his whole garden was swarming with birds.

Peacocks, cuckoos, pigeons, doves, parrots, and exotic birds in many varieties were happily pecking at this veritable feast. When he thought the birds had eaten enough, Krsna Dasa put away the seeds.

In this way Krsna Dasa fed the birds every morning and afternoon, and whenever the sack of seeds was empty, the next morning another fresh sack would be sitting outside his cottage.

Overjoyed that Srimati Radharani had directly engaged him in service to the winged residents of Vrndavana, Krsna Dasa continued, without fail, his daily practice of feeding them.

In time, locals and pilgrims alike came to know of Krsna Dasa’s service and brought feed for the birds. As soon as these other seeds came regularly, the sacks sent by Sri Radha ceased to appear.

Krsna Dasa deeply lamented that Radha no longer sent seeds Herself. In another dream She reassured him, “Don’t he sorry! I am sending food through others to give them the chance to serve Krsna’s dhama.

“Now I have given you service. Continue to feed the residents of the dhama without fail, and teach your followers to do the same. Those who follow you will come to Me at the end of their lives.”

And so it is that the practice of feeding birds has been going on at Paksi-sthana for many generations. May the residents of New Vraja-dhama receive Radha’s mercy by doing the same all year round.

**\* \* \* \***

At the far end of Ramana-reti, beside the first well dug in New Vraja-dhama, is one of many places that represent those arenas where the divine couple enjoy the exhilaration of the rasa dance.

All the residents of New Vraja-dhama, coming back and forth from their homes and the temple, daily pass through the purifying atmosphere of this transcendental pastime place.

In the summer months during festival times devotees sit around a large table in the shade of a nearby Chinese Emperor tree and blissfully cut vegetables to be cooked in the main kitchen.

Often, as I look out over this sacred place from my bedroom window, I meditate on its glories. I will here describe it as Vaisnava tradition does, in relation to Krsna’s excavating the well called Venu-kupa.

On the fourth day of the waning moon, Radha and Krsna, along with Their gopi friends, strolled leisurely through the forest of Vrndavana, happily praising one another’s transcendental virtues.

At times the divine couple walked, at other times They danced, and at one point They stopped to pick flowers to place behind Their ears before continuing on Their way, arms around each other’s waists.

Their honey-sweet smiles drew swarms of bees who hummed melodiously before Them, caused cuckoos to hop from tree to tree as They sang, and inspired peacocks to dance wildly around Them.

Wherever Radha and Krsna walked, the trees and vines burst into bloom even though it was night. And animals, like deer, followed along, sometimes coming forward to be petted by Their soft lotus hands.

The gopi’s were overjoyed to behold the bliss of Vrndavana forest, and they expressed their joy by singing beautiful songs while showering flower petals at the feet and over the heads of the divine couple.

The happy entourage stopped and rested for some moments at a beautiful clearing Kamadeva had apparently designed just for boys and girls who want to entertain each other with dancing.

As a gentle breeze laden with the sweet fragrance of flowers cooled Their limbs, the divine couple reclined in an attractive grove that overlooked the dancing arena and, below that, Vrndavana.

In groups, the gopis also rested. In one group a girl said to her companions, “Friends! Look at Radha and Krsna! Their radiant faces appear just like two full moons — one dark and one fair.”

Radha noticed Her friends whispering these sacred truths and said to Krsna, “How wonderful are My friends! Tonight their mastery at singing and playing instruments will be the key to Our happiness.

“But just now My mouth and throat feel very dry!” At this a maidservant immediately went to a well nearby and, after drawing a pitcher full of sweet water, offered Radha a cupful.

This well had come into being during the rasa dance on the full-moon night of autumn, when Krsna and Radha had left the other gopis behind to enjoy the beauty of the forest in each other’s company.

At that time also Radha had been thirsty. Since no water was available at that spot, Krsna pierced the ground with the sound of His flute. At once a fountain of crystal clear water rose out of the ground.

That fountain became famous amongst the gopis as Krsna’s liquefied flute-song. Thus, whenever they passed by that spot they would drink from it in order to savour the nectar of Krsna’s lotus lips.

While Radha was drinking, Vrnda-devi assembled the gopis into a large circle, making them hold hands (hallisaka). The musicians and singers started to play, and the circle gradually began to revolve.

As the ring of gopis whirled round, the girls clapped their hands in time with the beat of the drums, taking steps also synchronised with those rhythms — sometimes long and slow, sometimes short and quick.

The gopis gradually increased the tempo of their dance until they all appeared to merge into a golden halo that resembled Lord Visnu’s spinning cakra, mesmerising the divine couple.

As the circle again slowed, Radha sprang to Her feet and entered amongst Her friends, only to be lost in the swirling blur of gold, ringed by a rainbow made hy the gopis’ rippling, colourful skirts.

Krsna, unable to sit still a moment longer, also entered the circle. After bowing to the cheering gopis, He began to dance as only He could, but still coordinated His movements with the others.

The master of all arts clapped His flower-bud hands, making His bracelets jingle and His earrings sway. The graceful, wave-like movements of His body overwhelmed the moon and captivated Siva.

As the tempo of His dancing increased, Krsna’s sapphire bodily effulgence turned the golden band of gopis into a bright jade ring pulsating with the mellows of love that emanated from His form.

To increase their mutual bliss, Krsna would sometimes stop dancing to lead the singing as the gopis danced. At other times the gopi dancers would stop to sing as Krsna, the king of dancers, danced.

The peacocks became mad from their own dancing, the parrots lost their voices from chattering, and the trees teemed with singing cuckoos, but the dance had yet to reach its fullest climax.

All at once the gopis stopped dancing and singing and Krsna suddenly stood still. Then, as everyone — Krsna included — clapped wildly and called out “Jaya Radhe!” Sri Radha entered the circle.

Krsna took hold of Radha’s hand and slowly started dancing with Her while gazing at Her golden face, made all the more magnificent by Her flushed cheeks and the glint of perspiration on Her forehead.

At first the gopis watched Radha’s graceful movements and Krsna’s inimitable steps, Their mutual love-laden glances and occasional embraces, taking it all into their hearts for future remembrance.

When Radha danced alone, She looked like lightning decorating a stationary cloud, and when Krsna danced alone, He looked like a monsoon cloud surrounding a lightning bolt as it struck the earth.

And as the gopis danced around the flickering lightning bolt and the rumbling monsoon cloud, Kamadeva arrived, hoping to witness Krsna fan out amongst the cowherd maidens unlimitedly.

Who could tell whether Krsna had expanded into many forms or had remained one and simply moved so quickly amongst the many gopis that each thought He was dancing with her alone?

A multitude of stars brightened this scene alongside their companion, the moon, itself bewildered by seeing the host of dark and fair moons dancing beneath it and glinting in the dark of the night.

The moon wondered, “How many other moons glisten in the sky over Vrndavana?” And suddenly those moons transformed into countless gold vines embracing an equal number of blackish tamala trees.

Then, in an instant the vines transformed into charming golden lotuses vibrating the fifth note and the trees into black bumblebees humming mystic melodies, all of them resembling so many quivering lotuses.

These sweet sounds and beautiful forms charmed the hearts of those who beheld them and those who produced them alike, bringing all concerned to a single longing: “May tonight last forever!”

O my friends! If you see the beautiful garden in which the rāsa-līlā took place, decorated with vines broken by dancing, where the ground has been darkened by musk fallen from the gopīs’ bodies —

If you see that place where a special person — crowned with peacock feathers, dressed in garments splendid as gold, limbs as dark as a tamāla tree — filled all directions with glorious elegance —

If you see that place from which He flooded the world with bliss while glancing at a very special gopī and kissing a simple flute as it played mind-enchanting music — if you see it, your heart will feel great joy.

Beware! Don’t look at the charming cottage to which Rādhā and Kṛṣṇa retire after that splendid dance. If you do, you will be filled with such bliss that you will forget any worldly duty you may have.

Flashing His effulgence, Kṛṣṇa appeared in the centre of the hallīśaka circle. Fragrant with musk, flute to His lips, He exchanged loving glances with the gopīs. May that dancing Kṛṣṇa protect us!

**\*   \*   \*   \***

As already mentioned, Sevā-kuñja stretches as far as the Yamunā, and so Ramaṇa-retī is also part of Nikuñjavana, as is the area next to it, which is home to sacred Mohana-ṭeṛh and Jhāṛū-maṇḍala.

Allow me first to sing the glories of Mohana-ṭeṛh. To hear those glories is to free oneself from Cupid’s shackles, which keep even the greatest demigods —  not to mention ordinary men — in the grip of lust.

From Kṛṣṇa’s youth, the nocturnal rāsa dance and His other love-sports with the gopīs were the talk of the heavenly planets — from the private gatherings of heavenly goddesses to Indra’s court assemblies.

As Vaiṣṇavas, the demigods were enchanted by the spontaneous loving exchanges between the gopīs and Lord Kṛṣṇa, praising those exchanges as superior to their own motivated, regulated devotion.

Amongst the demigods, those who were advanced would recall the rāsa dance during their chanting and meditation and also meet in solitary places to discuss the esoteric truths of vraja-bhakti.

Cupid was not so spiritually advanced, as he was too passionate to fully appreciate the rāsa-līlā. But he had overheard enough from the talks of other demigods to feel a keen interest in seeing it for himself.

A long time ago Cupid had tried to entice Śiva to unite with Pārvatī, a venture that greatly enraged Lord Śiva, who then burned Cupid’s body to ashes, leaving the god of love in a ghost-like state.

After that time, Cupid — disembodied and invisible — continued to incite love within conditioned souls, annoyed that without a gross body he could no longer personally enjoy his art.

One day, Cupid — also known as Kāmadeva, the deity of lust, and Manmatha, the churner of hearts — decided that when he attended the rāsa dance he would incite the fire of passion within Kṛṣṇa.

Cupid could not believe the demigods’ glowing appraisals of Kṛṣṇa. He thought, “How is it possible that someone in the prime of youth can dance with beautiful young girls and still be untouched by lust?”

Determined to prove that Krsna was susceptible to his wiles, Cupid, armed with bow and arrows and accompanied by spring, a soft breeze, cuckoos, and humming bees, entered a garden of Nikunjavana.

Meanwhile, Krsna was enjoying Himself, dancing by moonlight in nearby Seva-kunja. Fully aware of Cupid’s intentions, He led Radha and the gopis on a stroll to view the splendour of Vrndavana.

Krsna took Radha to see Ramana-reti and the Krsna-Balarama tree, and from there — singing of each other’s love, qualities, and beauty — They headed west to the garden where Cupid lay in wait.

It was summer, but because Cupid had brought spring with him, as soon as Radha and Syama stepped into the garden, They felt refreshed and invigorated by the opulences of the prince of seasons.

Responding to the atmosphere they had entered, the divine couple sang a song in vasanta-raga, and as Cupid’s pet cuckoo sweetly sustained the fifth note, Radha and Krsna joyfully imitated it.

Cupid’s bees hummed around Radha and Krsna in another attempt to excite Their passions. But, in tribute to the bees, the divine couple took turns tasting the honey from each other’s lips.

Even though Krsna held Radha’s hand in His, Cupid could see that Their bodies radiated a most pure and sublime goodness, devoid of even the slightest trace of lust, passion, or ignorance.

Still intent on making his point, Cupid called upon a gentle breeze from the Malayan hills, fragrant with the smell of sandalwood, to excite Krsna, but Radha purified it of its ardour simply by Her praise.

By Their pure love, Radha and Krsna were neutralising all of Cupid’s attacks, and Cupid was unable to understand why his potencies could do nothing to inflame the hearts of the two lovers.

To Cupid, Radha and Krsna appeared to be just like the scores of other lovers he had excited to the heights of passion. But these two not only resisted his machinations but also counteracted them.

Indeed, Krsna was like a divine monsoon cloud, sprinkling the gopis, the forest creatures, and even the trees with a special sort of passion, saturated with love but lacking even the slightest trace of lust.

As the gopis filled the air with a shower of fragrant flower pollen, Krsna placed the flute to His lips and began to slowly dance with Radha, while the girls danced around Them in a circle, singing.

The sound of Krsna’s flute entered Cupid’s ear like a mystic mantra. And like a virus it spread through his being, sapping him of vitality until he felt his life coming to an end.

As a last resort, Cupid shot an arrow at his sapphire target. But the weapon that had aroused Brahma entered Lord Krsna’s body without any visible effect, like a pebble falling into a raging river.

Cupid released his arrows one after another, but instead of returning to his quiver after being shot, as per usual, they all disappeared into Krsna’s body, leaving Cupid weaponless, bewildered, and humiliated.

“Perhaps the demigods were right,” thought Cupid. Gradually, as he pondered the sublime nature of Krsna’s loving dealings, true faith bubbled up in Cupid, profoundly altering his vision of Lord Krsna.

As the gopis happily sang and danced around Krsna like a golden wheel, the Lord stood, His body bent in three places, accompanying His beloveds with His flute, all the while staring at Cupid.

Just as Krsna had revealed Himself to Brahma after the creator’s vain attempt to test his mystic powers on the Lord by stealing the cowherd boys and cows, He now revealed Himself to Cupid.

The god of love could now see Krsna as the original transcendental Cupid, who invests a perverted, material reflection of His amorous potency in the living entity known as Cupid in this world.

Because Cupid’s weapons and associates were reflections of Krsna’s own potency, using them against Him was like pouring a handful of water into the ocean—they had no effect whatsoever on the Lord.

Now Cupid could understand all of this. He gazed at Krsna’s unfathomable beauty, marvelled at His prowess, and regretted having impertinently challenged the Lord with such trivial enticements.

Noting Cupid’s repentant attitude, Krsna took pity on him. For a brief moment Krsna let Cupid taste the gopis’ love for Him, enabling Cupid to see Krsna exactly as the cowherd girls do.

His heart and eyes now bathed in love, Cupid felt an overwhelming upsurge of pure amorous attraction to Sri Krsna — sweet, selfless, and charitable love, not comparable to any mundane sentiment.

That divine ecstasy caused Cupid to forget his own identity. Deluded, he thought himself to be one of the gopis attending Krsna, and he foolishly tried to show his love for the Lord as the gopis did.

But Cupid had neither a gopis’ body nor the qualification to serve Krsna in the manner of the gopis; thus his mounting ecstasy could only express itself as bewilderment (mohana), which ended in a faint.

Krsna is known as Madana-mohana, the attractor of Cupid. Because Krsna’s attractiveness so overwhelmed Cupid that he fainted in ecstasy on this spot, this place is known as Mohana-terh.

Cupid was not visible to the gopis while all this was going on, and Krsna did not want to interrupt the rasa dance, so the gopis were aware of only their ecstatic exchanges with Sri Krsna.

But Krsna’s blessings freed Cupid from Lord Siva’s curse. Kamadeva was given not only a new body but also the opportunity to advance spiritually by joining the Lord’s earthly entourage. How?

That night Cupid merged with the Vasudeva aspect of Krsna’s body and later took birth from Rukmini in Dvaraka, appearing within the body of the Lord’s visnu-tattva expansion Pradyumna.

Having once again obtained a body, Cupid was reunited with his consort Rati. He lived with her in Dvaraka until the destruction of the Yadu dynasty, after which he returned to the heavenly planets.

Cupid’s intimate connection with Pradyumna indicates that Cupid’s devotion to Krsna is mixed, not spontaneous. For this reason, he cannot enter Vraja; nor do his weapons have any effect .there

Indeed, the dominion of Pradyumna — the spiritual Cupid — over Vaikuntha, where he influences the hearts of the Lord’s consorts with amorous attraction, extends only as tar as Dvaraka.

As Kaviraja Gosvami says, in Vrndavana there is one, and only one, Cupid, vrndavane ‘aprakrta navina madana': “In the spiritual realm of Vrndavana, Krsna is the spiritual, ever fresh Cupid.”

Let us worship that ever fresh Cupid by chanting Kama-gayatri and the Hare Krsna maha-mantra without offence, remaining immune to the arrows of the mundane Cupid who stalks conditioned souls.

The defeat of Cupid at Mohana-terh is a confidential pastime. Devotees who delve deeply into its significance will gain a rare fruit that even great demigods like Brahma long for — freedom from lust.

Rupa Gosvami quotes an anonymous Vaisnava’s advice on how to overcome the otherwise insurmountable influence of Cupid,

*kasayan na ca bhojanadi-niyaman no va vane vasato —*  
*Vyakhyanad athava muni-wrata-bharac cittodbhavah ksiyate*  
*kintu sphita-kalinda-saila-tanaya-tiresu vikridato govindasya padaravinda-bhajanarambhasya lesad api*

**“Not by wearing saffron cloth, not by restricting food and other sensual activities, not by living in the forest, not by discussing philosophy, not by observing a vow of silence —**

**“But only by taking up the process of devotional service to the lotus feet of Lord Govinda, who enjoys pastimes on the Yamuna’s wide banks, can one stop the influence of Kamadeva, Cupid.”**

**\* \* \* \***

On the full-moon night of autumn, Radha and Krsna left the gopis at Seva-kunja and walked throughout Nikunjavana, enjoying each other’s company as it They were alone in the world.

The divine couple explored the natural beauty of the torest, leisurely enjoying its lakes, gardens, and streams, and unaware that the other gopis were following Their footprints and quickly approaching.

By the time that They reached a large tree just south of Mohana-terh, Radha was too tired to continue. Just as She was about to climb up on Krsna’s shoulder, They heard the voices of the gopis.

Unwilling to face the gopis’ criticism, Krsna suddenly disappeared, casting Srimati Radharani into a bottomless chasm of loneliness, from which She was mercifully saved by a fainting spell.

The gopis found Radha in such a pitiful condition that even those amongst them who had envied Her stroll with Krsna were at once cleansed of their jealousy by the incontrovertible depth of Her love.

Devotees still gather at the tree under which Radha’s unbounded happiness transformed into fathomless sorrow to meditate on the rasa-lila, and to hear why Krsna abruptly left His lover.

Indeed, that tree would have perished long ago from the shame of being a reminder of Radha’s suffering, had Krsna not desired that it live to serve the dhama and the devotees who congregate there.

For centuries devotees have visited this place of separation (Varaha-sthana) and relished Krsna’s pastimes, their tears watering the tree that became known as the “tree of separation” (Viraha-taru).

This area (mandala) became a popular place for devotees to gather and hear hari-katha, and so the locals kept it clean by sweeping the area with a broom (jham). Thus it came to be known as Jharu-mandala.

Once, a blind old woman who made her living selling wheat flour took up residence nearby. She would grind grains day and night, including the times devotees gathered here to discuss Krsna.

Unfortunately, her grinding became a significant distraction for Jharu-mandala visitors, who could hear the noise from her mill more easily than they could the narrations of Radha’s and Krsna’s pastimes.

Repeated attempts were made to convince the woman to stop her grinding when devotees were in the area or move to another location, but she was too poor to refuse a customer and too old to relocate.

Finally the local pandas and resident devotees became so upset that they decided to choose another site to congregate, anywhere they could hear and be heard without disturbance.

Krsna, however, did not want His devotees to abandon this pastime place. Deciding to intervene their behalf, He appeared before the blind woman and empowered her to see Him.

To stop the racket she was causing, the Lord placed His loot on the grinding stone and said, “Don’t you know that you’re disturbing My devotees by creating this noise? You must stop this at once!”

The old lady was in ecstasy at seeing Krsna. She bowed repeatedly to Him and then finally replied, “O Lord! Whatever You order, I will certainly follow. But I must ask You, how I will live?

“My tiny income is from selling wheat flour. If I stop grinding, my income will stop. Then I won’t be able to afford to eat, not to mention buy the flowers and fruit I offer to You as a meagre service.”

Taking compassion on His devotee, Krsna pressed His foot firmly into the grinding stone, leaving a clear impression of His footprint. The old woman gazed quizzically at the footprint and then at the Lord.

Pointing to His footprint, Krsna said, “News that My footprint is impressed on your grinding stone will spread throughout Vraja. Locals and pilgrims alike will come to visit it and offer their respects.

“Worship this footprint with tulasi leaves and flowers. As its custodian, you can live from the coins people place on it. Have faith. I will ensure that your income is sufficient for your maintenance.”

Krsna then disappeared. The old woman stopped grinding grains and served the Lord’s footprint instead. Just as Krsna promised, she was able to subsist comfortably on the donations left by pilgrims.

From that time on, the pilgrims were not only free to hear Krsna’s pastimes at Jharu-mandala without disturbance but also to meditate upon Krsna’s lotus footprint as they listened.

This story is evidence of Krsna’s deep concern that we have the opportunity to hear about Him. He is willing to intervene personally when there is some interruption to that act of devotional service.

Srimad-Bhagavatam urges that in order to purify the heart and attain real devotion, we should hear Krsna’s glories continually and without interruption, nityam bhagavata-sevaya.

From Gajendra-moksana-kunda, I continued straight along what is known on Hungary’s official road maps as “Nitai Circular Road” until I reached Brahma-kunda, a place of great spiritual significance.

Brahma-kunda is situated within Asokavata, which is one of Vrnda-devi’s residences. It is full of birds, forest creatures, and sylvan goddesses, all ready to serve Krsna according to Vrnda’s orders.

On the southern side of Brahma-kunda in Vraja is a special white asoka tree that is said to hurst into bloom at noon on one day in the month of Vaisakha — only pure souls can understand how this is so.

Asoka trees are connected with the god of love because one of his arrows is made from asoka blossoms. It is also said that these trees burst into bloom when touched by the feet of beautiful girls.

Brahma-kunda was formed from the tears of joy Lord Brahma shed after he was humbled by the Lord’s display of power and he again saw Krsna as a little boy with food in His hands, searching for His friends.

Brahma had come from his abode to see for himself the cowherd boy that the demigods were saying was their Lord Narayana incarnate. Brahma was incredulous when he first saw child Krsna.

Unable to believe that Krsna was the Supreme Lord, Brahma stole Krsna’s calves and cowherd boyfriends, put them to sleep in a cave nearby, and then disappeared for a year of earthly time.

Unperturbed, Krsna expanded Himself into replicas of the kidnapped boys and calves and maintained those expansions for that year. In this way He replaced the offspring of Vraja’s cowherds and cows.

When Brahma returned to see how Krsna had reacted to his mischief, he was shocked to find that one set of boys and calves was playing with Krsna while another was still lying in mystic slumber.

At first Brahma could not tell which set of boys and calves were the originals, hut when Krsna’s expansions all manifested four arms and displayed unlimited potencies, the creator was completely baffled.

For some time Brahma lost all external awareness. When he finally opened his eyes, he saw the sweetness of Vrndavana, and Krsna all alone looking for His friends, just as He had been a year before.

Demigods are not supposed to touch the earth, hut out of intense humility, Brahma got off his swan carrier, fell to the ground, and bathed Krsna’s lotus feet with tears of joy from his eight eyes.

Brahma’s tears were transformations of his ecstatic love, and when they washed Krsna’s feet those tears became even more pure — divine caranamrta, collected in what is now known as Brahma-kunda.

A person who fasts for one night and then on the next day bathes four times in Brahma-kunda can attain Gandharvaloka, hut one whose aspirations are spiritual can even attain Vaikuntha.

Radha and Krsna sometimes come to Asokavata to collect flowers for Their Sanji decorations because of the abundance of flowering trees and vines there, especially on the hanks of Brahma-kunda.

The wooded area around Brahma-kunda is exceedingly beautiful, replete with groves in which the divine couple enjoy Their pastimes and cottages where self-realised souls reside.

Srila Prabhupada comments that Bilvamangala Thakura, the author of Krsna-karnamrta, lived at Brahma-kunda for seven hundred years. That made him a contemporary of the Six Gosvamis.

In Bhauma Vrndavana the kunda is in a dilapidated state. Aside from a small sandstone temple of Brahma and a temple in the form of a circular tower, not much remains to show the glory of this great place.

Before leaving Brahma-kunda, I recalled two of the prayers the creator offered to Lord Krsna at this place. In the first he praised the good fortune of the residents of Vrndavana.

*Aho bhagyam aho bhagyam nanda-gopa-vrajaukasam*  
*yan-mitram paramanandam purnam brahma sanatanam*

**“How greatly fortunate are Nanda Maharaja, the gopas and the other Vraja-vasis!**

**“There is no limit to their good fortune, because Sri Krsna, the Absolute Truth, the source of transcendental bliss, the eternal Supreme Brahman, has become their friend.”**

The residents of New Vraja-dhama share a similar good fortune, because the same Supreme Lord, accompanied by His eternal consort, has come to reside in their village as their worshipable Deity.

In the next well-known verse, Brahma desires to take the dust from the lotus feet of the Vraja-vasis, which is also the aspiration of the residents of New Vraja-dhama—to be servants of the Vraja-vasis.

*Tad bhumi-bhagyam iha janma kim apy atavyam yad gokule ’pi katam-anghri-rajo-bhis ekam*  
*yaj-jivitam tu nikhilam bhagavan mukundas tv adyapi yat-pada-rajah sruti-mrgyam eva*

**“My greatest possible good fortune would be to take any birth whatever in this forest of Gokula and have my head bathed by the dust falling from the lotus feet of any of its residents.**

**“Their entire life and soul is Sri Krsna, the Supreme Personality of Godhead, Mukunda, the dust of whose lotus feet is still being searched for in the Vedic mantras.”**

**\* \* \* \***

Adjacent to Asokavata is the forest called Gahvaravana, where renounced saints live. In New Vraja-dhama it is represented by the brahmacari-asrama, which is also the bhajana-kutira of Rupa Gosvami.

The sage Yajnavalkya describes brahmacarya as the vow

**“...to help one completely abstain from sex indulgence in work, words, and mind — at all times, under all circumstances, and in all places.”**

The literal meaning of brahmacarya is “acts for the sake of Brahman.” Those who live in the brahmacari-asrama give up all varieties of sex indulgence to engage exclusively in Radha’s and Syama’s service.

Brahmacaris are of two kinds: upakurvana, celibates receiving training for household life who later marry, or naisthiki, students who take lifelong vows of celibacy and simplicity.

Krsna says that celibate students who eventually marry are situated on the loins of His universal form, whereas those who take a vow of life-long celibacy are situated in the heart of that same form.

The brahmacaris of today may not be able to adopt the strict practices recommended for earlier ages, but they must embrace the basic principles of their order of life as laid out by Srila Prabhupada.

Those principles are purity, self-control, and obedience to the orders of the spiritual master. Moreover, a brahmacari should beg alms on behalf of his guru and accept for himself only what his guru allots.

In this way the spiritual master trains both kinds of brahmacaris for their respective goals in life, based on following the order of their guru, with devotional service to Krsna as their top priority.

The ascetics of Gahvaravana in Bhauma Vrndavana follow Svami Haridasa. They live an austere life, covering their bodies in the dust of Vrndavana-dhama and wearing only loincloths.

Srila Rupa Gosvami was the perfect exemplar, and thus teacher, of the principles of renounced life. In fact, Lord Caitanya personally set the standards of Rupa Gosvamis devotional life, or bhajana.

After leaving his government post in Ramakeli, Rupa Gosvami set out for Puri hoping to meet Caitanya Mahaprabhu, stopping at Fatehabad along the way to distribute a large portion of his wealth.

But Lord Caitanya had already left for Vrndavana through Jharikhanda, and so Rupa also set out for Vraja with Anupama, taking the jungle route and finally meeting the Lord in Prayaga.

After receiving instruction from Caitanya Mahaprabhu at the Dasasvamedha-ghata, Rupa left for Vrndavana. Meanwhile, the Lord went on to Varanasi and then returned to Jagannatha Puri.

By this time, some of Lord Caitanya’s associates were living in Vrndavana. Of these devotees, Sri Rupa first met Subuddhi Raya, who took him on Parikrama of the twelve forests of Vraja.

Keen to meet Sanatana, Rupa left after only one month and headed for Varanasi. Thus at that time he did not meet Sri Caitanya’s other followers in Vraja, such as Lokanatha Gosvami and Bhugarbha Gosvami.

Caitanya Mahaprabhu had told Rupa that his elder brother Sanatana had escaped from prison, and so Rupa Gosvami reasoned that he would meet Sanatana somewhere along the road leading to Mathura.

But because Rupa travelled along the bank of the Ganges while Sanatana took the main thoroughfare, they passed each other without meeting. Rupa thus arrived in Varanasi disappointed.

Rupa then decided to travel on to Puri in order to see Lord Caitanya, rather than return to Vraja. He spent the next ten months in Puri, where he stayed at the cottage of Haridasa Thakura.

Rupa Gosvami had been in the employ of Muslims, and so he regarded himself as untouchable, as did Haridasa, a Muslim hy birth. They saw Lord Caitanya’s kirtana and Ratha-yatra from a distance.

Every day the Lord would bring maha-prasada to the two saints, who were absorbed in their respective bhajanas — Haridasa Thakura chanting japa, and Rupa Gosvami composing devotional works.

Rupa Gosvami had already composed two books, Hamsaduta and Uddhava-Sandesa. He had begun work on a drama of Krsna’s pastimes, and Lord Caitanya told him to divide it into two separate works.

Caitanya Mahaprabhu had already empowered Rupa Gosvami to carry out his mission in Vrndavana, but in Puri that empowerment was magnified by the blessings of the Lord’s associates.

Great devotees like Ramananda Raya and Svarupa Damodara Gosvami took pleasure in Rupa’s devotion, humility, poetic skill, and deep realisation of the mellows of devotion, to its pinnacle of prema.

Finally, Sri Rupa took leave of the devotees and the Lord, who reminded him, “Write devotional books, preach krsna — bhakti, and rediscover the places of Krsna’s pastimes. In time I will visit you.”

But Lord Caitanya would not return to Vrndavana. And once again, as Rupa Gosvami was on his way to Vraja by the road through Visnupura, Sanatana was travelling to Puri through Jharikhanda.

Upon arriving in Mathura, Rupa Gosvami was disappointed to hear that he had again missed his brother. But their separation was shortlived, for Lord Caitanya ordered Sanatana to return to Vraja.

In 1519 Rupa and Sanatana finally met at Dhruva-ghata and realised their long-cherished dream of living together in Vrndavana as renunciants — a dream for which they had risked their lives.

Rupa Gosvami took up residence in Seva-kunja, in the area that would later he home to the Radha-Damodara temple, while Sanatana Gosvami lived a short walk away in the forest atop Dvadasaditya-tila.

Following Caitanya Mahaprabhu’s order, Rupa Gosvami compiled and wrote books, preached, found lost places of Krsna’s pastimes, and eventually established the worship of Govindadji at Govinda-sthali.

In all these efforts, hy his personal example Sri Rupa also set a high standard of strict renunciation and intense sadhana, which along with his devotional scholarship spread his fame throughout India.

Devotees would regularly go on pilgrimage to Vrndavana, and because Rupa and Sanatana were so dear to Caitanya Mahaprabhu and His associates, the pilgrims would visit the two Gosvamis while in Vraja.

When pilgrims returned from Vrndavana, the Lord’s associates would ask, “How are Rupa and Sanatana? How do they execute their duties as renunciants? How do they cat and what are their devotional acts?”

Those who had just returned from Vraja would say, “The brothers have no fixed residence. They live under a different tree every night, and they eat by begging a little food from the homes of brahmanas.

“From the flour and chickpeas they beg, Rupa and Sanatana make dry bread and fried chickpeas. They drink from water pots they carry with them and clothe themselves with torn, discarded quilts.

“The transcendental brothers are engaged in devotional service almost twenty-four hours daily. Each night they sleep for one and a half hours at most, although sometimes they do not sleep at all.

“They always chant Krsna’s names, discuss His pastimes, and dance in ecstasy. Sometimes they write devotional books, sometimes they discuss Lord Caitanya, sometimes they think about Him.”

The Lord’s associates would never tire of hearing about Rupa’s and Sanatana’s bhajana, repeatedly exclaiming, “What is wonderful for a person who has been granted the Lord’s mercy?”

May we too take inspiration from Rupa Gosvamis personal example and contemplate the exemplary service of those who receive the mercy of the Lord. May we too aspire to attain similar good fortune.

Rupa Gosvami has written about Lord Caitanya’s mercy in Bhakti-rasamrta-sindhu,

*hrdi yasya preranaya pravartito ’ham varaka-ru?? 'pi*  
*tasya hareh pada-kamalam vande caitanya-devasya*

**“Although I am the lowest of men and have no knowledge, the inspi ration to write transcendental literatures about devotional service has been mercifully bestowed upon me.**

**“Therefore I offer my obeisances at the lotus feet of Sri Caitanya Mahaprabhu, the Supreme Personality of Godhead, who has given me the chance to write these books.”**

**\* \* \* \***

As Rupa Gosvamis and Sanatana Gosvamis devotional fame spread throughout the land, pilgrims would visit Vrndavana not only to see holy places but also to study the principles of pure devotion from the two saints.

Caitanya Mahaprabhu had told Sanatana Gosvami that other devotees would follow them to Vrndavana. The Lord asked Sanatana to find living quarters tor such devotees and train them.

To assist his brother in his service to Caitanya Mahaprabhu, Rupa Gosvami assumed the responsibility of caring tor new residents and the growing number of pilgrims, whether alone or in groups.

When a new devotee came to Vrndavana, it was Rupa Gosvami who would give him a place to live, teach him how to maintain himself, and guide that devotee in his spiritual practices.

Of the other four Gosvamis, Gopala Bhatta was the first to arrive in Vraja. He arrived just before Caitanya Mahaprabhu left this world, an event that cast a cloud of sorrow over that great Vaisnava.

Next to arrive was Raghunatha Dasa Gosvami, who came from Puri intent on committing suicide by jumping trom Govardhana Hill but was dissuaded from the act by Sanatana’s preaching.

Then came Jiva Gosvami and Raghunatha Bhatta Gosvami. In due course, all these saints installed Deities, at first worshipping Them under humble circumstances and later in ornate temples.

Only Govindadji was installed while Lord Caitanya was still on the planet; indeed, Rupa Gosvami wrote to the Lord informing Him of how he had found and installed Govinda on Goma-tila.

Lord Caitanya responded to the news by sending His godbrother Kasisvara Pandita and a Deity of Himself—Gaura-Govinda — thereby fulfilling His previous promise to meet Rupa in Vraja.

Rupa Gosvami was a great scholar, and yet he displayed the humility typical of great devotees. His dealings with other Vaisnavas, with pilgrims, and even with inimical persons, reflected this humility.

Rupa Gosvamis fame also attracted proud scholars eager to assert the supremacy of their learning by debating with him, but Rupa would decline such debates in favour of signing certificates of defeat.

Vallabhacarya, whom Rupa Gosvami had met in Prayaga, once suggested a change to a verse of Bhakti-rasamrta-sindhu, which Rupa humbly accepted. Jiva, however, debated the acarya over the change.

Rupa Gosvami, angry with his nephew-disciple for this minor insolence, sent him away, but Sanatana Gosvami finally intervened on behalf of young Jiva and brought him back to Seva-kunja.

After residing in Vrndavana for thirty-five years, Sanatana Gosvami left his body at Manasa-ganga, an event as painful for the Vaisnava community of Vraja as was the departure of Lord Caitanya.

Rupa Gosvami performed the funeral ceremony, placed the ashes in a samadhi near Madana-mohana, arranged a grand feast for the devotees, and retired to his bhajana-kutira, never to come out again.

Absorbed in nama-sankirtana, bitterly lamenting the loss of his lifelong companion and well-wisher, Rupa Gosvami left the world for Goloka barely a month after Sanatana Gosvami had departed.

Srila Prabhupada paraphrases a verse from Caitanya-candramrta to glorify Srila Rupa Gosvami:

**“The qualities of one engaged in the service of Lord Sri Caitanya Mahaprabhu — such as reputation —**

**“Austerities, penances, and knowledge — are not to be compared to the good qualities of others. Such is the perfection of a devotee always engaged in the service of Caitanya Mahaprabhu.”**

Prabodhananda Sarasvati writes,

**“Let renunciation be multiplied millions of times! Let virtues, beginning with peacefulness, sense control, tolerance, and friendliness be multiplied millions of times!**

**“Let there be millions of meditations on the words tat tvam asi! Let there be devotion to Lord Visnu multiplied millions of times! All this taken together does not equal even a millionth part —**

**“Of the multitude of perfect transcendental qualities possessed by the great souls \[like Srila Rupa Gosvami\] who find transcendental bliss in the splendour of the toenails of Sriman Caitanya-candra.”**

In the woods south of the brahmacari-asrama is Jnana-gudari. It marks the place where Uddhava and Vidura met beneath a banyan tree and recalled the Lord’s pastimes after Krsna’s disappearance.

This place takes its name from the ascetics in Bhauma Vrndavana, who wear patchwork clothes (gudari) and are in search of the knowledge (jnana) Uddhava shared with Vidura.

Sukadeva Gosvami describes how both Uddhava and Vidura were travelling north on different roads from Prabhasa, where the Yadus had killed each other and Lord Krsna had left this world.

While passing through Kurujangala, Vidura reached the bank of the river Yamuna in Vrndavana. There he met and, out of great affection, embraced Uddhava, Krsna’s great devotee and cousin.

As they sat beneath a banyan tree, Vidura enquired from Uddhava about the welfare of Krsna, the Yadus, Kurus, and Pandavas, although he was already well aware that all those he named had left the world.

At first, Uddhava — great devotee that he was — fell into a trance of separation from Krsna, but then he came to external consciousness and gently informed Vidura that Krsna had departed.

Uddhava said, “My dear Vidura! The sun of the world, Lord Krsna, has set, and our house has now been swallowed by the great snake of time. What can I say to you about our welfare?”

As lamentable as Krsna’s disappearance was, Uddhava assuaged Vidura’s sadness by pointing out the greater misfortune of the Yadus, who thought that Krsna was the Supersoul incarnated in their family.

It was misfortune to lose the company of the Supreme Lord, Uddhava explained, but it was a greater misfortune not to have recognised Him for who He was while He was present in the world.

And like the Yadus, the demigods were also unable to recognise Krsna; for example, at the Rajasuya sacrifice of Maharaja Yudhisthira they mistook the Lord to be the best being created by Brahma.

But not everyone unaware of Krsna’s identity was unfortunate. Uddhava glorified the gopis, who, though blinded by their love for Krsna and thus ignorant of His divinity, relished the greatest bliss.

Uddhava then steered the topic to Krsna’s departure, explaining that Yogamaya had made it appear that Krsna was taking birth and dying, just as she reveals the Lord in various ways to various devotees.

Krsna is like fire in wood. He comes out when friction between the devotees and non-devotees induces His internal potency to make Him visible. With His mission complete, He again becomes invisible.

Thus Uddhava pacified Vidura’s feelings of separation from the Lord with words deeply philosophical, similar to those he had once delivered in a handwritten letter from Krsna to the gopis.

He assured Vidura that true Krsna consciousness is not influenced by lesser perceptions,

*devasya mayaya sprsta ye canyad asad-asritah*  
*bhramyate dhir na tad-vakyair atmany uptatmano harau*

**“Under no circumstances can the words of persons bewildered by the illusory energy of the Lord deviate the intelligence of those who are completely surrendered souls.”**

When Uddhava saw that Vidura was pacified, he narrated Krsna’s pastimes, first speaking of the Lord’s human-like conduct, the mere thought of which brought tears of distress to Uddhava’s eyes.

**\* \* \* \***

How wonderful it was that Krsna, who could destroy all demons hy the movement of His eyebrows, took birth in Karhsa’s prison, fled to Vraja, lived there incognito, and then fled again from Mathura.

It was still more wonderful how He apologised to His parents for not saving them in prison, or how He submitted reports to Ugrasena, saying, “O My lord! Please let it be known to you!”

But only fools would misconstrue these acts to be signs of Krsna’s human frailty! The Lord also liberated Sisupala in the Rajasuya sacrifice as well as those who were killed at Kuruksetra.

If we were to search for one reason why Krsna behaves like this, the reason would he that it is His nature to be kind. The proof: He shows kindness not only to His devotees but also to demons.

Speaking in this way, the waves of Krsna’s merciful nature carried the driftwood of Uddhava’s mind out to the sea of Krsna’s pastimes. Driven by ecstasy, he spoke these famous words:

**“Alas! How shall I take shelter of one more merciful than He who granted the position of mother to Putana although she was unfaithful and she prepared deadly poison to be sucked from her breast?”**

The demons saw Krsna while dying by His hand, but devotees like Uddhava would not see Krsna at the time of death, and so he praised Krsna’s kindness to the demons more than that given to the devotees.

Seeing Vidura distraught at this outpouring of emotion, Uddhava controlled his own ecstasy and remained silent for some moments, after which he again began to speak, now smiling.

One by one, Uddhava recalled Krsna’s childhood, boyhood, and youthful pastimes — concluding with the rasa dance — during the eleven happy years that the Lord had spent in Vrndavana.

Uddhava said, “In the third season of the year, the Lord enjoyed as the central beauty of the assembly of women by attracting them with His pleasing songs in an autumn night brightened by moonshine.”

Unable to stop speaking and urged on by Vidura, Uddhava continued to narrate Krsna’s pastimes in Mathura, especially those of His killing Kamsa and His studying with Sandipani Muni.

Uddhava then traced Krsna’s exploits in Dvaraka: His marrying many wives — headed by Rukmini — killing demons like Naraka, and luring the kings of the earth to their destruction at Kuruksetra.

Finally Uddhava came to recent events. He told Vidura how, by Krsna’s arrangement, the Yadus were cursed by brahmanas and then how they annihilated one another a few months later at Prabhasa.

That left only one more subject to discuss — Krsna’s disappearance. Although the Lord had instructed Uddhava to go to Badarikasrama, Uddhava had turned back and followed the Lord to Prabhasa.

Upon his arrival, Uddhava saw the carnage that was left after the Yadus had slain one another. He soon found Krsna sitting beneath a banyan tree. Only the sage Maitreya was in His company.

With great difficulty Uddhava recounted bis final meeting with the Lord, including the prayers he had offered to Krsna in full knowledge that these were the Lord’s last moments on earth.

Out of kindness to His devotee, Krsna instructed Uddhava one final time, repeating the same transcendental knowledge He had once spoken to Brahma and by which we may attain enlightenment.

Unable to continue speaking about the events at Prabhasa, Uddhava surveyed the beautiful moonlit forests by Yamuna’s banks, a scene that intensified his thoughts of Krsna and prompted these words:

“I have studied the path of understanding self-knowledge from my spiritual master, Sri Krsna, and thus after circumambulating Him I have come to this place, very much aggrieved due to separation.

“My dear Vidura! Now I am mad for want of the pleasure of seeing Him, and just to mitigate this I am now proceeding to Badarikasrama in the Himalayas for association, as I have been instructed hy Him.”

Vidura was keen to hear Krsna’s final instructions to Uddhava, hut enquire as he might, Uddhava would not repeat them; instead, he told Vidura to enquire from Maitreya, who had also heard the Lord speak.

Dear devotees! Here at Jnana-gudari let us contemplate the reason Srila Prabhupada gave for why Uddhava deferred to the sage Maitreya and would not accept Vidura as his disciple.

His Divine Grace refers to a serious breach of Vaisnava etiquette that constitutes the offence scriptures call maryada-vyatikrama, or “impertinently surpassing a greater personality.”

Uddhava was at least as qualified to instruct Vidura as Maitreya was, hut because Vidura was old enough to he Uddhava’s father, protocol demanded that he he instructed hy someone of comparable age.

Srila Prabhupada strongly warns,

**“The rule is that in the presence of a higher personality one should not he very eager to impart instructions, even if one is competent and well versed.”**

Pursuant to this rule, Uddhava declined to instruct Vidura any further. The two spent that night together beneath the banyan tree, resting occasionally and sharing their remembrances of the Lord.

At dawn the two saints bathed and performed their morning rites. As Uddhava prepared to leave for Badarikasrama, he disclosed to Vidura a final piece of information about the Lord’s departure.

Just as Krsna was about to leave this world, out of His causeless mercy He had remembered Vidura. Hearing this, Vidura was overwhelmed by the ecstasy of love; indeed, he cried uncontrollably like a child.

After Uddhava’s departure, Vidura remained for a few more days in Vrndavana, thinking of Krsna. Then, desiring to hear the Lord’s final teachings, he headed for Haridvara to find Maitreya.

O gentle readers! I have summarised the conversation at Jnanagudari so that you might briefly hear Krsna’s exploits outside of Vraja, including His departure. All the Lord’s pastimes are transcendental.

If you want to know what message the Lord left for His devotees before leaving this world, then please study Maitreya Muni’s teachings to Vidura and be similarly benefitted.

It is also said that Prayaga-tirtha once came to Jnana-gudari in the form of a black horse. The colour represented the many sins Prayaga had accumulated from the pilgrims who had bathed in his waters.

But when he bathed here in the river Yamuna, his black colour turned white, indicating that he had been purified of all sins. Pilgrims bathe here when the floodwaters of the Yamuna reach this point.

In the northern corner of Gahvaravana lies a temple of goddess Kali called Katyayani-pitha, or Bhadrakali-tirtha. This temple dates back to Puranic times when gods and demons waged war with one another.

The demigods found the demons’ leader, Raktabija, completely indestructible, so Durga, from her forehead, invoked Kali, who single-handedly slaughtered the demons in a terrible battle.

So intoxicated was Kali by her victory that she danced wildly on the battlefield, even over the body of her husband, Siva. Seeing this display, Lord Visnu cut her into pieces with His cakra.

The parts of Kali’s body fell in various places. A clump of her hair is said to have fallen at Bhadrakali-tirtha, where a deity of Kali was later established and worshipped by the Vraja-vasis.

With Dhanya as their leader, the young gopis who wanted to marry Krsna regularly came here to worship Kali in her form as Katyayani for the fulfilment of their desire, hence the name Katyayani-pitha.

Every morning those same girls would go to the Yamuna to bathe. They would fashion an image of Katyayani from clay on the riverbed, decorate it with flowers, and worship it then and there.

The Vraja-vasis revered the demigods, so even married gopis like Radha came here, hut on the pretext of praying for their families’ prosperity. What they really wanted was Krsna’s company.

Rupa Gosvami relates an incident in which the intrigues of Lalita and Visakha resulted in Radha agreeing to a full-moon meeting with Krsna at Bhadrakali-tirtha, where They exchanged loving words.

But the divine couple’s conversation was interrupted by Mukhara’s arrival, obliging Krsna to hide in the hushes nearby until the old lady was satisfied that her granddaughter was indeed worshipping Kali.

When Mukhara left, Krsna continued to woo Radha, at last tasting victory in the form of a moonlight stroll along the Yamuna’s shore bathed in the nectarean light of the springtime moon.

Let us remember Prabodhananda Sarasvati’s words:

**“Please meditate on the splendidly decorated divine couple, who sit on a colourful cushion in a great jewel-vine pavilion under a kalpa-vrksa tree —**

**“By the very sweet and blissful Yamuna shore, and who with sweetness, youthfulness, handsomeness, charm, playfulness, and brilliant intelligence, enchant all Their Vrndavana gopi friends.”**

*As Kṛṣṇa sped towards Mathurā the gopīs said, “Handsome Kṛṣṇa, who once splashed waves of playful, crooked loving glances at Śrī Rādhā, now rides into the distance on a chariot.”*

## Chapter 20 - Ranavari to Surya-kunda

West of Gahvaravana and Nikunjavana rests one of New Vraja dhama's most scenic overlooks. Marked hy a red pavilion, it is a part of Vrndavana forest and it lies just outside of Vrndavana village.

This area slopes gently towards the valley through which the Yamuna flows. It is dominated by two peaceful, secluded gardens that are named after Krsna and Balarama: Syamabagh and Ramabagh.

The entire hill, which slopes to the northwest all the way down to lovely Tamalavana, is named Krsna-kunda-tila, due to the attractive lake called Krsna-kunda found in a garden there.

From here the pavilions marking Kaliya-ghata and Bhramara-ghata can be seen on one side of the golden Yamuna. Balarama’s abode, with forests like Bhadravana and Bilvavana, can be seen on the other.

I love to sit alone with my thoughts of Radha and Syama and chant japa inside Rama-kunda’s pavilion while overlooking the panoramic beauty of the dhama and surveying its pastime places one by one.

Sometimes I meditate on New Vraja-dhama ornamented by a stream of nectar called the Yamuna, by fragrant jewelled courtyards, hy blossoming desire trees, and by the distant echo of Syama’s flute.

I find that in our day-to-day lives, service to Radha and Syama often keeps us so engaged that we do not take as much notice as we could of the holy places between our homes, the temple, and where we serve.

Almost all of us daily pass by the triangular area of Krsna-kunda-tila, home to many pastime places, decorated with lovely gardens and forests, but how often do we stop to appreciate its varied opulences?

My first stop from Jnana-gudari was Ranavari. Once, at the appearance of spring, the gopas and gopis clashed in a fierce Holi battle (rana) there, in which Radha’s and Krsna’s parties were both victorious.

The gopis’ mothers and mothers-in-law were usually reluctant to let the girls move freely throughout Vrndavana forest, but when the Holi festival arrived each spring they would allow the girls to celebrate.

Playing Holi gave the cowherd girls and Krsna a wonderful excuse to meet at times and places normally not available to them. Naturally, they made the most of this golden opportunity.

The ingredients needed for Holi came from three sources: the parents of the young boys and girls, Vrnda-devi’s storehouses, and the desire trees of Vrndavana. These three suppliers kept them playing all day.

One day, Krsna and the gopas and Radha and the gopis left their homes to play in their own separate groups in separate places, adding colour not only to one another but also to the forests of Vraja.

The spring season painted Vraja with impressionistic dabs of bright, cheerful colours: wide swathes of golden mustard fields, green trees, and vines speckled with white, orange, blue, and lilac.

On this already colourful canvas, Krsna and His triends wildly splashed red, turquoise, and vermilion hues, sparing neither the plants nor the birds nor the animals that came within range.

The gopis were the first to arrive at Ranavari, intoxicated hy sprintime anthems and coloured with dye, their fine clothes covered in the same shades that stained their golden complexions.

Their arsenal of colours included containers of dyed liquid mixed with unguents that they would spray with golden syringes as well as red scented flower pollen wrapped in delicate lac membranes.

During one interlude the gopis stopped to collect flowers and decorate themselves with fresh flower ornaments and garlands. Meanwhile, Krsna and the cowherd boys arrived, feeling equally ecstatic.

The cowherd boys had also thrown colours on their way to Ranavari. By the time they arrived, each one looked like a rainbow on two legs. This motley crew was looking for a friendly challenge.

As soon as the boys realised that the gopis were playing Holi nearby, they huddled around Krsna to come up with a plan of attack. As this was going on, Madhu launched his own verbal assault on the girls.

Madhu challenged Lalita, “Who has given you permission to defoliate this forest just to beautify yourselves? My friend, the prince of Vraja, received no formal request from you girls to secure such a privilege.”

Knowing that to argue with Krsna’s brahmana friend was futile, and eager to precipitate a battle with Krsna, the gopis grabbed Madhu and covered him from head to toe with red and purple dye.

Moved by the spirit of Holi, the girls surrounded Madhumarigala and pelted him with flower-pollen bombs until his eyes and ears were so caked with coloured powder that he could neither see nor hear.

With great difficulty Madhu called out, “O Krsna! Save me! I have been captured! I am blinded by fragrant powder, and I cannot escape from these shameless girls. Please save me lest I die!”

Krsna took Madhu’s capture as a personal affront; He ordered His friends to charge. The boys ran towards the gopis, their arms loaded with Holi weapons, followed closely by several cartloads of arsenal.

The gopis faced off with the charging boys, syringes raised. As soon as the two armies were within firing range of each other, streams of coloured liquid and battle cries in the vasanta-raga filled the air.

The close combat between the gopas and gopis was fierce. They tossed and lobbed balls of powder at each other, but for all their childish bravado, the scents and liquids actually felt soft and soothing.

By this war of colours, Krsna had instigated an exchange of love with the gopis in which the flower bombs and liquid volleys were but a colourful means to show and receive mutual affection.

Coloured powder rose into the sky in clouds so thick that sunshine could not pass through. The clouds created a darkness so dense that the boys and girls could see neither one another nor themselves.

But, like true warriors, the gopas and gopis continued to fire their weapons, completely blinded by the cloud of colours, not knowing whether they were hitting their enemies or their own ranks.

All of the combatants were intoxicated by the bliss of the melee. The sounds of their ecstasy mixed with the sound of the drums and other musical instruments being played by accompanying musicians.

From somewhere in the middle of that chaos came Madhu’s call, “O Krsna! The flute! The flute!” And somewhere else in the billowing cloud, captured by the gopis and bound by garlands, Krsna took heart.

Breaking free from His bonds, Krsna pulled the flute from His belt and placed it to His lips. As soon as He blew into it, the cloud of powder settled upon the combatants and visibility returned.

But clearing the air also made Krsna an open target. One signal from Radha’s dyed eyebrows was enough to employ Her gopi snipers, who grabbed their syringes and attacked Krsna without a wasted moment.

“Surround Him! Surround Him! Capture Him! Get Him!” the girls called out, encircling Krsna and dousing Him from head to foot with streams of sandalwood-scented water in a variety of colours.

The gopas were caught off guard hy the gopis, but when they saw the plight of their dear friend, they grabbed their own syringes and surrounded the gopis for a fierce attack.

The gopis beat a hasty retreat to the other side of the meadow, where they consulted Sri Radha. Meanwhile, the boys crowded around Krsna to dry Him off and plan their revenge on the clever girls.

At that moment, Madhumarigala, still staggering because his eyes were filled with dust, let out a series of loud groans, signalling that he was too weak to continue without being fed.

Madhu’s complaints reminded the gopas that they too were hungry, and so a messenger was sent to the gopis calling for a temporary cease-fire while both sides rested in the shade and enjoyed a picnic.

Let us meditate upon the colour-strewn battlefield in Ranavari, where the gopas ate in the shade on one side, the gopis shared jokes on the other, and Radha and Krsna exchanged furtive glances.

**\* \* \* \***

There is a village next to Ranavari called Nari-samari, known in Krsna’s time as Syamari-Kinnari— Syamala-sakhi’s resience and the place where a deity of a Kinnari goddess is worshipped.

Syamala-sakhi is one of the principal gopis of Vraja and a leader of her own group. But, unlike Candravali, Syamala is a well-wisher of Sri Radha, whom she often visits in the early morning hours.

Syamala is a left-wing gopi, proud and hold like Radha. Sukadeva mentions her in the following words, “Another gopi placed \[Krsna’s\] arm, anointed with sandalwood paste, on her shoulder."

Syamala’s name means that her complexion is blackish like Krsna’s. She is also one of Krsna’s girlfriends, and so He is known as Syamala-kama-lalasah, “He who yearns for love-sports with Syamala.”

Syamala-sakhi pleases Krsna hy her personal qualities and bodily beauty, by the sweet rice she cooks for Him, and by her expert vina playing, which only Srimati Radharani can surpass.

Madhumarigala and Krsna were once walking along the Yamuna’s shore when Madhu exclaimed, “Listen! How wonderful! Friend, some girl is playing a vina in the middle of the Yamuna."

Krsna smiled and replied, “That is Syamala’s glorious vina. It is singing the beautiful spiritual invocation to a drama of transcendental amorous pastimes that will bring Me great happiness.”

Devoid of envy, Syamala serves Krsna according to her own loving mood, while unreservedly admiring Radha’s love for Him as greater than hers, and His love for Radha as greater than His love for her.

Syamala once praised Radha to the other gopis as atyantika-adhika, “one whose love for Krsna is superior to all others.” She said, “Bhadra may speak fickle, charming words, Pali may blossom with happiness —
