“Vimala may abandon all shyness, Syama may become proud, and even Candravali may strut about with her head raised high, but only so long as the mantra ‘Radha’ docs not enter their ears.”

In service to the divine couple and their pastimes, Syamala plays the role of a heroine who is often the object of Krsna’s youthful affections. Rupa Gosvami describes her in this way:

“Syamala now goes to Gokula. On her hips she wears a silk garment that is the best friend of Krsna’s lotus navel. On her chest is a large necklace fond of meeting the mark of Srivatsa on Krsna’s chest.

“On her cars are earrings that are the intimate associates of Krsna’s shark-shaped earrings, and on her cheeks are decorations painted by the hand of Lord Krsna, who carries the Sudarsana *cakra.*”

On one occasion Syamala was waiting for Krsna to arrive at her grove when a messenger appeared at midnight and informed her that Krsna was not coming after all because He had gone to meet another *gopi.*

Syamala was stunned by this news. Her complexion quickly faded and — like a demigod or demigoddess, who arc known to never blink — she stared off into the darkness with wide-open eyes.

Sonic days later Syamala was peacefully conversing with Krsna in a secluded garden when a parrot on the branch above them began to list Krsna’s escapades with other gopis, making her jealous.

Krsna cursed the bird under His breath as He grasped at straws to appease Syamala. He said, “This rotten parrot is simply repeating the words of some cruel-hearted rumour-monger fond of quarrels.

“My dear Syamala! Do not put your faith in the meaningless words of this bird. Please do not give yourself over to jealous anger, which distresses Me to no end; instead, please be merciful to Me.”

By contemplating Syamala-devi’s devotional attitude, we will better understand how to render service in the presence of superior Vaisnavas without being envious of them. That is pure devotion.

Near Syamari-Kinnari, Krsna once so angered Sri Radha that not even Her closest friends dared intercede on His behalf for fear of being exiled to a distant and remote corner of Vrndavana.

Krsna went through His repertoire of tricks, but everything He tried simply added fuel to the fire of Radha’s huff. As a last resort He asked Visakha to save Him from being swallowed by the python of grief.

Syamala’s home was nearby. Visakha advised Krsna to ask Syamala for some clothes and ornaments, disguise Himself as a gopi, and then approach Srimati Radharani to try to pacify Her.

Visakha then added, “But if this plan backfires, don’t dare mention my name.” Krsna reassured Visakha and, with the help of Syamala and some other girls, dressed up as a new gopi, Syama-sakhi.

His hands slightly trembling as He plucked the strings of a Vina, Krsna approached the grove where Radha sat cursing all things black and taking oath after oath to never, ever see Vraja’s prince again.

Krsna stopped at the entrance to the grove and in a beautiful, feminine voice softly sang a song about unrequited love. Seeing the effulgent songstress at Her doorway, Radha exclaimed:

“Who is this girl? Her bodily features and soothing voice are both so beautiful. She appears to be a Kinnari.” Taking heart, Krsna entered the grove, sat down before Radha, and continued playing the *vina.*

Krsna’s heartfelt yearning passed through His fingertips and drifted up from the strings of the Vina into Sri Radha’s ear. The sound touched Her heart; She was overwhelmed with affection for Krsna.

Neither Krsna nor the gopis could achieve directly what the vina succeeded in doing indirectly. Radha wanted Krsna. And although Her heart admitted as much, She was not yet ready to give in.

Srimati Radharani sat for some time with Her eyes closed, teardrops falling, one hy one, from Her cheeks. She solemnly took a strand of pearls from Her neck and offered it to Syama-sakhi as a gift.

As Sri Radha placed the necklace around Krsna’s neck, He reached up and grabbed Her by the wrist and, in a voice that sounded at first like a woman’s but soon became His own, feelingly said, “O Radha!

“If You want to reward Me, then don’t give Me a strand of pearls but relinquish to Me that necklace of anger so dear to You, the one You wear so close to Your heart. That is the only gift I desire.”

Radha was startled to hear Krsna’s voice, and His request, but She did not show Her surprise; instead, She slowly leaned back with the necklace still in Her hand. Krsna held on to Her wrist.

Krsna’s touch and the tears glistening in His eyes wiped away every trace of anger from Srimati Radharani’s mind. Having cast aside Her pretense, a gracious smile blossomed upon Her lips.

Just as storm clouds sometimes part suddenly to reveal blue skies and sunshine, Radha’s anger suddenly vanished, allowing the Kinnari Syama to sit beside Her, and the gopis to chant, “Radha! Syama!”

The influence of a Kinnari deity pervades Nari-samari, a deity who derives its power in no small measure from the powerful feminine potencies of Radha, Syamala, and Syama in His Kinnari disguise.

Because Lord Balarama once had a barn and cow-pen for His herd in this place, Nari-samari features another Sankarsana-kunda and a temple dedicated to the Lord in addition to the Kinnari deity of Devi.

Nor far from Nari-samari in New Vraja-dhama is Nagendra-bhoga, which represents a small village that Krsna personally told Uddhava to visit on his way from Mathura to Nandagrama.

Krsna Himself says that this village is unique because the houses and lanes are always decorated with coloured flags and the residents’ cows are special, having been personally created by Brahma.

These cows give such abundant milk that the ghee produced from it can feed the entire universe, and there are so many churns turning this milk into butter that the sound can be heard two yojanas away.

The effect of handling such a large quantity of milk and milk products is that the village of Nagendra-bhoga gives off such an alluringly sweet fragrance that it rivals the aroma of the milk ocean.

Intoxicated by the pleasing atmosphere of Nagendra-bhoga, Krsna once laid down here and happily drilled off into deep sleep as Srimati Radharani massaged I lis lotus feet.

Next is the village of Soyano, also called Sehona and Sihana. Seyam means “clever.” A temple here houses deities of the four Kumaras. Soyano and several other places are tied to the killing of Aghasura.

The residents of this village, who were first to learn that Krsna had killed Agha by expanding Himself within the snake, praised the Lord by saying, “Clever! Clever!” Hence their village is known as Sihana.

Etched in Krsna’s mind by the furrows left by Akrura’s chariot wheels is the stretch of road beyond Sihana. Perhaps due to the sorrow it caused Him and the Vraja-vasis, this stretch of road bears no name.

As Krsna and Balarama rode Akrura’s chariot along this road to Mathura, the girls of Vrndavana followed behind and tried to stop Them, sometimes by crying and sometimes by smiling.

The gopis could not rake their eyes off Krsna. They repeatedly shot arrows of sidelong glances, hoping to topple Krsna, and although they hit their target over and over again, He remained steadfast.

As the chariot sped ahead of the girls running behind, they said, “Handsome Krsna, who once splashed waves of playful, crooked, loving glances at Sri Radha, now rides into the distance on a chariot.”

In the years following that incident, whenever Sri Krsna remembered these final words of the gopis, perspiration soaked His body. Stunned, He would stand motionless like a painted picture.

**\* \* \* \***

The next place is Ajhai. After the bewilderment of Brahma, the cowherd boys gathered together here and said amongst themselves, “Today (aja), Krsna killed that evil snake, Aghasura.”

Actually, the cowherd boys were mistaken. Krsna had killed Agha exactly one year earlier. Later that same day, Brahma had kidnapped the boys and put them into a mystic slumber in a cave nearby.

The gopas were unaware that Krsna had assumed their identities for an entire year, after which He had shattered Brahma’s pride and then returned them to the very spot from which they had been stolen.

The only thing Krsna’s friends remembered was that He had killed Agha. And so after lunch they discussed the Lord’s victory at Ajhai, and then boasted of their friend’s prowess to the residents of Sihana.

After spreading the news of Krsna’s superhuman achievement, the boys loitered in two beautiful gardens on the hillside of Krsna-kunda-tila, eating fruits and nuts in the pleasing shade.

Even though Balarama was not present with the boys on that day, it being His birthday, those two gardens were in time named after both brothers. Thus they are now known as Syamabagh and Ramabagh.

From Ajhai I walked along the circular road to where it intersects Prabhupada Walk, the main road leading to the temple, and passed on my right New Vraja dhama's administrative offices.

In Vraja, the foot of Krsna-kunda-tila is marked by a forest of tamala trees close enough to both Varsana and Chatikara to have been a convenient place for Radha and Krsna to meet without being missed.

This place is also known as Maghera, or Magahera. The gopis who had followed Akrura’s chariot came this far before losing all hope of catching up with Krsna. It was here they all fainted.

Some Vraja-vasis did not return to their homes; instead, they took up residence here and waited for Krsna’s return, constantly looking for any sign of a dust cloud that might indicate an approaching chariot.

Maghera is the perfect place for us to feel the mood of separation from Krsna, along with the anticipation that, just as Krsna could have returned to Vraja at any moment, He might also appear to us.

These feelings of separation and the anticipation of reunion were voiced by a gopi in a letter to Krsna:

“The gentle breeze blowing from the place where You stay makes the hairs of Her body stand up.

“She satisfies Her tear-filled lotus eyes by showing them the beautiful picture of Your blackish transcendental form. She satisfies Her ears by sprinkling them with the nectar of Your endlessly sweet name.

“Her words filled with songs in the fifth raga, day and night this slender girl who was once Your maidservant stays by the window overlooking the path upon which You might return.”

Next to Maghera is Ral. Once called Raola, it bordered Nanda Maharaja’s kingdom when he ruled from Chatikara. It also bordered King Vrsabhanu’s kingdom, which he ruled from nearby Vasati.

The presiding Deity of Ral is Balarama, whose form was installed there millennia ago by King Vajranabha. That Deity is still worshipped in His original form on the bank of Balabhadra-kunda.

That area was a great favourite of Krsna and Balarama, who would regularly go there with Their friends to tend the calves and cows and pass time playing frivolous games and enjoying nature’s bounty.

In Vraja, on the road from Vrndavana town to Radha-kunda, one passes Balabhadra-kunda, just as one who comes to see Radha-Syama in New Vraja-dhama passes Ral and receives the blessings of Dauji.

In referring to Krsna’s brother, Srila Prabhupada would often quote the statement of sruti, nayam atma bala-hinena labhyo: “One cannot attain the goal of life without the mercy of Balarama.”

Whether the perfection we aspire for is to be found in Radha-kunda or Ramana-reti, our success can only be secured with the spiritual strength bestowed upon us by Balarama. As Srila Prabhupada writes:

“If one has no connection with Balarama, then even though one is a learned scholar or has taken birth in a respectable family, these assets will not help him conquer the enemies of Krsna consciousness.”

To thus propitiate the mercy of Lord Balarama we can offer prayers while passing Ral, prayers like,

*revati-ramana tvam vai baladevacyutagraja*  
*halayudha pralamba-ghna pahi mam purusottama*

**“O husband of Revati! O Lord Balarama! O elder brother of Lord Krsna! O Lord who holds a plough-weapon! O killer of Pralambasura! O Supreme Personality of Godhead, please protect me!”**

Further up the hill is Bhadayara-grama, the residence of the gopi Bhadra, a close friend of Candravali. True to her name, which means “auspicious,” Bhadra spreads auspiciousness wherever she goes.

Bhadra, unlike Syamala, is transcendentally antagonistic to Sri Radha. She is a key supporter of Candravali’s efforts to monopolise Krsna’s affections, although such efforts very rarely succeed.

Also unlike Syamala, Bhadra is mild by nature; indeed, of all the right-wing gopis — exalted for their submissiveness — Bhadra and Candravali are the most tolerant of Krsna’s misbehaviour.

For example, when Krsna once offended Bhadra in an assembly of gopis by making her the brunt of His clever jokes, she at first patiently tolerated His criticism and continued to be kind to Him.

Only when she noticed one of her less submissive friends respond by moving her eyebrows in anger did Bhadra show any displeasure herself, knitting her eyebrows and shooting angry glances at Krsna.

Though Bhadra’s temperament is mild, she can put that aside to do the needful for Krsna’s pleasure. As some gopis once observed, she can be as soft as a rose or as hard as a thunderbolt.

For example, Bhadra is so faint-hearted that she is afraid to enter a shady grove in daylight, but in order to find Krsna, she once searched a forest of tamala trees on a moonless night, alone and unafraid.

Because of her great love for Him, Krsna often gives Bhadra ornaments like His famed gunja necklace. He is also pleased when His devotees call Him by names connected to her, such as:

*Bhadra-hrdaya-nidraluh* (“He who naps in Bhadra’s heart”) or *Abhisarollasad-bhadra kinkini-ninadonmukhah* (“He who waited at the meeting place, eagerly listening for Bhadra’s jingling anklets”).

In speaking of Bhadra, Krsna says, “Bhadra and her vina are very beautiful. Both speak sweetly, both are as graceful as newly flowering vines, and both enchant Me, robbing Me of My peacefulness.”

When Bhadra heard that Krsna had extinguished a forest fire by the Yamuna to save the Vraja-vasis, she placed Him in her heart to extinguish the fire of amorous passion burning there.

But Bhadra misjudged the effect of keeping Krsna in her heart, for His presence there merely doubled the flames of her desire; as a result, she appeared pale, as if burned to ashes.

After Krsna had left Vrndavana for Mathura, Bhadra continuously lamented His absence, as did the other gopis. Indeed, her expressions of grief still echo throughout the woods of Bhadayara to this day.

That echo exclaims, “O bird of my life-breath! Even though in your presence your beloved Krsna hastily climbed aboard the chariot, still you refuse to abandon this slain body that has become your nest!”

**\* \* \* \***

Once, Krsna came to nearby Konai, or Konhi, expecting to meet Radha. But when He arrived, She to worry!” He thought. “My beloved will soon arrive with Her friends.”

Krsna sat upon the roots of a banyan tree, like a king upon his throne, and gazed out over Vrndavana, regularly staring down the road, hoping to see the golden aura that would herald Sri Radha’s arrival.

The chatter of parrots combined with a cooling breeze to create an agreeable atmosphere in which Krsna’s mind could easily drift to pleasant thoughts of encounters He had had with Srimati Radharani.

He recalled how He had once played an enchanting tune on His flute, and all the gopis, except Radha, had come running to meet Him in Vrndavana forest. He was perplexed that His favourite had not come.

Without Sri Radha, no pastime is complete; therefore, in a conciliatory tone, Krsna asked the gopis to return home, assuring them that He would reciprocate their affections another day.

Krsna thought, “Where was Radha? Did She not hear the flute?” Quite the opposite! Sri Radha, entranced by the sound of the flute, had collapsed, unconscious, into the arms of Her attendants.

In spite of fanning Her body, massaging Her limbs, and applying damp cloths to Her forehead, neither Radha’s friends nor Her maidservants could revive Her. Panicked, they called for Paurnamasi.

By the time the aged ascetic arrived, Radha had been unconscious for three hours. Paurnamasi assessed the situation and instructed the gopis to take Radha to her cottage, where they laid Her on a cot.

Paurnamasi called Madhumarigala and told him to find Krsna and bring Him to her cottage at once, an errand that Madhu completed with more speed than expected from such a portly brahmana.

When Krsna arrived, Paurnamasi offered Him a seat and directed His attention to Sri Radha: “What’s done is done! Your flute has brought Radha to this state. But now You must remedy the situation.”

Embarrassed in front of Paurnamasi, Krsna timidly asked, “What should I do?” The brahmani replied, “I have no means to revive Her, but You do. Your touch is sufficient to bring Her back.”

Despite repeated requests and exhortations, Krsna remained silent and motionless and hung His head. Finally Paurnamasi asked, “O life of Vraja! Why are You silent? Why do You ignore my request?”

Krsna replied, “You are asking Me to do something irreligious.” Lifting His head and looking at Paurnamasi, He said, “Please give Me some way to revive Her without touching Her.”

Vrnda-devi, impatient with Krsna’s reluctance, blurted out, “Why are You so unfeeling? Just place Your hand on Her heart.” Shaking His head, Krsna said, “That would not be right.”

Unwilling to give up, Vrnda countered, “Then place Your feet upon Her heart.” Hearing this Krsna again fell silent, unsure of what to do in the presence of others, yet painfully aware of His beloved’s plight.

Seeing Krsna’s intractability, Vrnda fell at His feet and cried out, “Do something!” She then grabbed Krsna’s feet and touched His upper sole to Radha’s chest, causing Krsna to tremble violently.

At Krsna’s touch, Radha at once opened Her eyes and filled them with the vision of Her lover’s form. In turn, Krsna eagerly watched the blossoming of the golden lotus of His beloved’s face.

Catching a glimpse of Vrnda, Krsna again became conscious of watching eyes. Covering His head with His shawl, He offered His respects to Paurnamasi and left her cottage, followed by Madhu.

Still in shock from having seen and touched Sri Radha, Krsna took Madhu’s help to make it hack to His friends and cows, who were eagerly awaiting His return and ready to head home for supper.

Meanwhile, in Paurnamasi’s cottage, Radha slowly sat up, silently sobbing and rubbing Her eyes repeatedly. Did She dream that Krsna had been there, or was it real? And if it was real, where had He gone?

The gopis consoled Her and explained everything. Radha then offered Her respects to Paurnamasi. After some time, finally able to walk, She returned home along with Vrnda and Her friends.

Back at Konai, Krsna’s heart swelled with emotion as He recalled this pastime. He looked up at the branches of the trees and realised that the parrots had gone silent, as if listening to His thoughts.

Just then a brightly dressed gopi came into view. Having left the main road and come to where Krsna sat, she bowed to Him with respect and presented a garland that showed the signs of Radha’s expertise.

Krsna graciously accepted the garland and placed it on His neck, but He wanted Radharani, not flower garlands, and so He impatiently asked the girl, *kyo n’ai*: “Why has She not come?”

Since that incident, this place has been called Konai. Vaisnavas come here to chant nama-sankirtana in the shade of the trees, hoping to hear the answer given by that gopi to Hari.

In Bhauma Vrndavana there is a temple of Syamasundara and Gopala near a lake called Konai-kunda. The lapping waves of that lake also seem to make a sound like *kyo n’ai, kyo n’ai*.

Passing through Konaivana, I left the main road and walked to the peak of Krsna-kunda-tila, where I came to Surya-kunda, the famous meeting place of the divine couple over which the sun god presides.

Radha had continued Her childhood worship of the sun god after Her marriage, backed by Her mother-in-law Jatila’s conviction that Radha’s devotion to the sun was the cause of her family’s prosperity.

Sunday was the main day for worship at Surya-kunda, but Radha often goes on weekdays, reaching Surya-kunda at about three in the afternoon, after spending most of Her day at Radha-kunda.

Generally Jatila arrives in time for the worship, but due to Yogamaya’s influence she never seriously questions how her daughter-in-law has passed the five hours since leaving home.

Afterwards Srimati Radharani walks home with Jatila and suffers in separation from Krsna until She is again able to meet Him at night, once everyone else in Yavata has gone to sleep.

Surya-kunda is a beautiful lake in a secluded part of Vrndavana forest. A Deity of Lord Narayana, the sun and moon in His hands, appeared there to serve and witness the divine couple’s pastimes.

The temple of that effulgent one is on the northern side of the lake in a luxuriantly flowering grove where Radha and Krsna worship, making offerings with various obscure mantras.

If Krsna is not present, Srimati Radharani prays with joined palms to the sun god, Lord Surya-narayana: “O My Lord! May I, unhindered, by Your grace, attain Govinda’s lotus feet today!”

Surrounding Surya-kunda is a flower garden called Madana-rana-vatika (“the garden of Cupid’s battle”), which is extremely dear to Sri Radha. Krsna comes here to make garlands for Her.

And on the southern side of Surya-kunda is the bhajana-kutira of Jagannatha Dasa Babaji, where for years he did nama-sankirtana and worshipped Deities of Gaura-Nitai and Sadbhuja Gauranga.

It is said that Babaji Maharaja would not sleep at night; rather, he would stay awake constantly chanting the maha-mantra. In the morning he would bathe in the lake.

His servant, Bihari, would prepare an offering of flat rice and curd for the Deities. Jagannatha Dasa Babaji would then eat the Lord’s remnants, although he would often fast for three days at a time.

When Thakura Bhaktivinoda was in Radha-kunda or elsewhere in Vrndavana, he would visit Babaji Maharaja at Surya-kunda because the Thakura accepted Jagannatha Dasa Babaji as his siksa-guru.

Babaji Maharaja was reputed to be a perfected soul. He would receive aspirants who came to him for training, which he would begin by having them work in the flower and vegetable gardens by his cottage.

During one of Bhaktivinoda Thakura’s visits to Surya-kunda, some devotees complained that instead of training them in meditation Babaji Maharaja was having them do his gardening.

The Thakura explained that Babaji Maharaja was right. Until devotees are purified at heart by practical devotional service, their attempts at meditation are likely to result in daydreaming and sleep.

Another exalted person who would spend time with both Babaji Maharaja and Bhaktivinoda Thakura was Madhusudana Dasa Babaji, an acclaimed transcendentalist beyond the touch of matter.

**\* \* \* \***

Madhusudana Dasa Babaji was born in an aristocratic brahmana family, but when his parents began to make arrangements for his marriage, he disappeared to live in Vrndavana as an ascetic.

One day as Madhusudana sat on the bank of the Yamuna wondering when Krsna would send him a spiritual master, a saintly Vaisnava appeared and offered diksa to the young Babaji.

After Madhusudana had bathed in the river, the saint initiated him with a ten-syllable mantra but disappeared without giving his new student any instructions as to the kind of service he should practise.

Although Madhusudana consulted local saints on meditation techniques, because of the strict Babaji orthodoxy, his ignorance of the intricacies of his line made him an outcaste in the eyes of many.

Various stories describe how Sri Radha interceded on Madhusudana Dasa Babaji’s behalf. Whatever the truth of the matter may be, his singular spiritual credentials have been certified by our own acaryas.

Local tradition has it that Radha directed Madhusudana to excavate a large rock from Surya-kunda, upon which She had once placed Her crown, jewellery, and veil, leaving their imprints in the stone.

Babaji Maharaja found the stone and installed it in the wall of his bhajana-kutira, where it remains to this day. Chanting there helps us to remember and hence to have the company of these great souls.

It is sometimes misunderstood that Madhusudana Dasa Babaji initiated Jagannatha Dasa Babaji, but the fact of the matter is that Jagadananda Gosvami of Vrndavana initiated Jagannatha Dasa.

The unconventional nature of Madhusudana Dasa’s own initiation and the commonly accepted tact that he never initiated anyone both indicate that the two Babajis’ relationship was not based on diksa.

It is interesting to note that Madhusudana Dasa Babaji is commonly accepted in the Babaji community as a perfected soul, even though he did not receive the siddha-pranali required by babaji orthodoxy.

And yet the same babaji community denigrates the followers of Bhaktisiddhanta Sarasvati, who follow the same path of perfection taken by Babaji Maharaja — to depend on Krsna and His names.

If the Babajis are serious about following Lord Caitanya’s orders to attain spontaneous devotion as well as preach worldwide, they must adopt a spiritual practice relevant to all devotees.

That spiritual practice is aspiring to chant the maha-mantra without offence (harer nama eva kevalam) and having faith in both Krsna and the holy name to awaken mature vraja-bhakti from within.

Local Vraja-vāsīs mark the disappearance of Madhusūdana Dāsa Bābājī with a ten-day festival starting on the eighth day of the waning moon in the month of Agrahāyaṇa.

The festival concludes with cauṣaṭṭi-mahānta-bhoga, an offering of food to the sixty-four main associates of Lord Caitanya, with their names written next to each plate.

From Sūrya-kuṇḍa I continued my parikrama to Śālmala, a village Kṛṣṇa fondly described to Uddhava as the residence of the ābhīra class of Vraja-vāsīs, who once kept innumerable buffaloes.

The children of this village were all enamoured with Kṛṣṇa’s heroic deeds. Anywhere one would go the children could be seen acting out Kṛṣṇa’s killing demons, tending cows, or lifting Girirāja.

Rūpa Gosvāmī writes that the ābhīras are lower by caste than the vaiśyas because they are usually of mixed parentage — born to vaiśya fathers and śūdra mothers. They go by names such as Ghoṣa.

The pure-caste vaiśyas earn their livelihood by protecting cows, the ābhīras by caring for buffalo, and the gurjaras — a third, lower class, known for their girth  — look after goats and other animals.

These three types of Vraja-vāsīs — the vaiśyas, ābhīras, and gurjaras, who generally live on the outskirts of Vṛndāvana — protect the domestic animals that live in the dhāma. A fourth class, the learned brāhmaṇas, act as advisors, perform sacrifices, worship deities, and conduct religious ceremonies sponsored by the pious citizens of Vṛndāvana-dhāma.

The fifth and final social category of Kṛṣṇa’s eternal associates in Vṛndāvana are the bahiṣṭhas, who earn their livelihood by practising crafts, like sewing clothes or making ornaments.

The descendants of the Yadu dynasty hold occupations in these five categories. In terms of their relationship to Kṛṣṇa, however, they are often described in eight groups, beginning with respected superiors.

Kṛṣṇa is the emblem of religious principles, or dharma. He never fails to show due respect to these superiors — Nanda Mahārāja’s relatives, brothers, friends, and servants, as well as the elderly *gopīs.*

The seven remaining groups are Kṛṣṇa’s contemporaries: His brothers and sisters, His gopī messengers, servants, craftsmen, maidservants, cowherd friends, and dear gopī friends, like Rādhā.

Near Śālmala is Sāhāra, where Kṛṣṇa stopped on His way to Mathurā, putting Akrūra into anxiety by getting off the chariot and eating butter before proceeding on His way. To Uddhava, the Lord said —

Dūrād eṣa praṇayati purā labdha-sāhāra-nāmā premānandaṁ tava nayanayor aupanando nivāsaḥ / jaṅghālena kṣiti-pati-purīṁ syandanenānuvindan yatrāhāraṁ priyam akaravaṁ hāri haiyaṅgavīnam:

“Even from afar, Upananda’s home, called Sāhāra, will bring bliss and love to your eyes. When by chariot I swiftly travelled to the king of all earthly cities, I ate some delicious butter in that village.”

Kṛṣṇa was accustomed to visiting Sāhāra, the residence of Upananda and his family, whose love for Kṛṣṇa illuminated the whole area and obliged the Lord to stop and accept their offerings.

But Akrūra was afraid to stop the chariot. If the gopīs were to catch up with them, he thought, their departure from Sāhāra would again invoke the Vraja vāsīs’ feelings of ill will and violence towards him.

In addition to informal family visits to Sāhāra, Nanda Mahārāja would bring his entire clan here for lunch every year on Bhrātṛ-dvitīya, which takes place the day after Govardhana-pūjā.

Upananda has a pinkish complexion and a long beard. He dresses in green clothes and resides here with his family, including his wife Tungi-devi, whose complexion and clothes are both dark in colour.

Other prominent citizens of Sahara are Upananda’s sons Kandava and Dandava. These two brothers are friends of Subala and Subhadra, whose wife, Kundalata, everyone knows to be Radha’s close friend.

The palace in Sahara is often full of cowherd men and villagers, as well as messengers from Nandagrama carrying letters between the king of Gokula and his elder brother and counselor Upananda.

Near the palace is Subhadra’s house, behind which is a beautiful garden full of blossoming trees and flower beds that attract all varieties of birds and play host to many beehives.

This is Kundalata’s garden. Here, in a grove hidden from public view, Radha and Krsna would share intimate moments after the Bhratrdvitiya feast while everyone else was resting, unawares.

On one occasion, as Kundalata and her friend Sumukhi were strolling through this garden, Kundalata asked, “Where are Radha and Krsna now? What are They doing? Do you know?”

Hearing these wonderful leading questions from Kundalata, Sumukhi’s hair stood on end. She offered Kundalata an ornate seat and narrated Radha’s and Krsna’s toll pastime at Govardhana Hill.

Just like Sanjaya had revealed the events at Kuruksetra as they were unfolding, Sumukhi narrated the lovers’ quarrel between the gopis and Krsna just as He was levying taxes against them at Dana-ghati.

Raghunatha Dasa Gosvami discloses this wonderful dialogue in his Dana-keli-cintamani, which concludes with Kundalata and Sumukhi rushing out of the garden to meet Radha and Krsna.

**\* \* \* \***

Further on from Kundalata’s garden is Sankhi. Bhakti-ratnakara tells us that after killing Sankhacuda here, Krsna gave Balarama the spoils from that scuffle, the Syamantaka jewel, at nearby Rama-kunda.

It is worth noting that Garga Muni gives us a version of Krsna’s slaying Sankhacuda that differs enough from Sukadeva Gosvami’s version to suggest that the same pastime happened in a previous age.

To hear Garga Muni’s description of Sankhacuda’s death is instructive, for it shows how the same earthly pastimes of the Lord can vary significantly from one day of Brahma to the next.

The story of Sankhacuda, a Yaksa attendant of Kuvera, begins with his proceeding to Mathura, puffed up with pride over his unmatched skill at wielding a club and eager to challenge King Kamsa to a duel.

Kamsa happily obliged him, initiating a great battle in which neither fighter could gain victory over his opponent despite the best efforts of the two demons, who had both slain many a great hero in times past.

In the midst of this terrible altercation, Garga Muni appeared and informed the demons that neither would be able to slay the other, for their deaths were to be at the hands of Krsna, and no one else.

Hearing this, Kamsa and Sankhacuda decided to join forces. To cement their friendship, they set off along the royal road with a grand retinue that very night, planning to celebrate at Sarikhacuda’s home.

Meanwhile, Krsna and the gopis had arrived at Lohajanghavana, a beautiful place opulent with flowering madhavi vines whose blossoms Krsna used to decorate the cowherd girls’ hair.

With Radharani hy His side Krsna then walked along the Yamuna’s shore, which was especially pleasant due to the sounds of humming bees and the caress of soft, fragrant spring breezes.

Krsna and His entourage arrived at a forest of tamala and pilu trees. Inspired hy the romantic atmosphere and affectionate gopis, He sang st) beautifully that the trees cried blissful saptears.

Just as Indra dances with Apsaras in heaven, Krsna, with Radha hy His side, inspired the other gopis to dance around Them in concentric circles, each circle moving in the opposite direction of the next.

Just at that moment the two demons happened to pass by with their soldiers, and the sounds of tinkling ankle bells, melodious music, sweet singing, and clapping hands captured their evil minds.

Sankhacuda left his wicked companions on the road and followed the festive sounds until he arrived at the riverbank where Radha, Krsna, and the gopis were absorbed in the excitement of the rasa dance.

For a moment Sankhacuda admired the Cupid-like beauty of the youth who stood on the ball of His left foot, playing the flute and moving His eyebrows in accompaniment to His own music.

Thinking Krsna to be a delicate boy who would be powerless to stop him, Sankhacuda decided to kidnap one of the gopis. He boldly entered the dancing arena like a lion amongst a bevy of deer.

Sankhacuda was ten palm trees tall, blackish in complexion, with a face like a tiger’s and a long rolling tongue. As soon as the gopis saw him they scattered in all directions, screaming Krsna’s name in fear.

One gopi named Satacandranana, who was one of Krsna’s gatekeepers in Goloka, challenged Sankhacuda. He laughed with amusement, scooped her up like a doll, and headed hack to where Kamsa waited.

Terrified, Satacandranana shrieked, “O Lord! You saved us from demons before, please save me now.” When Sankhacuda heard this he thought, “Perhaps this is the Visnu of whom Garga Muni spoke.”

Sankhacuda now feared that the handsome youth was actually death personified, so he began to run away. Seeing Krsna angrily pursuing him with a sala tree in His hand, the demon dropped his victim.

But Krsna was relentless. He continued to close in on Sankhacuda like unavoidable death. Now fearing for his life, the Yaksa tried to evade the Lord, but Krsna followed close behind him, tree in hand.

Finally the two arrived at the Himalayas. His escape foiled hy an impassable wall of soaring mountains, Sankhacuda also uprooted a sala tree and stood his ground, determined to fight until the end.

Unintimidated yet determined to kill His foe, Sri Krsna nonchalantly threw the sala tree with His mighty arm, knocking Sankhacuda over as easily as a fierce wind uproots a tree in a storm.

Badly hurt, the demon still managed to get to his feet. With a roar he attacked Krsna, looking just like a moth swooping into blazing fire. Krsna countered the attack, amused by Sankhacuda’s foolishness.

The combatants fought for some time, shaking the earth with each step and rending the heavens with their cries, until Krsna remembered that He preferred rasa dancing to fighting.

Krsna grabbed hold of Sankhacuda like an elephant plucks a lotus from a pond and then punched the demon’s head so hard it was separated from his trunk and rolled down the hill like a large stone.

Eager to take the Syamantaka jewel back as a prize, Krsna slid down the hill, removed the gem from the demon’s crown, tied it in the edge of His upper cloth, and departed for Vrndavana.

The effulgent soul of Sankhacuda hovered around his body for some moments, lighting up the night; then, as Krsna was departing, it swiftly entered into the body of Krsna’s friend Sridama in Vraja.

Garga Rsi says that upon returning to Vrndavana, Krsna gave the Syamantaka jewel to Satacandranana as a reward and then happily resumed His dancing pastimes, undisturbed.

The versions of this pastime found in Sri Garga-samhita and Srimad-Bhagavatam differ significantly. Allow me to briefly summarise the differences between the two scriptural narrations.

In Garga-samhita Krsna is alone with the gopis in Lohavana, Sankhacuda steals only Satacandranana, Krsna kills the demon in the Himalayas, and gives the Syamantaka jewel to Satacandranana.

The version of Srimad-Bhagavatam is much more complex. Krsna and Balarama are together with Their own gopis, in Syamavana at Govardhana, absorbed in the festival of Holi.

Sankhacuda first rounds up all the gopis, senses danger, then abandons all the gopis except Radha, until Krsna catches up to him and kills him either at Lagmohana-kunda or Sankhi.

After taking the Syamantaka gem from Sankhacuda, Krsna gives it to Balarama, Balarama gives it to Madhu, and Madhu to Radha. There is no mention of Sankhacuda’s soul entering into Sridama.

By recalling cither of these narrations while visiting Sankhi, we are purified and taste spiritual happiness. Our spiritual intelligence is also strengthened and fixed in the conclusions of pure devotion.

Using one’s intelligence to analyse the scriptures is a part of healthy Krsna consciousness. As Kaviraja Gosvami writes,

*siddhanta baliya citte na kara alasa*  
*iha ha-ite krsne lage sudrdha manasa*

**“A sincere student should not neglect the discussion of such conclusions, considering them controversial, for such discussions strengthen the mind. Thus one’s mind becomes attached to Krsna.’’**

**\* \* \* \***

The last pastime place on our circuit around Krsna-kunda-tila is Alabai, previously known as Arabadi, which sits within Holivana, another place where Radha and Krsna play Holi.

I felt reluctant to leave Krsna-kunda-tila. Its scenic beauty, pastime places, and above all its sweet atmosphere naturally attract the heart in much the same way a magnet attracts iron fillings.

This special sweet atmosphere, found only in Vrndavana, is a display of Krsna’s pleasure potency. It is most relishable when hearing about the loving exchanges between the Lord and His devotees.

A special feature of Krsna’s pastimes is that His devotees’ pleasure increases His own bliss. In fact, He gets more pleasure from seeing His devotees enjoy the hladini — sakti than in enjoying it Himself.

Jiva Gosvami gives the example of a flute player, who blows air through his lips to make an ordinary whistling sound. But by blowing the same air through a flute, he makes an indescribably sweet sound.

Just as the flute player relishes the sound of the flute a thousand times more than the sound of his breath, Krsna relishes seeing His devotees taste the hladini — sakti a thousand times more than tasting it Himself.

When we visit pastime places or when we hear Krsna's pastimes, this sweetness touches and transforms our hearts, especially when we are in the company of those in whom the pleasure potency has manifest.

Amongst the many pastimes in which Krsna intensely enjoys the love of His devotees is the springtime festival of HOLI. Places like Alabai, Ranavari, and Holivana provide impetus to remember these occasions.

We can recall the following names of the divine couple connected to Holi. For Radha these names are,

*gandharvika svagandharva-vismapita-balacyuta*  
*sankhacudari-dayita gopi-cudagra-malika*

**“She who is expert at singing, dancing, and playing instruments, who surprised Krsna and Balarama at the HOLI festival, who is the beloved of Sankhacuda’s enemy, the crowning garland of all the** ***gopis.”***

And some names of Sri Krsna related to Holi are yah purvam nirmame hori-parvam yaksasya dhurvanam: “He who celebrated the Holi festival, and He who killed the Yaksa Sankhacuda.”

Holi is such a joyful event, so attractive to the heart, that its allure does more to cheer a pouting Radharani than the persuasive arguments of Her gopi friends, or even those of Sri Krsna Himself.

For example, the daughters of the nine gopas known as the magnanimous Upanandas once saw that Krsna was preparing to leave His home to play HOLI, and they wanted to join in.

Most of these girls had been goddesses in their previous lives. They performed austerities and pious activities to become gopis in Vraja — more specifically, family members of Krsna and friends of Sri Radha.

Being partial to Varsana’s princess, they hurried to Her house hoping to take Her with them, but when they arrived they saw that Radha was in a rather foul mood, full of jealous anger towards Krsna.

Without delving into the details of Her anger, the gopis offered Radha a positive alternative, “O moon-faced, honey-sweet Radha. Listen! The ornament of Vraja is leaving to celebrate Holi.

“The distress You feel now will become but a grain of sand in the vast desert of misery we will all be forced to aimlessly wander if we miss this chance to paint Vraja and its prince with the colours of love.”

One gopi said, “Devi! You should have seen Krsna’s eyes rolling under the intoxicating influence of youth, His black locks resting on His cheeks, and His yellow shawl draped over His shoulders.”

Another gopi excitedly added, “As He walked, the anklets on His feet and the bangles on His wrists tinkled like cooing doves, His earrings illuminated His cheeks, and His crown glistened like the rising sun.”

A third gopi whispered, “While walking along the road — body smeared with kunkuma, syringe in hand, friends surrounding Him — handsome Krsna repeatedly looked about to see if You were coming.”

Pulling on Her hands and pushing Her shoulders, the girls cheered Radha on, “Give up Your anger! Celebrate Holi. Look! Your maids are already bringing water mixed with dye, paste, and flower pollen.”

Radha’s reticence gradually melted with the girls’ prodding. “Stand up and come with us. If You don’t, You’ll have to wait another year. But with us, Krsna’s dyes will brighten the darkness of Your foul mood.”

Bitten by the contagious enthusiasm Holi had roused in Her friends, Radha set aside Her anger and leapt to Her feet. She quickly dressed for the occasion and went with Her friends to celebrate spring.

They wore colourful dresses and flashing ornaments, and carried jewelled syringes. Their servants followed them with pots of water mixed with aguru, musk, turmeric, kunkuma, and the essence of sandalwood.

As they walked through the village streets the girls managed to restrain themselves, but once on the forest path, their hands already red with dye, they gossiped, laughed, and sang songs about Holi.

The gopis' mirth electrified everyone and everything, causing the creepers, trees, birds, and forest animals to dance and sing along with them, turning all of Vraja into a roving festival.

What kind of songs did joyful Radha and Her friends sing.?Govinda Dasa Thakura composed the following song in vasanta-raga, glorifying the Holi festival and ushering in the passions of spring.

*Rtu-pati biharati nagara-syama radha rangini sangini bama*

**“With beautiful, playful Radha on His left, the dark hero Krsna enjoys pastimes in the springtime, the king of seasons.”**

*Cuya candana parimala kunkuma phagu-range saba anga bhari*  
*madana-mohana heri matala manasija yubati-yuta sataga-ota hori*

**“Their limbs are covered with sandal-water, powder, and kunkuma.**  
**“By gazing at Krsna, who is more enchanting than a thousand Kamadevas, hundreds of young gopis are overcome with amorous desire, inspiring them to sing graceful songs in the Holi festival.”**

*Kehu dhara ambara kehu hard hara kehu tanu pasiya rahalahi bhori*  
*kehu le-i mudari kehu le-i murali durahim dure kehu ga-ota hori*

**“\[For a change of dress after playing\] some gopis bring fresh clothes —**

**“Others bring flower necklaces and yet still others bring rings. \[For musical accompaniment\] some gopis bring flutes, and whether from near or afar, they sing songs of Holi while brimming over with bliss.”**

*Dampha rababa upanga pakhoyaja karatala tala su-meli kari*  
*gobinda- dasa pahum nata-bara sekhara nacata ga-ota tala dhari*

**“Gathering together, the gopis play damphas, rababas, pakhoyajas, and karatalas."**

Concluding his song of joy, Govinda Dasa writes,

**“As the unlimitedly talented gopis play graceful rhythms, Govinda Dasa’s master, the crown jewel of all dancers, dances even as girls spray Him with dye.”**

Unable to control their love, the gopis threw fistfuls of red powder on each other, which then rose up into the sky like a cloud, blanketing the earth and the trees in crimson and hovering on the horizon.

In time the cloud became so thick that only a flurry of reddish fists could be seen throwing fragrant red power in all directions, and only the sounds of singing and music could be heard amidst joyful shouts.

In this way the billowing cloud of red powder slowly moved through the forest until it reached Krsna and His friends, who stared at the spectacle, incredulous, until suddenly, without warning —

The cloud of colour attacked Krsna like a snake lunges at its prey. Before He could run away or even call His friends for help, the gopis surrounded Him, tightly restraining Him with their hands.

Like a monsoon cloud at sunset surrounded by lightning bolts, Krsna was encompassed by gopis who relentlessly smeared His face and limbs with red powder and drenched His body in coloured liquid.

Krsna called out to His friends — as they did to Him — hut the cloud of powder was so dense and the gopis so vigilant in guarding Him that He could not escape, nor could the boys rescue Him.

With no other resort than His mystic power, Krsna expanded Himself into as many forms as there were gopis and attacked each girl with halls of powder and separate syringes full of liquid.

To make the most of the special music and songs of the season, Krsna pulled Radha to the centre of the melee and, covered head to toe in brightly coloured powder, celebrated spring hy dancing with Her.

The cowherd boys were bewildered without their leader, their ranks in disarray. Seeing this, the gopis pelted them with powder and liquid, completely embarrassing the boys and putting them to rout.

But the Holi festival is, after all, a display of love, and so love itself directs it. Still, the nature of love is to always seek more of itself. We can thus safely assume that the battle of colours raged on for a time.

Exactly how long did it last? It is difficult to say. Time has no influence on Krsna’s pastimes. And even if it did, it would not be able to penetrate the billowing clouds of red powder.

But like all good things, the Holi festival came to an end after all. The gopas and gopis headed to different parts of the Yamuna to bathe, dyeing her swirling waters with the assorted colours from their bodies.

After changing into fresh garments, fully anointed with the sweetness of loving exchanges, Radha and Her friends then returned to their homes, Krsna and His friends to theirs.

As they walked along the road the boys and girls sang songs, like Govinda Dasa’s Holi anthem. But when they reached the outskirts of their villages they stopped out of fear their elders would disapprove.

*Khelata phagu brndabana-canda rtu-pati manamatha manor atha chanda*

**“He who is like a moon shining in Vraja’s forest and who is filled with Cupid’s desires, enjoys pastimes of throwing red powder.”**

*Sundari gana kara-mandali-majha rangini prema-tarangini saja*

**“At the centre of a circle of beautiful gopis flows a blissful, reddish, wave-filled river of spiritual love.”**

*Agu phagu de-i nagari nayane abasare nagara cumbaye bayane*

**“She who is the great heroine of amorous pastimes showers red powder on Him, while He who is the king of amorous pastimes kisses Her face.”**

*Cakita candra-mukhi sahacari gahane dha-i dha-ola gindharika eka sane*

**“Her moon-faced gopi friends are filled with wonder. Gathering together they come to Her rescue and attack Krsna.”**

*Tarala nayam turite eka yd-i kare sane kadi niurali le-i dha-i*

**“Trembling from the excitement induced hy Cupid, the fearless gopis run to Krsna, snatch His flute, and flee into the cloud of powder.”**

*Ghana karatali bhali bhali bola ho ho hori tumula utarola*

**“The din of the karatalas’ bhali! bhali! is overwhelming. Mixed with that sound are the gopis’ cries of ‘Ho! Ho! Holi! Ho! Ho! Holi!”’**

*Aruna taruna tarn arunahi dharani sthala jala-cara bhela sabe eka-barani*

**“In the land, in the air, or in the water; whether moving, unmoving, or otherwise, everything is one colour — red."**

*Concluding his song, the author writes, arunahi nire aruna arabinda aruna hrdaye bhela dasa gobinda*

**“The water is red, the lotuses are red, and even Govinda Dasa’s heart is covered now with red.”**

Dear devotees! Let us go to Krsna-kunda-tila, sit in Holivana, and chant Krsna’s holy names with a pure heart. Perhaps our hearts will also turn red with the powder of Radha’s and Krsna’s Holi festival.

*Let us bow to the Vṛndāvana forest of New Vraja-dhāma, where Rādhā-Śyāma reside on a golden throne, where devotees serve the samādhis of great souls, and where the mahā mantra always resounds.*

## Chapter 21 - Jhulanavana to Gopakrta

Bhauma Vrndavana, said to encompass eighty-four krosas, comprises twelve principal forests, of which Vrndavana, spread over sixteen krosas, is special. It thus lends its name to all of Vraja.

According to scripture, Vrndavana forest, which extends from the present town of Vrndavana as tar as Nandagrama, inconceivably contains all the Vaikuntha planets, indeed all created worlds.

The town of Pancakrosi Vrndavana, measuring live krosas, is nestled within the easternmost bend of the Yamuna. It is the place in Vraja most frequented by our acaryas, including Lord Caitanya.

Srila Prabhupada lived in Pancakrosi Vrndavana for nine years, before departing for America in 1965. It was also there he established the Krsna-Balarama temple and ultimately entered samadhi.

Our village in New Vraja-dhama is a replica of Pancakrosi Vrndavana, but because it is significantly smaller, we call it Vrndavana village. It is home to Radha-Syama and Their servants. Sometimes I daydream of standing behind the temple with Srila Prabhupada and showing him Vrndavana village, the homes of the devotees, and the different groves and gardens in the area.

Srila Prabhupada writes that whenever he saw Letchmore Heath it was like “...seeing from Kesi-ghata the other side of Vrndavana.” I would love to hear His Divine Grace describe our village.

Srila Prabhupada! By your grace Vrndavana is becoming visible in Hungary. And by Radha’s and Syama’s mercy it is complete in every way. Nonetheless, my heart tells me it lacks your physical presence.

I know you are present in your instructions, your deity, and your unmanifest feature, but for us conditioned souls who want to see you with our blunt senses, your physical form is sorely missed.

Dasa Gosvami saw Vraja as a desert, Giriraja a great python, and Radha-kunda as the mouth of a tiger without the presence of Rupa Gosvami. Can l not say that Krsna Valley is incomplete without you?

I take solace in serving you. When I preach I feel you beside me, and when some substantial result comes, I see your beaming smile and hear your resonant voice say, “Good boy!” I live for these rewards.

Kind reader! In this and the following chapter, the course of our Parikrama will take us to the sacred places in Bhauma Vrndavana that are found in Vrndavana town and Vraja’s northern petal.

Although the Parikrama of Bhauma Vrndavana would take us to these two locales separately, due to the proximity of these two areas of the dhama in New Vraja-dhama, they are here described together.

Let us then continue. As I retraced my steps from Krsna-kunda-tila, I thought of the holy places that lie on the southern side of Nitai Circular Road, the first of which is the Grantha-samadhi.

Tucked into a corner of Jhulanavana, near the samadhi of Sanatana Gosvami, the Grantha-samadhi is a unique shrine. It is a tomb that has been and always will be shrouded in mystery.

What exactly is buried there? Tradition tells us that the Gosvamis considered some of the books they had written too confidential for the general mass of people, and so they decided to bury them.

Rumours abound regarding the content of those books. Locals say the books are sealed within an iron or stone container. Even if this were true, some pertinent questions would still remain unanswered.

If the books were unsuitable for reading, why would the Gosvamis bury them, leaving open the possibility that they could be excavated, which would defeat the original purpose of putting them in samadhi?

Wouldn’t it have been more prudent to burn the books, disposing of them quickly, as Caitanya Mahaprabhu did when He tossed the book He had composed on Sanskrit grammar into the Ganga?

These questions will likely remain unanswered. But there is another explanation for what is in the Grantha-samadhi: Of the works of the Gosvamis there were at first only single copies, the originals.

When Srinivasa, Narottama, and Syamananda took those originals to Bengal and Orissa, tradition has it that Syamananda Prabhu had all the books copied. The copies were then sent back to Jiva Gosvami.

The idea is that Jiva Gosvami, concerned that these books be properly preserved, placed them in an airtight container and buried the container, thus creating the unique Grantha-samadhi.

This version is also not beyond doubt. Jiva Gosvami already had a large collection of books in a library at the Radha-Damodara temple. Why wouldn’t he also include the Gosvamis’ books in his library?

It is also reasonable to assume that additional copies could have been made from the duplicates. What, then, would be so special about them that they had to be buried to be preserved?

I paused at the site marking the Grantha-samadhi to recall how Vrndavana forest got its name, and how the Vraja-vasis’ residences appeared there the day after they arrived from Gokula.

Vrndavana, Krsna, and His entourage are all eternal; still, they all seem to conform to the laws of material time — apparently coming into being and changing over time — when they appear on earth.

A great devotee named Kedara lived during Satya-yuga. Kedara was the king of the seven continents. His religious merit qualified him for the post of Indra, king of heaven, bur he did not accept it.

In time, following the advice of brahmanas, the king placed his kingdom and his wife in the care of his sons and retired to the forest to engage in devotional austerities and think only of Lord Krsna.

Chanting the names of the Lord and remembering His pastimes in trance made Kedara so dear to Krsna that the Sudarsana cakra always hovered behind the king, protecting him from danger.

In time, Kedara left this world and went back to Godhead. His place of bhajana was so spiritually surcharged that any living creature that left its body there was guaranteed liberation.

Kedara had a daughter named Vrnda who was a partial expansion of the goddess of fortune. Inclined to asceticism, she refused to marry; instead, she accepted initiation from Durvasa Muni.

Vrnda left home and went to the forest, where for sixty thousand years she chanted the mantra given to her at initiation. Finally, Krsna appeared to her and offered to fulfil all of her desires.

Seeing the Lord in all His sweetness, Vrnda fainted in ecstasy. As soon as she regained consciousness she asked to become His maidservant, a boon that Krsna happily granted.

Like her exalted father, Vrnda also went back to Godhead. The forest in which she attained perfection continues to be known to this very day as Vrndavana (“the forest of Vrnda").

There is another pastime that explains how Vrndavana forest got its name. King Siradhvaja had two daughters: Tulasi and Vedavati. Vedavati later became Sita, the only wife of Lord Ramacandra.

Tulasi also wanted the Lord as her husband, so she came to Vrndavana forest to perform austerities. But because of Durvasa Muni’s curse she was forced to accept Sankhasura as her husband.

After her husband had died, Tulasi became the tulasi tree and Lord Visnu became the Salagrama-sila. Since that time, Vrndavana is known as the forest where tulasi abounds.

Yet another reason why this forest is called Vrndavana: Krsna manifested this pleasure playground to please Srimati Radharani, who is always surrounded by a multitude (vrnda) of gopis.

One of Sri Radha’s names is Vrnda because She and Her numerous groups of gopi friends are inseparable. Lord Krsna therefore chose the name Vrndavana for the forest He had created for Her.

Nowhere do Radha and Krsna enjoy Themselves more, nowhere are Their associates more eager to serve Them, and nowhere are Their devotees more happy to live and perform bhajana than in Vrndavana.

Dasa Gosvami prays,

*Vaidagdhyottara-narma karmatha-sakhi-vrndaih paritam rasaih pratyckam taru-kunja'*  
*Vallarhgirhdronisu ratrin-divam*  
*nana-keli-bharena yatra ramate tan navya-yunor yugam —*  
*Tat-padambuja-gandha-bandhurataram vrndavanam tdd bhaje*

**“Surrounded by Their witty gopi friends, the youthful divine couple, Radha and Krsna, enjoys many kinds of transcendental pastimes —**

**“Within the groves and mountain caves and amongst the blossoming vines of Vrndavana forest. I worship Vrndavana forest, which is delightful with the scent of Their lotus feet.”**

Let us bow to the Vrndavana forest of New Vraja-dhama, where Radha-Syama reside on a golden throne, where devotees serve the samadhis of great souls, and where the maha-mantra always resounds.

**\* \* \* \***

Scripture describes that Visvakarma, the engineer of the demigods, constructed the original town of Vrndavana overnight when the Vraja-vasis first arrived there from Gokula.

Nanda Maharaja was attached to living in Mahavana, bur his subjects wanted him to find another place to live where they would be free from the disturbances created by the demons.

The Vraja-vasis knew that Krsna was the source of auspiciousness for His father, and for them as well. What else, then, could have caused the misfortunes that had befallen them but their place of residence?

And so when Krsna was a little more than three years old the Vraja-vasis migrated to Vrndavana forest, which they knew to be the residence of Laksmi and opulent with lush grasses for their cows.

The journey took two whole days. On the first day they reached the Yamuna, where they encamped on the far shore. On the next day they forded the river along with their cows and personal belongings.

After the homes of the gopas were complete, Visvakarma mentally conceived of the palace of Nanda Maharaja, which was actually the eternal residence of Lord Krsna manifesting itself within his mind.

Nandagrama is Krsna’s eternal residence, hut while the Lord lived in Vrndavana the opulences and sweetness of Nandisvara Hill appeared in a different form, with Chatikara at its centre.

The palace Visvakarma built was hound in by a high wall, which was in turn surrounded hy a moat and flower gardens that made the moat perfect for water sports and other pleasing diversions.

Two large gem-studded gates gave access to the palace, which contained four separate living accommodations, each with one dozen rooms, including bedrooms, kitchens, bathrooms, and so on.

The pillars were of the finest marble. The walls were decorated with ornamental designs made of gems so brilliant that the light reflected off them from a single lamp would illuminate an entire room at night.

The rooms were replete with varieties of ivory and gold furnishings found only in the homes of exalted demigods. And everywhere hung fine silken draperies, curtains, festoons, and flags.

The floors were made of marble so translucent that walking on them felt like walking on water, and the steps to and from those marble floors looked like waves upon lakes of liquefied pure goodness.

Visvakarma, who had transcendental intelligence, was himself amazed at the wonderful edifice he had created. He could understand that, beyond his skill, the Lord’s potencies had created the city.

**\* \* \* \***

Having finished Pancakrosi Vrndavana, Visvakarma proceeded to construct a similar village and palace for King Vrsabhanu some distance from Nanda’s capital at what would later be called Vasati.

With the residences of the cowherds now complete, Visvakarma, to avoid confusion, had each family’s name engraved on a plaque and placed above the door of their allotted residence.

Still not satisfied with his offering to the Lord, Visvakarma built walls around each town, inside of which were roads, squares, fountains, meeting halls, and accommodations for animals, especially the cows.

Vrndavana town, undeniably the most beautiful of cities, now glowed in the dark night like a beautiful sapphire. But Visvakarma was still not satisfied. He wanted to decorate Vrndavana forest as well.

Knowing that Radha and Krsna would enjoy Their love-sports in the groves of Vrndavana forest, Visvakarma began by building a rasa-mandapa, a circular, rotating dancing arena for the rasa dance.

He then filled Vrndavana forest with benches, lakes, ghatas, pavilions, and pathways, all extremely opulent, yet made indescribably sweet by the influence of the forest’s rural ambiance.

Visvakarma landscaped certain parts of the forest, beautifying them with parks that put to shame the Nandanandana gardens of the heavenly planets. Where else is there such a place as Vrndavana?

In all Visvakarma created thirty such gardens, one of which stood above all the others for its verdant bounty and opulent grove-pavilion, which he personally designed and supervised to completion.

This garden surrounded a secluded banyan tree. Nearby was a natural lake with a golden staircase leading into its aquamarine waters, and surrounded by a grove of campaka trees of varied colours.

On the eastern hank of the lake, Visvakarma built an ornate structure that blended with the foliage. In some places it looked like a floral grove, in others like a palace, and yet in others, both.

The building was circular, its walls and ceilings decorated with gems inside and out. Its multiple domes were made of gold and silver, as were its lofty spires that shot up above the treetops.

The palace had entrances facing each of the eight directions. A staircase of seven steps with golden balustrades led to each entrance. Each step was made from a different type of precious stone.

Mirrors, garlands, banners, and flags hung everywhere. The palace’s interior showcased ornate furniture and decorations, which even Visvakarma admitted far surpassed those he had created for Brahma.

Before leaving, Visvakarma quickly toured his masterpiece, instructing workers on slight adjustments. Finally he had the entire palace washed with water mixed with sandal paste, aguru, and kasturi.

As dawn approached, Visvakarma toured Vrndavana town and its vicinity. After seeing everything, he was simply amazed that he had been able to create such wonders in so little time.

No doubt, Visvakarma was but an instrument of Yogamaya. He and his workers, like other demigods, had been drawn into Krsna’s earthly pastimes for the purpose of their own purification.

Visvakarma and his hosts gathered in the sky above the pavilion, where Krsna slept with His brother and mothers, and offered prayers to the Lord, who responded to their prayers by smiling in His sleep.

As the first rays of the rising sun touched the horizon and roused the birds from sleep, the demigods returned to their heavenly abodes, taking all leftover materials and the tools of their trade with them.

Pancakrosi Vrndavana was exceedingly beautiful. Far surpassing the great city of Dvaraka that Visvakarma would build some years later, it was a creation comparable only to itself.

The buildings all had ample space between them. Since not a tree, or even a bush, had been cut down to make way for construction, the city had the feeling of other worldy buildings amidst a village green.

The houses, although just built, appeared as if they had always been there. What’s more, none of them had ever been inhabited, but they all had the inviting feeling of having been lived in.

Thus, when the Vraja-vasis finally awoke to the chirping of birds and the lowing of their cows, they were amazed by what they saw. They could only look around and exclaim, “Astonishing! Astonishing!”

Once they had come to their senses, the cowherd men approached Maharaja Nanda and asked, “O King! How did this happen? Please explain to us where these buildings came from!”

Nandaraja reflected on the words of Garga Muni — who had told him that Krsna would be just like Lord Narayana — and put his subjects at ease by explaining the workings of the supreme will.

Nanda told them, “Omniscient Garga Rsi predicted that my son would bring all opulence to our families and cows, and now we are seeing for ourselves that his words are true.

“The Supreme Lord, who by the movement of His eyebrows creates and destroys the universes, is pleased with us due to our connection with Krsna, and so He has built these homes for us.

“Why should we question our good fortune? Rather, let us accept it and serve He who has blessed us. By taking care of Krsna, the brahmanas, and our cows, we can show Him our gratitude.”

The cowherd men and the women who had joined them all applauded their king and followed him throughout the village as he assigned them residences according to the names inscribed upon the houses.

At an auspicious time determined hy saintly brahmanas, Maharaja Nanda and Maharaja Vrsabhanu entered their palaces along with their families and servants, and then their subjects entered their homes as well.

The cows, bulls, and calves were led to their respective barns and pens, which opened out onto lush green pastures. The Vraja-vasis rejoiced in their new homes and tilled the air with divine vibrations.

Krsna and Balarama called Their friends out into the lanes, parks, and pastures of Vrndavana town. Their laughter, joyful voices, and playful commotion confirmed that the Vraja-vasis had truly arrived at home.

**\* \* \* \***

Behind the Grantha-samadhi, the open field and the woods to the right are known as Jhulanavana, named after one of many places Radha and Krsna enjoy swing (jhulana) pastimes with Their friends.

Swing pastimes invoke in Radha, or in any gopi Krsna manages to convince to join Him on the swing, a special sort of dependence that surpasses even Her otherwise unsurpassed dependence on the Lord.

We are all dependent upon Krsna in every way, hut in Vrndavana this sense of dependence is often covered hy a sense of familiarity with Him born of the different varieties of spontaneous love.

The gopis, especially when they are all together, will even show indifference to the Lord, thus increasing Krsna’s desire for them. These are the ways of spontaneous love, ways that cannot he imitated.

And when Krsna docs lure Radha onto a swing, He often makes it fly so high in the air that She loses Her composure. Afraid of falling, She holds tight to Krsna’s neck in utter dependence upon Her beloved.

Krsna kicks the swing higher and higher — sometimes wrapping it completely around the branch from which it hangs — so that, in front of Her giggling friends, Radha is forced to beg Krsna to stop.

These moods of humiliation, submission, and dependence are greatly relished by both Krsna and Radha — He as the enjoyer and She as the enjoyed. Jhulanavana’s atmosphere is thus saturated with these moods.

In the eastern corner of Jhulanavana is the samadhi of Sanatana Gosvami, the crown jewel of Vaisnava scholars, the exemplar of devotional life, and the embodiment of humility.

At length, Kaviraja Gosvami describes Sanatana Gosvami’s meeting with Caitanya Mahaprabhu, his escape from the service of the Nawab, and his attaining shelter at the Lord’s lotus feet in Varanasi.

Kaviraja Gosvami devoted five whole chapters of Sri Caitanya-caritamrta to Lord Caitanya’s teachings to Sanatana. More signify candy, the Lord spent two months continuously teaching him.

Sri Caitanya-caritamrta documents many incidents in Sanatana Gosvami’s life. I cannot recount all of them here. But some time later I will write more about Sanatana’s service to Madana-mohana.

But here at his samadhi I feel the need to mention some events that led to the passing away of this great acarya, whose influence on our disciplic line cannot be overestimated, nor overstated.

The following event in Puri documents the common understanding that, in terms of learning and devotion, Lord Caitanya considered Sanatana Gosvami the pre-eminent Gaudiya Vaisnava.

One day Jagadananda Pandita, a dear and intimate associate of the Lord, came to visit Sanatana Gosvami at his residence in the hut of Haridasa Thakura, at what is now called Siddha-bakula.

Sanatana’s mind was disturbed because while travelling to Purl be bad contracted a disease that produced oozing sores all over bis body, and yet every day the Lord would visit and tightly embrace him.

The oozing discharge from Sanatana's sores would moisten the Lord’s body, causing Sanatana such intense grief that be decided to commit suicide. But the Lord intervened and prevented him from doing so.

Sanatana solicited Jagadananda’s advice as the two sat beneath the bakula tree. Jagadananda reflected on the situation and decided that Sanatana’s best course of action was to return to Vraja.

Sanatana considered this to be fitting advice, and so when Caitanya Mahaprabhu arrived the next day, the Gosvami said, “I came here for my benefit, but I see that I am getting just the opposite.

“I am unfit to render service. I simply commit offences day after day. Moreover, blood is running from infected itching sores on my body, smearing Your body with moisture, but still You touch me by force.

“My dear Sir! You do not have even a pinch of aversion to touching my body, which is in a horrible condition. Because of this offence, everything auspicious will be vanquished for me.

“I have consulted Jagadananda Pandita for his opinion, and he has also advised me to return to Vrndavana after Ratha-yatra.” With this, Sanatana fell silent and awaited the Lord’s mercy.

Upon hearing of Jagadananda’s advice, Caitanya Mahaprabhu was angry. He said, “\[Jagadananda Pandita\] is only a new boy, but be has become so proud that he advises a person like you.”

The Lord said,

*vyavahare-paramairthe tumi— tara guru-tulya*  
*tomare upadese, na jane apana-mulya*  
*amara upadesta tumi— pramanika arya tomareha upadese — balaka kare aiche karya*

**“In affairs of spiritual advancement and even in ordinary dealings, you are on the level of his spiritual master, yet not knowing his own value, Jagadananda dares to advise you.**

**“My dear Sanatana! You are on the level of My advisor, for you are an authorised person. But Jaga \[Jagadananda Pandita\] wants to advise you. This is but the impudence of a naughty boy.”**

Sanatana Gosvami proceeded to express his wonder at the intimate relationship Lord Caitanya had with Jagadananda, lamenting that he himself had not achieved such intimacy with the Lord. Sanatana said:

**“It is my misfortune that You have not accepted me as one of Your intimate relations.” At this, Sri Caitanya Mahaprabhu was somewhat ashamed. Just to satisfy Sanatana Gosvami, He spoke the following:**

**“My dear Sanatana! Please do not think that Jagadananda is more dear to Me than you. However, I cannot tolerate transgressions of the standard etiquette (maryaida-langhana).**

**“You are an experienced authority in the sastras, whereas Jagadananda is just a young boy and his advising you is intolerable for Me. Therefore I am chastising him.**

**“Sanatana, try to understand! You have the power to convince even Me. In many circumstances you have already convinced Me about ordinary behaviour and devotional service.”**

The Lord concluded,

**“I offer you praise not because I think of you as being outside an intimate relationship with Me, but because you are actually so qualified that one is forced to praise your qualities.”**

Recalling this pastime reminds us not only of the high regard Lord Caitanya had for Sanatana Gosvami but also of how important the Lord considered the Vaisnava etiquette of dealings with superiors.

**\* \* \* \***

Lord Caitanya and Sanatana Gosvami were equally devoted to each other. Caitanya Mahaprabhu’s words of praise for Sanatana were matched by Sanatana’s staunch dedication to the Lord’s instructions.

In Varanasi and again in Puri, Caitanya Mahaprabhu reminded Sanatana of his mission to write books, rediscover Krsna’s pastime places, and teach the science of vraja-bhakti.

Caitanya Mahaprabhu said, *tomara sarira — mora pradhana ‘sadhana’*: “Your body is My principal instrument for executing many necessary functions. By your body I shall carry out many tasks.”

This order was Sanatana Gosvami’s life and soul. Even the bliss of serving Krsna directly could not deviate him from Sri Caitanya Mahaprabhu’s order, as the following story confirms.

After Sri Radha was installed beside the Lord, the Deities were named Radha-Madana-mohana. Sanatana Gosvami was lost in the bliss of serving the divine couple day and night.

But then he remembered the tasks Lord Caitanya had given him. He felt that by this blissful service to Madana-mohana he was neglecting his actual mission. Broken-hearted but determined, Sanatana prayed:

**“O master and mistress of my heart! Please forgive me but I must attend to the duties given to me by Lord Caitanya.”**

He then entrusted the temple and the Deities to Krsna Dasa Brahmacari.

In this way, by transferring the full-time service of Madana-mohana to someone else, Sanatana Gosvami showed that the order of the Lord is always more important than one’s own spiritual happiness.

An ever-increasing number of devotees came to Vrndavana, and Sanatana Gosvami began regularly circumamhulating the whole of Vraja-mandala, taking with him large contingents of eager pilgrims.

Sanatana Gosvami’s purpose was twofold: to introduce the pilgrims to the places of Krsna’s pastimes, and to use the presence of the devoted pilgrims to preach krsna bhakti to the local villagers of Vrndavana.

The Vraja-vasis already loved Sanatana as a pure Vaisnava, a great scholar, and a favourite of Sri Caitanya and Madana-mohana, so his sankirtana parties were always warmly welcomed throughout Vraja.

The villagers sat with the pilgrims to hear of Krsna’s pastimes and instructions, entranced by Sanatana’s erudition, purity, and above all his love for the land they also loved, and in which they lived.

When not leading his eighty-four-kro.su parikramas, Sanatana Gosvami would engage in even more personal, one-to-one exchanges with the residents of Vraja, treating them as if they were his children.

Sanatana Gosvami would offer the Vraja-vasis practical down-to-earth advice for their daily lives, as well as illuminating spiritual guidance to make the most of their privileged birth in the dhama.

In reciprocation, the Vraja-vasis were always alert to ensure that the ageing saint’s basic needs of food and shelter were met whenever he stayed at their villages in Gokula, Nandagrama, or Vrndavana.

When his annual parikramas and solitary travels through Vrndavana became too much for his ageing body to bear, Sanatana decided to pass the rest of his life on the banks of Manasa-ganga.

Sanatana Gosvami lived beneath a tree opposite the temple of Cakresvara Mahadeva, engaged in solitary bhajana, and did daily Parikrama of Govardhana until Krsna personally advised him to stop.

After that incident, he would sit beside the large sila Krsna had given him to circumambulate instead of Govardhana Hill, chant the holy names, and absorb his mind in the pastimes of the divine couple.

At times Sanatana Gosvami would be so absorbed in devotional service that he would neither eat nor sleep for days. On one such occasion, Madana-mohana personally brought him milk.

The Lord was not happy to see Sanatana Gosvami so exposed to the elements. In the guise of a local cowherd boy, Krsna petitioned the acarya on behalf of the Vraja-vasis living in that area:

“We Vraja-vasis feel aggrieved to see you living under a tree. Please make us happy by living in a cottage.” Unable to resist such a kind request, Sanatana allowed the villagers to construct a kutira for him.

Sanatana Gosvami lived alone in that cottage, continually absorbed in trance except when visited by Vaisnavas and pilgrims, whom he would welcome, offering spiritual advice if requested to do so.

In 1554, on the full-moon day of Asadha, which is also the appearance day of Vyasadeva, Guru Purnima, Sanatana Gosvami left this world and entered into the eternal pastimes of Radha and Krsna.

The Gosvamis of Vrndavana carried his body to the foot of Dvadasaditya-tila where, accompanied by almost all the residents and saints of Vraja, and amidst many tears, they placed him in *samadhi.*

Because everyone adored Sanatana Gosvami as their father, they followed the tradition of shaving their heads. Even today, the Asadha-purnima day is referred to as Mudiya-purnima.

And because Sanatana Gosvami was so fond of circumambulating Govardhana Hill, on that day everyone also circumambulates Giriraja in memory of the great associate of Caitanya Mahaprabhu.

Residents of New Vraja-dhama may consider adopting these two practices on this day as a means to offer their respects to the author of the legendary texts Brhad-bhagavatbinirta and Hari-bhakti-vilasa.

Brhad-bhagavatamrta extracts the essence of all devotional literature in a way that is easily understandable to all sincere Vaisnavas. In short, it summarises the principles of Vaisnava philosophy —

Prescribes nama-sankirtana as the easiest form of bhajana, glorifies the different incarnations of the Lord and their abodes, and systematically guides the reader to the service of Radha and Krsna.

In Varanasi, Lord Caitanya had ordered Sanatana Gosvami to write a book describing Vaisnava rituals and etiquette, which the acarya did in abbreviated form. It was later expanded on by Gopala Bhatta.

Since both acaryas contributed to the compiling of Hari-bhakti-vilasa, opinions differ as to who wrote it, but it is universally agreed that the Dig-darsini commentary is the work of Sanatana Gosvami.

Of Sanatana Gosvami, Dasa Gosvami wrote,

*vairagya-yug-bhakti-rasam prayatnair apayayan mam anabhipsum andham*  
*krpambudhir yah para-duhkha-duhkhi sanatanas tam prabhum dsrayami*

**“I was unwilling to drink the nectar of devotional service possessed of renunciation, but Sanatana Gosvami, out of his causeless mercy, made me drink, even though I was otherwise unable to do so.**

**“Therefore he is an ocean of mercy. He is very much compassionate to fallen souls like me, and thus it is my duty to offer my respectful obeisances unto his lotus feet.”**

**\* \* \* \***

Che samadhis of two other great Vaisnavas — Tapana Misra and Candrasekhara Acarya, both friends of Sanatana and associates of Caitanya Mahaprabhu — are also found here at Jhulanavana.

Tapana Misra lived in a village on the Padma River in East Bengal. He was devoted to Lord Krsna but he did not know how to achieve the Lord and he knew no one who could show him the way.

One night, after he had cried himself to sleep in desperation, a divine being appeared to him and said, “O brahmana! Nimai Pandita, the Supreme Lord in the guise of His devotee, will dispel your illusion.”
