Pleased with His devotee, Krsna said, “This book of yours is such nectar for My ears that I stand before you to offer you a boon. Make a request. What else do you desire besides seeing Me and My beloved?”

Bilvamangala paused for a moment and then said, “O Krsna-deva! For the next hundred days of Brahma, may this book, which is nectar for Your ears, flood the hearts of those who are devoted to You.”

Krsna fulfilled Bilvamangala’s desire and then spontaneously offered another benediction: “So be it! Your book will be dear not only to devotees in general but also to experts in devotion, even to My gopis.”

The Thakura, tilled with wonder that such an august audience would relish his book, thought, “How astonishing!” Krsna then tested him, saying, “Devotees will be attracted by your unprecedented poetry.”

Bilvamangala shook his head and said, “You asked me to make a request and so again I beg: Poetry aside, let devotees taste the fragrance of Your sweetness as it is glorified in this book.”

The Lord clapped His hands in pleasure and exclaimed, “Lilasuka! You have purchased Me with your compassion lor others and your love for Me. You have come to Vraja, and now you should taste it.

“While in that body, you will taste the happiness of seeing My rasa dance. And in the near future, when you cast ott that material body, you will enter into the pastimes you have seen and described.”

Radha and Krsna glanced at Bilvamangala with mercy and affection and then disappeared. As darkness fills a room as soon as a candle is extinguished, misery at once engulfed the Thakura’s heart.

Bilvamangala called out after the divine couple,

*anugraha-dviguna-visala-locanair anusmaran mrdu-murali-ravamrtaih*  
*yata yatah prasarati me vilocanam tatas tatah sphuratu tavaiva vaibhava*

**“O Krsna! While remembering the soft sounds of Your flute and Your eyes spread wide with mercy, I pray that wherever my eyes wander, You two will always manifest Your beauty and sweetness to me.”**

**\* \* \* \***

On the other side of the Parikrama path from Bilvamangala Thakura’s samadhi is the forest of Chattravana, also known as Chata. Here the gopas once enthroned Krsna and crowned Him king.

At the centre of Chattravana stood an expansive grove of blossoming trees, within which lay a beautiful marble throne inlaid with precious jewels, and other furniture that would have made Indra envious.

One day, as the gopas were herding their cows nearby, they came upon this sylvan palace. They consulted amongst themselves and decided to make it their capital for the day and to crown Krsna king.

The boys quickly picked flowers from the trees and creepers, where they were growing abundantly, and expertly fashioned garlands, ornaments, and an ornate flower crown to use for the coronation.

When everything was ready, Sridama, accompanied by boys blowing horns, playing flutes, and beating drums, led Krsna into the grove as other boys received Him by showering flowers and shouting, “Jaya!”

The forest animals and birds all crowded around the grove, eager to see what all the commotion was about. Along with the eager gopas, they watched as Sridama led Krsna to the throne.

Some servant boys brought the flower crown on a tray. To rousing applause, Sridama crowned Krsna, after which Krsna’s confidential friends garlanded Him as others adorned Him with flower ornaments.

Seated on a flower-decorated throne in that grove, adorned with flowers, Krsna resembled the presiding deity of blossoming forests, surrounded by His potencies, like colour and aroma.

Sri Krsna at once assumed the role of king, quickly distributing government posts to His closest friends, who then took their respective positions around the youthful sovereign.

Balarama sat on Krsna’s left side as prime minister, Sridama held the royal umbrella — a leafy branch — over Krsna’s head, Arjuna waved a camara from one side, while Subala offered Krsna pan from the other.

Madhumarigala, the self-appointed court jester, stood before the throne cutting jokes and making funny gestures to entertain the assembly, as musicians played and other boys blissfully danced.

On Balarama’s counsel, messengers were dispatched to surrounding forests to proclaim Krsna’s sovereignty over the residents. Madhu happened to come across Radha and the gopis in Umrao.

Blinded hy love for his king, Madhumangala neglected to consider the danger of asking the gopis to submit to Krsna as their master, until it was too late. Lalita angrily had Madhu bound with flower garlands.

From Her own throne, Radha sent Madhu back to Krsna with a message: at once end His insurrection and surrender to Her, or face the consequences and be humiliated like Madhumarigala.

To impress upon Krsna the gravity of Her ultimatum, Radha had thousands of gopis, armed with sticks and set to march on Chata, brought before Madhu, who was then set free to deliver Her message.

Chubby Madhumangala returned to Chata with startling speed. After catching his breath, he informed Krsna of the gopas’ inevitable destruction at the hands of a fully armed gopi army.

At first the boys opted to fight for their king to the very last man, but when a sentry returned to describe the size and strength of the advancing battalion, Balarama advised a more diplomatic approach.

Waving a white flag, Krsna and His parliament met the approaching gopis and returned with them to Umrao, where, with His ministers’ help, He brokered a treaty that satisfied everyone.

Krsna accepted subservience to Sri Radha, and Radha, as the guardian of Her subjects, agreed to give Krsna shelter from King Cupid, who constantly assailed Krsna with his sharp arrows.

While these confidential negotiations were taking place, the gopas rested (visrama) at Bidavala. Such wonderful (camatkara) pastimes took place in this forest, and so it is known as Chattravana.

The temple of Radha-vallahha is in the northeastern corner of Chattravana. There Syamasundara is worshipped as the original form of the Personality of Godhead — the lover of Srimati Radharani.

All true Vaisnavas — Krsna included — think of themselves as servants of Krsna’s servants; Krsna identifies Himself as a servant of servants like Balarama, Yasoda, and above all, Srimati Radharani.

The Radha-vallahha Deity was discovered in Nidhuvana by Gopala Bhatta Gosvami’s disciple, Hit Harivamsa, who, for promoting the practice of not fasting on Ekadasi, was rejected by his guru.

During the reign of Aurangzeb, Radha-vallahha left Vrndavana with the other Deities. Unlike the other Deities, however, He went no further than Kaman, returning in 1784 to a newly constructed temple.

Whatever disagreement Gaudiya Vaisnavas may have with the worshippers of Radha-vallahha, He is the Supreme Lord, worthy of our worship; He has come to this world to bless us with His presence.

Just south of Radha-vallabha’s temple is a block of six terraced houses that represent the Radha-Gokulananda and Vamsi-gopala temples, as well as the samadhis of the following illustrious Vaisnavas: Narottama Dasa Thakura, Lokanatha Gosvami, Visvanatha Cakravarti Thakura, and Ganga-narayana Cakravarti. The worship of such great souls makes this area especially potent spiritually.

Now let us hear about each terraced house, beginning with the northernmost one, Radha-Gokulananda’s temple, and proceeding one by one to the southernmost, Ganga-narayana Cakravarti’s samadhi.

In the Radha-Gokulananda temple, Syamasundara is worshipped as both “the one who gives bliss to the people and cows of Gokula” and as “the one who derives bliss from the people and cows of Gokula.”

In Vraja, the area of the Radha-Gokulananda temple was originally Lokanatha Gosvami’s place of bhajana, where the saint served the Deity of Radha-vinoda, which he discovered in Kisori-kunda.

Gokulananda was the Deity of Visvanatha Cakravarti Thakura, who came to Vrndavana one hundred years after Lokanatha Gosvami’s disappearance; thus the name of the temple must have been changed.

On the altar of this temple are the small Gokulananda Deity that Cakravarti Thakura kept in his head hag, Lokanatha Gosvami’s Radha-vinoda, and Baladeva Vidyabhusanas Vijaya-Govinda Deity.

The altar is also graced by the Deity of Lord Caitanya worshipped hy Narottama Dasa Thakura, and a small cube-like Govardhana-sila given to Raghunatha Dasa Gosvami by Caitanya Mahaprabhu.

Radha-Gokulananda’s temple and samadhi courtyard have a charming and intimate atmosphere conducive to feeling the transcendental presence of the Lord and His exalted devotees.

**\* \* \* \***

Next to Radha-Gokulananda Mandira is the puspa-samadhi of Narottama Dasa Thakura, the sole initiated disciple of Lokanatha Gosvami and an empowered representative of Lord Nityananda.

Narottama Dasa was born into a royal family of East Bengal in 1531, while Caitanya Mahaprabhu was still physically present. He displayed all the features and symptoms of a great person, a mahapurusa.

A well-behaved child, Narottama astonished everyone with his sharp memory and intellect. His parents, however, worried about his detachment from worldly things and his strong devotional impulses.

It is said that on His visit to East Bengal Lord Caitanya had called out Narottama’s name while bathing in the Padma River and placed the wealth of divine love in the river for Narottama to receive later.

The book Prema-vilasa tells that when the river Padma asked the Lord how she would recognise Narottama, the Lord replied that hy His touch Narottama would cause her waters to swell onto her hanks.

At the age of twelve, Narottama had a dream in which Lord Nityananda directed him to collect the gift of love that Lord Caitanya Mahaprabhu had left for him in the Padma.

As foretold, when Narottama entered the river, the water rose. At the moment he received the krsna-prema promised by Lord Caitanya, Narottama’s complexion changed from dark to golden in colour.

Narottama’s thoughts were thereafter fully absorbed in Krsna and Vrndavana. Seeing that his parents wanted to tie him with the ropes of family life and royal duties, Narottama left home for Vraja.

In Vrndavana, Jiva Gosvami took charge of Narottama’s welfare. He trained him, alongside Srinivasa and Syamananda, for the specific purpose of preaching the message of Lord Caitanya.

Narottama developed a strong attraction to Lokanatha Gosvami while living in Vrndavana, but Lokanatha had vowed, out of deep feelings of humility, not to accept disciples under any circumstances.

The situation was a serious obstacle for Jiva Gosvami, who could not send Narottama out to preach without his being initiated and directly trained by a genuine guru in the techniques of nama-sankirtana.

Finally Lord Caitanya directly intervened and in a dream persuaded Lokanatha to initiate one disciple. On the full moon day of Sravana, then, Narottama received the grace of both guru and Krsna.

One day Jiva Gosvami called his three proteges together and revealed his vision for them as travelling preachers and distributors of the books the Gosvamis had painstakingly composed.

Narottama, Srinivasa, and Syamananda had come to Vrndavana to live in Krsna’s abode, but they also knew the essence of Vraja to be service to Caitanya Mahaprabhu and His followers.

Caitanya Mahaprabhu’s associates in Bengal were dying in separation from the Lord. Jahnava-devi therefore requested Jiva to send the three saints to Bengal with the entirety of the Gosvamis’ literature.

On the way, the books were stolen at Visnupura and then miraculously retrieved by Srinivasa Acarya, after he had sent Narottama i ? Kheturi and Syamananda Pandita to Orissa.

Jiva Gosvami had given the title of “Thakura” to Narottama, who became known as Thakura Mahasaya. Narottama set up an asrama in Buddhari, a short distance from his ancestral home in Kheturi.

As Narottama Dasa Thakura’s fame grew, many people, including brahmanas, became his disciples. But because he was from a kayastha family, ritualists objected to his initiating those of higher birth.

These ritualistic brahmanas, who had no realisation of scriptural conclusions, remained lifelong opponents of Thakura Mahasaya. He fearlessly succeeded in his mission nonetheless, without compromise.

One of Narottama Dasa Thakura’s major achievements was to organise the first festival in celebration of Lord Caitanya’s appearance, which was attended by devotees from all over India.

That Kheturi festival of 1583, the first Gaura-purnima festival, is now celebrated by devotees worldwide. It was a landmark event that helped define Gaudiya Vaisnavism in many ways.

The presence of devotees from Vrndavana, Bengal, and Orissa established who the Gaudiya Vaisnavas were, especially in relation to the leaders of Caitanya Mahaprabhu’s sampradaya.

More importantly, talks amongst senior devotees clarified the philosophical foundations of Lord Caitanya’s teachings, drawing a clear line between orthodoxy and the heresies of sahajiya sects.

Caitanya Mahaprabhu personally certified the significance of this festival and its achievements by appearing along with His associates before all the assembled devotees during a kirtana led by Narottama.

Narottama Dasa Thakura’s literary works comprise two books of songs, Prarthana, and Prema-bhakti-candrika, which spell out in simple language the essence of devotional service in practice and perfection.

Gaura Kisora Dasa Babaji was once asked how to acquire love of God. He replied, “It only costs five annas." The Babaji’s audience could not understand what he meant, so he elaborated:

“Go to the market and purchase Narottama Dasa Thakura’s Prarthana and Prema-bhakti-candrika for five annas. If you read and meditate on these books daily, you will develop love for Krsna.”

An example of Narottama Dasa Thakura’s simple Bengali poetry is this song from Prarthana,

*golokera prema-dhana, hari-nama-sankirtana, rati na janmila kene taya*  
*samsara-bisanale — dibanisi hiya jvale, judaite na kainu upaya*

**“The treasure of divine love in Goloka Vrndavana has descended as the congregational chanting of Lord Hari’s holy names.**

**“Why did my attraction for that chanting never come about? Day and night my heart burns from the fire of the poison of worldliness, and I have not taken the means to relieve it.”**

Thakura Mahasaya was an empowered preacher who revived Lord Caitanya’s legacy by delivering rogues and aristocrats alike from the clutches of illusion and engaging all of them in nama-sankirtana.

According to some historians, Narottama Dasa also systematised the *p*adavali style of kirtana, based upon the training he had received in the dhrupada style from Haridasa Svami in Vraja.

This musical tradition was called Garanhati— the name of Kheturi’s administrative district — after Narottama presented it for approval of the Gaudiya Vaisnava community at the first Gaura-purnima festival.

Amongst Narottama’s diverse group of disciples and associates, Ramacandra Kaviraja, a disciple of Srinivasa Acarya, was especially dear to Narottama, who mentions Ramacandra in several of his songs.

Indeed, the bond between these three was so strong that when Ramacandra Kaviraja gave up his body in Vrndavana, Srinivasa soon followed suit. Life was quickly unbearable for Narottama as well.

Taking disciples such as Ganga-narayana with him, Narottama went to the river Padma. After worshipping Lord Caitanya, Thakura Mahasaya entered the water amidst devotees chanting kirtana.

While the kirtana was going on, Narottama asked his devotees to pour water over his body. As they did so, he merged into the Padma like milk into water, vanishing from the sight of his followers.

Some of Narottama Dasa Thakura’s personal items were brought to Vrndavana and placed in samadhi beside the shrine of Lokanatha Gosvami; thus, master and disciple were reunited.

Narottama Dasa Thakura disappeared on the Krsna-pancami in the month of Karttika. However, as important as he is in the lineage of Gaudiya acaryas, the actual year of his disappearance is not known.

In his song of lamentation about being separated from the Lord and the Lord’s devotees, Narottama Dasa Thakura teaches us how a Vaisnava feels and behaves at the samadhi of a saint:

**“Being unable to obtain the association of Lord Gauranga, accompanied by all of these devotees in whose association He performed His pastimes, Narottama Dasa simply weeps.”**

**\* \* \* \***

Lokanatha Gosvami’s samadhi stands at the solitary site near Kesi-ghata where he used to remain absorbed in the nectar of nama-sankirtana and the service of his beloved Deity, Radha-vinoda.

Born in 1483 in North Bengal, Lokanatha studied at the school of his father. At the age of fourteen he was sent to Santipura to study Srimad-Bhagavatam from his father’s guru, Advaita Acarya.

In Navadvipa, Lokanatha was just as enamoured with Nimai Pandita as he was with Krsna’s pastimes. By the time he had completed his studies and returned home, he could think of nothing but Nimai.

At home Lokanatha started his own school, hut he constantly thought of returning to Navadvipa. Then, one day, Nimai Pandita appeared at Lokanatha’s house while on a tour of Bengal.

Happy to see His friend, Nimai took Lokanatha with Him as He went from village to village, defeating all other scholars and meeting with devotees like Tapana Misra and Purusottama.

The two friends parted ways when Nimai returned to Navadvipa. Lokanatha stayed with his parents for five more years, but as soon as they died he renounced the world and hurried to Navadvipa.

Lokanatha’s idea was to live always in the company of Nimai Pandita, but Nimai had other plans: He wanted Lokanatha and his friend Bhugarbha to go to Vraja and reclaim Krsna’s lost pastime places.

Only with great effort was the Lord able to convince Lokanatha to leave for Vrndavana. The Lord’s winning argument was that a servant can be truly happy only when his master is happy.

Lokanatha and Bhugarbha tearfully accepted the order of the Lord, arriving in Vraja three months later to begin the work of locating sacred sites, first in Mathura and then in Vrndavana.

Their work was arduous. Vague scriptural references did little to help pinpoint geographical locations, and aside from tribals, who had no interest in Krsna, no one lived in the wilderness that was then Vraja.

Nityananda, Advaita, and others had come earlier, but they had visited only a few obvious sites like Govardhana and the Yamuna, and they left without searching for or marking any pastime places.

Nevertheless, Lokanatha Gosvami and Bhugarbha Gosvami were resolute. Gradually, one by one, they found covered ponds, temple ruins, and landmarks resembling those portrayed in scripture.

The two friends kept records of their findings and began to draw rough maps of Krsna’s pastime places, until news from Bengal brought their work to a halt — Nimai Pandita had taken sannyasa.

The following account proves that no one can see the Lord by any effort that goes against His order. When Lokanatha and Bhugarbha heard of the Lord’s sannyasa, they decided to go see Him.

Meanwhile, Caitanya Mahaprabhu had left for a tour of South India. The two friends wandered throughout the continent for years in a determined — but ultimately unsuccessful — effort to find the Lord.

Meanwhile, Caitanya Mahaprabhu came to Vrndavana and discovered Radha-kunda and Syama-kunda Himself. But before Lokanatha and Bhugarbha returned the Lord had left for Puri.

Indeed, the two friends, having passed five years in search of Lord Caitanya, arrived in Vrndavana just a few days after His departure. Heartbroken at having missed the Lord, they yet remained resolute.

Once again the two set out, but this time Lord Caitanya, in His sannyasa dress, appeared to Lokanatha in a dream and said, “I am always with you in Vrndavana. Return and never leave again.”

Humbled, Lokanatha and Bhugarbha returned to Vrndavana and the work assigned to them by the Lord; they would now be satisfied by seeing Him, not with their eyes, but through their service to Him.

While residing in a forest by the banks of Kisori-kunda in Umrao, Lokanatha Gosvami found the Deity of Radha-vinoda, who had been submerged in the waters of the lake for a very long time.

Lokanatha served Radha-vinoda by lovingly offering Him tulasi leaves, forest fruits, and vegetables. In turn, Radha-vinoda helped Lokanatha locate more of the places of Krsna’s pastimes.

Gradually Rupa and Sanatana and the other Gosvamis arrived in Vrndavana to expand upon the work Lokanatha and Bhugarbha had begun, finally making Lord Caitanya’s desire a reality.

The Gosvamis lived in huts scattered throughout the groves of Vrndavana, and as Deities like Govindadji appeared, temple spires pierced through the treetops and memorials marked sacred sites.

Rupa and Sanatana reviewed the records of Lokanatha’s and Bhugarbha’s work. No longer carrying on the Lord’s mission alone, the two pioneers were now able to spend more time in bhajana.

Just as the revival of Vrndavana was going from strength to strength, Caitanya Mahaprabhu disappeared from this world, casting a shadow of gloom over the attempts to reclaim Krsna’s land.

And as fate would have it, the Lord’s associates also began to depart, either from the pain of separation from Caitanya Mahaprabhu, or from the inevitable influence of time on their temporal bodies.

One by one they disappeared: Lord Nityananda, Advaita Acarya, Gadadhara Pandita, Ramananda Raya, Sanatana Gosvami, Rupa Gosvami, and the two Raghunathas, to name a few.

Finally, only Lokanatha, Bhugarbha, Gopala Bhatta, and Jiva Gosvami remained to shoulder the responsibility of handing down Lord Caitanya’s mission to successive generations of Vaisnavas.

Jiva Gosvami wanted to send his three most brilliant students to Bengal on a preaching mission. Only one obstacle stood in their way: Lokanatha Gosvami’s reluctance to initiate Narottama.

Jiva told Lokanatha, “Narottama is the son of a powerful king. He is the most suitable person to preach in Bengal. If you do not initiate him, we will be unable to empower him for that service.”

But Lokanatha had taken a vow. Bowing his head to his joined palms, he pleaded for Jiva’s understanding. Sri Jiva could not convince the senior Vaisnava, but Narottama was determined to do so.

Narottama worshipped Lokanatha in his heart. He would also go every day before sunrise to where Lokanatha would evacuate. He would leave a pot of fresh water for washing, then hide in the bushes.

After Lokanatha had answered the call of nature, washed his hands, and left, Narottama would come with a broom and sweep the place clean, feeling bliss in doing menial service for his guru.

This went on for one year. Finally, disturbed that someone was continually serving him in this way, Lokanatha went very early one morning to catch his anonymous benefactor red-handed.

Narottama arrived, and Lokanatha called out, “Who are you! Come here!” Narottama fell at Lokanatha’s feet and said, “I am your servant, Narottama.” Lokanatha Gosvami was moved.

As he contemplated the situation and debated within himself, he recalled the instruction Caitanya Mahaprabhu had given him long ago: “Lokanatha! You will have to accept one disciple in this life.”

Finally Lokanatha Gosvami agreed. Nodding his head, he told Narottama, “On the purnima of next month, I will accept you and give you diksa. Please inform Sri Jiva Gosvami of my decision."

Who can describe Narottama’s happiness? And what of the bliss of the other Vaisnavas when they heard about the long-awaited initiation of the ideal disciple hy the ideal spiritual master?

After the initiation, Lokanatha Gosvami took Narottama Dasa Thakura into confidence, reaching him the esoteric art of devotional meditation practised during nama-sankirtana.

Later, Jiva Gosvami requested Lokanatha, who was approaching one hundred years of age, to bless his disciple with success in his mission to rake the Gosvamis’ hooks to Bengal and preach their message.

Lokanatha Gosvami turned to Narottama and said, “I shall he very happy if Narottama preaches. My son! I bless you to succeed in your mission and make Caitanya Mahaprabhu happy.”

Narottama, who would soon be leaving his spiritual master, fell overwhelmed. He said, “Allow me to come and visit you from lime to time.” Recalling how Lord Caitanya had eluded him, Lokanatha said:

“No my child! You need not come. This will be the last time you see me.” Hearing this, Narottama fainted from despair. After regaining consciousness, he bathed Lokanatha Gosvamis feet with tears.

Struggling to see his divine master through tear-filled eyes, Narottama offered his respects again and again. Finally Jiva Gosvami led him away to prepare for the long journey to Bengal.

Lokanatha left this world on the Krsna-paksastami of Sravana. His divine body was interred on the spot where he had worshipped Radha-vinoda, initiated Narottama, and chanted Hare Krsna.

Kavi-karnapura writes,

**“Lokanatha Gosvami was the incarnation of Lila-manjari.”**

Narottama Dasa Thakura, in his Prarthana, also writes about his spiritual master and his gopi identity:

**“O Lokanatha! O my master! Please be merciful to me. Bless me that Radha’s and Krsna’s lotus feet will he always fixed in my mind, and that I may associate with you in your form as a gopi.**

**“O master! When, by your mercy, will l be allowed to serve the lotus feet of Sri Radha, the best of all gopis?**

**When that time comes, service to the divine couple will certainly flood me with boundless bliss.**

**“O Sri Rupa-manjari! O friend of the divine couple! Please cast your glance of mercy upon me and sprinkle this very distressed Narottama Dasa with the nectar of devotional service to you.”**

**\* \* \* \***

The next terraced house marks the samadhi of the great author, Visvanatha Cakravarti Thakura. Our Srila Prabhupada favoured his Bhagavatam commentaries when compiling his own purports.

Visvanatha Cakravarti Thakura was born in a high-class brahmana family in 1638. He accepted formal devotional training from Krsna-carana Cakravarti, a granddisciple of Narottama Dasa Thakura.

Visvanatha’s diksa-guru was the son of Krsna-carana, Radha-ramana Cakravarti. Visvanatha married at a young age but renounced the world while still in his youth and took a vow to live in Vrndavana.

In Vrndavana, Cakravarti Thakura formally accepted the dress of a renunciant and received the name Sri Hari-vallabha, a name by which he is rarely known but sometimes used to refer to himself.

Visvanatha lived in various places throughout Vrndavana — Radha-kunda, Yavata, Govardhana, and the compound of Gokulananda’s temple — as onfirmed by statements made in Ins books.

The entire Vaisnava community recognised the self-effulgent superiority of Cakravarti Thakura’s devotion, scholarship, and renunciation, and thus he was naturally chosen as their leader.

In the hundred years or so that had passed since Lord Caitanya’s departure, various deviations had sprung up in the name of Gaudiya Vaisnavism, and Visvanatha vigorously fought against them.

Growing factions of sahajiyas tried to legitimise t heir deviant behaviour by claiming to he followers of Narottama Dasa Thakura, whom they had given the concocted title of Sahajiya-kula bhusana.

Rupa Kaviraja and his followers, for example, taught that only sunnyasis could he gurus, that spontaneous devotion did not require discipline, and that one could practise smarana without sankirtana.

Other examples were Nityananda Prabhu’s descendents and Advaita Acarya’s rejected sons, who assigned the title Gosvami to “seminal” householder successor acaryas who could not control their senses.

Cakravarti Thakura refuted all these philosophers and philosophies. Moreover, he taught devotees living and studying in Vraja the proper Vaisnava and sastric conclusions and the means to defend them.

On one occasion Cakravarti Thakura entered into a public debate with scholars who could not accept Krsna’s paramour relation with the gopis as the highest eternal expression of divine love.

By quoting from Vaisnava scriptures, especially Srimad Bhagavatam, the acarya soundly defeated the argument that Krsna was married to the gopis, but some of his defeated opponents took a vow to kill him.

One day those evil-minded scholars, armed with knives, waited for Visvanatha at a place he would pass on parikrama, hut instead of the Thakura, they saw a charming young girl walk hy picking flowers.

The rogues asked, “Lali! Did you see Visvanatha come by this path?” “I saw him earlier,” she replied, “but he seems to have disappeared.” The girl’s sublime radiance distracted the men from their mission.

One of them asked, “Whose daughter are you and where do you live?” “I am Radha’s maidservant,” the girl answered. “She is now at Her in-laws’ house in Yavata. She sent me to pick flowers for Krsna.”

As soon as she had spoken these words, the girl disappeared, and in her place stood Cakravarti Thakura. The scholars then understood that living evidence of Krsna’s unwedded love stood before them.

The influence of Visvanatha Cakravarti Thakura’s personal presence— in both his suddha and sadhaka forms — transformed the scholars’ hearts. They fell at his feet and begged for his forgiveness.

The Ramanandi seet at Govindadji’s temple in Jaipur once seriously challenged the authority of the Gaudiya sampradaya. Visvanatha sent his disciple Baladeva Vidyabhusana to meet their challenge.

I will relate the details of this very important narration at the samadhi of Baladeva Vidyabhusana, situated just on the outskirts of Atalavana on the other side of Vrndavana village.

Cakravarti Thakura’s prolific literary output includes eighteen original titles and fourteen commentaries. Other titles are known to have been lost, but scholars suspect that there are more lostworks.

Cakravarti Thakura is well known as an empowered representative of Rupa Gosvami, and as the guardian and acarya of the middle period of Gaudiya Vaisnavism’s historical development.

He worshipped a small Deity of Krsna called Goknlananda. When he left this world in I708, his body was placed in samadhi at Visvanatha-kunja near the Yoga-pitha, where he had performed bhajana.

In 1951 the Thakura’s samadhi was moved to the compound of the Radha-Gokulananda temple, beside his worshipable acaryas Narottama Dasa Thakura and Ganga-narayana Cakravarti.

Srila Bhaktisiddhanta Sarasvati Thakura offers this beautiful prayer to Visvanatha,

*visvasya natha-rupo ’sau bhakti-vartma-pradarsanat*  
*bhakta-cakre varttitatvat cakravarty-akhyayabhavat*

**“Because he has shown the visva-vasis (residents of the universe) the path of bhakti, he is called ‘Visvanatha,’ and because he is situated amongst the cakra (circle) of devotees, he is called ‘Cakravarti.’”**

The next terraced house to the south represents the Vamsi-gopala temple, Srila Prabhupada’s first residence in Bhauma Vrndavana, where he lived from 1959 until 1962.

In moving to Vrndavana, Srila Prabhupada followed his predecessors. However, he went there not to retire hut to find the inspiration he needed to fulfil his guru’s order to preach in the West.

The Vamsi-gopala temple is just down the street from Radha-Gokulananda temple in the direction of Kesi-ghata and the Yamuna. For the followers of His Divine Grace, it is an historic place.

The temple is a small three-storey building with domes, pillars, and arches. A narrow set of stairs near the entrance leads to the roof and the solitary room where Srila Prabhupada once lived.

From the rooftop, on one side the river Yamuna is visible hardly a stone’s throw away, stretching out in both directions, and on the other, Vrndavana town with its countless temple domes and spires.

A metal grate covers the skylight over the inner courtyard, but there was an arrangement by which Srila Prabhupada could lower a bucket through the grate to the well below and draw water for bathing.

Here Srila Prabhupada would rise early and write by the light of a kerosene lamp, bathe at sunrise, then visit the temples of Vrndavana and return with groceries to cook lunch in his three-tiered cooker.

After a short rest he would continue to write until evening, when he would go out again to visit temples and sometimes sell his Back to Godhead serial, returning again to write well into the night.

On 17 September 1959, Purnima, Srila Prabhupada, then known as Abhay Caranaravinda or “Abhay Babu,” left the VamsI-gopala temple for Mathura. That evening he returned as Bhaktivedanta Swami.

Srila Prabhupada had taken the renounced order of life from Kesava Maharaja, fulfilling the order Bhaktisiddhanta Sarasvati had repeatedly given him in dreams to accept sannyasa.

His Divine Grace would regularly take the train to Delhi to print and distribute his Back to Godhead and, later, the first volume of Srimad-Bhagavatam, when the manuscript was ready.

One day, as Srila Prabhupada was visiting the Radha-Damodara temple while on parikrama, the proprietor of the temple, Gauracandra Gosvami, invited him to come and live there.

Srila Prabhupada accepted the proposal, and in time he moved from the VamsI-gopala temple to the eternal home of Jiva Gosvami and Rupa Gosvami. It would be more suitable for his writing and translating.

Srila Prabhupada’s poem, Vrndavana-bhajana, reflects his stay on the roof at the Vamsi-gopala temple:

**“I am sitting alone in Vrndavana-dhama. In this mood I am getting many realisations. I have my wife —**

**“Sons, daughters, grandsons, everything, but I have no money, so they are a fruitless glory. Krsna has shown me the naked form of material nature; by His strength it has all become tasteless to me today.**

**“*****Yasydham anugrhnami harisye tad-dhanam sanaih*****: ‘I gradually take away all the wealth of those upon whom I am merciful.’ How was I able to understand this mercy of the all-merciful?”**

**\* \* \* \***

The last terraced house represents the samadhi of Ganga-narayana Cakravarti, one of Narottama Dasa Thakura’s foremost disciples, who was born into a brahmana community critical of Narottama.

One day, two new initiates of Narottama — Hari-rama and Rama-krsna — were visiting Gamvila, the village of Ganga-narayana, who carefully studied the newly acquired effulgence of these two visitors.

The spiritual transformation in the two brothers was so profound that Ganga-narayana, the head of his community, was deeply affected. He decided on the spot that Thakura Mahasaya was his spiritual master.

Greatly troubled by his offences to Narottama, Ganga-narayana slept fitfully. But late at night the Thakura appeared in Ganga-narayana’s dream and told him to come to the Ganges in the morning.

Ganga-narayana Cakravarti awoke early, quickly bathed, and hurried to the Ganges where he found Narottama and his disciples sitting as though they had been waiting for Ganga-narayana to arrive.

The once proud brahmana offered his prostrated obeisances and surrendered himself to Thakura Mahasaya, who embraced Ganga- narayana and requested that he meet him at Kheturi for initiation.

Following his guru’s order, Ganga-narayana went to Kheturi on the appropriate day and, after being instructed by Narottama Dasa, received initiation and the personal empowerment of the Thakura.

In ecstasy Ganga-narayana Cakravarti fell at his guru’s feet. By the power of the diksa-mantras he had been given, all the truths of pure devotional service appeared within his heart.

Although Ganga-narayana was overjoyed at having found the ultimate goal of life, the ritualistic brahmana community he had been the leader of was dismayed at his “fall” into sentimentalism.

Those smartas generally worshipped forms of Durga. They considered Vaisnavas to be uneducated in Vedic truths and neglectful of the proper conduct the scriptures prescribed for the different varnas.

For them Ganga-narayana’s conversion was the final straw. They complained to the local king that “the sudra" Narottama had somehow mesmerised Ganga-narayana and then initiated him.

A conquering scholar (digvijaya-pandita) was visiting the court of the king. He suggested that they all go to Kheturi to challenge Narottama Dasa to a debate — when he lost, they could imprison him.

News of this plan reached Ganga-narayana Cakravarti who, along with Ramacandra Kaviraja, made a plan to intercept the king’s brahmana convoy before it reached Kheturi.

They dressed up as a potter and hetel nut vendor and set up shop in the village just next to Kheturi. As they sold their pots and hetel to the group of brahmanas travelling to Kheturi, they spoke in Sanskrit.

The brahmanas wondered how shopkeepers were able to speak perfect Sanskrit. Enquiring further from the two disguised devotees, they were drawn deeper and deeper into a debate on the Vedic conclusion.

In a short time the two “shopkeepers” had defeated all the false arguments of the brahmanas and the digvijaya. They then disclosed their identities as humble servants of Narottama Dasa Thakura.

The king scolded the ritualists: “If you can’t defeat two shopkeeper disciples of Narottama, how will you defeat Narottama himself?” Humiliated and speechless, the brahmanas returned home.

Merely to challenge a great devotee was in itself a great offence. That night Durga-devi appeared to the king and the brahmanas and told them, “Either you surrender to Narottama, or I will behead you all.”

The next day that caravan of brahmanas again headed for Kheturi. But this time they went in a humble mood, admitting their need for spiritual guidance to free them from their illusory conceptions.

Ganga-narayana Cakravarti would accompany Thakura Mahasaya on his preaching tours and pilgrimages to holy places. He also convinced Narottama not to give up his body on a funeral pyre.

On that ocassion, Narottama Dasa was feeling such separation from the already departed Ramacandra and Srinivasa that he wanted to immolate himself, hut Ganga-narayana pleaded with his guru.

“If you perform this act, the ritualistic brahmanas will say that you were atoning for the sins of initiating those of a higher caste. Why feed the fire of their misconceptions? That was not your mission.

“You have preached that anyone who is a pure Vaisnava can be a spiritual master, but hy this act you will appear compromised, and these brahmanas will blaspheme you, making life intolerable for us.”

With arguments such as these, Ganga-narayana Cakravarti pacified his spiritual master, who then returned to Kheturi and continued his mission until finally merging into the Padma, as already described.

Ganga-narayana’s remains were placed beside those of his spiritual master in the courtyard of Radha-Gokulananda, to be joined later by the remains of his spiritual great-grandson, Visvanatha Cakravarti.

Glorious is the forest of Chattravana, in which Krsna enacted wonderful pastimes, and more glorious still is the courtyard of Radha-Gokulananda, where so many wonderful Vaisnavas reside.

**\* \* \* \***

Facing Chattravana, on the opposite side of the *parikram-marga,* is the famous pastime forest called Kevarivana, which shelters two temples and several *samadhis* — beginning with Ramananda Raya’s.

As we will see as this pastime unfolds, Kevarivana got its name on the night Krsna triumphed over the hundred-hooded Kaliya serpent and banished him from Vrndavana to Ramanaka Island.

By the time Krsna had warmed up on Dvadasaditya-tila, it was dark. The Vraja-vasis had grown weak from hunger, thirst, and the anxiety of watching Krsna’s exploits in the coils of the great serpent.

They decided that it would be wise to camp out for the night, and so they sought out a place free from the fumes of the poisoned river where they could keep an eye on Krsna.

Some women feared that Kaliya might return, hut the men reassured them: “If he comes hack to take revenge, we will all fight him with clubs, sticks, and knives. We will again drive away the demon.”

The Vraja-vasis pleaded exhaustion, hut the truth of the matter was that they camped out with Krsna in order to pacify the anxiety they had felt earlier hy seeing Him held for so long in the coils of Kaliya.

We may wonder why the Vraja-vasis did not simply satisfy their hunger hy drinking milk from their cows. The answer is that they were afraid the milk may have been contaminated by Kaliya’s poison.

Krsna’s presence, however, was a soothing halm that drove away all discomfort, including hunger. With cowherds standing guard around them and their herds, the Vraja-vasis happily drifted off to sleep.

In that hot summer month, Kevarivana was like a tinder box, making it easy for a fire demon to try to kill Krsna and His kin. The fire he started eluded all efforts to extinguish it and quickly spread around.

Our acaryas say that the fire demon was either a loyal friend of Kaliya seeking revenge, or a follower of Kamsa dutifully carrying out his master’s ongoing campaign against Krsna and Balarama.

By the time the Vraja-vasis awoke, the fire had surrounded them. Unable to save either Krsna or themselves and feeling helpless at seeing their death draw nigh, they called out to Krsna.

The Vraja-vasis surrendered to Krsna spontaneously, remembering Garga Muni’s assurance, anen? sarva-durgani yuyam anjas tarisyatha: “By His power you will easily he able to cross over all obstacles.”

They assured one another, “Lord Narayana will surely empower this boy to save us.” Then, remembering that Balarama was also invested with supernatural power, they prayed to both boys.

“O Lord!” the Vraja-vasis cried. “We are Your true friends and devotees. Please protect us from this insurmountable fire of death. We can never give up Your lotus feet, which drive away all fear.”

Were the people of Vraja praying to Krsna out of fear of death? Yes and no! They were afraid of death, but not in the same way as conditioned souls. For them death meant being separated from Krsna.

The Vraja-vasis were liberated souls, detached from their bodies. Why would they fear death? Nonetheless, they were so attached to Krsna that they did fear death because it would take them away from Him.

The Vraja-vasis cried out, “O Lord! If we die in this fire, we will he separated from Your lotus feet. Even the thought of this is unbearable for us. Please save us so that we can continue to serve You.”

Krsna saw that for the Vraja-vasis the forest fire was like a cooling breeze compared to the blazing fire of impending separation from Him, so He opened His mouth and swallowed the cause of their pain.

The same expansion of Yogamaya that would protect Krsna from Indra’s devastating downpour on Giriraja, the samharika potency, also protected Krsna by swallowing the fire, unseen by others.

Suddenly the fire was gone. The Vraja-vasis looked around quizzically, then stared at one another in wonder. “Who put it out?” (ke Novari) they exclaimed. Since then the forest has been called Kevarivana.

On the southern end of Kevarivana is Davanala-kunda. It marks the place where the Vraja-vasis were surrounded by the ring of fire. Further north, just opposite the terraced houses, is Visrama kunda.

Visrama means “to rest.’’ After Krsna had consumed the forest fire, the Vraja-vasis went to this place. Now, truly exhausted from a day and night of harrowing events, they slept soundly.

While visiting Kevarivana, we naturally remember the forest fire that once threatened to consume each one of us, and how Krsna, through His representative, the spiritual master, has saved us. Let us sing —

*Samsara-davanala-lidha-loka-tranaya karunyu-ghandghanatvam*  
*praptasya kalyana-gunarnavasya vande guroh sri-caranaravindam*

**“The spiritual master receives benediction from the ocean of mercy.**

**“Just as the cloud pours water on the forest fire to extinguish it, so the spiritual master extinguishes the blazing fire of material existence. I offer respectful obeisances unto the lotus feet of my spiritual master.”**

Next to Visrama kunda, within a building containing four studio suites, are the samadhis of Srinivasa Acarya, Ramacandra Kaviraja, Hemalata Thakurani, and Gauridasa Pandita.

Fortunate are the devotees living in remembrance of such exalted saints who, by their teachings, example, and presence, extinguish the difficult — to — reach fire that blazes within the heart.

Of these four persons, Srinivasa Acarya is the most prominent and influential. After offering obeisances to his lotus feet, let us hear something about this embodiment of Lord Caitanya’s ecstasy.

Srinivasa’s parents were pure devotees, fully dedicated to Lord Caitanya, so much so that people renamed Gangadhara— Srinivasa’s father — Caitanya Dasa because of his absorption in Mahaprabhu.

Caitanya Dasa and his wife went to visit Caitanya Mahaprabhu in Puri, where the Lord affectionately received them and instructed His servant Govinda to take the couple to see Lord Jagannatha.

The couple was given a residence near the Lord’s quarters, and they lived there happily. But their hearts harboured an unfulfilled desire: they wanted a son who would serve Lord Caitanya’s mission.

The couple spoke to no one of their desire, hut Caitanya Mahaprabhu knew their hearts. One day, through Govinda, He told them that they would soon give birth to a great devotee named Srinivasa.

The Lord said,

**“Through Rupa and Sanatana I will manifest the bhakti-sastras, and through Srinivasa I will distribute them. Govinda! Tell Caitanya Dasa that they should return to their village at once.”**

Overjoyed to have received such a direct blessing, the couple returned to their home, Cakhandi. In time, as predicted, Caitanya Dasa’s wife Laksmipriya gave birth to a beautiful boy — Srinivasa.

**\* \* \* \***

The first words Srinivasa spoke were “Caitanya,” “Radha,” and “Krsna.” As a youth, Srinivasa studied all branches of knowledge, including religion, logic, poetry, and grammar.

His tutor, Dhananjaya Vidyavacaspati, declared that he had nothing more to teach Srinivasa and directed him to one of Lord Caitanya’s associates, Narahari Sarakara, who affectionately received the boy.

In Narahari’s association, ecstasy first appeared in Srinivasa. Thereafter, Narahari Sarakara sent the youth to Puri on a quest to find Caitanya Mahaprabhu and His eternal companions.

Unfortunately, his father’s passing and his family’s move to Jajigrama delayed Srinivasa’s trip to Jagannatha Puri for some time. By the time he finally reached Puri, Lord Caitanya had disappeared.

Fortunately, Caitanya Mahaprabhu is always the all-cognisant well-wisher of His devotees. He had arranged for Gadadhara Pandita to teach Srinivasa the truths of Srimad-Bhagavatam.

But once again, divine fate seemed to be teasing Srinivasa, for Gadadhara Pandita passed away from this world before he could transmit the teachings of Srimad-Bhagavatam to his student.

Srinivasa encountered obstacle after obstacle in cultivating Krsna consciousness, but his sense of loss nurtured his ecstasy, and the Lord’s associates in Puri recognised a special quality in him.

Devotees like Ramananda Raya and Sarvabhauma Bhattacarya saw a singular, intense ecstasy in Srinivasa Acarya. They identified this ecstasy with that of Caitanya Mahaprabhu (gaura — sakti).

Still, Srinivasa was perplexed about his mission in life. Wherever he turned, obstacles stood in his way. Finally, word arrived from Rupa and Sanatana: Srinivasa should come to Vrndavana-dhama.

Heartened, Srinivasa set out for Vraja, stopping along the way in Navadvipa, Khadadaha, and Varanasi to meet the Lord’s associates. But in Mathura he learned that Rupa and Sanatana had also passed away.

Again Srinivasa felt that fate was mocking him. But Rupa and Sanatana had requested Sri Jiva to take charge of Srinivasa, and so when he arrived in Vrndavana he was warmly welcomed.

Indeed, not long after his arrival, as foretold by Narahari Sarakara, Gopala Bhatta Gosvami initiated Srinivasa, and Sri Jiva began instructing him in Srimad-Bhagavatam and the Gosvamis’ books.

Narottama Dasa had already arrived in Vraja, and Duhkhi Krsna Dasa — later known as Syamananda — arrived not long alter Srinivasa. The three became fast friends and Sri Jiva’s prize students.

Indeed, Jiva Gosvami had very special plans for these three devotees. Jahnava-devi requested that the Gosvamis’ hooks he sent to Bengal, where devotion was strong but philosophical understanding weak.

Jiva Gosvami gradually prepared his students for that mission. They all wanted to stay in Vrndavana, hut they understood that the Lord had other plans and they were eager to he His instruments.

To ensure that the hooks — the treasure of the Gaudiya Vaisnavas and life work of the Gosvamis — were safe, Jiva Gosvami arranged for an armed escort and the best travel documents available.

The three saints left Vrndavana with heavy hearts and tearful eyes, hut also with the blessings of their gurus and the devotees of Vraja. They headed east without incident, until they reached Visnupura.

The king of Visnupura, Virahamvira — who employed highwaymen to rob travellers and fatten his treasury — came to know that three mendicants were carrying a “priceless treasure” through his kingdom.

The king ordered his men to acquire the treasure by any means. One night, as the devotees and their guards were fast asleep, the trunk of books was stolen and taken to Virahamvira’s palace.

When Srinivasa Acarya and the others awoke and saw that the hooks were gone, they fainted again and again and considered giving up their lives for having failed in their mission to Caitanya Mahaprabhu.

But Srinivasa rallied his friends and assured them that he would recover the hooks. He told the others to proceed to their assigned preaching areas and begin spreading Krsna consciousness.

The biography of Srinivasa Acarya, Prema-vilasa, describes in detail how Srinivasa recovered the books and, in the process, converted King Virahamvira, his court, and his entire kingdom to Vaisnavism.

This great victory broadcast Srinivasa’s name all over East India, and Virahamvira’s patronage gave Gaudiya Vaisnavism an unparalleled foothold from which to spread faster than it ever had before.

Srinivasa Acarya informed Jiva Gosvami that the books had been recovered and the thief transformed into a Vaisnava. The devotees of Vrndavana rejoiced at this news and praised Srinivasa’s greatness.

Visnupura transformed into Vaikuntha on earth, as the king made chanting Hare Krsna compulsory for all citizens. Srinivasa was served and worshipped as good as God, but his life’s mission still called.

From Visnupura, Srinivasa travelled to Jajigrama to visit his mother, then on to Srikhanda to see Narahari, who requested Srinivasa to marry. Finally he went to Katwa to meet the great Gadadhara Dasa.

After confidential talks in which Srinivasa was given blessings to marry, Gadadhara Dasa predicted, “One day you will taste the nectar of nama-sankirtana with the Lord and His intimate associates.”

By the arrangement of Raghunandana Thakura, Srinivasa Acarya married two chaste devotee women, Isvari and Gauranga-priya, who bore him three daughters and one son respectively.

Isvari’s daughters were named Hemalata, Krsna-priya, and Kancana; Gauranga-priya’s son was named Gati Govinda. They were all great Vaisnavas who would later give shelter to many conditioned souls.

In due course, Srinivasa Acarya became the most prominent spiritual figure in Bengal. His stature as an acarya, a devotional scholar, and a representative of Lord Caitanya was unquestioned and unchallenged.

As Srinivasa Acarya’s fame grew, the Gosvamis’ teachings were embraced and their books widely copied and distributed. Vaisnavism had re-entered the mainstream of Indian society.

Of Srinivasa Acarya’s many disciples, the foremost was Ramacandra Kaviraja. This was due in no small measure to his being extensively trained by Srinivasa’s most intimate friend, Narottama Dasa Thakura.

Another notable disciple of the acarya was Hemalata Thakurani. Widely recognised for her learning, leadership, and purity, she initiated many men and women who in turn became great devotees.

Having fulfilled the desire of Jiva Gosvami, Srinivasa triumphantly returned to Vrndavana with Ramacandra Kaviraja, met by a warm welcome from the Vaisnavas, including his spiritual master.

Gopala Bhatta Gosvami had planned to put Srinivasa in charge of Radha-ramana’s worship, but when he saw the greater importance of his disciple’s preaching, he ordered him to return to Bengal instead.

Syamananda Prabhu also arrived in Vrndavana at that time, and the two friends resumed their studies under Jiva Gosvami, worshipping Vrndavana’s Deities and revelling in its sublime atmosphere.

The devotees of Vraja could readily see Srinivasa’s spiritual achievement and his blossoming ecstasies, which they attributed to the special mercy Lord Caitanya awards to those who preach.

After several months, Jiva Gosvami and Gopala Bhatta Gosvami reminded Srinivasa of his unfinished mission in Bengal. Srinivasa Acarya left Vrndavana once again to serve the order of his gurus.

Dear readers! Please consult books like Prema-vilasa, Bhakti-ratnakara, and Vaisnava-abhidhana, which contain more details of the pastimes of this extraordinary soul, Srinivasa Acarya.

Certainly Srinivasa Acarya’s most striking achievement is the way he transformed the kingdom of Visnupura into a kingdom of Vaisnavas, setting the template for how to make the world Krsna conscious.

Srila Prabhupada’s desire was no less than to make states, countries, and ultimately the entire world Krsna conscious. In Visnupura, Srinivasa Acarya showed by example how this was indeed possible.

The head of state, King Virahamvira, was transformed into a pure Vaisnava. The king’s great devotion spread into every corner of the kingdom and every facet of his subjects’ lives.

He would consult Srinivasa Acarya on all matters, transcendental and practical. Even in political and legal decisions his judgements reflected the truths of Vedic scriptures, chiefly Srimad-Bhagavatam.

As already mentioned, chanting the maha-mantra a prescribed number of times each day was compulsory for all citizens. To not chant was a punishable offence, as was performing animal sacrifice.

The citizens observed national holidays on important Vaisnava festival days, and temples were constructed throughout the land, even in primitive tribal areas. Vaisnavism thus became the way of life.

Consequently, the citizens of Visnupura were Krsna conscious. Their conduct was virtuous, humble, and courteous; in short, we could say that they were pure in heart and deed. Visnupura was an ideal state.

Srinivasa Acarya was revered throughout Visnupura as the spiritual authority for the state (the raja-guru), but he never lost his humble demeanour as an instrument of the previous acaryas.

He was worshipped at every festival, offered all manner of comforts, and was personally attended to by the king. But Srinivasa was content to live in a straw hut and distribute his wealth to the Vaisnavas.

Even more impressive was the effect Srinivasa and Virahamvira had on future generations of Visnupura’s rulers, who not only maintained the integrity of the Vaisnava government but also expanded it.

The influence of Visnupura’s kings was so powerful that even today its citizens are accustomed to make offerings of food to Lord Caitanya in the name of the king, at home and on occasions of public worship.

Srinivasa Acarya’s literary output was small: five songs and a commentary on the four seed verses of Srimad-Bhagavatam. His main service was to spread the knowledge propagated by the acaryas.

Of the songs he composed, Sad-gosvamy-astakam is a favourite amongst Gaudiya Vaisnavas. Srila Prabhupada sang it often and quoted from it profusely in his books, lectures, and conversations.

In a lecture on Srimad-Bhagavatam, for example, Srila Prabhupada quoted the eighth verse of that bhajana to expose those who claim to already see Krsna, while the Gosvamis aspired to see Him.

**“Actually, our worship should be in separation. The Gosvamis also taught this mood... .They were also searching after Krsna. They never said, ‘We have got Krsna.’...They were lamenting in separation.’’**

Srinivasa Acarya’s departure from this world was precipitated by the shocking disappearance of Ramacandra Kaviraja during the latter’s visit to Vrndavana. As a result, Srinivasa simply lost his will to live.

How intimate is the relationship between spiritual master and disciple that one literally cannot live without the other! The samadhis of both saints lie in a quiet courtyard called Srinivasa-kunja.

**\* \* \* \***

Ramacandra Kaviraja’s father was Ciranjiva of Sri Khanda. The Caitanya-caritamrta describes Ciranjiva as the fortyfirst branch of the Caitanya tree. In Krsna’s pastimes he was Candrika-gopi.

Ciranjiva had two sons, Ramacandra and Govinda. Both were disciples of Srinivasa Acarya, and both were awarded the honorific title of “Kaviraja” by Jiva Gosvami in Vrndavana.

We heard about Ramacandra Kaviraja in relation to the lives of Narottama Dasa Thakura and Srinivasa Acarya; in truth, Ramacandra was the prized treasure of both his friend and his guru.

Govinda Kaviraja, younger than his brother Ramacandra, writes of the two friends,

*jaya re jaya re jaya thakura narottama prema-bhakti-maharaja*  
*yakara mantri abhinna kalevara ramacandra kaviraja*

**“All glories, all glories to Thakura Narottama, the great king of pure loving devotion; his intimate friend and unfailing counselor Ramacandra Kaviraja is veritably his second body.”**

In his songs, Narottama Dasa Thakura repeatedly proclaims his deep affection for Ramacandra Kaviraja; in this way, he stresses the importance of intimate friendships in Krsna consciousness.

The acarya writes,

**“I desire the association of Ramacandra Kaviraja, without whose company the entire world is a desert. If I must take birth again, I would feel myself fortunate to have his friendship.”**

Here Narottama praises not only devotional friendships but also the practice of keeping company with pure devotees, especially those who are as deeply devoted to their gurus as Ramacandra was to Srinivasa.

It is said that Srinivasa Acarya embodied Lord Caitanya’s ecstasy, Narottama Dasa Thakura manifested Lord Nityananda’s potency, and Ramacandra Kaviraja personified (Gadadhara Pandita's bliss.

As is common with great souls, Ramacandra’s character and appearance were so extraordinary that the people of his village would talk of him as a descended demigod or the incarnation of a deity.

Learned Vaisnavas also wondered whether Ramacandra, like Nimai Pandita, was concealing a depth of devotion that might blossom in the company of a pure devotee. They were absolutely right.

Ramacandra’s meeting with Srinivasa was like dry tinder coming in contact with a flame; devotional ecstasy erupted in Ramacandra. But because he was so exalted, he remained a humble servant of his guru.

Ramacandra visited Vraja and toured its twelve forests, visiting Govardhana and Radha-kunda. Upon seeing the Deities of Vrndavana, he would often display varied symptoms of ecstasy.

Jiva Gosvami would take great pleasure in watching Ramacandra assist Srinivasa Acarya in his service to Gopala Bhatta Gosvami. The eternal truth of the disciplic succession was appearing before his eyes.

In Vraja, Ramacandra Kaviraja once dreamt of Lord Caitanya. Dressed as a brahmana, a long fragrant garland on His chest, the Lord had a golden complexion, black silky hair, and a divine aura.

Ramacandra Kaviraja fell at the Lord’s feet. Caitanya Mahaprabhu embraced him and said, “O Kaviraja! By My desire you are unaware of your real identity, but you are as dear to me as Narottama Dasa.

“Dedicate yourself to spreading the science of pure devotion throughout the land. Join Narottama in rescuing men from sin and suffering.” Then Caitanya Mahaprabhu disappeared.

That same night Lord Caitanya appeared to Srinivasa and told him of the orders He had given to Ramacandra. The acarya awoke, amazed at how the Lord had favoured his disciple.

After that incident, Narottama and Ramacandra were inseparable. They were like one soul in two bodies. People spoke their names in one breath, in just the same way as they would say “Nara-Narayana.”

Srinivasa Acarya was never envious of his disciple’s great devotion to his best friend; rather, he encouraged it, for he knew that their friendship was sanctioned by Caitanya Mahaprabhu.

Ramacandra Kaviraja and Narottama Dasa Thakura ate and bathed together, performed sankirtana together, preached from village to village together, and tasted the ecstasies of devotion together.

As they fulfilled the order of Lord Caitanya, they relished within themselves service to the divine couple as described in their literary works, which Vaisnavas today revere as good as scripture.

One day in Kheturi, Narottama Dasa held a wonderful kirtana with his disciples and then told them all to return home and attend to their household duties. Only Ramacandra stayed behind.

As they sat together in a secluded place, the two exchanged some very confidential words. Ramacandra then returned to Jajigrama, looking pensive. Clearly, his mind was disturbed about something.

Some days later a message arrived in Kheturi with the news that Srinivasa and Ramacandra had gone to Vrndavana. From that day Narottama Dasa began to withdraw from the outside world.

Some months later the news arrived: Ramacandra had left this world, and Srinivasa Acarya had departed only days later. Srinivasa was laid to rest in the area of Dhira-samira, beside Ramacandra.

We have already heard about Narottama Dasa’s grief and subscquent determination to quit this world in order to regain the company of his beloved friends. Such are the bonds of love amongst pure Vaisnavas.

Narottama sings of this love,

*doya koro sri-acarya prabhu srinivasa ramacandra-sanga mage narottama-dasa*

**“O Srinivasa! Please he merciful to me. Narottama prays for the company of Ramacandra.”**

In Vraja, a third person shares the samadhi in Srinivasa-kunja, along with Srinivasa Acarya and Ramacandra Kaviraja: the distinguished Vaisnavi and daughter of Srinivasa, Hemalata Thakurani.

As a daughter tends to inherit the qualities of her father, so Hemalata followed in the footsteps of her great saintly father; she was like a golden creeper of love for Lord Caitanya and the divine couple.

With the blessings of her father, Hemalata became a spiritual master and initiated many devotees, including the poet Yadunandana Dasa, who on her order wrote a biography of Srinivasa called Karnananda.

Yadunandana writes that Hemalata took him into confidence and revealed the glories of Ramacandra; later, she told him how Srinivasa had made Ramacandra Kaviraja the tutor of Virahamvira.

Srinivasa Acarya gave his daughter’s hand in marriage to Gopijanavallabha, another one of Srinivasa’s disciples. Husband and wife were both pure devotees of the Lord, beyond the modes of material nature.

Little information is available about Hemalata Thakurani, also called Sri Isvari. Let us offer our obeisances at her lotus feet and pray to her for pure devotion to the lotus feet of Radha and Syama.

**\* \* \* \***

The last samadhi here belongs to Gauridasa Pandita, Suryadasa Sarakhela’s younger brother, the emblem of loving devotional service in friendship, and the cowherd boy Subala in Krsna’s pastimes.

The birthplace of Gauridasa Pandita is in the village of Saligrama in East Bengal. Once a large palace, that sacred spot has now fallen into ruin, overgrown with vegetation on the edge of a forest.

Gauridasa Pandita was a recluse. He took permission from his elder brother to move further down the river Ganges near the town of Ambika-kalana, across the river from Santipura.

From Santipura, Caitanya Mahaprabhu once crossed the river by boat to visit Gauridasa at Ambika-kalana. Upon arriving, He playfully handed the Pandita an oar and said, “I crossed the Ganges with this.

“I am giving you this oar, for with it mankind can cross the ocean of this material world.” The Lord and His devotee then embraced. That oar can still be seen at the temple-residence of Gauridasa Pandita.

This exchange took place beneath a large tamarind tree that still stands today. Afterwards, Lord Caitanya took Gauridasa with Him back to Navadvipa and submerged him in the nectar of sankirtana.

It is said that Gauridasa owned a copy of the Bhagavad-gita in Caitanya Mahaprabhu’s own handwriting. The Pandita would regularly read it in great bliss, caressing the letters with affection.

Lord Caitanya and Lord Nityananda were everything to Gauridasa Pandita: his life and soul, the light of his eyes, the only absorption of his mind. Their names and praises always danced upon his tongue.

On another occasion, Lord Caitanya told Gauridasa to carve Deities of Him and Nitai from a neem tree. The Pandita protested, “I am not a craftsman or an artist. How could I make a suitable likeness of You?”

Lord Caitanya smiled and replied, “Do not worry! You will have no problem carving the Deities.” As Gauridasa began to carve the wood, the Deities manifested Themselves without much effort on his part.

Gauridasa was beside himself with bliss when the Deities were coni' plete. He organised an elaborate installation ceremony and dedicated his life to worshipping Their Lordships with love.

Like a few other great souls, Gaurldasa directly saw the two Lords within the Deities. He made no distinction between Gaura-Nitai and the Deities of Gaura-Nitai. He spoke to Them and They spoke to him.

One day the Deities said, “O Subala! O friend! Can you remember your previous life and the great fun we had together taking the cows to the bank of the Yamuna, where we played and swam?”

Gaurldasa was surprised to hear this, but he was even more surprised to see golden Gaura-Nitai transformed into black and fair Krsna and Balarama, dressed and ornamented like gopas with flutes and horns.

Both the worshipper and the worshipped were constantly absorbed in such pastimes of love, often losing track of time. Only after Lord Caitanya left this world did life change for Gaurldasa.

The Pandita still lived with his Deities, but he strongly felt the absence of Gaura and Nitai. Placing the Deities in his disciples’ care, he travelled to Vrndavana, hoping that Vraja would ease his pain.

Gaurldasa took up residence on the banks of the Yamuna by Dhira- samira, worshipped the Deity of Syama-raya, and absorbed himself in thoughts of Krsna and Balarama through nama-sankirtana.

On the bright fortnight of the month of Sravana, Gaurldasa Pandita left this world to reenter the pastimes of Krsna and Balarama in Vraja and Gaura and Nitai in Navadvipa. Gaurldasa Pandita ki jaya!

Just west of these four samadhis is the samadhi of Baladeva Vidyabhusana, the great eighteenth-century Vaisnava scholar, to whom Srila Prabhupada dedicated his Bhagavad-gita As It Is.

Baladeva Vidyabhusana was horn near the famous town of Remuna in Orissa. After his initial schooling in Sanskrit, rhetoric, and logic, he left home to visit places of pilgrimage and to hear from saints.

After some time he settled down in a Tattvavadi temple to assimilate the teachings of Madhvacarya. His studies complete, he accepted the renounced order and travelled and preached throughout India.

Baladeva came to Jagannatha Puri, where he met a disciple of Rasikananda Prabhu, Pandita Radha-Damodara, who infused Baladeva with the glories and teachings of Caitanya Mahaprabhu.

Baladeva was exceedingly moved. Accepting initiation from Radha- Damodara, he studied Jiva Gosvamis Sandarbhas from his guru, quickly mastering Gaudiya Vaisnavism’s philosophical conclusions.

Pandita Radha-Damodara instructed Baladeva to travel to Vrndavana and study Srimad-Bhagavatam under Visvanatha Cakravarti Thakura, the acknowledged leader of the Gaudiya Vaisnavas.

After touring the places of Lord Caitanya’s pastimes in Navadvipa, Baladeva travelled to Vrndavana. There he took shelter of Cakravarti Thakura and first learned the principles of acintya-bhedabheda.

This philosophical thesis appealed to Baladeva’s logical intellect. He understood that Lord Caitanya’s teachings, as presented by His followers, constituted the pinnacle of theistic thought.

Baladeva was a great devotee, a prolific author, and an empowered preacher, but his most significant and lasting contribution to our spiritual heritage was his defeat of the Ramananda-vadis of Galita.

These scholars of Galita, near Jaipur, insisted that there were only four authorised sampradayas, and since Gaudiya Vaisnavism was not a sampradaya, its priests were not fit to worship Govindadji.

The Ramanandis complained to the king. Although the king was a Gaudiya he had to accede to the request that the Gaudiya Vaisnavas either prove their authenticity or relinquish their service to Govinda.

The king wrote to Visvanatha Cakravarti Thakura and asked him to answer the challenge of Galita’s scholars. The acarya was too old and infirm to travel, however, so he sent Baladeva in his stead.

The Ramanandis had put more into question than whether or not the Gaudiyas could worship Govindadji. Their true intention was to challenge the authenticity of Lord Caitanya’s followers.

In due time Baladeva arrived at the king’s court and entered into an animated debate with the scholars; ultimately, none of them could stand before his impressive scholarship and keen intellect.

But the Ramanandis’ key argument was that without a commentary on the Vedanta-sutra the Gaudiyas had no systematic philosophy, and no bona fide disciplic succession lacks a codified philsosophy.

Baladeva explained that Lord Caitanya accepted Srimad-Bhagavatam as the natural commentary on Vedanta-sutra by its author, but the scholars insisted that the Gaudiyas must have their own commentary.

Adamant on this point and refusing to accept Baladeva’s answer, the brahmanas insisted that he produce a commentary on the Vedanta-sutra, the Upanisads, and the Bhagavad-gita within three months.

Baladeva was competent to write these commentaries, hut the deadline given by the panditas was unreasonable. It was unlikely he would finish in time, but he had no alternative.

Baladeva’s only recourse was the Lord Himself: he went before Govindadji, related all that had happened, and prayed for the Lord’s intercession. That night Govindadji appeared to Baladeva in a dream.

The Lord smiled benignly at His devotee and said, “Do not worry! Just write the commentary. I will guide you. By My blessings the work will be not only completed in time but also faultless.”

The next morning Baladeva requested writing material and reference books from the king, first taking up the commentary on Vedanta-sutra, then the Upanisads, and finally the Bhagavad-gita.

At the conclusion of the three months Baladeva placed his books before an incredulous audience of his adversaries, who could do nothing but eulogise Baladeva after reviewing what he had done.

Baladeva had proven that the Gaudiyas’ philosophical conclusion, acintya-bhedabheda, was the synthesis of other sampradayas’ views, establishing Gaudiya Vaisnavism as a sampradaya in its own right.

The king granted the Gaudiya Vaisnavas exclusive rights to the service of Govindadji. By the grace of Baladeva Vidyabhusana and Govindadji, they continue to enjoy that honour to the present day.

The king was so impressed by Baladeva’s achievement that he gave the saint the honorific title “Vidyabhusana.” It is thought that prior to this time the acaryas name had actually been Damodara Vipra.

Baladeva Vidyabhusana received a hero’s welcome in Vrndavana. He was awarded the stewardship of Radha-Syamasundara’s temple, a service that he performed up to his last breath.

The acarya is thought to have departed from the world around 1768, although he continues to live in his voluminous writings. His samadhi lies in a secluded part of Syamasundara,s The temple compound.

**\* \* \* \***

Just south of Baladeva Vidyabhusana’s samadhi in New Vraja-dhama is the danta-samadhi of Gadadhara Pandu a. In Bhauma Vrndavana it contains only the tooth of Lord Caitanya’s intimate associate.

Gadadhara Pandita was horn a year after Caitanya Mahaprabhu. The two were inseparable friends, playing together, attending ihe same school, and living just a stone’s throw from each other.

Nimai was literally the life and soul of Gadadhara. When Nimai first displayed symptoms of ecstatic love after returning from Gaya, Gadadhara — an incarnation of Radha — also revealed his divinity.

Unaware that Caitanya Mahaprabhu had left Navadvipa to accept sannyasa, Gadadhara did not go with the Lord. But soon thereafter the Pandita also accepted sannyasa and joined the Lord in Puri.

Lord Caitanya found the ancient Deity of Gopinatha in a sand dune (Cataka-parvata). He requested Gadadhara to accept ksetra-sannyasa — to never leave Puri — in order to worship Gopinatha.

Gadadhara Pandita accepted initiation from the great Vaisnava Pundarika Vidyanidhi, who in Krsna’s pastimes was Maharaja Vrsabhanu, the King of Varsana and the father of Srimati Radharani.

Along with worshipping Tota-gopinatha, Lord Caitanya requested Gadadhara Pandita to recite Srimad-Bhagavatam regularly and to instruct others, like Raghunatha Bhatta, in that devotional art.

After Lord Caitanya’s disappearance, Gadadhara Pandita found the separation unbearable. In fact, it is said that he aged so quickly that Tota-gopinatha sat down to facilitate Gadadhara’s service to Him.

Others say that the Deity was always in a seated position, and yet others say that at the end of that year, unable to tolerate his misery, Gadadhara Pandita left the world hy entering the body of Gopinatha.

One of Gadadhara Pandita’s disciples, Nayanananda, brought a tooth that had belonged to the Lord’s eternal associate to Vrndavana, where he made a samadhi for it near the Vamsi-gopala temple.

Thakura Bhaktivinoda sings of Gadadhara Pandita, smara gaura-gadadhara-keli-kalam bhava gaura-gadadhara-paksa-carah / srnu gaura-gadadhara-caru-katham bhaja godruma-kanana-kunja-vidhum:

“Remember the playful pastimes of Gaura-Gadadhara, become a follower of Gaura-Gadadhara, listen to the captivating stories of Gaura-Gadadhara, and worship the moon of Godruma’s bowers.”

Opposite the danta-samadhi is the temple of Davanala-bihari. Krsna stood there as He swallowed the forest fire. Nearby is Davanala-kunda, where the fire actually broke out and raged out of control.

Thakura Bhaktivinoda says that this forest fire represents quarrelling between Vaisnavas, amongst groups of Vaisnavas, and amongst various Vaisnava sampradayas, all of which disturb the Vraja mood.

Sectarianism is a serious obstacle to advancement in Krsna consciousness. By its influence we become unable to accept anyone who thinks or acts differently than we do.

This makes it difficult to associate with devotees, to accept authority, and to live together peacefully in devotee communities, leading to offences and causing our devotional creepers to wither.

Krsna thus swallowed the forest fire to save us from the dangers of sectarianism. We can take a little earth from Davanala-kunda and keep it beside a picture of Davanala-bihari to remind us of this truth.

In the southwestern corner of New Vraja-dhama’s Kevarivana is the samadhi of Bhugarbha Gosvami. We heard something about him at the samadhi of Lokanatha, as the two were inseparable friends.

To avoid distractions to his bhajana, Bhugarbha would often live in an underground (blue) cave (garbha), thus fulfilling the meaning of his name. Kavi-karnapura says he was the gopi Prema-manjari.

Bhugarbha Gosvami was a disciple of Gadadhara Pandita. Vaisnava authors, including Srila Prabhupada, frequently comment on Bhugarbha’s intimate lifelong friendship with Lokanatha Gosvami.

Bhugarbha Gosvami was always searching the forests of Vraja for the places of Krsna’s pastimes, and so he often performed his bhajana in the caves and groves scattered throughout Krsna’s land.

After others arrived to assist with the revival of Vraja, he could be found mostly in Khadiravana, Radha-kunda, or Vrndavana, absorbed in nama-sankirtana or discussing topics of Lord Caitanya.

Bhugarbha Gosvami was also one of the devotees of Vrndavana who accompanied Rupa Gosvami to see and serve Gopala while the Deity resided at the house of Viththalesvara in Mathura.

In Vrndavana he would sometimes watch with great satisfaction as Gopala Bhatta worshipped Radha-ramana. At other times he would watch his disciple Caitanya Dasa carefully worship Govindadji.

Vaisnava texts speak little of his later life, and so the date of Bhugarbha Gosvami’s disappearance is unknown. Jiva mentions his leaving, however, in this letter to Srinivasa Acarya:

**“Glory to Sri Krsna, the master of Vraja! Greetings to Srinivasa Acarya, whose feet bring all happiness to one with the name Jiva, who offers obeisances and discloses the following news:**

**“I always wish you well. For many days we have not heard news of you, who are the source of our great happiness. As for us here in Vrndavana, at present we are all in good health.**

**“The only exception is Bhugarbha Gosvami, who surrendered himself, body and soul before Lord Krsna, Vraja’s master. Please write to us about Sri Vrndavana Dasa. Does he read yet, or not?**

**“Also, the editing of Bhakti-rasamrta-sindhu, Madhava-mahotsava, the second part of Sri Gopala-campu, and Hari-namamrta-vyakarana are not yet complete. They will not be ready this year.**

**“If the Lord is favourable, I will finish them eventually. Everyone here offers respectful obeisances to all of you there. Please also give my blessings to the saintly king.”**

Bhugarbha Gosvami ki jaya!

On the other side of Davanala-kunda is the samadhi of the great soul Sivananda Sena, a resident of Kumarahatta and the father of Paramananda Sena, also known as Puri Dasa or Kavi-karnapura.

Kavi-karnapura writes about Sivananda Sena:

**“The two gopis Vira — devi and Duti-devi, who acted as messengers to arrange the gopis' meetings with Lord Krsna, combined to appear as my father.”**

Sivananda Sena was very dear to Caitanya Mahaprabhu and to the devotees of Navadvipa. Every year he would organise and finance their trip to see the Lord and the Ratha-yatra festival in Puri.

Sivananda Sena was involved in many adventures: he was kicked and cursed by Lord Nityananda and he became attached to a dog, an animal that returned to Vaikuntha by Lord Caitanya’s mercy.

Sivananda Sena was an exemplary grhastha. He took proper care not only of his own family but also of the neighbourhood children, such as Jagadananda, who was later known as Pandita.

In this regard, Jagadananda Pandita writes,

**“All glory to Sri Sivananda Sena, the father of Sri Kavi-karnapura. In my early years he taught me Bhagavad-gita and Srimad-Bhagavatam.**

**“He took me to Nadiya and placed me at the lotus feet of Lord Gauranga. Srila Sivananda Sena has always been my saviour and mentor in times of happiness and distress.**

**“In his house he taught me to cook and allowed me to prepare offerings for the Deities. He taught me well so that I could cook for my Lord, Sri Gauranga, and satisfy Him with my service.”**

From talks held in the house of Sivananda Sena, Kavi-karnapura collected much of the material for his Gaura-ganoddesa-dipika, which reveals the eternal identities of Lord Caitanya’s associates.

Kavi-karnapura sometimes quotes Sivananda Sena when he identifies certain associates — like Lord Nityananda’s identity as Balarama — or when he corrects devotees’ mistaken conclusions. For example:
