**“The vraja-brahmani Nandimukhi-devi appeared as Saranga Thakura. Some people think he was the incarnation of Prahlada Maharaja, but my father \[Sivananda Sena\] disagreed with them.”**

Sivananda Sena left this world on the second day of the bright fortnight of Asadha. Thakura Bhaktivinoda says, “Just remember Kavi-karnapura and his family, especially his father, Sivananda Sena!”

Certainly Sivananda Sena was special. When he and his family were in Puri, Lord Caitanya ordered Govinda to daily give them the remnants of His food. No one else received that favour from the Lord.

**\* \* \* \***

The *parikrama* path around Vrndavana village turns west along the southern side of Keviirivana. Bui I Mimed east so that I could visit Vrndavana forest and the pasi imes \[daces along the southern petal.

There are many temples, samadhis, and holy places in this area, of which I will mention hut a few, beginning with the garden called Pe that is commemorated just south of Sivananda Sena’s samadhi.

One day, as Krsna and His friends were herding their cows in that area, the boys became thirsty from the intensity of their play and the heat of the midday sun. They looked for water, but found none.

Everywhere in Vraja are crystal clear lakes or quickly flowing streams, but this area appeared to be different. Although verdant, it lacked water, which only increased the intensity of the boys’ thirst.

Just as the gopas were melodramatically discussing how to keep from dying of thirst, they saw a gopi walking toward them carrying a pot on her head. Naturally they hoped the pot was filled with water.

The boys approached the young cowherd girl and begged her for water, but she replied, “This is not water, it’s yogurt! But if you think it will satisfy your thirst, then you’re welcome to it.”

The boys accepted the gopi’s offer, praising her for her charitable disposition. Each boy drank a little and then passed the pot to the next. No matter how much they drank, there was still more.

Subala stared into the full pot of yogurt. “How is this happening after so many of us have drunk?” he thought. But the gopi urged him on, saying, “Aur piyo! Aur piyo!" (Drink more! Drink more!) And he did.

When Subala finished, he gave the pot to Sridama, who also drank as much as he could. As the pot went around to hundreds of boys, the gopi kept saying, “Aur piyo! Aur piyo!” But the pot remained full.

The boys started chattering amongst themselves. “What’s going on here!” “Is she some kind of white witch?” “Maybe this is another one of Krsna’s tricks.” Now all eyes fell on Gopala.

But Krsna had just finished drinking. With yogurt smeared all over His mouth and cheeks, He gazed at His friends with a completely sweet and innocent smile, saying, “Aur piyo! Aur piyo!”

Intoxicated hy the elixir of their love for Krsna, mixed with the beauty of His face, they too called out, “Aur piyo! Aur piyo!” But drink as they might, the pot remained full.

As soon as all the gopas had drunk to their satisfaction, the gopi disappeared with her jug. The boys, wide-eyed, laughed and laughed as they jumped up and down and shouted, “Aur piyo! Aur piyo!”

Even today the shouting cowherd boys can be heard there. The gopas’ cries — “Aur piyo! Aur piyo!” — sound like “Pe! Pe!” as they reverberate through the trees. And so this place is know as Pe.

East of Pe is Viharavana, the forest of pleasure. Krsna would meet with Srimati Radharani in this secluded forest made lush by flowers, vines, and groves, while the gopas herded the cows nearby.

Krsna once came here alone, but Radha was nowhere to be found. He sat on the bank of Vihara-kunda and played His flute until the gopis were forcibly drawn into His presence, despite their resistance.

Why Radha did not want to meet with Krsna was a mystery. But once She saw Krsna’s glowing sapphire form in this beautiful sylvan setting, Her reluctance melted into the waters of Vihara-kunda.

Radha whispered to Herself, “Pious girl! Your good deeds have reached perfection, for Krsna has appeared before Your eyes. The moon of His face made doubly refreshing by its sweet tenderness —

“His magic flute made doubly enchanting by the stream of nectar dripping from it note by note, You have been blessed to attain Your mind's goal even though You are slightly deranged.”

Srimati Radharani’s mind and senses were flooded with pleasure to see Krsna, and He was just as overjoyed to see Her. Because this forest sheltered such exchanges, it is called Viharavana.

Lord Nityananda stayed here when He visited Vrndavana. He remembered His pastimes as Balarama, herding cows with Krsna nearby, and Krsna’s pastimes with the gopis all over Vrndavana.

This is a prominent excellence of Lord Caitanya’s pastimes: nama- sankirtana makes Radha’s and Krsna’s pastimes accessible to devotees in any mellow, be it servitude, friendship, or parenthood.

This does not mean that such devotees’ constitutional positions change, but when they are in full absorption it does allow them to taste amorous love or other mellows as observers.

Caitanya-bhagavata describes, for example, that in Navadvipa Advaita Acarya once danced in the mood of a gopi. He entered that mood through nama-sankirtana, though He is always Sadasiva.

Nityananda Prabhu was so absorbed in Krsna’s pastimes while in Vrndavana that He was unaware of whether it was day or night. He forgot about eating, but if locals offered Him milk He would accept it.

Nitai knew that Lord Caitanya had already appeared in Navadvipa, but He decided to stay in Vraja, sporting in the Yamuna and playing with the Vraja-vasi children, until the Lord revealed Himself.

I bowed before Lord Nityananda’s sitting place and offered my favourite prayer to Him,

*nityanandam aham naumi sarvananda-karam param*  
*hari-nama-pradara devam avadhuta-siromanim*

**“I bow down to the Supreme Lord Nityananda Prabhu, who is the awarder of the highest joy to all, the presiding Deity of the holy name, and the crest jewel of all paramahamsa mendicants.”**

In the northwestern corner of Viharavana is a well that devotees know as Radha-kupa. Devotees on Parikrama meditate on their spiritual aspiration and loudly shout into the well, “Radhe-Syama!"

Off the main parikrama-marga, the path to Kosikala (Kosi) is especially tranquil. From its vantage point one gets an expansive view of Krsna Valley and the countryside beyond, as far as Lake Balaton.

To the right is the samadhi of Jagadananda Pandita, who would quarrel with Lord Caitanya every day, earning lor himself the nickname Kandali Jagai, “quarrelsome Jagai.”

A childhood friend and classmate of Nimai, Jagadananda was inseparable from the Lord, not unlike Gadadhara. But Jagadananda would often disturb the pleasant atmosphere amongst the devotees.

Jagadananda’s emotional upheavals were expressions of pure spiritual love of a left-wing nature, similar to those of Lalita-devi in Vrndavana or Queen Satyabhama in Dvaraka.

Indeed, Kavi-karnapura writes,

**“The person who appeared in Lord Caitanya’s pastimes as Jagadananda Pandita was actually an incarnation of Satyabhama-devi. What devotee can say otherwise?”**

Sri Caitanya-caritamrta repeatedly draws a parallel between the moods and identities of Satyabhama and Jagadananda.Moreover, the Pandita himself writes:

**“One day the Lord jokingly said to me, ‘You are the chief queen of Dvaraka, and I am just a poor beggar. Why do you serve Me? You can get so many hundreds of renunciants like Myself to serve.’**

**“I replied, ‘Give up Your pretension and deceit. Why do you call me a queen instead of a maidservant at the lotus feet of Vraja’s queen, Srimati Radharani? It is She who I am longing to serve.’**

**“I continued, ‘You have stolen the lustre of my dear mistress Radharani, hence, You are a thief, whom I shall now apprehend and send back to the land of Vraja for punishment.**

**“‘My desire is to serve Radha, but You want to send me away to Dvaraka so that you can hoard service to Her for Yourself. I can see through this deception, as well as Your sannyasa vows.’”**

Jagadananda’s fiery words and behaviour testify to the intense sentiments within his heart that obsessed his mind, transformed his body, and filled his eyes with constant streams of tears.

The Lord’s associates knew that whatever Jagadananda said and did was symptomatic of an extraordinary and unique love for Caitanya Mahaprabhu, before which they could only stand in awe.

Of the many dealings between Sri Caitanya and Jagadananda in Navadvipa, the pastimes that took place in the homes of Srivasa Pandita and Candrasekhara Acarya were most important.

After accepting sannyasa, Lord Caitanya returned to Santipura to meet with His associates. When He departed for Puri, Jagadananda Pandita was amongst the devotees who accompanied Him.

En route to Puri, Jagadananda begged alms at the homes of brahmanas, cooked the Lord’s meals, and shared the Lord’s remnants with His companions. Sometimes he also carried the Lord’s danda.

After Caitanya Mahaprabhu had settled in Puri, He assigned a residence to Jagadananda Pandita on Sadasana Road, where the Lord would regularly accept His lunch and tease the Pandita, as before.

Jagadananda Pandita once visited Vrndavana, staying with Sanatana Gosvami. On another occasion he returned to Navadvipa and stayed in the home of his mentor Sivananda Sena.

At that time Sivananda Sena collected a large quantity of sandalwood oil with which to massage and cool Lord Caitanya in the summer. He sent the oil in a pot with Jagadananda Pandita to Puri.

But Caitanya Mahaprabhu considered massages with sandalwood oil an inappropriate luxury for a sannyasi, and so He asked Jagadananda Pandita to offer the oil to Lord Jagannatha instead.

Jagadananda Pandita, furious at the Lord’s strict austerity, threw the pot to the ground, breaking it and spilling its valuable contents. He then locked himself in his room and fasted for three days in anger.

I have often sat in the courtyard of that house in Puri, remembering Jagadananda’s loving anger and Caitanya Mahaprabhu’s sweet ways of subduing it. Radha-Damodara came to me from that house.

I offer my obeisances to Jagadananda Pandita, who opened his home to me, allowing me to eat where Lord Caitanya had eaten, and who blessed me with the service of Radha-Damodara.

Narottama Dasa Thakura explains that associates of Gauranga like Jagadananda Pandita are not ordinary conditioned souls,

*gaurangera sangi-gane nitya-siddha kari’ mane, se yaya vrajendra-suta-pasa*

Indeed, Srila Prabhupada says that if we accept and understand the eternally liberated status of devotees like Jagadananda, then we “immediately become eligible to enter into the abode of Krsna.”

Let us pray that we may develop the kind of faith in Lord Caitanya’s associates that will qualify us to enter into and thus perceive the fully transcendental features of Radha’s and Krsna’s abode.

O Srila Prabhupada! You are the embodiment of all great Vaisnavas and acaryas. My abiding faith is that you are an eternal associate of Caitanya Mahaprabhu. Please deliver us from all duality.

*When Lord Balarāma returned from Dvārakā to Vraja He stopped His chariot in a village of cowherd men and asked, “What is the good news?” (khaira) Since that time that place is known as Khayero.*

## Chapter 23 - Kosikala to Marava

As I walked to Kosikala, I listened to the sounds of the forest. The rustling leaves, singing birds, whispering breeze, and humming insects sounded like an orchestra praising Krsna in concert.

To my right was the area of Phalain, home to Prahlada-kunda. This place, although not on the official Parikrama route, is well because of the annual Holi festival held there.

Prahlada-kunda is said to be the home of the great devotee Prahlada, and the place where the Holika-dahan festival — a precursor to the famous throwing of colours — is celebrated.

The Holika festival celebrates the death of Holika, Hiranyakasipu,s whom the terrible demon employed to kill his saint ly Prahlada simply because Prahlada was a devotee of Lord Visnu.

Narada Muni describes Prahlada Maharaja’s terrible ordeal hands of his diabolical father:

**“Hiranyakasipu could not kill his son by throwing him beneath the feet of big elephants —**

**“Throwing him amongst huge, fearful snakes, employing destructive spells, hurling him from the top of a hill, conjuring up illusory tricks, administering poison, starving him, \[or\] exposing him to severe cold.”**

Finally, the demon thought to burn Prahlada to ashes. On the night of the full moon in the month of Phalguna (Gaura-purnima) he had his servants construct a gigantic pyre around a wooden seat.

Hiranyakasipu wanted to make sure that Prahlada could not escape from the pyre, so he called on his sister Holika, who had received a boon from Brahma that she could never be burnt by fire.

Holika sat on the chair with Prahlada in her lap while the pyre was lit, igniting Hiranyakasipu’s hopes that his son’s non-violent challenge to his absolute authority would soon be burnt to ashes.

But Prahlada continually chanted Krsna’s names and emerged from the fire unscathed. Holika was consumed by the flames because her offence against her pure devotee nephew nullified Brahma’s boon.

As Hiranyakasipu and his demon cohorts looked on in disbelief, Prahlada Maharaja stood up and walked out of the blazing inferno, his clothes and body completely untouched by the flames.

Seeing this, Hiranyakasipu thought, “All my endeavours to kill Prahlada have failed. Somehow he saves himself by his own powers, without being affected at all by my machinations.’’

Hiranyakasipu was correct in assuming that his son could not be killed, but he was wrong in thinking that Prahlada possessed some kind of supernatural mystic power greater than his own.

Prahlada’s strength was his full dependence on Krsna and the chanting of Krsna’s names. In response to this full surrender, the unlimitedly powerful Lord would not allow Prahlada to be harmed.

This pastime is annually re-enacted in Phalain on the hanks of Prahlada-kunda. At night an effigy of Holika is burnt, and the next day revellers throw colours to celebrate Prahlada’s victory.

This story was current during Krsna’s time as well, and thus the Holi festival began with a celebration for the appearance of spring and ended with this festival commemorating the burning of Holika.

Prahlada Maharaja lives eternally on Lord Nrsimha’s Vaikuntha planet, but in Vraja, Krsna remembered Prahlada every year and celebrated His devotee’s strong faith in divine protection.

Later, Krsna also thought of Prahlada when He reassured Arjuna, kaunteya pratijanihi na me bhaktah pranasyati: “O son of Kunti, declare it boldly that My devotee never perishes."

In one of Vraja’s ancient ceremonies a local ascetic would enter into a trance — empowered by Prahlada Maharaja — and literally walk through fire, often a blaze more than fifty cubits from end to end!

I have never witnessed this fear, but reliable Vraja-vasis of Bhauma-Vrndavana told me that they have directly seen such a performance. No one should attempt such a thing in New Vraja-dhama, however.

Rather than imitate Prahlada’s resistance to fire, we should aspire for his level of devotion. As Lord Nrsimha says, “You are the best example of My devotee, and others should follow in your footsteps."

To achieve devotion as pure as Prahlada Maharaja’s is the greatest feat. The Lord says,

*yatra yatra ca mad-bhaktah prasantah sama-darsinah*  
*sadhavah samudacaras te puyante 'pi kikatah*

**“Whenever and wherever there are peaceful, equipoised devotees who are well behaved and decorated with all good qualities, that place and the dynasties there, even if condemned, are purified.”**

Kholaveca Sridhara’s samadhi also graces the area of Phalain. Srila Prabhupada calls Sridhara “a typical example of how a poor man with no material possessions can become a most exalted devotee.”

Kholaveca Sridhara was a poor brahmana who lived in Simantadvipa, Nadiya. He eked out a living by selling cups and plates made from banana leaves, as well as bananas, banana flowers, and banana pulp.

Despite having hardly any income, he spent fifty percent of his profit on worship of Mother Ganges and somehow lived off the rest. In this regard he was an exemplar for all householder devotees.

In Nimai Pandita s non-violent protest against the Kazi, Sridhara danced in bliss. And the Lord, who regularly visited Sridhara’s house, once drank water from the iron pot Sridhara used to wash his feet.

On the evening before Nimai left to accept sannyasa, He asked Mother Saci to cook a preparation called lau. It was Sridhara who provided the main ingredient: squash from his own garden.

Every year the devotees of Navadvipa would travel to Jagannatha Puri, and Sridhara was always present in that party. He was eager to see the Lord but unable to leave Navadvipa to join Him permanently.

Kavi-karnapura reveals Sridhara’s identity:

**“The cowherd boy known as Kusumasava in krsna — lila later became Kholaveca Sridhara during Caitanya Mahaprabhu’s pastimes at Navadvipa.”**

The next place of pastimes in the forest of Kosivana is Kosikala, or simply Kosi. It is one of Nanda Maharaja's principal granaries. Learned Vaisnavas accept Kosi as Dvaraka Puri manifest in Vraja.

Nanda Maharaja once desired to visit the sacred place known as Kusasthali on the bank of the Gomati River. Maharaja Kakudmi built this ancient kingdom’s capital city beneath the sea.

Maharaja Kakudmi’s daughter Revati, also called Raivata, later become the wife of Lord Balarama, and Kusasthali would eventually he the site at which Krsna constructed Dvaraka.

Krsna, as usual, was against His father’s plans to leave Vrndavana on pilgrimage; He did not want His father or the Vraja-vasis to think they needed to visit any sacred places outside Vraja for purification.

Krsna addressed Nandaraja: “O father! Why do we need to leave Vraja to see Kusasthali? Kusasthali and Dvaraka, the eternal abode of Lord Vasudeva, are always present in the area of your granary.”

Nanda recalled how Krsna had proven the Ganges was present by Govardhana, so when his son proposed that Kusasthali was also present in Vraja, he wanted to believe Him but needed proof.

Nanda Maharaja replied, “If what You say is true, I should see more than Kosivana. I should see the ocean, the Gomati River, and the holy city of Kusasthali. Then, and only then, I will believe You.”

Taking His father hy the hand, Krsna and I lis friends playfully led their elders to Kosivana, laughing and joking along the way. Upon arriving at the beautiful lake called Gomati-kunda, Krsna said:

“Dear father! Look. In this beautiful lake you will see Gomati-ghata and also the wonders of Kusast hali.” Some of Nanda’s friends nudged him and sarcastically said, "O Raja! What can you see?”

Krsna felt slighted hy the adults’ sarcasm. Emphatically He declared, “O King of Vraja! We can only see a holy place by its grace. Let us all bathe in Gomati-kunda and pray for its favour.”

Though doubtful, the Vraja-vasis’ were accustomed to humouring Krsna. They didn’t need to be persuaded. To satisfy their beloved little friend they put on bathing clothes and entered the lake.

The Vraja-vasis, after offering palmfuls of Gomati-kunda’s water to itself, did indeed see the wonders of Kusasthali: the ocean, Gomati-ghata, and the invincible, eternal residence of Vasudeva— Dvaraka.

Nandaraja returned to his capital and told his subjects what had happened. From that time on the Vraja-vasis accepted that a visit to Gomati-kunda and Kosikala was equal to a pilgrimage to Dvaraka.

The forest Kosivana got its name from a conversation Sri Radha once had with Krsna. She had locked Herself in a grove. When Krsna knocked on the door, She asked, “Who are you?” (ko ‘si).

A humorous exchange then took place between the divine couple.  
**Radha**: “Who taps on My door with one finger?”  
**Krsna**: “O crooked girl! Don’t You know? Madhava is at Your door.”  
**Radha**: “Madhava is a name of spring. Has springtime come knocking on My door?”  
**Krsna**: “Certainly not! I am the holder of the Sudarsana wheel.”  
**Radha**: “Potters hold wheels. Arc You a potter?”  
**Krsna**: “I am not! I am the person who holds up the entire world.”  
**Radha**: “The king of serpents Ananta holds up the world. Are You Ananta?”  
**Krsna**: “No! I am a person who crushes serpents.”  
**Radha**: “The king of birds, Garuda, crushes serpents. Are You Garuda?”  
**Krsna**: “No. I am Lord Hari.” Radha: “Hari is a name of the famous monkey Hanuman, the servant of Lord Rama. Are You he?”

An anonymous poet recorded this humorous banter. He prayed,

**“May Lord Krsna, who holds the Sudarsana cakra, and who smiled at these clever words from Srimati Radharani, protect us all.”**

**\* \* \* \***

From Kosivana I walked in the direction of Sesasayi through ancient Kotaravana, a favourite ??stime forest of the gopas. In Krsna’s time, the trees of that forest were so old that many were hollow (kotara).

Krsna would bring His cows there to graze, and the boys would hide from each other in the hollows of the trees. The-also played Holi there, painting the green foliage with a rainbow of colon is.

Once, after playing all morning in Kotaravana, the gopas had worked up an especially fierce appetite. But their lunch did not aktive on time. The boys waited and waited — still no sign of lunch.

Krsna sent a boy up a tree and called out, “Do you see them coming?” “No!” the boy cried and climbed down. The gopis conterred. What could they do to keep from starving? They were getting desperate.

Balarama advised, “Friends, let’s not despair. Just nearby is a village of pious souls. Why not go there and ask them lor some milk, to… us over? Surely they will be sympathetic to our plight.’’

Cheered by good counsel and the prospect of food, the boys tallied behind Krsna and Balarama and headed for the village now called Paya-grama, where, sure enough, the boys were given milk (…).

In a nearby corner of Kotaravana is Dadhi-grama. This place is well known because here Krsna once stole yogurt (dadhi) from the gopis to bring them pleasure and, once again, to feed His friends.

A deity of goddess Parvati was worshipped on a mound beside a lake here, and because the gopis would daily offer yogurt to her, she was known as Dadhi-devi, and the lake, Dadhi-kunda.

One day, as the gopis were bringing their offerings, Krsna and a group of gopas boldly blocked the way and demanded the girls’ yugurt. A gopi said, “Are you not afraid to steal from the gods? What a sin!

Krsna smiled and pressed His walking stick into the ground. “Foolish woman,” He said. “Don’t you know that Parvati, Sarasvati, and Kali are all My maidservants? The best way to serve them is to please Me!”

At this some of the gopis covered their ears while others shrieked in shocked disbelief. Taking advantage of the gopis' distraction, Krsna and the gopas plundered their pots of yogurt.

To prove His point, Krsna ran to the shelter of Dadhi-kunda. Right in front of Parvati-devi He distributed the yogurt to His friends and they happily ate. He then exclaimed, “Just see how pleased Devi is!”

Indeed, as the gopis looked at the deity of the goddess, they saw a smile distinctly growing upon her lips. Unwilling to trust their eyes and minds, however, the girls covered their faces with their veils.

On the western side of Kotaravana is Kharero, or Khayero. Lord Balarama, returning to Vraja from Dvaraka, stopped His chariot here and asked some cowherds, “What is the good news?” (khaira).

In Dvaraka, Lord Balarama had told Krsna of His strong desire to return to Their childhood home. He yearned to once again enjoy the friendship, affection, and love of the residents of Vrndavana.

Krsna also wanted to go, but the Yadus, out of fear that the ropes of Yasoda’s and the gopis’ affection would tie Him to Vraja, protested. In response to His relatives' love, Krsna decided to stay in Dvaraka.

Lord Balarama, aware that the Vraja-vasis were living in a scorching desert of misery without the soothing downpour of the Krsna rain cloud, sent a messenger to inform them of His imminent arrival.

The Lord anticipated that this news would hearten the Vraja-vasis, who naturally loved Him, and that they would also expect Him to be carrying letters from Krsna and news of Krsna’s activities in Dvaraka.

It was for this reason that Balarama asked the group of cowherds for good news. He expected them to say that the Vraja-vasis were blissfully making all kinds of preparations for His reception.

Cakravarti Thakura enjoins us to pray to the Lord,

*nityananda-svarupo ’pi prema-tapto vrajaukasam*  
*yayau krsnam api tyaktva yas tam ramam muhuh stumah*

**“Let us repeatedly glorify Lord Balarama.**

**“He is the original personality of eternal bliss, Nityananda, but still He felt pained by His love for the residents of Vraja, and thus He went to see them, even at the cost of leaving Lord Krsna.”**

South of Kotaravana is a dense forest called Bhulanvana. Some say it got its name because many have lost their way there over the years, others say it was so named because of the following pastime.

The gopas were once playing all kinds of games there, intoxicated by the opulences of the forest: its fruits, lakes, and groves. But even though the boys went wild with joy, Krsna was morose.

The boys ran here and there, but Krsna sat silently beneath a kadamba tree, lost in sad thought of Radharani — both because She was so far away, and because He had no prospect of meeting Her.

Noticing that Krsna was absent from their games, Subala sought Him out and found Him alone, sulking beneath a tree. “O friend!” Subala said, “Why are You lamenting instead of playing with Your friends?”

Krsna was unresponsive, but Subala sat beside Him, hung his arms over Krsna’s shoulders, and asked, “Friend! Tell me what is keeping You away from the bliss of our game, and from our love for You?”

Krsna nodded and said, “Yes, I will tell you. The truth is that when I remember Sri Radha, I forget where I am and what I am doing. I am incapable of playing or of reciprocating anyone’s affection.”

Subala rubbed Krsna’s shoulders and said cheerfully, “Radha is in Varsana and we are here. Just put thoughts of Her aside for now. Neither of us can do anything at the moment to bring Her here.

“When we take the cows hack to Nandagrama, I will make arrangements for You to meet Her. I promise.” Krsna’s face blossomed with happiness at these encouraging words and He replied:

“Subala! You are a man of your word. I will come and play, hut don’t forget (bhulano) what you just promised.” Because Krsna warned Subala not to forget, this forest is called Bhulanvana.

Beneath a large bakula tree in Bhulanvana is another significant pastime place, known as Karamana. Late one evening Krsna set out from Nandagrama in search of Srimati Radharani.

Either bewildered hy love, lost in the dark, or both, Krsna somehow wandered and wandered, from forest to forest, attracting a caravan of sympathetic forest animals along the way.

By the time He arrived at Karamana His feelings of separation from Radha had reached (ever pilch. Hope that He would find Her was so remote that Krsna fainted upon the bed of soft moss beneath a tree.

That night Krsna did not meet Radha. Such arc the arrangements of Yogamaya. But Krsna did relish the loving concern of the forest animals, and they felt the force of His love in separation.

At the southern edge of Bhulanvana is Hodal, an ancient dwelling place. It is said that the Pandavas lived there incognito during their last year of exile, near what is now called Pandava-kunda.

Great devotees that they are, the Pandavas took unlimited pleasure in visiting Vrndavana-dhama, whether on earlier outings from Hastinapura, or when they were later in exile from Indraprastha.

They lived in Kaman and Hodal, travelling through the forests of Vraja. Knowing that Duryodhana would try to expose them, they went on to Virata to avoid risking a conflict in Krsna’s land.

I remembered the adjoining forests as I happily walked towards Lord Sesasayi, who resides in the forest of Sesasayanavana, where Krsna once assumed the form of Ksirodaksayi Visnu, and Radha of Laksmi.

Once, while His cows were grazing nearby and the cowherd boys were resting some distance away, Krsna met Radha and the gopis in this secluded forest, confident that no one would interrupt them.

Somehow, in the course of Their pastimes, an image of Krsna lying on a serpent in an ocean of milk came into Radharani’s mind. It was not long before it became the topic of Their conversation.

“O Hari!” Radha said. “I’ve heard that You are Ramacandra, Visnu, and other incarnations. If You think Me worthy, please show Me the form You assume as You rest on Sesa-naga in the ocean of milk.”

Pleased by Radha’s request, Krsna began to play His flute. In response, His cows soaked the ground with unending streams of milk, quickly creating a lake that would come to he known as Ksira-sagara.

In this vast expanse of milk — also called Maha-dadhi-kunda— appeared the serpent Sesa, His thousand heads decorated with glistening gems, His mouths singing the Lord’s unlimited glories.

As Krsna walked hand in hand with Radha along the body of the celestial serpent, He became four-armed Visnu—with conch, club, disc, and lotus — and She took on a four-armed form of Laksmi.

Krsna laid hack against the hoods of the serpent and placed His feet on Radha’s lap. Massaging His feet, She wanted to put them on Her breasts, but restrained Herself, thinking Her breasts too hard.

In this regard, Dasa Gosvami writes:

**“Although for Her own happi ness She yearns to place Lord Krsna’s handsome and delicate lotus feet on Her breasts, Srimati Radharani is reluctant to do so —**

**“Fearing that, even though Her body is very delicate, Her breasts are very hard. I pray that Krsna, who also places His lotus feet on the hood of Ananta Sesa, will permit me to reside in His land of Vraja.”**

**\* \* \* \***

Caitanya Mahaprabhu once had darsana of the Sesasayi Deity. The sight reminded the Lord of the pastime Krsna and Radha played here. Overwhelmed by loving ecstasy,He joyfully sang and danced.

Those who saw Lord Caitanya’s ecstatic symptoms and effulgent form concluded that He could not be an ordinary person, but perhaps Sesasayi Visnu Himself in the guise of a sannyasi.

In 1932 Srila Prabhupada joined the Parikrama party of Bhakti- siddhanta Sarasvati Thakura at Kosi, just as the devotees were preparing with great excitement to see Sesasayi Visnu.

It was announced that since this was Sarasvati Thakura’s last night on the Parikrama, the pilgrims could cither stay back to hear him speak, or go out to see Sesasayi Visnu. Practically everyone went out.

Only Srila Prabhupada, an uninitiated grhastha at the time, and ten or twelve men, senior sannyasi disciples of Bhaktisiddhanta Sarasvati Thakura, decided to stay back and hear their spiritual master speak.

His Divine Grace later explained why he did not go to see Sesasayi “And I thought it wise, ‘What can I see at this Sesasayi? Let me hear what Srila Bhaktisiddhanta Sarasvati will speak. Let me hear.”

Later, in Allahabad, the then Abhay Caran De was introduced to Sarasvati Thakura. The Thakura said, “Yes, he likes to hear. He does not go away. I have marked him. I will accept him as my disciple.”

Rupa Gosvami says that the Sesasayi Visnu manifested by Krsna in Vraja is greater than the original Sesasayi Visnu expanded from Lord Narayana. When the former appears, the latter is present in Him.

The difference between the two is similar to the difference between the original form of Krsna and His expansion Narayana. The Sesasayi Krsna manifested is the original, the other an expansion.

Just as when Krsna appears, Narayana is present within Him, so in the Sesasayi manifested by Krsna, the Vaikuntha Sesasayi is present. The same is true for any incarnation Krsna manifests in Vrndavana.

In various places, at various times, Krsna manifests various avataras, all eternally present in Him: Vasudeva, Lord Ramacandra, Nrsimha, Varaha, Narayana Rsi, Ajita, and innumerable others.

For instance, after Brahma had playfully stolen Krsna’s calves and boyfriends, Krsna assumed the form of Lord Narayana, who in turn displayed millions of universes along with their presiding Deities.

Krsna manifested a four-armed form of Vasudeva when Sridama assumed the form of Garuda. And when the Adityas came to warm Krsna on top of Dvadasaditya-tila, He assumed twelve arms.

The demons who came to disturb Vraja were killed by Sankarsana. And once, after His cows had created an ocean of milk, Krsna, as Ajita, along with gopas as the demigods and demons, churned it.

All these expansions of Krsna are wonderful: They are the origins of the avataras of Narayana who appear at various times in the creation, and They arc also eternally present in Vrndavana, just like Krsna.

All glories to Sri Krsna, whose beautiful cowherd form inconceivably contains within it innumerable personal expansions, unlimited potencies, and the limitless material and spiritual realms.

Encompassing Sesasayanavana is Kadambavana, where Krsna roams in the shade of the trees and delight in the heady fragrance of kadamba blossoms that permeates the forest in flower-bearing spring.

The pious kadamba trees and their flowers play an integral part in Krsna’s pastimes, so much so that they are closely identified with Vrndavana and thus intimately related with Krsna.

The yellow cloth covering Krsna’s waist is often compared to the saffron dust of the nipa, which is known as the “dust kadamba" and has larger flowers than the more aromatic kadamba proper.

The honey that flows from the hollows of the kadamba tree, when thinned with water and appropriately spiced, makes an intoxicating beverage Krsna enjoys, often with the gopis.

Lord Balarama’s attraction to intoxicating beverages is well known. Amongst them all, the wine made from kadamba flowers He holds in high regard. Intoxicated, the Lord once revealed this ecstatic truth:

“My dear Krsna, tell Me at once why the whole world is trembling and why the moon has become elongated! And ? you members of the Yadu dynasty, why are you laughing at Me?

“Please give Me back My liquors made of honey from the kadamba flower!” In recording this incident, Rupa Gosvami prays that in His intoxicated state Lord Balarama will be pleased with us all.

Kadamba trees play a role in many of Krsna’s pastimes. For example, He jumped from one into the Yamuna to fight with Kaliya, and He climbed up on the branch of another with the gopis’ stolen clothes.

Krsna is also known as *Kadamba-kanana-rasa-parayana*— He is especially fond of rasa dancing within kadamba forests — and Sri Radha likes to see Him standing against a backdrop of kadamba trees.

During the Ratha-yatra festival, Lord Caitanya expressed the mood of Radha and Her desire to see Krsna within the kadamba forest,

*yah kaumara-harah sa eva hi varas ta eva caitra-ksapas—?? conmilita-malati-surabhayah praudhah kadambanilah*  
*sei caivasmi tathapi tatra surata-vadpara-lila-vidhau reva-rodhasi vetasi-taru-tale cetah samutkanthate*

**“That very personality who stole away My heart during My youth is now again My master. These are the same moonlit nights of the month of Caitra. The same fragrance of malati flowers is there —**

**“And the same sweet breezes are blowing from the kadamba forest. In Our intimate relationship, I am also the same lover, yet still My mind is not happy here. I am eager to go hack —**

**“To that place on the hank of the Reva under the Vetasi tree. That is My desire.”**

Most devotees present could not understand this song, hut Rupa Gosvami explained it in his translation.

The spherical-shaped kadamba flower is ideal for making garlands. Often we hear of the gopis or Sri Radha making kadamba garlands for Krsna. Sometimes He strings such garlands for them.

Once, while waiting for Radha by Yamuna’s shore, Krsna started to make a garland of kadamba flowers. When Radha arrived, Krsna, intent on finishing His gift to Her, just gave Her a loving glance.

The kadamba trees of Vrndavana are all desire trees, and so they often actively participate in Krsna’s pastimes; the Aimtha-kadamba of Paitha that Krsna twisted into a knot is one example.

When Krsna left the rasa dance, the gopis asked the kadamba trees where they could find Him. And after Krsna left Vraja, Yasoda addressed the kadamba trees in madness, asking, “Where is my son?"

Even the kadamba trees lament when they cannot see Krsna. For example: “One devotee tried to console a kadamba tree when the tree was lamenting because Krsna had not touched even its shadow.

“The devotee said, ‘My dear kadamba tree! Do not worry. After defeating the Kaliya snake in the River Yamuna, Krsna will come and satisfy your desire.”’

All glories to Kadambavana, whose trees are embraced hy different flowering vines, in whose branches beautiful birds sing of Radha’s and Krsna’s glories, and in whose hollows intoxicated bees collect honey.

**\* \* \* \***

Walking north along the parikrama path, I came to one of my favourite spots in New Vraja-dhama. Merely beholding beautiful and secluded Nandavana relieves one’s heart of the burning pangs of lust.

Nandavana is where Nanda Maharaja would come to absorb Himself in bhajana; with so many subjects to care for and so many cows to protect, Nanda had precious little time for bhajana.

The king of Vraja, rapt in love for his son, was naturally absorbed in the ultimate goal of life — Sri Krsna. Still, Nanda thought himself an ordinary cowherd and wanted time to pursue self-realisation.

Nandaraja would thus make time to visit this forest, which bears his name. He would sit here in seclusion, chant his diksa-mantra, and meditate upon the Supreme Lord Narayana.

As soon as he was absorbed in thoughts of Visnu, however, the fourarmed form of the Lord would transform into that of his son, and Nandaraja would he swept away in remembrance of Krsna’s pranks.

After some time, Nanda would notice that his mind had drifted from meditation on Lord Visnu. Thinking himself inattentive in japa, he would again bring his mind back to the Lord of Vaikuntha.

But no matter how hard and how many times he tried, his thoughts of Narayana would transform into thoughts of Krsna. Despite the bliss he felt, he worried that he was neglecting his sadhana.

While facing such dualities, Nanda Maharaja would suddenly remember the words Garga Muni had spoken at Krsna’s name-giving ceremony, “O King! This child of yours is as good as Narayana.”

“If my son is as good as Lord Narayana,” Nandaraja would think, “thinking about Him is also as good as thinking about Narayana.” His mind pacified, the king would plunge into thoughts of Krsna.

Absorbed in Krsna’s pastimes as a child, boy, and youth, Nanda Maharaja would lose himself in the bliss of krsna-smarana, his diksa-mantra abandoned in favour of krsna-nama-sankirtana.

Nandavana is the ideal place, then, to pray lor the blessing of spontaneous attraction to Krsna, so that not even the form of Narayana, not to speak of His maya, will distract our minds.

Thus we pray,

*srutim apare smrtim itare bharatam anye bhajantu bhava-bhitah*  
*aham iha nandam vande yasyalinde param brahma*

**“Those who are afraid of material existence worship Vedic literature.**

**“Some worship the corollaries to the Vedas, and others worship the Mahabharata. As far as I am concerned, I worship Maharaja Nanda, the father of Krsna, in whose courtyard the Absolute Truth plays.”**

Who can possibly describe every achievement of every Vaisnava acarya honoured in New Vraja-dhama? Still, I feel compelled to write about Kavi-karnapura, whose samadhi is nearby.

This Kavi can be counted alongside great Vaisnava authors like Rupa Gosvami and Krsnadasa Kaviraja Gosvami. Gaura-ganoddesa-dipika and Ananda-vmdavana-campu are certainly amongst his greatest works.

In New Vraja-dhama, Kavi-karnapura’s samadhi is just south of Pe, in an area well suited to reciting his books, to receiving inspiration for writing, or to composing prose in glorification of the Lord.

Jagadananda Pandita writes:

**“All glories to the greatly blessed Kavi-karnapura! We both hail from the same village. He has revealed in his writings some of the marvellous qualities of the holy name.**

**“A recipient of Lord Gauranga’s mercy, he is surely the most fortunate soul in the universe. Having been thus blessed at the tender age of seven, Kavi-karnapura was respected by all as a great poet.”**

Sivananda Sena had three sons, the youngest was named Paramananda Dasa. But Lord Caitanya gave him the name Puri Dasa before he was born and jokingly called him by that name thereafter.

But once the devotees had heard the nectar of Puri Dasa’s poetic skill, Svarupa Damodara Gosvami predicted that in the future he would be known as Kavi-karnapura. Thus the Kavi had three names.

Sivananda Sena once brought Puri Dasa into Caitanya Mahaprabhu’s presence. At that time the Lord put His toe into the child’s mouth and allowed him to suck on it. Who else has received such mercy?

Some years later, when Puri Dasa had reached the age of seven, Lord Caitanya repeatedly asked him to say Hare Krsna, but the boy would not. Despite his father’s coaxing, Puri Dasa remained silent.

Caitanya Mahaprabhu was perplexed. How could He, who could make even wild animals chant Krsna’s names, not induce His devotee’s son to do the same? Svarupa Damodara Gosvami gave the answer:

“My Lord,” he said, “You have given him initiation into the name of Krsna. After receiving the mantra, he will not repeat it in front of everyone, instead he chants the mantra in his mind.”

Sri Caitanya Mahaprabhu accepted this explanation, and Sivananda Sena felt greatly relieved. On another occasion, Lord Caitanya commanded the boy, “Recite, My dear Puri Dasa.”

This time Puri Dasa broke his silence and amazed everyone with this composition,

*sravasoh kuvalayam aksnor anjanam uraso mahendra-mani-dama*  
*vrndavana-ramaninam mandanam akhilam harir jayati*

**“Krsna is just like a bluish lotus flower for the ears. He is ointment for the eyes, a necklace of indranila gems for the chest, and a universal ornament for the gopi damsels of Vrndavana. May He be glorified.”**

A common misconception is that Kavi-karnapura wrote the book Caitanya-carita at the age of sixteen, but Srila Prabhupada tells us that the Kavi’s eldest brother, Caitanya Dasa, was the author.

Sri Natha Pandita, an eminent disciple of Advaita Acarya, initiated Kavi-karnapura, who began to compose books at the age of forty and continued to do so for a period of ten years until 1576.

Kavi-karnapura disclosed the identities of Caitanya Mahaprabhu’s associates in Krsna’s pastimes, but that humble soul kept his own identity a secret. Let us offer our respectful obeisances unto him.

In Vraja, the forest that is abundant with sandalwood trees adjoining Nandavana is called Candanavana, beyond which are open pastures with sweet grasses just suitable for grazing cows, and a beautiful lake.

This lake, known as Lotana-kunda, has wide sandy banks. Krsna used to graze His herd there. One day the cows, feeling the heat of the sun, began to playfully roll (lot) in the cool sand.

The gopas gathered around to see the fun. They cheered, blew their horns, and urged the cows on, calling them hy name. Finally the boys rolled in the sand with the cows, laughing and making lowing sounds.

The bond between the cows and the gopas is one of love. The cows are the Vraja-vasis’ very means of subsistence, and the cowherds treat them as family members, calling them “mother” and “father.”

This consciousness pervades Vraja. All the cowherd boys show their cows the same affectionate respect they show their own mothers. And Krsna, the foremost lover of cows, is always pleased to see this.

By the time the children returned home that day, they looked especially dusty. Their parents asked why they looked such a mess, and the boys laughed, “We rolled (lot) in the sand with the cows.”

**\* \* \* \***

The next pastime place is Phalena, a small forest where the gopis — and sometimes the gopas — play Holi. On one special occasion at that place, Radha and Krsna became mere playthings of Their girlfriends.

At the centre of Phalena is a large banyan tree, beneath which sits a throne fit for the king and queen of Vraja. The ground around the throne is paved with gems that outshine those in Indra’s court.

In the heart of spring, a group of gopis once asked Radha and Krsna to sit upon the throne and permit Their girlfriends to show Them unrestrained love. How could the divine couple refuse?

Some gopis played instruments and sang songs about the divine couple’s mutual love and unlimited qualities, while others covered Radha and Krsna with red, vermilion, and blue dye.

First the girls approached the throne and, one after another, gently smeared a small bit of dye on Radha’s and Krsna’s faces. Then, as the music sped up, the gopis sprinkled dye all over Their bodies.

As the jubilant exchange reached a climax, the gopis used syringes and flower bombs to so cover Radha and Krsna that They became indistinguishable from Their throne, and from each other.

Indeed, the entire area surrounding the banyan tree was engulfed in a cloud of gulala rising higher and higher into the air until it could be seen by amused Vraja-vasis from as far away as Nandagrama.

While all this was going on, Radha and Krsna sat hand in hand, apparently unaffected, but subtly responding with submissive smiles and glances to the laughter, gaiety, and affection of Their friends.

By mental speculation, the impersonalists falsely try to become God, only to fall back into conditioned life, but by pure love the Vraja-vasis make God their submissive plaything and are thus exalted.

As Srila Prabhupada would say,

**“God is great! But devotional service is even greater than God because it attracts Him.”**

Because devotees’ only shelter is service, by devotion they become greater than God.

Phalena is where the gopis showcased the result (phalena) of loving devotion. By coming here we can try to understand this truth and thereby gain inspiration for realising it in our own lives.

From Phalena I walked past the field called Kerala. In Vraja, Krsna grazes the cows by starting at Lotana-kunda, continuing to Kerala. Beneath a tree in the southernmost corner of Kerala lies Cauki.

Krsna was once tired from walking and used the exposed root of that tree as a seat (cauki). Leaning against the tree’s trunk, He closed His eyes and dreamed of His meeting with Radha, later that night.

Turning back towards Vrndavana village, I looked out over the houses of the devotees who have dedicated their lives to serving New Vraja-dhama, and whose homes have become places of pilgrimage.

Some homes mark the samadhis of great souls, others commemorate places of krsna-lila, but because Radha and Syama accept service in every home, those homes are all places of Their Lordships’ pastimes.

To the northeast of Cauki is Randhera. In search of water, thirsty Krsna once met a group of gopis there who were carrying pitchers of water they had just drawn from a kunda called Sri Radharani.

Krsna asked the girls for water to quench His thirst, and they happily obliged. After drinking His fill, He commented on the especially sweet taste of the water. The gopis told Him where it came from.

The Lord said, “Water in Vrndavana is never ordinary, yet this water is extra special because it comes from a lake that bears tbe name Sri Radharani. It must be imbued with Her sweetness and qualities.”

Because this is the place where Krsna declared the water to be saturated with Radha’s transcendental qualities (radhera), this area is known as Randhera. It is always fondly remembered by the Lord.

In the area of Randhera is the samadhi of Govinda Ghosa Thakura. His residence and temple are in Danihata, Bengal, where his Gopinatha Deity continues to perform his sraddha ceremony.

Govinda Ghosa and his two brothers were born in a family of kayasthas. All three were famous as great leaders of sankirtana (kirtaniyas) who sang both for Lord Caitanya and Lord Nityananda.

Lord Caitanya sent Govinda Ghosa’s brothers with Lord Nityananda to preach in Bengal but kept Govinda in Puri. Govinda Ghosa was ecstatic to stay behind and keep company with the Lord.

During the Ratha-yatra festival, Lord Caitanya formed seven kirtana groups. Govinda Ghosa was the lead singer in one of those groups, with Junior Haridasa, Visnudasa, and Raghava as responding singers.

After the Lord disappeared, Govinda Ghosa returned to Agradvipa to worship Gopinatha. Then his only son died. Feeling morose that no one would perform the sraddha ceremony for him, Govinda lamented.

In krsna-lila, Govinda Ghosa is Kamalavati-devi, who sings the songs composed by Visakha. Actually, he had no need for a sraddha rite, a ritual performed after death to free the soul from sinful reactions.

But Govinda thought himself an ordinary conditioned soul; he felt hopeless without a son. The Gopinatha Deity therefore promised to perform the annual ceremony as a proxy for Govinda’s son.

Srila Prabhupada has repeatedly mentioned Gopinathah commitment to serve His devotee. When I visited Agradvipa, I was told that the Deity was still observing the promised sraddha every year.

Indeed, the head priest testified that Gopinatha — to prove He participates in the ceremony on Govinda Ghosa’s disappearance day — changes colour as if He were accepting the sins of His devotee.

Further north is expansive Gopavana. After Krsna subdued Kaliya, Nanda Maharaja donated an untold number of cows to qualified brahmanas, hoping to protect his son from further dangers.

It is a principle of Vedic culture that, hy the right kind of charity, pleasing the Supreme Lord and His representatives — the brahmanas — invokes auspiciousness in life.

To counteract or avoid reverses in life, to celebrate an auspicious occasion, to share one’s wealth, or just to express joy, one should distribute charity. The best act of charity is to donate cows.

Krsna Himself declares that no one, regardless of how spiritually advanced he or she may he, should abandon charitable acts, which purify even great souls. The Lord set this example in His pastimes.

Krsna’s father, Vasudeva, was imprisoned when Krsna was horn and could only mentally give herds of cows in charity. But later, in Vraja, Nanda Maharaja donated 200,000 decorated cows to local brahmanas.

Once, on the occasion of His birthday, child Krsna was busy playing with His friends when Mother Yasoda called Him to bathe and to charitably distribute cows to the brahmanas.

While living as a wealthy king in Dvaraka, every morning after rising and performing His ablutions and offering respect to His elders, Krsna gave away herds of well-decorated cows to exalted brahmanas.

To celebrate Krsna’s victory over Kaliya and to avert future demonic attacks, Nanda Maharaja houniilully distributed wealth, grains, and cows to the qualified twice-born citizens of Gopavana.

In this age most, devotees do not keep cows, but we can fulfil the purpose of giving charity — to please the Lord and the brahmanas — by regularly distributing books, prasada, and a portion of our wealth.

**\* \* \* \***

Within Gopavana in New Vraja-dhama are samdhis of Paramesvara Dasa, Sundarananda Dasa, Ramananda Vasu, and Raghava Pandita, as well as the pastime places called Varoli, Taroli, and Na-grama.

In vraja lila, Paramesvara Dasa is Krsna’s cowherd friend Arjuna, and Sundarananda Dasa is His friend Sudama, the brother otRupa-manjari.The book Preyo-bhakti-rasarnava is about Sundarananda.

Written by Nayanananda Thakura, a devotee in the line of Sundarananda Thakura, Preyo-bhakti-rasarnava gives insights into the daily pastimes of Krsna and Balarama through the eyes of a gopa.

Ramananda Vasu was the son of Satyaraja Khan, both of whom were residents of the famous Kulina-grama, where Haridasa Thakura once chanted the holy names during the period of caturmasya.

During those four months of the rainy season, the namacarya would often visit the home of Satyaraja Khan. Both father and son were fortunate enough to receive the full mercy of Haridasa Thakura.

Ramananda Vasu was a devotee of Lord Nityananda. Every year, on the order of Caitanya Mahaprabhu, he and his father would bring silken ropes for the Ratha-yatra festival at Puri.

The silken ropes used to carry Jagannatha were made by residents of Kulina-grama, so when they broke one year, the Lord asked His devotees to take up the service of providing stronger ropes.

After happily accepting this service, Satyaraja Khan and Ramananda Vasu placed an important question before the Lord, a question they would ask again on two other occasions: “Who is a devotee?”

Caitanya Mahaprabhu’s three separate answers to that question define the qualifications of the third class, second class, and first class devotees, all based upon the quality of their chanting Hare Krsna.

The third-class devotee is one who chants the holy name of the Lord once, a second-class devotee chants constantly, whereas the mere sight of a first-class devotee inspires others to chant.

Kavi-karnapura says that Ramananda Vasu and Satyaraja Khan were two Gandharva dancers in Vraja called Kalakantha-devi and Sukantha-devi respectively. Let us offer our obeisances to them!

The next samadhi is that of Raghava Pandita. The original samadhi of Ragh ava Pandita is in Panihati, the village on the Ganges where Lord Nityananda held His chipped-rice festival beneath a banyan tree.

Every year Raghava Pandita’s sister Damayanti would cook a variety of sweets, condiments, and pickles that would then be carried to Puri to be eaten by the Lord over the course of that year.

These parcels of food became famous amongst devotees as “the bags of Raghava” (raghavera jhali). Caitanya Mahaprabhu relished the taste of their contents and the love with which they were prepared.

Caitanya Mahaprabhu once disclosed how Raghava Pandita’s pure love for his Deity of Krsna inspired him to go out of his way to always offer the very best ingredients to the Deity of the Lord.

Sometimes, just to please Raghava Pandita, Krsna would completely consume whatever Raghava had offered so that there was nothing left. Naturally, that would give Raghava Pandita great satisfaction.

These dealings between Raghava and Krsna are eternal, for Kavi karnapura states that Raghava is the gopi Dhanistha ha devi, who feeds Krsna with limitless varieties of opulent foods in Vraja.

Just north of the parikrama-marga is a playground lor the children of New Vraja-dhama, which also marks Gopakrta, one of the playgrounds of the cowherd boys. It has two hills, Taroli and Varoli.

In Vraja, the cowherd boys split up into two groups Balarama with the elder and Krsna with the younger boys — each group claiming one of the hills as their capital and the base of ope rations for their games.

Sometimes they play “dacoits and constables" or some sort of game involving conquest and battle. At other times they run races against each other or play any one of a dizzying variety of ball games.

The two hills are their base camps, and the area between them is the playing field, while the surrounding forest gives shelter to their cows, who rest contentedly and watch the exuberant boys enjoy themselves.

Rupa Gosvami tells us that in this competition and mock fighting between the two parties, whenever Balarama’s party defeats Krsna’s, the elder boys exhibit their transcendental pride.

The defeated boys from Krsna’s group then hang their heads in shame, lamenting: “If Balarama’s party is always victorious, then who in the world can be weaker than us younger boys?”

The last lila-sthana in Gopavana is Na-grama, which literally means “no village.” Once, the gopis were feeling extreme separation from Krsna, and so they went out in search of Him.

Is He in that grove? No! That is just a shadow! Is He behind that bush? No! That is a curious doc! Is that Him on the other side of the kunda? No! That is just a gopi drawing water.

Wherever the cowherd girls searched, they found no sign of Krsna, and when they came here, they sat beside the Parikrama path, despondent that they had lost Him completely.

Whenever they thought they had seen Krsna, their hopes were dashed again, and yet again. In this secluded place, the gopis lost all hope and cried out, “No (na) Krsna!” So this place is called Na-grama.

But all was not lost. Remembering that Krsna shows Himself only to those whom He selects, the gopis sat down and dove headlong into the sankirtana of Krsna’s names, glories, and pastimes.

In no time Krsna appeared there in their midst. By that time, however, the gopis felt mistreated and somewhat indignant. In response to Krsna’s entreaties, they continued to cry out, “No!”

West of Gopavana is sacred Atalavana, where Krsna declared to the cowherd boys that Me had eaten so much of the brahmanis’ cooking that He was now as immovable (atala) as the food in His overfull belly.

Atala — tirtha is where Krsna, who could go no further, stopped to rest with His frends, who were just as full as He was. Madhumarigala, however, could not even make it that far; he had stopped long before.

In Bhatrola the cowherd boys had begged the local brahmanas to fill their bellies, without success. But Krsna had sent them hack to see the brahmanas' wives, who had amply rewarded the boys.

The ladies led Krsna and the gopas with so many opulent dishes cooked in ghee that the boys could hardly stand. And when they finally did, they dragged their feet as they followed behind their cows.

In such a state, the distance to Chatikara was too far to go without some respite. So when the boys came to Atalavana, they asked Krsna how He was feeling, hoping I Ie too might be stuffed full and tired.

The boys spread out in the shade of the trees around Atala — tirtha. Some slept, while others quietly relived the wonderful feast they had just honoured by the arrangement of their Krsna.

North of Gopavana is Gauravana. It is named after Caitanya Mahaprabhu, for this was the forest in which the Lord would rest beneath a large banyan tree when He visited this part of Vrndavana.

Caitanya Mahaprabhu arrived in Mathura in 1515. After touring Mathura and its sacred places along the Yamuna, He made His way through Madhuvana, Talavana, Kumudavana, and Bahulavana.

Caitanya Mahaprabhu heard of Madhavendra Puri’s Gopala Deity on Govardhana Hill, which He circumambulated. At Arit-grama He discovered the twin lakes Radha-kunda and Syama-kunda.

The Lord was unwilling to climb Govardhana, so Gopala came to see Him. The Lord then visited Kaman, Nandagrama, Khadiravana, Sesasayi, Khela-tirtha, Bhandiravana, and other places to the east.

Lord Caitanya then returned to Mathura, but He was attracting the attention of so many people that He escaped to secluded Akrura-tirtha. From there He would go out daily to visit places in Vrndavana.

Often the Lord would sit by Imli-tala in Seva-kunja, or come to Gauravana to rest. While resting, He would take in the sights of the forest, the sounds of the birds, and the fragrance of the trees.

Yet wherever Lord Caitanya went, the spectacle caused by His ecstasies attracted people by the thousands. What’s more, He would sometimes jump into the Yamuna when in ecstasy and nearly drown.

He had no chance for solitude, and His servant, Balabhadra Bhattacarya, was beside himself with anxiety, afraid for the Lord’s safety and unable to deal with the invitations the Lord was receiving.

After consulting their travelling companion, the Sanodiya brahmana, Balabhadra concluded that the Lord should end His visit and return to Jagannatha Puri. To please His servant, the Lord agreed.

Also found in Gauravana in New Vraja-dhama are the samadhis of Vakresvara Pandita, Subuddhi Raya, Ramadasa Abhirama Dasa, Sikhi Mahiti, and four other outstanding Vaisnava saints.

Across from the terraced houses, each apartment of a four-studio complex represents one of the other four samadhis here: Bhaktisiddhanta Sarasvati, King Virahamvira, Satyaraja Khan, and Gopala-guru Gosvami.

Now let us hear about these great souls, of whom I will only he able to describe a few in detail. I hope that the caretakers of these samadhis will research and prepare detailed biographies of each Vaisnava.

Krsna tells that the way to please Him is to serve His devotees:

**“Those who are My direct devotees are actually not My devotees, but those who are the devotees of My servant are factually My devotees.”**

**\* \* \* \***

Vakresvara Pandita was one of Caitanya Mahaprahhu’s lifelong companions. Kavi-karnapura tells us that Vakresvara is Aniruddha, the fourth member of the quadruple expansion of the Lord.

Lord Caitanya said that Krsna personally danced in Vakreswara’s heart. Sometimes the Pandita would dance continuously tor three days and nights without stopping, pleasing Sri Caitanya to no end.

Once, as Lord Caitanya sang, Vakresvara Pandita blurted out in ecstasy, “O Candramukha! Please give me ten thousand Gandharvas. Let them sing as I dance, and then I will be greatly happy.”

Lord Caitanya replied, “I have only one wing like you, hut if I had another, certainly I would fly in the sky!” Vakresvara would also take part in the dramas performed at the house of Candrasekhara.

Caitanya-bhagavata tells of an incident in which Vakresvara Pandita transformed Devananda Pandita — an offender of Srivasa Thakura — from a Mayavadi into an intimate associate of Lord Caitanya.

Vakresvara Pandita established his headquarters in the house of Kasi Misra, which adjoins Caitanya Mahaprabhu’s residence at the Gambhira and is presently known as Radha-kanta Matha.

Of Vakrcsvara’s disciples, Gopala-guru is very famous, as is his disciple Dhyanacandra Gosvami, who writes in his Padhati that Vakresvara Pandita is an incarnation of Tungavidya-sakhi.

I already mentioned that Subuddhi Raya greeted both Sanatana Gosvami and Rupa Gosvami upon their arrival to Mathura and showed them some of the holy places in Krsna’s capital.

Originally from Bengal, Subuddhi Raya was a rich landlord, until fate arranged for the local Muslim king, instigated by his wife, to sprinkle water on Subuddhi’s head from a pitcher a Muslim had used.

Smarta regulations state that such a thing deprives one of his caste. Subuddhi Raya, now a social leper, took this opportunity to leave home and go to Varanasi, where he tried to purify himself.

He received contradictory instructions from different brahmanas in Varanasi, but then Caitanya Mahaprabhu arrived and told Him, “Go to Vrndavana and chant the Hare Krsna mantra constantly.”

Subuddhi Raya obeyed the Lord. The once wealthy landlord became a wood collector, selling bundles of wood for five paise each. He lived on one fifth of his income and used the rest to serve the devotees.

He would provide visiting Bengali devotees with yoguri and rice, and did his bhajana in the same garden with Raghunatha Bhatta Gosvami and Kasisvara Pandita, with whom he became close friends.

That garden is now the place of the sixty-four samadhis mentioned earlier. When Subuddhi Raya left this world, he was placed into samadhi near the resting place of his dear friend Raghunatha Bhatta.

From Subuddhi Raya’s life we learn that if one wants to enter Vrndavana, one should leave aside all past opulence and lead a life of simplicity and service to the Vaisnavas. That pleases Krsna.

Ramadasa Abhirama, also known as Abhirama Thakura, is Krsna’s friend Sridama. Ramadasa lived in the village Khanakula-krsnanagara and married one Malini-devi, under the order of Lord Nityananda.

Always absorbed in the mood of fraternal love of God, Abhirama Thakura was one of three devotees to accompany Lord Nityananda to preach in Bengal. There the Thakura became a great acarya.

Upon arriving in Khanakula-krsnanagara, Bengal, Abhirama Thakura had a dream in which Krsna appeared to him in the form of the Gopinatha Deity and revealed where He could be found.

Abhirama Thakura awoke and took the villagers to that spot. In uncovering the Deity, a pond was simultaneously created. That pond, known as Rama-kunda, is now a holy tirtha.

Whenever Lord Nityananda visited Khanakula-krsnanagara, Abhirama Thakura would be overjoyed. But when the Lord was absent, the Thakura kept to himself and refused to speak.

Srinivasa Acarya once visited Abhirama Thakura and received the blessing of being touched by the Thakura’s whip, known as Jaya Mangala. Anyone the whip touched received love of God.

Abhirama Thakura was so powerful that he could tell an ordinary black stone from a Salagrama-sila simply by offering obeisances to it. If the stone was not a sila, it would break into pieces.

When Lord Nityananda’s wife Vasudha gave birth to a boy, Abhirama Thakura came and offered his obeisances to the baby. To the parents’ dismay, the child immediately died.

Surprisingly, Lord Nityananda lost a few more sons to Abhirama Thakura in the same way, so the Lord did not invite His friend to his next son’s birth ceremony. But Abhirama Thakura came anyway.

When Ramadasa again prepared to offer his respects to the newborn, Nityananda’s wives pleaded that he spare the child. The Thakura said, “If He is the Lord Himself, my obeisances will not affect Him.”

This time nothing happened when Abhirama Thakura bowed down. And to everyone’s joy he declared, “Gauranga has again appeared as this boy, so that those who did not see Him then can do so now.”

Let us offer our obeisances to the manifestation of divine love, Abhirama Thakura, the husband of Malini, the foremost of Krsna’s cowherd boyfriends, and brother of Srimati Radharani, Sridama.

Sikhi Mahiti hailed from the same village as Ramananda Raya. He was one of Lord Caitanya’s constant companions and was counted as one of the Lord’s three-and-a-half most intimate associates.

Those three-and-a-half associates were Ramananda Raya, Svarupa Damodara Gosvami, Sikhi Mahiti, and Sikhi Mahiti’s sister Madhavi Mahiti, who counted as half an associate (ardha-jana).

Kavi-karnapura says that Sikhi Mahiti was an assistant of Srimati Radharani called Ragalekha. Like his sister and his brother Murari, Sikhi Mahiti could not forget Lord Caitanya, even for a moment.

Srila Prabhupada, citing an Oriya book called Caitanya-carita-mahakavya, tells us that in a dream Sikhi Mahiti saw Lord Caitanya entering into and out of the body of Lord Jagannatha.

Let us pray to Sikhi Mahiti that we, who are so distant from the Lord, may be transcendentally fortunate enough to attain the position of a servant of the servants of these intimate associates of the Lord.

**\* \* \* \***

North of Sikhi Mahiti’s samadhi arc four more samadhis, beginning with the puspa-samadhi of iskcon’s parama-guru, Srila Bhaktisiddhanta Sarasvati Thakura.

Born in 1874 in Puri as Bimala Prasada, Bhaktisiddhanta Sarasvati Thakura was the “ray of Visnu” that his father, Thakura Bhaktivinoda, had prayed for to help him in his preaching work.

Some authorities refer to a prediction in the Padma Purana, a verse that concludes, hy utkale purusottamat, which means “from Puri in Orissa devotional service will spread all over the world.”

This is not a direct reference to Bhaktisiddhanta Sarasvati, hut it certainly applies to him: after taking birth in Puri, he took up the mission of his father and spread krsria-bhakti throughout India.

Moreover, hy empowering our Srila Prabhupada to spread Krsna consciousness all over the world, Bhaktisiddhanta Sarasvati Thakura certainly fulfilled Vyasadeva’s prophecy in the Padma Purana.

Bhaktisiddhanta Sarasvati was a child prodigy. He could memorise anything he read. He shunned all games, preferring to read his father’s hooks, and displayed extreme renunciation at an early age.

After finishing secondary school, he attended university, preferring to read hooks in the library rather than attend his classes. After becoming proficient in mathematics and astrology, be quit his studies.

At the age of twenty-five he edited the ancient astronomical treatise Surya-siddhanta. In recognition of his outstanding scholastic achievements, he received the honorific title “Siddhanta Sarasvati.”

Some years later, under the guidance of Bhaktivinoda Thakura, Bhaktisiddhanta Sarasvati accepted initiation from Gaurakisora Dasa Babaji, receiving the name Sri Varsabhanavi-devi-dayita Dasa.

Then came a period of intense bhajana in Mayapur, in which he lived alone in a simple thatched hut, slept on the ground, and ate only once a day after chanting his daily quota of 300,000 holy names.

Gaurakisora Dasa Babaji would say about his disciple:

**“In my Prabhu \[Bhaktisiddhanta Sarasvati\] I see the same kind of renunciation that was seen in Sri Rupa and Sri Raghunatha.”**

Another perfected soul of Navadvipa, Vamsidasa Babaji, also had high regard for Bhaktisiddhanta Sarasvati Thakura. Upon seeing him, Babaji would say, “Someone very close to my Gaura has come to me.”

When Gaurakisora said, “preach the Absolute Truth and keep aside all other work,” that order echoed Bhaktivinoda Thakura’s desire, a desire that forever set the course of Siddhanta Sarasvati’s life.

Significant milestones before he established the Gaudiya Matha were his beginning to accept disciples, his meeting with the brahmanas at Medinipur, and establishing printing presses and distributing books.

In 1915 both Bhaktivinoda Thakura and Gaurakisora Dasa Babaji departed from this world. These events left Bhaktisiddhanta Sarasvati Thakura feeling alone and helpless in carrying out his mission.

One night he had a vision of the Panca-tattva and their associates, including his gurus, who encouraged him to preach boldly and assured him that all facilities, assistance, and success would come.

In 1918, on Gaura-purnima, he accepted the order of tridndi-sannyasa. In that year he also established the first branch of the Gaudiya Matha, Sri Caitanya Matha at Vrajapattana.

Bhaktivinoda Thakura, in his final years, had instructed Bhaktisiddhanta Sarasvati to develop Mayapur, establish Navadvipa parikrama, print and distribute books, and to preach widely.

For the next eighteen years Bhaktisiddhanta Sarasvati Thakura preached tirelessly throughout India, fulfilling his father’s desires and Lord Caitanya’s mission by establishing sixty-four temples.

He also sent preachers to Germany and England. Their success was limited, but on his order our Srila Prabhupada ventured to New York in 1965 and inaugurated the international “Hare Krsna explosion.”

Bhaktisiddhanta Sarasvati Thakura’s achievements testify to his spiritual power. Despite great opposition from ritualistic brahmanas, he established annual parikramas of Navadvipa and Vrndavana.

He published the works of Thakura Bhaktivinoda and other Gaudiya acaryas as well as his own writings. He also encouraged his disciples to write and print books, an order he gave directly to Srila Prabhupada.

Repeatedly travelling throughout India, he not only opened temples and installed Deities, but also set up free diorama exhibits, teaching the precepts and history of Gaudiya Vaisnavism in a modern way.

Bhaktisiddhanta Sarasvati Thakura not only constructed a new temple at the birthplace of Lord Caitanya but also commemorated, with temples, many places of Sri Caitanya’s pastimes in Navadvipa.

He built a temple in Jagannatha Puri, where he would spend his last days, and installed Caitanya Mahaprabhu’s divine footprints in all the significant places the Lord visited on His tour of South India.

In 1930 he opened a marble temple in Bagh Bazaar, Calcutta. As important as this was, he spoke at the opening ceremony of the greater importance of preaching and book distribution.

He said,

**“...to establish internal hari-bhajana in this world, quite a few books have to be written and published. The temple which is within the books and the temple which is within the devotees are more important than the temple built with bricks, stones, and mortar, because by constructing such temples hari-katha can be preached in this world much longer.”**

We all know how Bhaktisiddhanta Sarasvati, the “lion guru," fearlessly exposed sahajiyas, Mayavadis, and pseudo-spiritualists, and how he preached to scholars, ideologues, and high-ranking officials.

Finally, in October 1936 he went to reside in Jagannatha Puri, where he displayed symptoms of illness. On the sixth of December he returned to the Bagh Bazaar temple and passed away on January first.

Let us emulate the mood of spiritual purity and enthusiastic preaching taught by Bhaktisiddhanta Sarasvati Thakura, and let us pray to be instruments in fulfilling his final instructions:

**“All of you please preach about Sri Rupa and Sri Raghunatha with great enthusiasm. The supreme goal of all our desire is to become specks of dust at the lotus feet of the followers of Sri Rupa Gosvami.**

**“When faults in others misguide and delude you, have patience, introspect, find faults in yourself. Know that others cannot harm you unless you harm yourself.**

**“I wish that every selfless, tender-hearted person of the Gaudiya Matha be prepared to shed two hundred gallons of blood tor the nourishment of the spiritual corpus of every individual of this world.”**

Next to the samadhi of Bhaktisiddhanta Sarasvati Thakura is the samadhi of King Virahamvira, about whom I have spoken extensively in connection with his spiritual master, Srinivasa Acarya.

Next to the samadhi of King Virahamvira is that of Satyaraja Khan. I have spoken of him while recounting the character of his son Ramananda Vasu. Both father and son are transcendentally glorious.

The last samadhi here in Gauravana is that of Gopala-guru Gosvami, who from childhood was a servant and disciple of Vakresvara Pandita and an object of Caitanya Mahaprabhu’s affection.

His name was actually Makaradhvaja Pandita, but because Lord Caitanya would regularly call him “Gopala” — and on one occasion “Guru” — devotees came to know him as Gopala-guru Gosvami.

Once, when Caitanya Mahaprabhu went to answer the call of nature, Gopala followed Him with a pitcher of water. During the course of his service, the boy noticed that the Lord was biting His tongue.

Gopala asked the Lord why He was doing this, and the Lord replied, “Answering nature’s call is contaminating, so I stop My tongue from chanting Krsna’s names by holding it between My teeth.”

The Lord continued, “Krsna is all-pure and non-different from His name; therefore, the name should not be chanted when one is in a contaminated state or in a contaminated place.”

Gopala joined his palms and answered, “O Lord! You have taught that Krsna and His name are not different, but You have also taught that Krsna’s name is more merciful than even Sri Krsna Himself.

“It is offensive to worship the Deity in a contaminated state, but there arc no hard and fast rules for chanting the holy name; indeed, it should he chanted always, even while answering nature’s call.”

When Lord Caitanya heard Gopala’s reasoning, He embraced the boy and exclaimed, “Your words are full of wisdom. Today I have learned the truth of Krsna’s name from you, and so you are now My guru.”

Caitanya Mahaprabhu told His followers of this incident, and thereafter everyone called the boy Gopala-guru. All Gaudiya Vaisnavas know him by that name even today.

Gopala-guru was fully blessed to have as his teachers Svarupa Damodara, Raghunatha Dasa Gosvami, and his own spiritual master, Vakresvara Pandita — not to mention Lord Caitanya Himself.

After the disappearance of Vakresvara Pandita, Gopala-guru Gosvami installed the Deities of Radha-Radha-kanta— along with Gaura-Nitai Deities in beautiful dancing poses — in the house of Kasi Misra.

Gopala-guru Gosvami wrote a book called Smarana-paddhati that outlines the general practices of spontaneous devotional service, summarising what he had gleaned from the teachings of his gurus.

As Gopala-guru entered old age, he prayed to the Lord to send someone to help him with the service of the Deity. In a dream Krsna told him, “The first person you meet tomorrow will be your assistant.”

The next morning a young brahmana appeared at Gopala-guru’s door. Gopala-guru Gosvami later initiated him as Dhyanacandra Gosvami. Bhaktivinoda Thakura would write about him in Jaiva-dharma.

Gopala-guru Gosvami departed from this world on the ninth day of the bright fortnight of the month of Karttika. His body was placed in samadhi in Jagannatha Puri, where he had lived and served the Lord.

One day a devotee returned to Puri from Vrndavana with news that Gopala-guru was living near Dhira-samira. Doubtful, Dhyanacandra went to Vraja to see for himself; indeed, he found his guru there.

Sitting beneath a tree, Gopala-guru Gosvami told his disciple that the form he was seeing was spiritual and that he should carve a deity of him from a nima tree found in the garden at Siddha-bakula.

Dhyanacandra returned.to Puri and made a deity of his guru. On a later visit to Vraja he established a Radha-kanta temple and installed a second deity of Gopala-guru in a puspa-samadhi.

All glories to Gopala-guru, through whom Caitanya Mahaprabhu taught us that a great person can take instruction even from a young child, if that child happens to speak the truth.

**\* \* \* \***

The area north of Abhirama Thakura’s *samadhi* bound by Gauravana to the east and Atalavana to the west, extending to the bank of the Yamuna up to Jagannatha-ghata is called Radhavana.

After bathing in the Yamuna, gopis like Lalita and Visakha comb and braid Sri Radha’s hair and decorate Her with the fragrant flowers that grow abundantly in the forest of Radhavana.

Similar to Radha-kunda in many ways, Radhavana is as dear to Radha as is Her lake. It is named after Her, and it is a place of confidential pastimes with Krsna, whose affection for this forest is boundless.

Once, anticipating where Srimati Radharani would bathe in the Yamuna, Krsna hid Himself within a cluster of blue lotus flowers and waited until She and Her friends arrived at a *ghata* nearby.

The gopis entered the water, joking, splashing, and chattering about Krsna. But once Radha had strayed into the deeper water near that cluster of blue lotuses, She got the scare of Her life.

Radha was swimming peacefully, Her mind submerged in thoughts of Krsna, when the cluster of lotuses in front of Her suddenly exploded with a roar and inundated Her with water, lotus leaves, and foam.

Was it a demon or a crocodile? Sri Radha shrieked for help. But as soon as the waves subsided, She saw Krsna bobbing up and down in the water, laughing and very pleased with Himself.

But to Radha this was no joking matter. Her fear quickly transformed into anger. She turned Her hack on Krsna, waded out of the water, and ran into the forest, followed by Her amused friends.

As the girls’ voices trailed off into the distance, Krsna was left alone, standing shoulder-deep in the Yamuna. He had played a joke on Radharani, hut now the joke was on Him — He was alone.

Krsna waded back to the shore, sat down upon a riverside bench, and wondered what to do. How could He pacify Radha, who must be pouting in a nearby grove as Her friends dressed Her?

Krsna knew well that any direct attempt to pacify Radha when She was this angry was likely to make Her more angry and result in Her showing Him to the door of Her grove. He needed another strategy.
