As He was absorbed in these thoughts, Vrnda walked by, trailed by a hand of sylvan goddesses who were carrying a variety of clothes and ornaments, obviously on their way to a forest storehouse.

“This must he the hand of fate,” thought Krsna. He humbly approached Vrnda, described His predicament, and placed Himself at her mercy, saying, “O *devi.* Please help Me regain Radha’s favour.”

Vrnda quickly found a solution. With the clothes her servants were carrying, she dressed Krsna as a beautiful young girl. Satisfied with His disguise, she said, “The rest is up to You.”

Krsna followed the *gopis’* footprints deep into Radhavana and right up to the entrance of a grove where Radha, surrounded by *gopis*, was complaining to Her friends about Krsna’s prank in the Yamuna.

When Srimati Radharani saw this new *gopi* standing at Her doorway, She could not recognise that it was Krsna. His sweetness and beauty enchanted Her and completely bewildered Her mind.

Somehow She felt a surge of affection for this dark-complexioned girl and at once invited Her in. “Moon-faced one! Who are You? You are so sweet and beautiful. Please he My dear friend,” She said.

Krsna entered the grove, hut Radha noticed His reticence and enquired, “O friend! You seem to be trembling with fear. I wonder, is this because You know that Krsna lays in wait for You somewhere?”

Radha reached out and took hold of Her new friend’s bangled wrist. The instant She touched Krsna, however, She felt exhilarated, Her words stuck in Her throat, and Her limbs went stiff.

The other gopis were taken aback by Radha’s reaction, for they too had been fooled by Krsna’ disguise. But upon closer inspection of this dark beauty, they quickly recognised who the new *gopi* was.

In Krsna’s proximity, amidst Her giggling girlfriends, Radha bad no choice but to abandon Her anger. Slowly and dramatically She reenacted Krsna’s earlier antics, laughing along with Him.

No one can count bow many pastimes Radha and Krsna enjoy in Radhavana. Every pastime is novel, even it it happens hundreds of times, and devotees derive ever-expanding bliss bearing about them.

Govinda-kunda lies in the western part of Radhavana. It is known for the beauty of the many gardens that surround it, gardens that resound with bumming bees, chirping birds, and rustling leaves.

Four dense groves decorate the four corners of the lake, forming veritable walls of greenery that make it a perfect place for Krsna’s confidential pastimes, or for a peaceful nap in the beat of the day.

In Bhauma Vrndavana, a space between the groves on Govinda-kunda’s eastern side forms a gate through which cows wander in to drink the lake’s honey-sweet waters and rest on its sandy shores.

Krsna and Balarama and Their cowherd friends also come to rest by Govinda-kunda. They water Their cows and bathe in that large lake, splashing, swimming, and enjoying games in the water.

Rupa Gosvami and Sanatana Gosvami engaged in *bhajana* there, bathed there, and prayed there for the mercy of Govindaji; indeed, Govinda-kunda is closely linked with the Yoga-pitha of Govindaji.

Govinda-kunda is part of the unfurling lotus of the Yoga-pitha. Scriptures also call it Govindasvami-tirtha. A bath there is said to guarantee liberation from birth and death to sincere Vaisnavas.

Rupa Gosvami cites verses from the *Sama Purana* that glorify Govinda-tirtha:

**“The great holy place named Govindasvami-tirtha is very dear to Lord Visnu, who became the son of Vasudeva.**

**“Here the Deity of the Supreme Lord Acyuta, Lord Govindasvami, presides. The saintly devotees bathe nearby, worship the Deity, and yearn for release from the world of birth and death.”**

In New Vraja-dhama, Govinda-kunda is in an area rarely visited by devotees. It therefore offers a charming place for Radha and Syamasundara to enjoy each other’s company in private.

Dear devotees! Chanting Hare Krsna, give up all desires for sense enjoyment and for freedom from suffering and worship the lotus feet of Radha and Syama within this blissful forest of New Vraja-dhama.

Srimati Radharani is the form of complete spiritual power. Syamasundara is the source and controller of that spiritual power. Yet scriptures tell us that the two are not different from one another.

Just as musk and its scent or fire and its heat are inseparable, Radha’s and Syama’s identities are also inseparable, appearing in two forms to immerse Themselves in loving pastimes.

And just as the scent of musk perfumes a whole forest and the heat of fire warms an entire house, the mellows of Radha-Syama’s pastimes surcharge New Vraja-dhama with sweetness, joy, and affection.

As Srila Prabhupada says,

**“This is the specific qualification of Vrndavana. In Vrndavana everyone is happy. The calf is happy, the cat is happy, the dog is happy, the man is happy — everyone.”**

**\* \* \* \***

Four *samadhis* can be found in Radhavana, those of Govindananda, Madhava Ghosa, Vasudeva Ghosa, and Kasisvara Pandita. The very memory of these saints purifies all ten directions.

Govindananda was a singer in Lord Caitanya’s *sankirtana* party. At the annual Ratha-yatra in Puri, he and others would respond to the singing of Svarupa Damodara as Advaita Acarya danced in ecstasy.

When Gaitanya Mahaprabhu wanted to dance for Jagannatha’s pleasure, His seven *kirtana* groups would all join together. At those times He would deeply relish the sweet singing of Govindananda.

Kavi-karnapura says that, just as Murari Gupta was Hanuman in the pastimes of Lord Rama, Govindananda was the king of the monkeys, Sugriva, with whose help Ramacandra rescued His wife,Sita.

As already mentioned, Madhava and Vasudeva Ghosa were the brothers of Govinda Ghosa. In *krsna-lila,* as the gopis Rasollasa and Gunatunga, they would sing songs composed by Visakha.

According to varying tastes, opinions differ as to which of the three was the best singer. Srila Prabhupada, for instance, says that no singer in the world could compete with Madhava Ghosa.

Kaviraja Gosvami, however, says that Vasudeva’s songs about Lord Caitanya and Nityananda would melt even wood and stone. But Lord Caitanya chose to have Govinda lead and his brothers respond.

It is worth noting that Caitanya Mahaprabhu also chose Govinda to stay with Him in Puri, whereas He sent Madhava and Vasudeva back to Bengal to preach with Lord Nityananda.

In Panihati, Madhava Ghosa once sang about Krsna’s *dana-keli* pastime in a way that inspired Lord Nityananda and Gadadhara Dasa to dance wildly in the throes of loving ecstasy.

Srila Prabhupada comments that Vasudeva Ghosa’s songs about Lord Caitanya are as authorised as those of Vaisnava saints like Narottama Dasa Thakura, Bhaktivinoda Thakura, and Locana Dasa Thakura*.*

Let us offer our respects to all of the Ghosa brothers. Their mastery of congregational chanting—the only way to worship the Personality of Godhead in this age — greatly pleased Sri Caitanya Mahaprabhu.

Let us pray to Madhava and Vasu Ghosa that we may develop taste, attachment, and pure love for chanting Hare Krsna so that we too may please the Lord in His pastimes as a devotee and as a cowherd.

The *asrama* closest to Govinda-kunda houses the *samadhi* of Kasisvara Pandita, who utilised his great strength to serve the Lord as a body-guard, despite having been born in a *brahmana* family.

Kasisvara Pandita was a disciple of Isvara Puri and hence a godbrother of Caitanya Mahaprabhu, yet he spent his life rendering menial service to Lord Caitanya in Jagannatha Puri.

At the time of his passing from this world, Sri Isvara Puri ordered both Govinda and Kasisvara to render personal service to the Lord; thus they, and the Lord, were bound by their guru’s order.

Govinda travelled at once to Jagannatha Puri, while Kasisvara Pandita first went on pilgrimage. But both were warmly received and treated with great respect by Caitanya Mahaprabhu.

Govinda took care of Lord Caitanya’s bodily needs, and Kasisvara, being unusually strong, always walked before the Lord to keep others from touching Him. He would also serve *prasada* to the devotees.

When the Lord — unaware of His surroundings — danced with abandon, Kasisvara instructed devotees to stand in a protective circle around the Lord. If the Lord fell, Kasisvara would pick Him up.

Kavi-karnapura says that Sri Krsna’s personal servant named Bhrngara, who appeared in Vrndavana in Dvapara-yuga, appeared in Kali-yuga to serve Caitanya Mahaprabhu as Kasisvara Pandita.

Kasisvara Pandita and Govinda Dasa both showed by example that devotees should nor hesitate to serve a more spiritually advanced godbrother, especially if ordered to do so by the spiritual master.

In this regard, Srila Prabhupada comments:

**“If a godbrother is more enlightened and advanced in Krsna consciousness, one should accept him as almost equal to the spiritual master.”**

Still, devotees should not be eager to be served by their godbrothers or godsisters, for in the absence of the spiritual master’s authorisation, requesting personal service from a peer may result in downfall.

Accordingly, when Govinda Dasa arrived in Puri to serve Caitanya Mahaprabhu, the Lord was at first reluctant to accept him, saying, “The servant of the spiritual master is always respectable for Me.”

But Sri Caitanya Mahaprabhu consulted Sarvabhauma Bhattacarya and concluded that the order of the spiritual master supersedes any subordinate principle of Vaisnava etiquette.

Lord Caitanya, despite being pleased with his service, asked Kasisvara Pandita to go to Vrndavana and help with the worship of Govindaji. Kasisvara, however, did not want to leave the Lord.

Caitanya Mahaprabhu then gave a Deity of Himself to Kasisvara Pandita — a golden-formed Krsna playing the flute. That Deity, named Gaura-Govinda, is still worshipped alongside Govindaji.

Once in Vraja, in the area of the Yoga-pitha, Kasisvara Pandita performed *bhajana* with Vaisnavas like Raghunatha Bhatta Gosvami. Upon passing from this world, he returned to Krsna’s eternal service.

Let us offer him our respects with these words from *Sadhana-dipika:*

**“I worship Kasisvara Pandita, whose love and devotion brought Lord Caitanya in the form of a Deity to western India."**

In this and the three preceding chapters I have included brief biographies of some of the great Vaisnava associates of the Lord who are formally remembered and revered in New Vraja-dhama.

It is not possible to remember Krsna’s pastimes — not to speak of participating in them — without remembering those Vaisnavas who have already attained perfection by dint of their purity of heart.

Krsna says,

**“The pure devotee is always within the core of My heart, and I am always in the heart of the pure devotee.”**

From this we can tell that Krsna’s companions hold the key to attaining Him.

Since pure devotees are in the Lord's heart, we too can enter the Lord’s heart by connecting with such devotees through remembering them and serving their mission to preach Krsna consciousness.

Likewise, because Krsna is in the hearts of His pure devotees, Krsna will manifest Himself in our hearts as well if we keep pure devotees there by resolutely following their teachings and example.

In short, the secret of success in devotional service is to sincerely serve the pure devotees of the Lord, who like desire trees can quickly remove all obstacles that stand in the way of devotional perfection.

This prayer is therefore most suitably offered to great devotees, nay, to all devotees,

*vancha-kalpatarubhyas ca krpa-sindhubhya eva ca*  
*patitanam pavanebhyo vaisnavebhyo namo namah*

**“I offer my respectful obeisances unto all the Vaisnava devotees of the Lord. They can fulfil the desires of everyone, just like desire trees, and they are full of compassion for the fallen souls.”**

The dust from the feet, the water that has washed those feet, and the remnants of the food of pure Vaisnavas will free us from offences to Krsna’s names and from desires for enjoyment and liberation.

Without being freed from offences and selfish desires, how can we expect to attain service to Radha and Krsna? And aside from the company of pure Vaisnava *sadhus,* what other hope do we have?

With complete confidence that all unwanted things will he driven far away, let us cover ourselves in the dust of the Vaisnavas’ lotus feet. If we do, we will see the results for ourselves, all in good time.

Thakura Bhaktivinoda gives this example to warn us not to dishonour great Vaisnavas: A petty merchant is naturally respectful to a great merchant who owns many ships, and will not pick a fight with him.

Moreover, pure devotees who possess wealth do not hoard it; rather, practising saintly conduct, they convert their wealth into service to Vaisnavas, kindness to others, and worship of Radha and Krsna.

Humble service to the memory of pure Vaisnavas and to their sacred feet frees our hearts of impurities, impels our attraction to the Lord to mature, and awakens in us the urge to serve the divine couple.

At the mature stage of devotion, making the dust from the feet of a Vraja-vasi our only means of support, we will engage in *nama- sankirtana* and live in Vrndavana in our original spiritual form.

**\* \* \* \***

In the preceding sections, we have heard about the pas-time places and Vaisnava *samadhis* that lie to the north of the grazing pasture called Kerala, from Randhera to Radhavana.

Now let us hear about the pastime places to the south, which devotees pass through whenever they choose to follow the *outer parikrama* path and follow the circumference of the lotus of New Vraja-dhama.

From Kerala I looked out over the southern petals of New Vraja-dhama, petals that spread across the large agricultural field lying on the opposite side of the road that leads to Somogyvamos village.

Because this field slopes up and away from the road, it is visible from most of the elevated places in New Vraja-dhama. It has always been a part of my meditation on the topography of the holy *dhama.*

Just south of Bhulanvana is Camelivana, a place where wonderful pastimes of the divine couple occur. Some say that Camelivana and Bhulanvana are one forest, while others insist that they are separate.

Camelivana got its name because of the abundance of *cameli* trees that grow there; indeed, the enchanting fragrance of these trees perfumes the area of Camelivana in Bhauma Vrndavana.

The scent of *cameli* flowers induces amorous feelings, as does the scent of the oil made from its leaves. That scent thus makes personal dealings very pleasant, and so Krsna likes to meet Sri Radha there.

The forest of Camelivana has an intimate relationship with Radha and Krsna. Its trees blossom when Krsna is there — day or night — greeting Him with the wealth of its flowers’ bouquet.

South of the road at the far corner of Camelivana is Vanacari, which received its name because Krsna often wanders there, herding His cows, playing with His friends, or strolling with Radha.

We are accustomed to singing the song,

*(jaya) radha-madhava (jaya) kunja-bihari, (jaya) gopi-jana-vallabha (jaya) giri-vara-dhari*  
*(jaya) yasoda-nandana, (jaya) vraja-jana-ranjana, yamuna-tira-vana-cari*

The phrase *yamuna-tira-vana-cari* means “wandering in the forests along the banks of the Yamuna." Why does Krsna wander in forests like Camelivana? Has He nothing more pressing to do?

Srila Prabhupada answers:

**“Krsna, the Supreme Personality of Godhead, wanders on the hanks of the Yamuna to please the gopis, the cowherd boys, the birds, the bees, and the cows and calves.”**

Vanacari is a place where we can celebrate Krsna’s submission to the love of His devotees, as well as the extraordinary spiritual stature of the Vraja-vasis in all varieties: trees, birds, cows, humans, and more.

The Vraja-vasis who now associate with Krsna are either His eternal companions or practising devotees who have attained perfection. May they mercifully share with us their wealth of *ragatmika-bhakti*.

The *gopis* like to bring Radha and Krsna to Vanacari, to seat Them on a crystal throne within the *cameli* grove, and then to decorate Them with a variety of ornaments, all fashioned from flowers.

Rupa Gosvami describes that the divine couple’s ornaments, generally made of gold set with precious gems, can also be fashioned from flowers or from a combination of flowers and gems.

These flower decorations for Radha and Krsna include crowns, earrings, necklaces, armlets, sashes, bracelets, anklets, bodices, and ornaments for the forehead or hair, amongst other accessories.

Fashioning these ornaments is an esoteric art, and Sri Radha is the chief artist who teaches the others — beginning with Lalita-devi— the finer details of pleasing Krsna with such embellishments.

Other items of service — like umbrellas, couches, awnings, and cottages— can also be made from flowers. Perfected devotees learn valuable skills such as these in order to serve Radha and Krsna.

Making flower ornaments is one of sixty-four devotional arts. Srila Prabhupada teaches that, like Srimati Radharani, perfected devotees are trained in these arts before entering Krsna’s entourage:

**“Just like Srimati Radharani, She was trained up in sixty-four arts. Do you think to captivate Krsna is easy thing? How much qualified She must have been so that Lord Krsna was attracted.”**

Brahma also glorifies these arts,

**“I worship Govinda, the primeval Lord, residing in His own realm, Goloka, with Radha, resembling His own spiritual figure, the embodiment of the ecstatic potency —**

**“Possessed of the sixty-four artistic activities, in the company of Her confidantes** ***\[sakhis\],*** **embodiments of the extensions of Her bodily form, permeated and vitalised by His ever-blissful spiritual** ***rasa."***

These sixty-four fine arts — beginning with singing, playing musical instruments, dancing, theatrics, painting, and applying cosmetics — exist in their complete and perfected forms in Goloka Vrndavana.

When Krsna appeared in this world with His associates and abode, He also brought with Him these arts, which He and Balarama learned from Sandipani Muni, and the gopis learned from their mothers.

Conditioned souls learn and teach perverted reflections of these divine arts in a bid to enjoy their senses, but such efforts merely drive them further away from their true purpose — serving the Lord.

When we attain our spiritual forms we will again learn these arts, as Dasa Gosvami indicates:

**“I pray that Visakha, whose voice is sweeter than the cuckoo’s, may teach me the art of beautiful singing.”**

Just east of Camelivana is Sondha, Sannanda’s place of residence. Also known as Sunanda, Saunanda is Krsna’s uncle and Nanda Maharaja’s younger brother and trusted wise counselor.

Maharaja Sannanda has a whitish complexion and black hair with a few streaks of white. He wears dark-coloured clothes. His wife, Kuvalaya-devi, has a bluish complexion and wears reddish clothes.

Sannanda frequently offers wise counsel to his elder brother. For example, he advised Nanda to move from Gokula to Vrndavana and to accept Krsna’s proposal to worship Giriraja instead of Indra.

After Yasoda bound Krsna to a mortar, and Krsna pulled down the two *yamala-arjuna* trees, Nanda consulted the elders of Mahavana: “Please tell me what to do in the face of these calamities."

After hearing his brother’s appeal, Sannanda sat Krsna and Balarama on his lap and said to the king, “O Raja! We should all leave this place for a safer one, where there is no tribulation.”

Smelling Krsna’s head, Saunanda said, “This boy is as much the life and wealth of the Vraja-vasis as he is yours. By Lord Narayana's grace He escaped Putana, Sakatasura, Trnavarta, and the two falling trees.

“Who knows what dangers lie in store for Him and tor our children in this forest of Mahayana? I advise that we move to Vrndavana, and when all dangers have passed from this place we may return.”

Nanda Maharaja had full faith in his brother’s wisdom, hut to clear the doubts of his kinsmen he asked, “How tar is Vrndavana, what is it like there, and why do you say it is superior to Mahavana?”

Smiling sweetly at his brother, Sannanda replied, “O King! We know that Mathura-mandala is northeast of the country of Barhisat, south of the Yadus’ capital city, and west of the city of Sona.

“Once, on a visit to the palace of Vasudeva, I heard the sage Garga Muni say that even the king of holy places, Prayaga, worships Mathura-mandala, which is twenty-one *yojanas* in circumference.

“Vrndavana is the best of all forests within Mathura-mandala. Beautified by the pastimes of the Supreme Lord, it is inconceivably superior even to the abode of Narayana, Vaikuntha.

“In Vrndavana a mountain named Govardhana is splendidly manifest, the shores of the Yamuna are abodes of auspiciousness, and sub-forest after sub-forest adorn its twenty-four *krosas.*

“O King! What more can I say! With unlimited luscious grass, Vrndavana is an ideal place for cows. It is therefore a suitable place of residence for us *gopas* and *gopis,* including your two sons.”

After the assembly of wise cowherd men heard Sannanda’s speech, they all agreed that it would be pointless to stay in Mahavana any longer. They at once began preparing to move to Vrndavana.

Some years later, as Nandaraja was preparing to worship Indra for prosperity and liberation, Krsna requested him to desist and instead worship the cows, *brahmanas,* and GovardhanaHill.

Nanda Maharaja was bewildered. He thought it his duty to maintain the tradition of worshipping Indra. But he also wanted to satisfy his son, who was his very life’s breath. What should he do?

Again Sannanda, who was well versed in the principles of religion, resolved the king’s quandary. He explained that carrying out Krsna’s suggestion would also accomplish the worship of Indra.

Smiling, Sannanda then addressed Krsna: “O son of Nanda! Since You have such a wealth of transcendental knowledge, tell us how You would worship Govardhana, the cows, and the *brahmanas*.”

After hearing the wise Sannanda support Krsna’s proposal, Nanda Maharaja — and after him, the other Vraja-vasis — agreed to worship Giriraja with the ingredients collected for the Indra-yajna.

Sannanda set up his capital on the edge of Camelivana; thus the town Sondha was named after him. With the words of Raghunatha Dasa Gosvami, let us praise Sannanda-gopa:

**“Nanda Maharaja’s younger brother Sannanda is very gentle, affectionate, and generous. His complexion is fair and his beard black. Ever so devoted to his brother, he is the father of Sunanda-gopi.**

**“He pleases Krsna by giving Him palatable dishes made with buffalo yogurt and by worshipping Him with each breath. May Lord Krsna’s uncle Sannanda, the master of a great buffalo herd, protect us.”**

**\* \* \* \***

Further south of Vanacari, on the border of Vrndavana, is Khambi. Lord Balarama is very fond of this small hillock, upon which the boys play games while their cows graze on the rich grass below.

Krsna and Balarama once explained to Their friends that Vrndavana ends at Khambi. Venturing beyond it, They said, would put one under the jurisdiction of the modes of nature, which hold no sway in Vraja.

Balarama drew a line along the summit of this hill and said, “Inside this line we are protected by the Vaikuntha atmosphere of Vraja; outside the line we can be victimised by Maya.”

The cowherd boys were all transcendentalists, completely free from material influence, but they thought themselves to be ordinary persons whose only refuge was Krsna and the land of Vraja.

They actually thought that if they crossed the line they would risk becoming demons like Kamsa. But still, as spirited young boys, they could not resist the urge to take up Balarama’s challenge.

At first the boys gingerly — and momentarily — stepped over the line. Then they darted out some distance and ran back. Finally, they ventured out a ways and jumped up and down, taunting Maya.

When it came time for lunch, the boys sat together beneath the trees, safely within Vraja, and exuberantly boasted how they had journeyed into the land of Maya but did not succumb to her illusions.

Balarama said, “You cannot fall into illusion because Krsna shelters you wherever you go. But those who are not favoured by Him will be victimised by temptation outside of Vraja and forget all about Him.”

The cowherd boys were mortified at the thought of forgetting Krsna. With one voice, and with tearful eyes, they petitioned Balarama to warn others never to go beyond the line at the top of the hill.

Taking compassion on His friends, who themselves were showing so much compassion tor others, Balarama used the blade of His plough to cut a gigantic stone pillar out of the mountainside.

While the *gopas* looked on in amazement, Balarama lifted the pillar *(stamba)* and set it in place on the peak of the mountain, saying, “This marks the boundary between Krsna’s realm and Maya’s realm.”

Since that incident, the mountain and the area that surrounds it have both been called Khambi. Balarama’s pillar still stands today, marking the boundary between the material and spiritual worlds.

The Vraja-vasis are immune to the effects of *maya;* their minds and hearts are eternally fixed on Krsna’s lotus feet. To them, the difference between the spiritual and material worlds is negligible.

But conditioned souls who have turned away from Krsna — even the most accomplished *karmis, jnanis,* and *yogis* — are dragged into the domain of Maya to be punished by her threefold miseries.

But if, by the grace of the Vaisnavas and the spiritual master, these conditioned souls regain Krsna’s shelter, they can fix their minds on the Lord once again, and in due time become immune from *maya.*

The pillar at Khambi is a reminder for conditioned souls — especially for the devotees who reside in New Vraja-dhama — to never again turn away from Krsna and leave the shelter of Vrndavana.

As Jagadananda Pandita says,

*krsna-bahirmukha hand bhoga-vancha*  
*kare* *nikata-stha maya tare japatiya dhare*

**“When a living entity wants to enjoy material nature, he is at once victimised by** ***maya."***

The forest of Hasyavana is the tip of New Vraja-dhama’s southern petal. This forest is home to sacred Hasanapura, the capital city of the secondary mellow of humour *(hasya-rasa).*

Liberated souls whose pure chanting allows them to hear echoes reverberating in the trees will recognise them as the gopas’ laughter and will themselves be overtaken by laughing spells and smiling.

Once, the gopas sat in a circle beneath a large tree at Hasanapura, eating their lunches and questioning Krsna, who appeared to be sitting beside each and every one of them simultaneously.

The boys asked, “Hey, Gopala! Tell us truthfully and don’t mislead us. How did You, then just a baby, kill Putana and Trnavarta? And how did You, when only a few years older, liberate Aghasura?”

One of the boys added, “Do You know some demon-killing mantra that You have not shared with us?” Krsna just shook His head, smiling. “He doesn’t trust us with His mantra,” another boy teased.

A third boy, his mouth full of food, mumbled, “Maybe Krsna’s power is not in mantras but in the amulets His mother ties around His wrist and waist. Go ahead! You can tell us. We won’t steal them from You.”

The gopas had a good-natured laugh at Krsna’s expense, and Krsna drank their joking words and friendly laughter through His ears, as if they were liquefied, tasty, honey-sweet draughts of love.

Krsna is dearer to the cowherd boys than they are to themselves; their every word and gesture is an expression of divine love, completely free from envy or acrimony. They can only love Krsna.

Suddenly Krsna raised His right hand and caught the boys’ attention with the *jnana-mudra.* “Friends!” He said. “I will disclose My secret to you. But do you have the faith to accept what I say?”

The boys looked at one another and then at the sweet smile spreading across Krsna’s lips and the twinkle in His eye. Krsna’s expression somehow created an enchanting aura that obliged them to say yes.

Krsna moved His head from side to side in approval, and the cowherd boys, as if hound to His smile by ropes of sweetness, also moved their hypnotised heads from side to side, like mirror images.

“All right,” Krsna said, “because you have faith in Me, I will reveal to you the source of My powers.” The cowherd boys all leaned forward, eager to hear every word of what Krsna was about to say.

“At the time of My name-giving ceremony, Garga Muni disclosed that I am just like Narayana, and that by My power I destroy all obstacles, as well as anyone or anything that threatens My devotees.”

The cowherd boys nodded in recognition as Krsna continued, “What you consider superhuman feats are nothing to Me; for Me, killing the demons who terrorise Vraja is as easy as binding a calf.

“O My friends! Know for certain that although I am Your friend, I am also the Supreme Personality of Godhead, for whom nothing is difficult, and who has appeared as a *gopa* out of love for you.”

When the cowherd boys heard these words of absolute wisdom, they thought to themselves, “How wonderful! Not only is Krsna our village hero, He is also the Supreme Narayana. He is so clever.”

The *gopas* gazed at Krsna’s sweet face and thought, “Amazing! He is the Supreme Lord.” They dove and surfaced in waves of smiles and laughter, and their hearts swelled with pride for their Gopala.

The boys laughed at the endless stream of new wonders Krsna was able to manifest. And Krsna laughed at the *gopas’* boundless love for Him, which even knowledge of His divinity could not impinge upon.

Indeed, the more the gopas thought of Krsna as God, the more they loved Him as one of them. And the more Krsna saw the *gopas'* pure love for Him, the more eager He was to be their cowherd friend.

In this way the exchange of sweet laughter between Krsna and the gopas intensified, infecting the forest creatures, birds, trees, creepers, and even insects with its magic, until all of Hasyavana was laughing.

All glories to the mellow of laughter, which is especially enjoyable amongst youths like the gopas and gopis, which has Krsna as its cause, and which gives rise to jubilation, laziness, and concealment.

All glories to Hasyavana, where the sweet laughter of Krsna and the gopas still permeates the ether, and where pure-hearted devotees can hear and taste this wonderful laughing ecstasy of love.

North of Hasanapura is Mahali, which was known especially for the Krsna-conscious standard of its residents. Srila Prabhupada gave us this same standard, a standard his sincere followers emulate.

Indeed, he wanted all ISKCON devotees, including those in New Vraja-dhama, to reflect the conduct of the perfect Vaisnava gentlemen and Vaisnavi gentlewomen found in Mahali.

Because of the dhama’s influence on Mahali, its residents always had the names of Krsna upon their lips, His pastimes within their minds, and attachment to Him within their hearts.

A resident of Mahali, before speaking to another person, always said, “Krsna, Krsna, Krsna!” And in replying to any enquiry, a resident of Mahali always preceded his reply by saying, “Rama, Rama, Rama!”

Whatever they did, wherever they were, the residents of Mahali always dressed in devotional attire, with tilaka and neck beads. And they always addressed each other hy their initiated names.

The devotees of Mahali had varieties of work and service, but they made no distinct ion amongst themselves as to who was higher or lower. Their dealings were free of envy and malice.

Srila Prabhupada declares this Vaikuntha atmosphere—just as it appeared in Mahali— to be achievable anywhere by the potency of transcendental sound vibration.

Indeed, Srila Prabhupada says that the purpose of the Krsna consciousness movement is to create Vaikuntha, even in this material world, through the transcendental potency of nama-sankirtana.

**\* \* \* \***

In Bhauma Vrndavana, the pastime place after Mahali is Marava-grama, Markandeya Rsi’s *asrama* on the bank of the river Yamuna. In New Vraja-dhama, the Yamuna is not visible in this area.

The history of Markandeya Rsi demonstrates how determined and patient a practising devotee must be in order to attain the perfectional state of Krsna consciousness — *vraja-bhakti.*

Born in the previous day of Brahma, Markandeya, after being initiated, accepted a vow of lifelong celibacy. He then strictly followed the Vedic process and worshipped the Supreme Lord.

For six lifetimes of Manu (manvantaras), the sage steadily worshipped the Lord in five forms: the sacrificial fire, the sun, his spiritual master, the brahmanas, and the Supersoul within his heart.

Then, in the seventh manvantara, King Indra, fearful of Markandeya’s austerities, commanded Cupid to break the sage’s vows. But despite his best efforts, Cupid was not successful.

Lord Hari, pleased by Markandeya Rsi’s determination, appeared before him as Nara-Narayana Rsi. The sage duly worshipped the Lord, who asked Markandeya to request a benediction from Him.

Markandeya requested to see the workings of the material energy. Lord Nara-Narayana happily granted that boon and then returned to Badarikasrama, His own abode in the Himalayas.

One evening, as Markandeya was worshipping the Lord, the waters of devastation flooded the three worlds, leaving the sage tossing alone in the fearsome waters, until he floated hy a banyan tree.

Upon a leaf of that tree lay a baby boy, whose effulgent beauty attracted Markandeya. When the sage approached the child, the baby inhaled and effortlessly drew Markandeya into His body.

Within the baby’s body, Markandeya could see the universe as it was before the flood. As soon as he had seen this display, however, the Lord exhaled, casting the Muni hack into the waters of annihilation.

Markandeya realised that the baby was the Supersoul within his heart. But as the sage tried to embrace the divine infant, the Lord at once disappeared, as did the entire scene of devastation.

Once again Markandeya Rsi was in his asrama, left to contemplate what and whom he had just seen. As he meditated, Lord Siva appeared. Pleased with the sage, Siva offered him a benediction.

Markandeya realised he had been hasty in requesting a benediction from Nara-Narayana, so this time he was careful to ask for something that would directly benefit his spiritual advancement.

The sage thus said, *bhagavaty acyutam bhaktim tat-paresu tatha tvayi*: “I ask to have unfailing devotion for the Supreme Personality of Godhead and for His dedicated devotees, especially you.”

Pleased to offer this benediction, Lord Siva also blessed the sage with transcendental realisation of the Supreme Lord, which would fulfil Markandeya Rsi’s quest to understand the identity of Sri Hari.

At the start of his austerities, Markandeya Rsi thought that the Supersoul was the Supreme Lord, who first appeared to him as Nara-Narayana and then as the infant on the banyan leaf.

But by contact with baby Krsna, Markandeya Rsi was blessed with the company of a fully realised devotee, Lord Siva, by whose grace the sage came to understand that Krsna is the Supreme Lord.

This is confirmed in a conversation between Markandeya and Vajra, who asked, “Who is that child you see again and again at the end of each millennium? I am extremely eager to know.”

Markandeya replied, “Again and again I saw the Supreme Lord, the master of the universes. But as each kalpa came to an end, I was bewildered by maya and unable to comprehend who He was.

“But when this devastation had passed, I could understand that He was your father, Aniruddha, the master of the universes. He was an avatara manifested from your greatgrandfather, Lord Krsna.”

After seven manvantaras, spanning two days of Brahma, Markandeya Rsi understood that Krsna is the Supreme. He came to Marava-grama to engage in bhajana under the protection of Sri Vrndavana-dhama.

Markandeya Rsi is the exemplar of determined faith (drdha-vrata), required by Krsna for success in spiritual life. The sage’s quest continned for 2,147,040,000 years. How fortunate are we?

What took Markandeya millennia to realise, Srila Prabhupada very quickly reveals to us upon our first joining his movement: Krsna, the cowherd boy, is the Supreme Personality of Godhead.

A professor of the Puranas, Markandeya Rsi now propounds this knowledge and the glories of sankirtana. He writes, “One who pleases Krsna with sweet songs attains the result of studying the Vedas."

Lord Siva also blessed Markandeya to live until the end of this day of Brahma, and so Markandeya still travels around the world, often residing on a hill in Marava, fully absorbed in pure devotion to Krsna.

Following the course of the Yamuna from Marava-grama are two villages, Tilakagadhi and Manigadhi, which are both connected to the history of an unnamed, hut empowered Babaji who once lived there.

This babaji performed his bhajana in a cottage just outside Tilakagadhi. In time the villagers were respectful to him, treating him as their local saint and a representative of Krsna.

The Babaji impressed upon the villagers who had developed faith in him the importance of chanting Lord Krsna’s holy names and decorating their bodies with Gaudiya Vaisnava tilaka.

Gradually the men began to mark their bodies with gopicandana and regularly chant rounds of the maha-mantra. In turn, their families and eventually the entire village followed suit.

Because everyone in this village wore tilaka — broadcasting that they were the property of Krsna — the village came to he known as Tilakagadhi, a name and a practice that endures to the present day.

One day, as the Babaji and some of his followers walked along the bank of the Yamuna chanting Hare Krsna, a brilliantly shining object amongst the stones on the riverside caught the babaji’s eye.

He picked up a fist-sized gem, which shimmered with an unearthly lustre and felt very pleasing to the touch. Some followers said it was a diamond, others insisted that it was a gem from Svarga.

As he tossed the jewel in the air as if it were a hall, the Babaji said, “No, this is a cintamani stone from celestial Vrndavana." Hearing this, his followers imagined the priceless things the gem could create.

To the amazement of everyone present, the Babaji suddenly threw the gem into the middle of the river’s blackish water, forever to he lost in the depths and currents of the Yamuna.

The babaji said, “Great wealth is of value in the hands of those empowered to use it in Krsna’s service, hut an obstacle on the path of devotional service for ordinary souls like me. Better we just chant.”

In time, people settled in the place where the babaji had found the cintamani gem (mani). The village that grew up there on the hanks of the Yamuna River was therefore called Manigadhi.

Rupa Gosvami instructs us to use the things of Krsna’s creation in service to the Lord. Such a principle should be practised only to the extent that material things do not distract us from Krsna’s service.

Further along the Yamuna are two places connected hy a shared pastime: Chinna-pahari and nearby Basau. There Krsna and His friends once again displayed their skill at stealing milk products.

Once the cowherd boys were feeling exceedingly hungry, despite having recently had a snack. They decided that they would he satisfied hy either yogurt or butter hut preferred to relish both.

By that time Krsna had more or less abandoned His habit of stealing milk products, having learned to simply ask for them instead. Still, His reputation followed Him, and the boys reminded Him of His skill.

The boys rubbed their stomachs and grimaced as if in pain, saying, “Gopala! If we do not eat soon, You will have to cremate our bodies on the hanks of the Yamuna. Please! Can’t You do something?”

One boy said, “You can kill great demons with Your arms and melt stone with Your flute-song. Can’t You use Your skills at thievery to pilfer us some delicacies from that village over there?”

Krsna looked in the direction of a cluster of neatly placed houses, whose barns indicated that the residents were cowherds and would surely have stocks of milk, butter, ghee, and curd.

Not one to let His devotees suffer, or to neglect a chance to hone His world-famous skills, Krsna looked at Balarama and with a movement of His eyebrows invited His brother to join Him in the caper.

Krsna and Balarama placed Their turbans and ornaments in the care of Their friends, who waited in the forest as the two brothers darted like rabbits from bush to tree until They reached the back of a house.

Turning around to Their friends, They placed Their forefingers to Their lips and then disappeared into the shadows of a small lane, determined to find the cowherds’ storehouse and sample its contents.

Barely able to control their glee, the boys whispered to each other, “What if They get caught?” “That’s impossible!” “I wonder what They will bring back.” “Whatever it is, I hope there’s a lot of it!”

Suddenly Subala said, “Here They come!" All eyes watched Krsna and Balarama run out of the village. The two brothers were grinning from ear to ear and They both carried pots under each arm.

The cowherd boys greeted the brothers with muted cheers, but Balarama, breathing hard, said, “I think someone might have seen Us. Let’s go before the villagers come after Us with sticks.”

The boys sped through the forest, laughing and shrieking with glee, either passing the large pots to one another as they ran, or holding them tightly to their chests as if the collected wealth of Kuvera.

Finally the gopas reached a shady grove on the bank of the Yamuna. Exhausted from running, they sat (basa on the roots of an expansive banyan tree and eagerly partook of their ill-gotten gains.

The grove where Krsna and His friends enjoyed the special taste that stealing (chinna) lends to milk products is called Basau. And the village where those milk products originated is known as Chinna-pahari.

May that dark-complexioned thief who stole the sins of surrendered devotees, the life-breath of Putana, the garments of the young gopis, and the butter of Chinna-pahari, also steal our hearts.

## Appendix 28

**Deliberation on the Ages of Krsna and His Associates**

(This appendix follows from a description of Krsna’s playful nature in chapter eighteen — as it is displayed in His childhood, boyhood, and youth—for the purpose of addressing certain questions regarding His and His associates’ ages.)

In the spiritual science of Krsna consciousness, there are transcendental stimuli that both nourish a practitioner’s specific devotional mood and awaken a perfected devotee’s ecstatic love. Such stimuli are known as uddipanas. Spiritual awakening in both conditioned and pure souls is inspired by Krsna’s appearance, activities, or qualities. Krsna’s age is one of those qualities; indeed, since Krsna appears and behaves differently according to the age He happens to be at any given time, we may say that Krsna’s various ages are themselves stimuli that have an effect on other stimuli. Therefore, hearing about the specific qualities that accompany Krsna’s three main ages is of paramount importance in awakening our devotion to Him. The purpose of this appendix, then, is to study the ages of Krsna and of His associates in order to enhance our devotion to and love for them.

As we shall see, Krsna’s features in different ages display characteristics remarkable enough to cause wonder in devotees and confusion in non-devotees. The occasional anomalous statement of scripture and the inconceivable nature of Krsna Himself are often the causes such confusion. But that confusion is dispelled by the wisdom of the acaryas, who are also able to answer that oft-asked question: How old is Krsna?

May Sri Syamasundara, the gopis’ eternally adolescent cowherd lover, who also appears as a child to give joy to His parents and as a boy to play with His friends, be pleased with us.

**Krsna’s Three Primary Ages**

Rupa Gosvami states that in the course of His pastimes Krsna displays the three ages preceding adulthood — childhood (kaumara), boyhood (pauganda), and youth *(kaisora).*

Each of these three ages lasts for five years. Of note to the reader, however, is that in the Vedic culture this calculation begins with the birth of the child. In other words, the celebration of Krsna’s birth is considered His first birthday. His childhood (kaumara) years then extend to the end of His fifth year. Boyhood (pauganda) begins with His sixth birthday, extending to the end of His tenth year. And youth (kaisora) begins with His eleventh birthday and extends to the end of His fifteenth year — just before Krsna’s sixteenth birthday. Just before reaching His sixteenth year, Krsna’s transcendental body stops aging. Thereafter He is referred to as yauvana, or nava-yauvana, an ever fresh youth whose transcendental body everlastingly possesses the characteristics of the last phase of His kaisora age.

Devotees attracted to the parental mellow are most attached to Krsna’s childhood, those most attracted to the fraternal mood arc fond of His boyhood, and devotees inclined to enjoy an amorous relationship with Krsna especially relish His youth. Of the three ages, youth is appreciated by all devotees, regardless of their transcendental relationships with Krsna. Youth is also the only age Krsna displays outside of Vraja, in Mathura and Dvaraka.

Each of Krsna’s three primary ages is subdivided into three one-year-eight-month sub-periods called adya (“beginning”), madhya (“middle”), and sesa (“end”). Krsna displays unique characteristics in each of these sub-periods, in addition to the general characteristics of the full period of each primary age.

Now let us hear about some of Krsna’s features during these three divisions of His three primary ages.

At the beginning of childhood Krsna is very chubby, His limbs are as tender as a blue lotus, the edges of His eyes are white, and His small teeth are just beginning to show. Mother Yasoda cannot take her eyes off her son as He sucks His big toe or throws His legs up in the air, sometimes crying, sometimes laughing. Yasoda covers her son with protective talismans, hanging a tiger’s claw around His neck, tying a cord around His waist and a string around His wrist, and painting tilaka marks on His body. She also smears mascara around Krsna’s eyes to protect Him from the glare of the sun; there is nothing, however, she can smear on her own eyes that can distract them from the beauty of her son, not even for an instant.

Towards the end of the first period of childhood Krsna occasionally crawls about. But when He reaches the middle of childhood, although rarely dressed in clothes, He becomes proficient at crawling. At that time He regularly pleases His parents with the beauty of His swaying locks of hair and the charming way He speaks in broken language as He begins to form words. Although the holes in Krsna’s earlobes and septum are eternally present, they become visible at that age and thus Yasoda places small pearls in them to match the string of bells she ties around His waist. Perhaps the most notable feature of this age is Krsna’s discovery that He can cat things other than His mother’s breast milk, such as butter and other delicacies, which He does with enthusiasm, although not all the time.

By the final stage of childhood Krsna is on His feet walking about. He and His friends then begin to herd calves and play games on the outskirts of Gokula. Dressed in a short dhoti, He carries a stick in His hand in imitation of the cowherd men and wears a peacock feather in His hair, which is braided and tied in a topknot. Once, when Krsna was late in returning home, Nanda Maharaja climbed to the roof of His palace, from where he could easily watch his son. From that vantage point Nanda relayed Krsna’s antics to his wife below.

Let us remember the embodiment of parental love and how she responds to the beauty of her darling boy.

*nava-kuvalaya-dama-syamalam komalangam*  
*vicalad-alaka-bhrnga kranta-netrambujantam*  
*vraja-bhuvi viharantam putram alokayanti*  
*vraja-pati-dayitasit prasnavotpida-digdha*

**“On seeing her son, who had the complexion of a fresh, blue lotus, who had a soft body with lotus eyes touched by bee-like locks of hair, Yasoda became wet with the milk forcefully flowing from her breasts.”**

Let us also meditate upon how Srila Prabhupada — in the last days of his life, as he compiled his Bhaktivedanta purports on the first chapters of Srimad-Bhagavatam’s Tenth Canto — envisioned child Krsna and elaborated upon the Lord’s joking and play.

Sukadeva Gosvami said:

**“Krsna is yajna-bhuk — that is, He eats only offerings of yajna— but to exhibit His childhood pastimes, He now sat with His flute tucked between His waist and His tight cloth on His right side and with His horn bugle and cow-driving stick on His left. Holding in His hand a very nice preparation of yogurt and rice, with pieces of suitable fruit between His fingers, He sat like the whorl of a lotus flower, looking forward toward all His friends, personally joking with them and creating jubilant laughter among them as He ate. At that time, the denizens of heaven were watching, struck with wonder at how the Personality of Godhead, who eats only in yajna, was now eating with His friends in the forest.”**

Srila Prabhupada gives his purport:

**“When Krsna was eating with His cowherd boyfriends, a certain bumblebee came there to take part in the eating. Thus Krsna joked, ‘Why have you come to disturb My brahmana friend Madhumangala? You want to kill a brahmana. This is not good.’ All the boys would laugh and enjoy, speaking such joking words while earing. Thus the inhabitants of the higher planets were astonished at how the Supreme Personality of Godhead, who eats only when yajna is offered, was now eating like an ordinary child with His friends in the forest.”**

**\* \* \* \***

The next age is Krsna’s jolly boyhood. In its first phase He ventures out into the forest to herd the calves with His friends, whom He fills with bliss by mastering different games, wrestling holds, and dancing steps. He wears a short silken dhoti and is adorned with ginja-berry necklaces, a peacock feather crown, jasmine garlands, and flowers on His ears.

After having visited Vrndavana when Krsna was just a baby, a group of travelling minstrels returned to Vraja during His boyhood. They were amazed to see what a beautiful boy He had become. They said, “O Krsna! Your abdomen is like a beautiful asvattha leaf, Your throat has the three lines of a conch, and Your lips have stolen the redness of rubies. Like a moon on earth, Your attractive features give bliss to the eyes of Your friends \[as they do to ours\].”

In the middle stage of boyhood Krsna is fully equipped with the implements and dress of a cowherd and, having gained expertise in this duty, He herds His calves to distant pastures. He wears a colourful turban decorated with a golden rope for tying calves and bristling with flower buds and peacock feathers. He also holds a black, gold- capped staff in His hand to control His calves, play-fight with His friends, and to declare Himself the heir to Vraja’s throne.

That His facial features gain finesse at this stage is visible in His raised nose and neck as well as His rounded chin and cheeks. But the most striking ingredient of Krsna’s cloud-blue beauty at this age is the excessive sweetness that emanates from His form, sweetness reminiscent of the first phase of kaisora. In other words, even in boyhood Krsna displayed some qualities of youth.

Krsna’s final stage of boyhood is marked with striking features and a changing mental disposition. Locks of hair, like bumblebees, dance playfully on His forehead. His hair is so long that it reaches to His hips when braided. Krsna’s wide shoulders, tilted turban, saffron tilaka, and the lotus He twirls in His hand are the talk of Vraja’s young cowherd girls, who have recently become the subjects of the discreet comments Krsna makes to His confidential friends about His attraction to the opposite sex.

Referring to Krsna’s awakening adolescence and the characteristics in Him that arouse the interest of the gopis, Subala once said, “O Kesava! Your attractive clothing—the tilted turban, the play lotus in Your hand, the golden tilaka on Your forehead, and the dot of musk — are making me, courageous Subala, completely dizzy! How much more will this affect the naturally susceptible gopis!”

The third of Krsna’s three primary ages is youth. As already mentioned in this book, Lord Caitanya accepted Krsna’s fresh youth as the best of the Lord’s three primary ages.

While meeting with Raghupati Upadhyaya in Adaila-grama (on the opposite side of the Ganges River from Prayaga), Lord Caitanya asked him, “Of the three ages of Krsna known as childhood, boyhood, and fresh youth, which do you consider best?"

Raghupati Upadhyaya replied, *vayah kaisorakam dhyeyam*: “Fresh youth is the best age.”  
Next the Lord enquired, “Amongst all the mellows, which do you consider best?”  
Raghupati Upadhyaya replied, “The mellow of amorous love is superior.”  
Sri Caitanya Mahaprabhu then said, “You have certainly given first-class conclusions.”

Youth has the same three divisions as childhood and boyhood: beginning, middle, and end. I shall now briefly describe these three stages of youth from the vantage point of the gopis’ amorous mood, concluding with fresh youth (nava-yauvana), the third and final stage, and the source of Krsna’s other ages and forms. This is the eternal age of the Supreme Lord. At this age the Lord relishes the fountainhead of all other mellows of love of Godhead, the mellow of amorous love, madhurya- or ujjvala-rasa.

At the first stage of youth there is a marked change in Krsna’s appearance. His hodily hue becomes dark and glossy, more like the splendour of a sapphire t han the colour of a blue lotus. The soft, fine hair appearing on His limbs adds to both the darkness and texture of His complexion. Because His eyebrows become thicker, they now resemble Cupid’s legendary bow. Well aware of His new-found opulence, Krsna is adept at shooting arrow-like glances from this bow. And finally, whereas the whites of His eyes were previously clear, their corners are now tinged with a distinctive reddish hue.

Krsna’s appearance is further enhanced by a new manner of dressing - like a dancing actor on stage — and by elaborate, knee - length Vaijayanti garlands. And while both the peacock feather and flute are not new companions in Krsna’s amorous adventures, they are now proud emblems of His youth. To this it should be added that Krsna’s ability to play sweet, mind-enchanting flute melodies has become even more entrancing. According to some gopis, this is due to Krsna’s having taken lessons from Srimati Radharani.

Two further bodily characteristics appear at this age without any conscious effort on Krsna’s part: the sharpness of His fingernails and the redness of His teeth. Instead of being round like full moons, Krsna’s fingernails appear like a line of sharp arrows. And, as a result of chewing pan, Krsna’s attractive teeth are red like dawn.

The gopis once began to speak about Krsna’s transition into youth, but when they thought of Him, Cupid caught their tongues. Sukadeva Gosvami reveals their minds in the following famous verse:

*barhapidam nata-vara-vapuh karnayoh karnikaram*  
*bibhrad vasah kanaka-kapisam vaijayantim ca malam*  
*randhran venor adhara-sudhayapurayan gopa-vrndair*  
*vrndaranyam sva-pada-ramanam pravisad gita-kirtih*

**“Wearing a peacock-feather ornament upon His head, blue karnikara flowers on His ears, a yellow garment as brilliant as gold, and the Vaijayanti garland, Lord Krsna exhibited His transcendental form as the greatest of dancers as He entered the forest of Vrndavana, beautifying it with the marks of His footprints. He filled the holes of His flute with the nectar of His lips, and the cowherd boys sang His glories.”**

The middle stage of Krsna’s youth is characterised by maturity in His limbs and the amorous nature of His activities. As He enters His thirteenth year Krsna’s arms become muscular, His chest broad, and His thighs clearly masculine. The wealth of His sweetness is visible in the increased lustre of His face, His endearing soft smile, and His restless eyes that constantly agitate the minds of young girls by hinting at amorous intentions. Just as Krsna perfects His flute playing in the first stage of youth, He now perfects His singing, enchanting the world and inducing chaste women to break their vows of marriage.

Now abundantly endowed with the jewels of amorous attraction, Krsna distributes His wealth to the young girls of Vraja like a true philanthropist, joking with them and teasing them in diverse ways, and fulfilling His romantic desires with them in the groves of Vrndavana. Most significantly, at this age Krsna first enjoys the rasa dance with the gopis, thereby disclosing that rarest and most exalted of love festivals to the residents of all three worlds.

Now let us hear about the final portion of youth. Already mentioned as nava-yauvana, this stage of Krsna’s youth, also called sesa-kaisora, is Krsna’s original, eternal age. To describe this age is to depict the constitutional identity of the Supreme Personality of Godhead. Therefore this description is superior to descriptions of all previous ages. This is the meaning of Raghupati Upadhyaya’s words, vayah kaisorakam dhyeyam: “Fresh youth is the best age.”

Jiva Gosvami comments that the word sesa, or “end,” means that Krsna remains (sisyate) eternally at this age because it is the most complete condition of youth. And Rupa Gosvami says:

*atha sesam —*  
*purvati,py adhikotkarsam badham angani bibhraty*  
*tri-vali-vyaktir ity adyam kaisore carame sati*

**“When the final portion of youth begins, all \[Krsna’s\] limbs become more alluring than previously, with three lines clearly manifest on His navel.”**

Commenting further on the word sesa, Jiva Gosvami refers to his own aforementioned definition as well as the word carama, a word related to carati, which indicates that after making its appearance, this youthful age continues (carati) eternally, neither declining nor surrendering its beauty, as childhood and boyhood do, to the next oncoming age. Sesa-kaisora develops through a period of one year and eight months to its conclusion, which is just prior to the stage of mature youth. This sesa-kaisora stage — characterised by the intense feelings of separation Krsna goes through as He prepares to depart from Vraja — heralds the arrival of the Lord’s eternal fresh youth.

Srila Prabhupada also gives his conclusive opinion on Krsna’s eternal nava-yauvana age, which he calls “pre-youth” in the following purport. Srila Prabhupada writes:

**“Krsna does not grow like an ordinary human being, even though He exhibits His pastimes of childhood, boyhood and pre-youth. When He reaches the age of pre-youth, kaisora, I le does not grow any older. He simply remains in His kaisora age. He is therefore described in the Brahma-Samhita (5.33) as nava-yauvana... This nava-yauvana, or pre-youth, is the eternal transcendental form of Krsna. Krsna never grows older than nava-yauvana.”**

In nava-yauvana Krsna’s physical appearance and personal qualities reach full maturity, thus fuelling His unparalleled amorous pastimes and filling the gopis with unprecedented bliss. Great devotees have given numerous descriptions of Krsna’s physical appearance at this age, which can also he appreciated by the effect that appearance has on others.

Krsna’s paramount sweetness at this age defeats Cupid’s arrows, makes women lose all composure, is a playground for the sixty-four amorous arts, and causes an awakening of amorous love not relishable during His previous ages.

The effect Krsna’s eternal youth has on the gopis is described in the following way:

**“O Krsna! Today Your age of youth, in the role of guru, is teaching the golden gopis the art of whispering in each other’s ears, the methods of making verses of praise for messengers when alone, cleverness in cheating husbands, practice in sneaking to the forest at night, deafness to the words of elders, and rapt hearing of the flute sound.”**

Krsna’s youthful beauty is not the only thing that inspires His amorous dealings with the gopis. His supreme dominion and expertise in ruling over the kingdom of romantic love also has its influence.

In his samhita, Lord Brahma also describes Krsna’s eternally fresh youth. The following verse was much appreciated by Caitanya Mahaprabhu and is well known by Gaudiya Vaisnavas.

*advaitam acyutam anadim ananta-rupam*  
*adyam purana-purusam nava-yauvanam ca*  
*vedesu durlabham adurlabham atma-bhaktau*  
*govindam adi-purusam tam aham bhajami*

**“I worship the Supreme Personality of Godhead, Govinda \[Krsna\], who is the original person — absolute, infallible, without beginning. Although expanded into unlimited forms, He is still the same original,the oldest, and the person always appearing as a fresh youth. Such eternal, blissful, all-knowing forms of the Lord are usually understood hy the best Vedic scholars, but they are always manifest to pure, unalloyed devotees.”**

One sentence of this verse is particularly relevant to our discussion: “Although expanded into unlimited forms, He is still the same original, the oldest, and the person always appearing as a fresh youth.” Here the creator confirms that Krsna’s childhood and boyhood forms in the material world expand from His youth, because His youthful form is His original eternal form in Goloka. Srila Bhaktisiddhanta Sarasvati Thakura confirms this in his purport:

**“Though He indulges in exhibiting the pastimes of births, etc., still He is without a beginning. Though He disappears after the pastimes of His appearance, still He is eternal. Though without origin, yet He is with an origin in His pastime of appearance; and although eternal in essence, He is still a person in the full bloom of youth.”**

In other words, Krsna’s form of youth is not a result of growing from childhood, it is eternal.

In describing Krsna’s three primary ages, Sanatana Gosvami points out that although they each have their own distinctive features, they also have some characteristics in common. In other words, endowed with qualities of all ages at any point in time, Krsna is able to enchant devotees of all mellows of devolion, thus adding an eternal freshness to His personal qualities. For instance, even as a baby Krsna may show some interest in boyhood sports, or display some qualities of adolescence. When this phenomenon is applied to Krsna’s most attractive form of adolescence it lends greater meaning to the term nava-yauvana. Adolescence is ever fresh all by itself, but when enriched by certain qualities of childhood and boyhood the resultant combination constantly manifests shades of contrast that serve to sweeten youth’s eternal freshness, raising its glories to inconceivable new heights.

In Sanatana Gosvami’s own words:

**“Whatever age \[Krsna\] appears to be, He always maintains the special charm of childhood, He accepts the service from the pastimes of mature youth, and He also partakes of the attractiveness of adolescence. And at every moment His personal qualities appear newer and newer.”**

And although each of Krsna’s ages inspires various moods appropriate to its own complementary mellow, the moods of all His ages are always simultaneously present in Krsna; furthermore, those moods do not compete with the dominant mellow in the particular age Krsna happens to be going through at the time. For instance, even though the quality of amorous attraction is present in Krsna as a child, because it is not compatible with parental love — the dominant mellow of childhood — the presence of the amorous mellow docs not bring out the mood of romance in Krsna. Rupa Gosvami therefore advises us not to describe Krsna’s pastimes inappropriate tor the age we are discussing, even it they exist, for such discussion will not invoke the proper mellow (*natirasa-vahitvan*).

Having introduced Krsna’s features, qualities, and pastimes in His three primary ages, I now hope to illuminate and glorify some of the mysteries that surround those ages in the sections to follow.

**The Mystery of Krsna’s Accelerated Maturation**

That Krsna’s nava-yauvana age and qualities fully manifest themselves in Vrndavana leads one to the conclusion that He left for Mathura in His fifteenth year. And Rupa Gosvami’s analysis of Krsna’s three primary ages as being five years each confirms that conclusion. Srimad-Bhagavatam, however, says that Krsna lived in Vrndavana for only eleven years before leaving for Mathura. By that account He left Vrndavana at the beginning of adolescence, not the end.

Sri Uddhava says to Vidura:

*tato nanda-vrajam itah pura kamsad vibibhyata*  
*ekadasa samas tatra gudharcih sa-balo vasat*

**“Thereafter, His father, being afraid of Kamsa, brought Him to the cow pastures of Maharaja Nanda, and there He lived for eleven years like a covered flame with His elder brother, Baladeva.”**

One may argue that we should not nitpick over such details, saying, “Krsna left Vrndavana during His kaisora age. That’s all we need to know.” But such an attitude may not prove helpful in our quest to understand Krsna’s pastimes. We should want to know as much as we can about Krsna. And as far as possible we should try to resolve such contradictions, especially when they could lead to further inconsistencies.

What further inconsistencies could arise hy not pinpointing at what age Krsna left Vrndavana?

If we were to accept that Krsna left Vrndavana in the first phase of youth, we might conclude that Rupa Gosvami is saying that Krsna could not yet have enjoyed the rasa dance. That would leave us in a difficult position. In addition to minimising Rupa Gosvami’s authority and thereby making an offence to his lotus feet, such a conclusion would leave us without an explanation for the five chapters of Srimad-Bhagavatam that deal with the rasa dance.

Since those five chapters do indeed exist, our only alternatives are to question either Rupa Gosvami’s statements regarding the age at which Krsna began rasa dancing, or the length of the periods into which he divides Krsna’s three ages. But Gaudiya Vaisnavas hold the words of Srimad-Bhagavatam and the words of Rupa Gosvami in equally high esteem; indeed, they are both infallible. We therefore cannot, in good conscience, live with the consequences of that first choice.

Moreover, how would we explain Krsna’s apology to Vasudeva and Devaki, who gave birth to Him, for the fact that they were unable to enjoy His youth, which is to say that He had already passed pre-youth and entered eternal youth. Krsna said, “Dear Father! Because of Us, your two sons, you and Mother Devaki always remained in anxiety and could never enjoy Our childhood, boyhood, or youth \[balya-pauganda-kaisorah\].” This statement seems to indicate Krsna’s own acknowledgement that He remained in Vrndavana until the end of youth.

But then again, if we favour Krsna’s nava-yauvana departure from Vrndavana on the basis of Rupa Gosvami’s authority, we are left to explain Uddhava’s equally infallible assertion that Krsna left Vraja after only eleven years (*ekadasa samas tatra*).

To complicate things further, there is another piece of evidence that adds weight to Uddhava’s account: In Mathura, Krsna’s contemporaries saw Him as a new kaisora and not as a fully developed youth.

When Krsna and Balarama entered Kamsa’s wrestling arena, the assembled ladies said, “What comparison can there be between these two professional wrestlers, with limbs as strong as lightning bolts and bodies resembling mighty mountains, and these two young, immature boys with exceedingly tender limbs?”The words the ladies use to describe Krsna and Balarama are, kisorau napta-yauvanau: “two adolescents who have not yet attained maturity.” It seems that, like Uddhava, the women of Mathura saw Krsna to be around eleven years old.

And so we are still left with two statements, both equally contradictory and equally absolute.

Accepting that both statements must be correct, it is justified to wonder, “Is there a way to reconcile the two different ages?”

Luckily, Jiva Gosvami has given a hint. In discussing the five-year periods given for each of Krsna’s three ages, the acarya says,

**“This is a general description. Krsna should be understood to be special.”**

And to our further good fortune, Visvanatha Cakravarti Thakura further elaborates on this subject. He explains that Uddhava has correctly measured Krsna’s stay in Vraja at eleven years, hut within that time the Lord progressed through each age at an extraordinary pace — fifty percent faster than a normal human being does — to reach nava-yauvana. In other words, it look only ten years for Krsna to reach the point of maturity that others reach at fifteen.

Both Jiva and Visvanatha quote Sukadeva Gosvami to substanti ate Krsna’s accelerated development:

*kalenalpena rajarse ramah krsnas ca gokule*  
*aghrsta-janubhih padbhir vicakramatur anjasa*

**“O King Pariksit! Within a short time Rama and Krsna began to walk very easily in Gokula on Their legs, hy Their own strength, without the need to crawl.”**

Visvanatha Cakravarti Thakura’s comment on this verse states that this kind of accelerated maturation is sometimes found even in great people of this world, such as the sons of kings, not to mention Krsna, the Supreme Personality of Godhead. Srila Prabhupada once referred to a similar phenomenon:

**“The boys and girls were married just after sixteen years, especially in the ksatriya families. They are very developed because they can eat very nicely, they live very nicely. So their body development is very nice. They can beget children even at fifteen years old — in those days, especially. So Krsna had, at that time, great-grandchildren when He was fighting in the Kuruksetra.”**

Considering this phenomenon, Visvanatha Cakravarti Thakura explains that Krsna completed the usual five-year span of childhood in three years and four months at Gokula. In the same amount of time He passed His boyhood in Vrndavana, and once again in the same span, His adolescence at Nandagrama. Thus Krsna arrived at adulthood in ten years, a process that usually takes fifteen. After those ten years Krsna’s physical appearance was that of nava-yauvana.

An interesting side note is that Krsna remained in Vraja for seven months past His tenth birthday, and on the eleventh day of the dark fortnight of the month of Caitra He left for Mathura. Three days later He killed Kamsa. Dhyanacandra Gosvami’s explanation for this extra time Krsna spent in Vrndavana is that Krsna was enjoying Himself so much in His nava-yauvana that He extended His stay there by an extra seven months.

Our acaryas resolve the two authoritative yet contradictory statements regarding Krsna’s age by referring to the Lord’s accelerated growth. When Krsna appears on earth, as regards His physical and psychological development from birth to “full maturity,” time passes fifty percent faster for Him than it does for ordinary human beings. In this way the eleven years Uddhava allots for Krsna’s stay in Vrndavana are equivalent to about sixteen years by worldly calculation.

Thus we find Srila Prabhupada often referring to Krsna as being sixteen years of age: “Krsna is representing as a young boy, sixteen years. Similarly, Radharani is also a young girl.” When Krsna’s age is expressed in terms of traditional earthly calculation, His romantic relationships are easier to understand; thus, this is the standard way in which our acaryas refer to Krsna’s eternal age. To portray Krsna’s amorous pastimes as occurring between His seventh and tenth year would confuse conditioned souls and additionally complicate an already difficult topic.

But from another perspective, when introducing the concept of Krsna’s romantic affairs to new audiences, it may sometimes be advantageous to present the transcendental nature of Krsna’s amorous pastimes with the gopis by explaining that the Lord was just a young boy. Srila Prabhupada says that the rasa-lila took place in Krsna’s eighth year. Eight-year-old boys have not yet attained puberty, and therefore any interaction they may have with girls even younger is generally accepted as innocent. Srila Prabhupada writes:

**“Another word used here is apta-kama. Some may take it for granted that Krsna was very lusty amongst young girls, but Pariksit Maharaja said that this was not possible. He could not be lusty. First of all, from the material calculation He was only eight years old. At that age a boy cannot be lusty.”**

Before concluding this section by establishing Krsna’s original, eternal age, we must bear in mind the implications of Krsna’s humanlike pastimes, both in the material world and in Goloka Vrndavana. Even though Krsna ages at an accelerated rate, His associates perceive that accelerated rate as normal. The gopis, then, do not think that Krsna is eight years old when dancing with Him, but rather they see Him to be several months past twelve.

And that is Krsna’s eternal age, in round figures: sixteen years.

**The Ages of Krsna’s Associates and Further Considerations**

To summarise, Vaisnava scriptures and acaryas describe Krsna’s age according to the following conventions:

(1) The day of Krsna’s birth is considered His first birthday and the year that follows, His first year, at the end of which He celebrates His second birthday, and so on, year after year. Therefore, on Krsna’s sixteenth birthday fifteen full years have passed.

(2) Krsna matures from birth to “full maturity” — His eternal age, known as nava-yauvana — fifty percent faster than by earthly calculation. For every year that passes according to material time, Krsna ages by a year and a half. Thus He reaches His sixteenth birthday within ten years of material time.

(3) Krsna’s eternal age is fixed in the final portion of adolescence (sesa-kaisora) also called pre-youth (nava-yauvana). It is calculated to be fifteen years, eleven months, and three days — roughly sixteen years.

Conventions one and two also apply to Krsna’s associates because, as the Lord states Himself, they share His own opulences (mayatma-bhuyaya ca kalpate vai). And, like Krsna, the Lord’s associates also assume fixed, eternal ages and forms. In that connection, Jiva Gosvami says, “In Vraja, just as Krsna is eternally kaisora age, His parents, brothers, friends, and others have permanent ages such as middle-age, youth, and the end of kaisora age. They never accept a different age.”

Based on these principles, the eternal ages of Krsna’s associates can he determined and most of the contradictory assertions regarding their ages resolved. For example, Dhyanacandra Gosvami writes that Srimati Radharani’s eternal age is situated in the middle of the sesa-kaisora period, which She reaches in nine years, five months, and twenty days. This makes Her, hy human calculation, fourteen years, two months, and fifteen days old.

To accept this calculation, which conforms to the conventions just outlined, some references that appear to contradict this calculation need to he reconciled, otherwise we will he confused.

For example, Srila Prabhupada made these statements:

**“Radharani is fifteen days younger than Krsna,” and “Krsna is a hoy, sixteen-years-old boy, and Radharani is also a fifteen-years-old girl....Not even one year’s...I think, fifteen days younger.”**

Since neither the scriptures nor our previous acaryas suggest that Radha is just two weeks younger than Krsna, it is appropriate to examine Srila Prabhupada’s statements more closely.

Are Radha’s and Krsna’s birthdays separated hy fifteen days?If we calculate according to earthly perception the answer is yes. Calculations show that Radha’s first birthday is fifteen days after Krsna’s, but one yearlater.This reconciles Srila Prabhupada’s and Dhyanacandra Gosvami’s statements: Srila Prabhupada means that Radha’s birthday is celebrated fifteen days after Krsna’s, but She is one year younger.

Rupa Gosvami also gives a different age for Sri Radha. In his Radha-krsna-ganoddesa-dipika Rupa Gosvami lists the ages of some of Krsna’s associates including Sri Radha, about whom he writes,

**“Srimati Radharani is fifteen years old and full of the lustre of youth.”**

Rupa Gosvaml’s exact fifteen years has most probably been rounded up. Taking the preceding calculations, if Radha is a year younger than Krsna, and if Krsna’s age is commonly rounded up to an even sixteen years, then Radha can be said to be fifteen.

This conclusion is confirmed by Jiva Gosvami, who states that Radha was born a year after Sri Krsna. He writes that when Radha appeared in King Vrsabhanu’s home, poets praised Her with the following words:

*satyam bahusuta-ratnakaratam*  
*sa prapa gopa-dugdhabdhih*  
*kintvamrita-dyuti-radha*  
*laksmi-jana nadgat purtim*

**“The milk ocean of Vrsabhanu was a mine of gems in the form of his many sons, but became perfect in producing nectar through the birth of Radha.”**

And Sri Jiva comments:

*sa khalu sri-krsna-janma-*  
*varsanantara varse sarva-sukhasatre*  
*radha namninaksatre*  
*jateti radhabhidhiyate*

**“She took birth when the moon was in Anuradha constellation, bringing joy to all, one year after the birth of Krsna.”**

We can reconcile in a similar way statements made by Srila Prabhupada and Rupa Gosvami regarding Lord Balarama’s age.

Srila Prabhupada writes,

**“Balarama was only fifteen days older than Krsna.” And Rupa Gosvami writes, “He \[Balarama\] is sixteen years old and full of the lustre of youth.”**

Both these quotes seem to indicate that Balarama is roughly the same age as Krsna. But when we examine the narrative and chronology of Lord Balarama’s appearance and pastimes, it is clear that they cannot be the same age; therefore, these quotes require elucidation.

Sukadeva Gosvami describes Balarama’s appearance in Devaki’s womb as her seventh (saptamah) pregnancy, telling how Krsna instructed Yogamaya to transfer His brother to the womb of Rohini, who was then living in Vrndavana. Srila Prabhupada quotes the Harivamsa Purana, which states that Rohini was already pregnant and that Balarama was transferred to her womb in the seventh month of her pregnancy. Meanwhile, the residents of Kamsa’s palace perceived that Devaki had had a miscarriage (aho visramsito garbha). According to the Harivamsa Purana, in the following month Balarama, like Krsna, was born prematurely during the eighth month of pregnancy.

Some time after Balarama’s birth and Devaki’s apparent miscarriage, Devaki again became pregnant with Krsna. At the same time, Yasoda became pregnant with Yogamaya. Both pregnancies lasted eight months, at which time both children were born simultaneously and exchanged by Vasudeva.

Krsna was in Devaki’s womb for eight months after Balarama was transferred from Devaki’s womb and then born to Rohini. How could He be just fifteen days His brother’s junior?

Moreover, the following pastime further confirms that Balarama had appeared in Vrndavana well before Krsna was born. Upon His birth, although perfect and beautiful in every way, Balarama would neither move nor make any sound, and thus His parents and the villagers were in great anxiety. One day Yasoda, who was pregnant, placed Balarama on her lap and He immediately began to laugh and play like a normal child. But when she put Him down He would become inactive, as before.
