This mysterious behaviour continued until Krsna finally took birth.

Why was Balarama so joyful being in Yasoda’s lap? Because, according to Harivamsa, Yasoda was actually pregnant with twins: Yogamaya and Krsna. The Krsna born to Devaki was Vasudeva-Krsna, while the Krsna born to Yasoda was the original Krsna, Syamasundara. The main point to be gleaned from this pastime is that, in Yasoda’s lap, Balarama was not an infant of only a few days, but a growing child.

Thus when Srila Prabhupada says that Balarama is only fifteen days older than Krsna it should be taken to mean that Balarama’s birthday is only fifteen days before Krsna’s. In the same way that Srimati Radharani is one year and fifteen days younger than Krsna, Balarama is one year and fifteen days older than His brother.

And in a similar way, Rupa Gosvami’s assertion that Lord Balarama is sixteen years old should be taken to mean that Lord Balarama is in His sixteenth year. Krsna is in His fifteenth year, so this interpretation accounts for Balarama being a year older than His brother.

Finally, this conclusion is explicitly supported by the Brahma-vaivarta Purana, in which Lord Narayana says, “Sankarsana was one year older than Krsna.”

**\* \* \* \***

Authoritative sources may present similarly differing accounts regarding the ages of Krsna’s other associates. I have here dealt with the two most prominent contradictions, but by adhering to the principles outlined at the beginning of this section, readers may attempt to resolve such apparent contradictions for themselves.

There is, however, one allegation that demands attention: Some say that Srimati Radharani is actually older than Krsna.

There is a common misunderstanding amongst people, especially of Indian origin, who, by citing various stories, assert that Sti Radha is anywhere from five to as much as sixteen years Krsna’s senior. Obviously a state of affairs such as that would be untenable to anyone versed in the science of devotional mellows.

Krsna eternally remains a sixteen-year-old pre-youth because that age provides the greatest facility for enjoyment. Since it is Sri Radha, His pleasure potency, who facilitates His enjoyment, how could She be of an age that would cripple Her ability to give Krsna pleasure? If the female in a relationship is significantly older than the male, their relationship will be tinged by the maternal mellow. Feelings of maternal affection would certainly hamper romantic exchange and hence disturb the amorous pastimes Radha and Krsna embody. Simple logic and the principles of devotional mellows demand that the divine couple he in the kaisora age.

One of the divine couple’s names is Kisora-Kisori, which clearly indicates Their youthful stature. Srila Prabhupada writes, “Krsna with Radharani is worshipped as Kisora-Kisori. Krsna does not increase His age. Although He is the oldest personality and has innumerable different forms, His original form is always youthful."

Nonetheless, are there scriptural statements that give life to the notion that Radha is older than Krsna? Two scriptures — the Brahma-vaivarta Purana and Gita-govinda — contribute to this misunderstanding.

In the Brahma-vaivarta Purana there are two references worth examining in this regard, the second of which is cited as the introductory verse to Gita-govinda and thus lends weight to the argument that Jayadeva Gosvai’s Radha was older than Krsna.

Let us examine the first of these two statements, in which Lord Narayana is said to praise Krsna’s beloved with the words, “She came to this earth first, She is said to be little older than He.”

First we may note from this excerpt that Radha is said to be only a little older than Krsna. It does not indicate that She is many years His senior. But, still, how do we explain this clear scriptural statement? As a Purana intended for those in the mode of passion, the statements of the Brahma-vaivarta are not above doubt; in fact, when they contradict the statements of transcendental authorities, they should be rejected.

Does the Brahma-vaivarta Purana contradict authoritative statements?

In light of the assertions of liberated acaryas like Jiva Gosvami and Srila Prabhupada, yes, it does. We can therefore safely conclude that this statement of the Brahma-vaivarta Purana is either a variant reach ing, an interpolation, or an inaccuracy. It can therefore he rejected.

The second account of the Brahma-vaivarta Purana, echoed in the nandi-sloka of Gita-govinda, reproduced here, describes the marriage of Radha and Krsna:

*meghair meduram ambaram vana-bhuvah syamas tamala-drumair*  
*naham bhirur ayam tvam eva tad imam radhe grham prapaya*  
*ittham nanda-nidesatas calitayoh praty-adhva-kunja-drumam*  
*radha-madhavayor gayanti yamuna-kule rahah-kelayah*

**‘“O Radha! All directions are covered by dense and ominous clouds. The forest floor has been cast into darkness by the shadows of blackish tamala trees. Krsna is naturally timid. He cannot be alone at night, so take Him home with you. ’Sri Radha turned Her face towards the bower of desire trees beside the forest path and, bewildered by intense joy, She honoured the words of Her friend. When She arrived on the banks of the Yamuna River, She initiated love-play in a secret place. May the sweetness of this confidential pastime of the divine couple be victorious by manifesting in the hearts of devotees.”**

In this verse, as well as in the more detailed description found in the Brahma-vaivarta Purana, Nanda Maharaja is carrying baby Krsna, who becomes afraid of the darkness caused by a sudden storm. Just at that time, Sri Radha appears in Her eternal form as the Lord’s pleasure potency. Nanda worships Her as Krsna’s eternal consort and gives His son to Her for care. Radha then takes the baby to a private bower, where He manifests His kaisora form to match Sri Radha’s, and They enjoy amorous pastimes.

At first, the Brahma-vaivarta Purana depicts Radha as a youth and Krsna as a baby. In that context She certainly appears older than He. What some readers misunderstand, however, is that in that instanceKrsna’s form and age are of the temporarily manifest realm, whereas Radha’s form and age are of the eternal unmanitest realm. In other words, it is more than likely that Radha had not yet taken birth when this pastime occurred. Comparing Krsna’s age in His incarnation with Radha’s eternal age is like comparing apples and oranges.

While the version of Brahma-vaivarta Purana is accurate, it is misinterpreted due to insufficient spiritual vision, and thus the misconception that Radha is older than Krsna takes root and spreads.

And what of the nandi-sloka of Gita-govinda? That verse is merely an invocation. The remainder of the hook is in another context; indeed, that verse is followed hy Jayadeva’s own preface and the famous Dasavatara-stotra before continuing with the hook’s storyline, in which Radha and Krsna are clearly exhibiting Their earthly pastimes as we know them. In other words, there is nothing to indicate that the age of Radha implied in the invocation has any relationship to the Radha who appears in the songs of Gita-govinda.

The arguments I have given here prove that Radha could not possibly he older than Krsna. Rest assured that any so-called scriptural evidence one may hear cited to assert otherwise has been similarly misconstrued.

**\* \* \* \***

I conclude this appendix by considering a philosophical question that Rupa Gosvami raises while discussing how Krsna’s age acts as a stimulus for ecstatic love.

At the outset I suggested that by analysing the features of Krsna’ s three primary ages, our increased remembrance of the qualities of the Lord would stimulate our devotion to Him. But Krsna and Krsna’s age are not different, just as Krsna and Krsna’s qualities are not different. Is it possible, then, that Krsna’s age is not a stimulus for hut an object of devotion?

Jiva Gosvami gives the following answer to this question by using another one of Krsna’s qualities, His beauty, as an example. When we think of Krsna’s body as being beautiful, then His beauty is indeed the object of our devotion. But when we think of Krsna’s beauty apart from His other innumerable transcendental qualities, then Krsna’s beauty itself becomes a stimulant for devotion to Krsna rather than its object.

Now let us take Krsna’s youth as an example. If we think of Krsna as eternally youthful (nava-yauvana), because Krsna’s age is not different from Him, His youth is then the object of our devotion. But if we meditate on the qualities of that youth, then, by the wonderful features of those qualities, Krsna’s youth acts to stimulate our affection for Krsna.

May this appendix serve its readers by enhancing their taste for serving and loving Krsna through hearing about some of the wonderful features of Krsna’s three primary ages.

Finally — and before listing the eternal ages and qualities of some of Krsna’s gopa and gopi associates — I must admit that in spite of the effort I have made in this appendix to resolve seeming variances in authoritative opinions about the eternal ages of Krsna and His associates, the fact is that there remain differences of opinion on such subjects, both in scriptures and amongst acaryas. Such differences are an unavoidable facet of Krsna consciousness because of the various ways that various devotees perceive the one Absolute Truth. After all, the Lord manifests Himself differently according to the desires of His devotees.

Yet we see that our acaryas have given specific descriptions of the Lord and His associates so that those descriptions will paint clear pictures for our devotional meditations. When our meditations mature to the point of full spiritual realisation, we will appreciate the qualities of the Lord and His associates through transcendental experience, according to our unique relationship with the Lord. And in the inconceivable realm of simultaneous oneness and difference, we will directly perceive the unity that exists within diverse and contradictory opinions.

To enhance our attachment to Krsna and His associates, and to give us a fuller sense of Krsna’s pastimes, Rupa Gosvami has described some of the qualities of those associates, including their eternalages.

Beginning with Krsna’s gopi friends, the acarya lists the most confidential ones: Lalita, Visakha, Citra, Campakalata, Tungavidya, Indulekha, Rangadevi, and Sudevi.

Of these eight gopis, Lalita is foremost. Being the dear friend of the divine couple, she is twenty-seven days older than Sri Radha and thus the eldest of the gopis. Lalita is famous as Srimati Radharani’s constant companion and follower. She is contrary and hot-tempered by nature, her complexion is yellow like gorocana, and her garments resemble peacock feathers.

The next most prominent gopi is Visakha. Because she is horn at the same moment as Sri Radha, Visakha is very much like Radha in qualities, activities, and nature. Her clothes are decorated with stars, and her complexion is the colour of lightning.

Third is Campakalata. One day younger than Srimati Radharani, with qualities much like those of Visakha, Campakalata’s complexion is the colour of a blossoming yellow campaka flower, and her garments are the colour of a blue jay seen from a distance.

Citra is twenty-six days younger than Sri Radha, her fair complexion is slightly reddish, as if dabbed by kunkuma, and she prefers to wear clothes that are white like crystal.

The greatest scholar amongst the gopis, Tungavidya, is fifteen days younger than Srimati Radharani. Her complexion is flushed like kunkuma, the fragrance ot her body is like sandalwood mixed with camphor, and she favours white clothing. She is known to be hot-tempered and very clever in arguments.

Like Tungavidya, Indulekha is also contrary and hot-tempered, but her complexion is tan and her garments, the colour of a pomegranate flower. She is three days younger than Srimati Radharani.

Rangadevi and Sudevi are identical twins, born seven days later than Sri Radha, and so they are often mistaken for each other. This is all the more understandable when one considers that they both wear garments the colour of red roses. Although Rangadevi is somewhat contrary by nature, Sudevi is very sweet and charming.

While all these gopis are in their fourteenth year, the manjaris are generally a year younger, although in some cases, even two. For example, Rupa-manjari is thirteen years and six months old; Rati-manjari is thirteen years and two months; Rasa manjari, exactly thirteen years; and Vilasa-manjari, twelve years, eleven months, and twenty-six days old.

Having described the ages and attributes of some of the gopis, Rupa Gosvami then lists Krsna’s most important friends, also describing their ages and attributes in brief. These boys are Subala, Arjuna, Gandharva, Vasanta, Ujjvala, Kokila, Sanandana, and Vidagdha. Because he is so prominent in Krsna’s pastimes, Madhumarigala should also be added to the list, to say nothing of Balarama, Lord Krsna’s older brother, and Sridama, the brother of Sri Radha.

Although Subala is only twelve and a half years old, he glows with the lustre of adolescence and serves Krsna in a variety of ways, including making arrangements for the divine couple’s many rendezvous.Always cheerful, of fair complexion, wearing blue garments, and decorated with an abundance of jewels and flowers, Subala is Krsna’s dearmost friend.

Two years senior to Subala and also full of affection for the divine couple, Arjuna has a complexion like a red lotus and wears cloth that shines like moonlight. He is decorated with many jewels and is very fond of wearing flower ornaments and garlands.

At exactly twelve years old, Gandharva is even younger than Subala. But, like Subala, he loo appears mature for his age, and his playful nature endears him to Krsna. He is handsome, with a complexion fair like moonlight, and he dresses in red garments, ornaments, and flowers.

Vasanta is in the category of Krsna’s youngest intimate friends. He is only eleven years old and thus on the cusp of adolescence. In his presence, Krsna always feels like it is spring (vasanta), and Vasanta’s fair complexion, glittering white garments, and flower decorations sing of the purity of his love for Krsna.

Ujjvala is a youthful thirteen. His reddish complexion, star-spangled clothes, and pearl decorations make him look very dashing. His ability to keep Krsna submissive to his loving control is as unique as his appearance, thus he is known as the crest jewel of playful boys.

Kokila is just four months over eleven. Like Balarama, his complexion is fair and his garments blue. His attractive features are enhanced by the many kinds of sparkling jewellery he wears.

Sanandana is fourteen and he too is of a fair complexion and fancies blue garments, dressing himself with a combination of ornaments and flower garlands. Revelling always in the companionship of Sri Krsna, he is known amongst the gopas as the splendid monarch of transcendental mellows.

Vidagdha is fourteen as well. He also dresses like Balarama, but his complexion is yellow like a campaka flower, and he wears strings of pearls. Steeped in the mood of youth, he is experienced in loving service to Radha and Krsna. As Sudama’s younger brother, he is especially dear to Krsna.

Rupa Gosvami does not give an age for Sudama, but he writes that Sudama is of fair complexion and also wears blue. Sudama covers himself in ornaments and is skilled in playing games, making him an endless mine of love for Krsna and Balarama.

Srimati Radharani’s handsome brother, Sridama, is the same age as Krsna, sixteen years old. Like Krsna, his complexion is swarthy, and, like Krsna, he wears yellow silks and jewel necklaces. Srldama’s appearance is indicative of his close relationship to Krsna and of his intimate place in the innumerable pastimes of the Lord.

Madhumangala’s age is not mentioned, but we know he has a tan complexion and wears yellow garments and forest flowers. He is Krsna’s constant companion, not only because of his humorous ways but also because he is a connoisseur of the most elevated mellows of pure devotional service.

The last gopa we will hear about, and Krsna’s dearest friend, is Lord Balarama. Being one year senior to Krsna, He is in His sixteenth year. Powerful, with a complexion like shining crystal, it is to follow His example that so many of Krsna’s friends wear blue cloth. His thick hair is tied in a topknot, priceless earrings swing against His cheeks, His neck is graced with forest-flower garlands and jewel necklaces, and His serpentine arms are thick with bracelets and armlets. Upon His feet tinkle splendid cintamani anklets that have acquired their philosophers’ stone nature from their constant resting place. All know Him as the emblem of youthful lustre, as Krsna’s protector, and as the greatest reservoir of nectarean mellows. Obeisances unto Him!

I offer my obeisances unto the original fountain of youth, whom ignorant persons search for in different parts of the world, thinking Him to be some magical wellspring. But He is always within their hearts, ready to guide them to His eternal land of fresh springtime known as Vraja. Obeisances to eternally youthful Sri Krsna!

I offer my obeisances to Krsna’s ever youthful associates, who, by their pure love for the Lord, mirror His unlimited ages, respective qualities, and bodily characteristics. Obeisances unto the residents of Vraja!

For those inclined to pray to the aforementioned gopas and gopis, hoping to become the object of the divine couple’s mercy, I offer the following payers of Rupa Gosvami.

*disi vidisi viharam acarantah*  
*saha pasupala-varenya-nandanabhyam*  
*pranayi-jana-ganas tayoh kurudhvam*  
*mayi karunam bata kakum akalayya*

**“O affectionate people of Vraja who always enjoy transcendental pastimes in this place with the son and daughter of the best of the gopas! Please hear my choked! words and be merciful to me."**

*giri-kunja-kutira-nagarau*  
*lalite devi sada lavasravau*  
*iti te kila nasti duskaram*  
*krpayangi-kuru mam atah svayam*

**“O Queen Lalita! The hero and heroine who enjoy transcendental pastimes in the cottage, forest, and hill are always obedient to you. Therefore, since nothing is difficult for you, please be kind and accept me.”**

*subala ballava-varya-kumarayor*  
*dayita-narma-sakhas tvam asi vraje*  
*iti tayoh purato vidhuram janam*  
*ksanam amum krpayadya nivedaya*

**“O Subala! You are the intimate friend of the son and daughter of the best of the gopas in Vraja. Please be merciful and introduce this unhappy person to your two friends.”**

*srnuta krpaya hanta pranesayoh pranayoddhurah*  
*kim api yad ayam dinah prani nivedayati ksanam*  
*pravanita-manah kim yusmabhih samam tilam apy asau*  
*yugapad anayoh sevam premna kadapi vidhasyati*

**“O you great lovers of the master and mistress of life. Please hear my appeal with compassion! This poor lowly hearted living entity, who is not even a sesame seed in comparison to you, asks, ‘Will I be able to attain the loving service of the divine couple?”’**

## Appendix 29

**Worship of Vaisnava Samadhis**

(This appendix follows from the description of Rupa Gosvamis nama-samadhi in New Vraja-dhama, the first of many samadhis described in chapters nineteen through twenty-three.)

Alongside New Vraja — dhama's many holy tirthas, which mark the places of Krsna’s pastimes, are the samadhis of great Vaisnavas — our departed acaryas — established at the homes of New Vraja — dhama's residents. Why?

Because more important than worship of the Lord is worship of the Lord’s devotees, by the grace of whom we are truly able to enter Vrndavana. In this regard, Srila Prabhupada would often quote the following words of Narottama Dasa Thakura:

*rupa-raghunatha-pade haibe akuli*  
*kabe hama bujhaba se yugala-piriti*

**“When will I be attracted to the instructions of the (Gosvamis so that I will be able to understand what is Radha and Krsna and what is Vrndavana?”**

As devotees in ISKCON we all revere Srila Prabhupada and our own diksa- and siksa-gurus, yet we cannot neglect the other distinguished members of our disciplic succession, especially the personal associates or Sri Caitanya Mahaprabhu. Therefore, since Srila Prabhupada and his disciples and granddisciples who act as diksa-guru are already worshipped in every house, a distinctive flavour has been added to devotees’ vaisnava-seva by also making them custodians of the samadhis of the Lord’s direct associates.

This appendix explains the philosophical principles related to different kinds of Vaisnava samadhis, why and how they are maintained and worshipped, and how the appearance and disappearance days of Vaisnavas should be celebrated. My hope is that the residents of New Vraja-dhama will make their personal service to the samadhis for which they are responsible an integral part of their Krsna consciousness, develop a relationship with the Vaisnavas eternally residing there, and become recipients of his or her special mercy.

Faithful, pure-hearted devotees may receive inspiration or instructions from these great souls, either indirectly through dreams, or directly, in the more rare form of personal darsana. A deceased Vaisnava will continue to reside in his samadhi in the form in which he practised devotional service in this world, even while he serves in the Lord’s pastimes in his eternal, perfected form in the spiritual world. Connecting to such great souls is for us but a matter of faith and purity and, of course, the mercy of those great souls.

**The Philosophical Principles Underlying Samadhis**

In defining the word samadhi, Srila Prabhupada cites the Nirukti, or Vedic dictionary, samyag adhiyate ’sminn atma-tatwa-yathamyam: “When the mind is fixed for understanding the self, it is said to be in samadhi." And in summary Srila Prabhupada writes that samadhi means “fixed mind.”

Technically, samadhi is the eighth and final stage in controlling the mind, as outlined in the Yoga-sutras. And while all of these descriptions are general, in the context of Gaudiya Vaisnava philosophy the phrase “fixed mind” refers to a mind undeviatingly fixed on Krsna. Thus Lord Kapila says:

*sva-dhisnyanam eka-dese manasa prana-dharanam*  
*vaikuntha-lilabhidhyanam samadhanam lathatmanah*

**“Fixing the vital air and the mind in one of the six circles of vital ailcirculation within the body, thus concentrating one’s mind on the transcendental pastimes of the Supreme Personality of Godhead, is called samadhi, or samadhana, of the mind.”**

While Lord Kapila accepts the activities of mystic yoga as elements in the external form of samadhi, He points out that samadhi’s essential characteristic is constant remembrance of Krsna’s form, pastimes, and qualities. As Krsna tells Uddhava, “Amongst the eight progressive states of yoga I am the final stage, samadhi, in which the soul is completely separated from illusion.” Thus, a Vaisnava is said to he in samadhi when he has attained the state of full absorption in the Supreme Lord.

This full absorption in the Lord is what Vaisnavas aspire for at the time of death, because, as Krsna states, such samadhi will assure attainment of the Lord’s eternal association. Krsna says, “If one thinks of the Supreme Personality of Godhead and quits his body, he will certainly reach the spiritual planets.”

Thus devotees who have practised and perfected full absorption in Krsna consciousness in this life will, at the time of death, certainly be in samadhi, the natural and eternal state of the soul. And since this state of samadhi is eternal, the tomb of a Vaisnava marks the place where, like the yoga-pitha, the spiritual and material worlds come together.

The word samadhi is also defined as “completion” or “joining,” which for our purposes means that the place where a Vaisnava attains samadhi is not only the place where he forever solidified his connection with Krsna but also where he has provided us with an opportunity to be joined with him and with the spiritual realm.

For these reasons, the tomb of a devotee who has attained samadhi is also referred to with the word samadhi.

Bhismadeva is a wonderful example of someone who entered the state ot samadhi at the time of death. He describes his own attainment in the following verse:

*tam imam aham ajam sarira-bhajam*  
*hrdi hrdi dhisthitam atma-kalpitanam*  
*pratidrsam iva naikadharkam ekam*  
*samadhi-gato ’smi vidhuta-bheda-mohah*

**“Now I can meditate with full concentration \[samadhi\] upon that one Lord, Sri Krsna, now present before me because now I have transcended the misconceptions of duality in regard to His presence in everyone’s heart, even in the hearts of the mental speculators. He is in everyone’s heart. The sun may be perceived differently, but the sun is one.”**

To summarise, the word samadhi signifies both a state of perfect Krsna consciousness and a tomb that contains the remains — or at least in some way invokes the presence — of one who has everlastingly attained that state of perfect Krsna consciousness.

Having briefly defined the former meaning of samadhi — a state of consciousness — let us now focus on the latter: the sacred tomb of a fully realised saint. A samadhi is a tomb that contains the remains or invokes the presence of a great Vaisnava and is therefore a place of intense spiritual potency and a juncture between the material and spiritual worlds. In short, a samadhi is a very holy place.

Naturally, devotees may wonder, “If a Vaisnava has left this world in divine trance, his soul transferred to the spiritual world, how does his spiritual potency remain at his samadhi?”

The sublime process of pure devotional service fully spiritualises the body of such a devotee, and his physical remains continue to transmit that purity from the location of his samadhi.

Krsna personally describes how a devotee’s activities — and whatever is utilised during the performanee of those activities — are fully spiritualised when connected to Him through the process of pure Krsna consciousness:

*brahmarpanam brahma havir brahmagnau brahmana hutam*  
*brahmaiva tena gantavyam brahma-karma-samadhina*

**“A person who is fully absorbed in Krsna consciousness is sure to attain the spiritual kingdom because of his full contribution to spiritual activities, in which the consummation is absolute and that which is offered is of the same spiritual nature.”**

In the process of instructing his school friends, Prahlada Maharaja also explains this transformation: “The devotee is then freed from all material contamination because he constantly thinks of the Lord’s pastimes and because his mind and body have been converted to spiritual qualities.”

And in his purport to this verse, Srila Prabhupada gives the oftcited example of an iron rod in fire:

**“An iron rod put into a fire becomes warmer and warmer, and when it is red hot it is no longer an iron rod but fire. Similarly, when a devotee constantly engages in devotional service and thinks of the Lord in his original Krsna consciousness, he no longer has any material activities, for his body is spiritualised.”**

Moreover, Srimad-Bhagavatam gives an example of how the spiritual potency of a great saint is retained in his earthly remains even after he has left the body. After reciting the Narayana-kavaca to Indra, the brahmana Visvarupa related the story of a brahmana named Kausika who had perfected his chanting of the Narayana-kavaca and given up his body in the desert. Once, as a Gandharva known as Citraratha was flying over the area where Kausika had died, his airplane was forcibly pulled to the ground by the spiritual power of Kausika’s skeleton. Only after putting Kausika’s hones into the nearby Sarasvati river was Citraratha able to activate his plane and continue on his way.

**\* \* \* \***

Although this example confirms that a devotee’s spiritual potency is retained in his physical body after his so-called death, we should not conclude that that spiritual potency is confined to those remains. Because a devotee’s spiritual potency is not restricted to his lifeless body, samadhis also may entomb a Vaisnava’s cremated ashes, the flowers that have decorated his body after his departure, his personal belongings, or his name.

This broad distribution of spiritual potency is supported by the brahmarpanam verse of Bhagavad-gita already cited. But an even more precise reference to the phenomenon can be found in the following well-known verse, in which Lord Siva uses an important term — tadiya.

*aradhananam sarvesam visnor aradhanam param*  
*tasmat parataram devi tadiyanam samarcanam*

**\[Lord Siva told the goddess Durga:\] “O Devi! The most exalted system of worship is the worship ot Lord Visnu. Greater than that is the worship of tadiya, or anything belonging to Visnu.”**

It is often suggested that the word tadiya in this verse refers specifically to Vaisnavas, implying that it is even better to worship Krsna’s devotees than it is to worship Lord Krsna Himself. The word tadiya is however not restricted to this interpretation, as its specific meaning is “anything belonging to Visnu.” Thus tadiya can also refer to anything that belongs to or is connected with devotees — surely, everything belonging to a Vaisnava also belongs to Visnu.

Therefore a devotee’s pure spiritual potency may also be transferred to the ashes of his cremated body, to flowers offered to him, indeed to any artefact that was connected with him, such as his clothing, shoes, or any other personal belongings.

The spiritual potency of a samadhi that contains any one of these different items belonging to a pure Vaisnava (tadiya) is invested thereby the will of the Lord, and is therefore completely independent of material considerations.

Why is this the Lord’s will?

As Lord Siva says, the Lord is more pleased when we worship His devotee than when we worship Him. He is more pleased because, just as Krsna’s devotees love and serve Him exclusively, He is exclusively devoted to His servants. Actually, Krsna watches with great pleasure from within the hearts of His devotees, taking note whenever they are lovingly served by others. That is how we, the servants of the servants, can make ourselves very dear to Lord Krsna.

The Lord explicitly explained this to Durvasa Muni when He said, “I am completely under the control of My devotees. Indeed, I am not at all independent. Because My devotees are completely devoid of material desires, I sit only within the cores of their hearts. What to speak of My devotees, even those who are devotees of My devotees are very dear to Me.”

And when it comes to Vaisnava acaryas, Krsna equates them with Himself, insisting they be worshipped as He is worshipped. To Uddhava, Krsna says:

*acaryam mam vijaniyan navamanyeta karhicit*  
*na martya-buddhyasuyeta sarva-deva-mayo guruh*

**“One should know the acarya as Myself and never disrespect him in any way. One should not envy him, thinking him an ordinary man, for he is the representative of all the demigods.”**

Evident in these statements are both the Lord’s affection for His devotees and His desire for them to be worshipped. Moreover, Krsna wants the worship of His devotees to continue even after they have left this world. But it is common for people to only offer worship if they feel some type of reciprocation. Therefore Krsna empowers the tadiya of His devotees with their spiritual potency. In a similar way, Krsna’s Deity form, name, abode, and paraphernalia are all empowered with Krsna’s potencies, which devotees can perceive even in the physical absence of the Lord.

In summary, when great devotees leave this world their samadhis are empowered with their divine presence hy Krsna’s will. That divine presence mirrors the Lord’s own qualities. In this regard the Lord tells Uddhava, “A realised soul no longer sees anything as separate from Me, for his realised knowledge of Me has destroyed such illusory perception. Since the material body and mind were previously accustomed to this kind of perception, it may sometimes appear to recur; hut at the time of death the self-realised soul achieves opulences equal to Mine.”

**The Kinds of Samadhis and Their Histories**

As in other religious and spiritual traditions, Vaisnavas want to remember their saints and the exemplary lives they lived by commemorating them. Hence, a culture and tradition of Vaisnava samadhis has developed since the time of Ramanujacarya and, in Gaudiya Vaisnavism, especially since the time of Caitanya Mahaprabhu.

Historical and archeological records indicate that in the Vedic tradition dead bodies were disposed of in one of three ways: by burial, cremation, or hy committing them to sacred rivers. Of the three, cremation was the most common, while burial was reserved for saints or infants, and river burials were intended for those bodies too impure for cremation. These practices are known as bhumi-samadhi (burial), agn-samadhi (cremation), and jala-samadhi (commitment to a sacred river).

The prevalence of cremation in Vedic culture is attested to by the following verse of Sri Isopanisad spoken by a great devotee who envisions his body being cremated at death:

*vayur anilam amrtam athedam bhasmantam sariram*  
*om krato smara krtam smara krato smara krtam smara*

**“Let this temporary body be burnt to ashes, and let the air of life be merged with the totality of air. Now, o my Lord, please remember all my sacrifices, and because You are the ultimate beneficiary, please remember all that I have done for You.”**

However, from ancient time, the bodies of those great saints who have been spiritualised by pure devotion are a blessing to the earth, and hence their remains are placed into the ground and preserved hy being completely surrounded by salt.

Srila Prabhupada comments on this as follows:

**“Cinmaya means if you cultivate spiritual knowledge in large quantity, then your body is no more material. It is piritualised. Cinmaya. Therefore great saintly person’s body, after demise it is not burned; it is buried, samadhi, because it is cinmaya.”**

The samadhis that contain the bodies of great saints are like temples where devotees can pray to, worship, and remember the Lord’s associates, and in so doing advance in Krsna consciousness. Unlike temples, where the object of worship is the one inimitable Supreme Lord, samadhis are home to the saints interned there, whose examples their worshippers can all attempt to follow. For this reason devotees often perform bhajana in close proximity to the samadhis of great souls, knowing that the mercy of those souls may he more readily attained than the mercy of the Lord.

**\* \* \* \***

The samadhis in New Vraja-dhama are smrti-samadhis — a concept we shall shortly explain at length — where Vaisnava saints have been invoked solely by the request of sincere devotees. Making a tomb and placing a picture of a revered Vaisnava predecessor upon it, such devotees pray, “O Mahasaya! O beloved of Gauranga! Your original samadhi is in Bhauma Vrndavana, but I cannot reside there. Thus I request you to please take up residence in this place we have provided for you and give us your mercy. Please allow us to serve you.”

In the portions of Srimad-Bhagavatam that depict the passing of great souls — even the departures of Krsna and Balarama — the general inference is that their bodies are cremated and there is nothing to suggest that shrines or tombs resembling current-day samadhis are constructed to venerate them. That does not necessarily mean that such samadhis did not exist, simply that there is no reference to them.

And while Krsna’s great-grandson Vajra went to great pains to establish Vrndavana as a place of pilgrimage by populating it, constructing villages, building temples, and installing many deities, there is no mention of his establishing any samadhis there.

Biographies of Ramanuja consistently relate how his body was placed into samadhi. Thus if we are looking for a time when the practice of creating samadhis was first recorded, if not also inaugurated, then we can reasonably suggest the date of the acaryas death in 1137. Ramanujacarya’s samadhi is unique in that he was not interred but remains encased in a shrine in the Sri Rangam temple; his body is seated in a lotus position and is covered with sandalwood paste, his eyes and right thumbnail still visible.

Krsnadasa Kaviraja Gosvami’s very detailed account of the passing of Haridasa Thakura includes a description of the process of making his samadhi, which was supervised hy Caitanya Mahaprabhu Himself. This account marks the oldest historical reference to the creation of a Gaudiya Vaisnava samadhi.

Upon the death of Haridasa Thakura a hole was dug on the beach at Jagannatha Puri and the acaryas body was placed into it along with Lord Jagannatha’s remnants. While devotees chanted Hare Krsna, Lord Caitanya filled the hole with sand, then a platform was erected on the site and the entire area was fenced in. To this day, a large festival called tirobhava-mahotsava is held there annually to commemorate the disappearance of Haridasa Thakura.

There is, however, another samadhi that predates the namacarya’s. It is a tomb for Lord Caitanya’s hair, collected when His head was shaved clean at the time of His sannyasa initiation. Located at Katwa, it is called the kesa-samadhi, or “hair samadhi." Mention of it naturally leads us to a discussion of the traditional types of samadhis.

In his book Gaudiya Vaisnava Samadhis, Mahanidhi Swami lists three kinds of samadhis: deha (body), puspa (flower), and smrti (remembrance).

Haridasa Thakura’s samadhi is an example of a samadhi lor a saint’s physical remains. This t of samadhi was also constructed for Srila Prabhupada at the time of his departure in 1977. Today devotees from all over the world visit that beautiful white-marble tomb in Vrndavana to feel Srila Prabhupada’s divine presence, offer him prayers, and receive inspiration for serving his mission.

The puspa-samadhi is a shrine for flowers that have been sanctified by being offered to the body of a departed Vaisnava. These flowers are placed in an urn and then buried in the ground according to appropriate rituals. Srila Prabhupada personally selected the sites of both the samadhi for his physical remains (the courtyard of Krsna-Balarama temple in Vrndavana) and also his puspa-samadhi (the grounds of the Mayapur Candrodaya temple).

The term puspa-samadhi refers not only to interred flowers but also to the cremated remains of Vaisnavas’ bodies. During the Mogul occupation of India, for example, the remains of many departed devotees were cremated for fear that the soldiers of kings like Aurangzeb would otherwise desecrate their samadhis. Around this time Raghunatha Dasa Gosvami and Raghunatha Bhatta Gosvami, amongst others, were cremated, their ashes placed in samadhi at Radha-kunda.

Puspa samadhis serve as examples of how the spiritual potency of a Vaisnava is transferred to his tadiya. They enable a Vaisnava’s presence to he felt and served in places other than where his body is interred, allowing him to be present in multiple places and to reciprocate with his followers.

The third type of samadhi, and the one of immediate relevance to New Vraja-dhama, is the smrti-samadhi, which invokes remembrance of a departed Vaisnava through his tadiya. The spiritually surcharged article or articles interred in a smrti-samadhi may vary. For instance, it may contain one or several of a Vaisnava’s belongings, like his japa-mala; it may be solely dedicated to the inscription of a Vaisnava’s name — a nama-samadhi; or it may have a part of a Vaisnava’s body, like the danta-samadhi containing the tooth of Gadadhara Pandita, or the danta-samadhi of Srila Prabhupada, which has the unique characteristic of being within the deha samadhi of Srila Tamala Krsna Goswami in Mayapur.

I have already explained how the belongings of a devotee are surcharged with his personal potencies and are therefore as worshipable as he. In a similar way, just as Krsna’s names are empowered with His personal potencies, the names of great Vaisnavas are similarly empowered. Thus a nama samadhi is one in which a departed Vaisnava is present by his name.

There is evidence for how the names of great Vaisnavas are empowered with their potencies in Kaviraja Gosvami’s list of Sri Caitanya Mahaprabhu’s associates, at the conclusion of which he says:

*ei tina skandhera kailun sakhara ganana*  
*yan-saba-smarane bhava-bandha-vimocana*

**“Simply by remembering the names of all these branches and subbranches of the three trunks I have described \[Nityananda, Advaita,and Gadadhara\], one attains freedom from the entanglement of material existence.”**

*yan-saba-smarane pai caitanya-carana*  
*yan-saba-smarane haya vanchita purana*

**“Simply by remembering the names of all these Vaisnavas, one can attain the lotus feet of Sri Caitanya Mahaprabhu. Indeed, simply by remembering their holy names, one achieves the fulfilment of all desires.”**

From these statements we see that by remembering the name of Vaisnavas one is liberated, attains the grace of the Lord, and fulfils all spiritual desires. In short, one attains perfection.

The Vaisnava samadhis in New Vraja-dhama are of this nama category, containing the image and name of a departed acarya. The verses of the Caitanya-caritamrta just cited should reassure devotees of the efficacy of such worship and of the dynamic connection they can develop with such transcendental persons.

Nevertheless, we may doubt whether the spiritual potency of or the benefit of worshipping a nama samadhi equals that of other types of samadhis.

It is generally accepted that because the body of a Vaisnava accumulates spiritual potency throughout a lifetime of devotional service it consequently radiates a greater spiritual energy than, for example, flowers that have only touched the body of that devotee for a short time. The physical remains of the brahmana Kausika give us one example of the great potency a samadhi of a great Vaisnavas body may possess.

But because the purpose of all samadhis is to remember and serve great Vaisnavas, by Krsna’s will devotees can avail themselves of equal benefit from all kinds of samadhis. However, the ability to do so is dependent upon the faith that devotees have in the presence of the Vaisnava whom they revere.

The argument that a nama samadhi can bestow the same spiritual benefit as a samadhi of the same Vaisnava’s physical remains canbe supported by the statements of Kaviraja Gosvami already presented. Further supporting evidence can be found in the pastimes of Haridasa Thakura, as described in the Caitanya-bhagavata and reproduced here.

One day, possessed by Anantadeva, a snake charmer extolled the glories of Haridasa Thakura in the midst of a crowded market place. In truth, Anantadeva was speaking through the snake charmer. After having described the spiritual attainments of the Thakura, the snake charmer said, “I assure you that one who simply chants the name of Haridasa without offence will certainly attain the abode of Krsna.”

In conclusion, while there is a gradation of spiritual power that emanates spontaneously from different kind of samadhis, faithful devotees who are free from offence can derive equal benefit from them all. Indeed, we should also remember that Krsna gives His associates free will to bestow their mercy upon practising devotees, as those associates desire. Thus there is no limit to the extent to which they can manifest themselves to the faithful through the medium of their samadhis.

**The Smrti-samadhis of New Vraja-dhama**

The smrti-samadhis of New Vraja-dhama are nama-samadhis, though in certain cases they may contain flowers, dust, or other items taken from the “original” samadhi of that Vaisnava’s physical remains, whether in Bhauma Vrndavana or elsewhere. The presence of these additional items is meant to increase devotees’ faith, but based on what has already been said about the potency of nama-samadhis, such items are not required.

Even after great Vaisnavas have returned to the spiritual world in their perfected forms, they continue to reside within their samadhis in spiritual forms that resemble those in which they walked this earth, commonly referred to as sadhaka forms. Therefore, the samadhis situated at the homes in New Vraja-dhama give residence to great acaryas, and the devotees of New Vraja-dhama may meditate that they are living in the company of these great souls. If we remain keenly aware of that unparalleled opportunity, then not only will our Krsna consciousness he greatly enhanced, hut by some good fortune we may also be able to see those Vaisnavas with whom we reside, either in our dreams or in person. In order to increase devotees’ faith, or perhaps to offer guidance, these great souls may give indications of their presence in some other tangible way.

For instance, after Raghunatha Dasa Gosvami’s ashes were placed in samadhi on the banks of Syama-kunda, he appeared to Jiva Gosvami and instructed him to move his samadhi to its present location on the bank of Radha-kunda. There are other reliable accounts of Dasa Gosvami and Sanatana Gosvami making their presence known, and there is also Srila Prabhupada’s own account of being instructed by Rupa Gosvami.

In this regard Bhaktisiddhanta Sarasvati Thakura writes,

**“They \[the associates of Lord Caitanya\] can show themselves to us if they choose to do so. Appearing as self-realised souls, the eternal associates of Sri Krsna Caitanya come down to this material world. And they act in such a way that they will not be misunderstood hy conditioned souls.”**

Thus by attentively serving the samadhis established at their homes, the devotees of New Vraja-dhama live under the shelter of “guardian angels” and feel enriched in all respects.

Although the residents of a particular house are its samadhi’s natural custodians, our acaryas have appeared in the dhama for the benefit of everyone and therefore all are welcome to visit any samadhi to offer their respects. Of course, on the appearance and disappearance days of these departed Vaisnavas, everyone should try to visit their respective samadhis. On those days the custodians of the relevant samadhi may host a simple festival of kirtana, hatha, and prasada according to their means.

What is expected of devotees who wish to show their respect to a Vaisnava in his samadhi?

The overall mood is very similar to the way in which we approach and serve Radha-Syamasundara, or Srila Prabhupada in his daily guru-puja. Although there is an ontological difference between the Lord and His devotee, the two are worshipped on an equal level. That is the meaning of *saksad-dharitvena*, “The spiritual master is to be honoured as much as the Supreme Lord.”

When devotees visit a samadhi they first offer obeisances and, if it is known to them, the specific pranama-mantra of that Vaisnava. Thereafter they may offer a flower or a stick of incense and then circumambulate the samadhi three times. These ritual aspects of samadhi worship grant spiritual benefit in proportion to the devotion with which they are performed, as do any offered prayers, which are the real essence of service to Vaisnavas.

After having shown due respect by worship, devotees may take the opportunity to personally connect with great souls through prayer. Prayers offered to the lotus feet of a Vaisnava are similar to those offered to Krsna; one should always begin a prayer by offering words of praise for the special achievements and qualities for which that empowered soul is known.

Prayer is very much a personal communication between the worshipper and the worshipped, and so devotees may repeat standard prayers or offer prayers of their own. And though we may reveal our minds, describing the tribulations of our own spiritual journeys to these venerable Vaisnavas, we should always keep in mind that our acaryas are like desire trees — they can provide material necessities, but they can also award the topmost goal of life. Therefore we should always remember that whatever our material needs or desires, our revered saints are forever fixed in the knowledge that our greatest necessity is whatever will help us return to Krsna.

The mood of samadhi worship is one of its special features. When we say that devotees should cultivate the mood of service in separation, samadhi worship is a practical way of doing just that, so practical that it is accessible even to beginners. Although a great Vaisnava has departed from our vision, by serving, remembering, and praying to him, we can tangibly feel his association.

Bhakti-ratnakara describes how Srinivasa Acarya prayed at and worshipped the samadhis of Rupa Gosvami and Sanatana Gosvami. His conduct, as it is described in that text, is a perfect example for devotees to emulate. And his glorification of and prayers to those two acaryas recorded there can serve as models for our own devotional compositions. What follows is a summary of that account.

**\* \* \* \***

When it was decided that Srinivasa, Narottama, and Syamananda would take the books of the Gosvamis to Bengal, Srinivasa visited the various Deities of Vrndavana to pay his respects and to beg for Their blessings. He also visited Sanatana Gosvamis samadhi, where he fell upon the ground and with great emotion called out, “O Prabhu Sanatana! O friend of the fallen and unfortunate.”

Srinivasa was deeply attached to Sanatana Gosvami, whom Caitanya Mahaprabhu respectfully referred to as the greatest scholar and would often call His favourite devotee. Srinivasa remembered the prayer Raghunatha Dasa Gosvami had composed to glorify Sanatana:

*vairgya-yug-bhakti-rasam prayatnair*  
*apayayan mam anabhipsum andham*  
*krpambudhir yah para-duhkha-duhkhi*  
*sanatanas tam prabhum asrayami*

**“I was unwilling to drink the nectar of devotional service possessed of renunciation, but Sanatana Gosvami, out of his causeless mercy, made me drink it, even though I was otherwise unable to do so. Therefore he is an ocean of mercy. He is very compassionate to fallen souls like me, and thus it is my duty to offer my respectful obeisances unto his lotus feet.”**

Sitting beside Sanatana Gosvaml’s samadhi, Srinivasa lost control of himself and cried incessantly. After some time Jiva Gosvami was able to pacify him. Srinivasa then continued on to Rupa Gosvaml’s samadhi where he offered his respects again and again, being careful to control his emotions. But when be bowed before Rupa’s tomb, tears fell from his eyes, and as he remembered the wonderful characteristics of Rupa Gosvami, Srinivasa’s heart melted just like wax melts in fire.

Srinivasa recited prayers to glorify Rupa Gosvami, and then within his mind he meditated upon the Gosvamis wonderful characteristics, sometimes speaking them aloud. He also offered a prayer composed by Kavi-karnapura:

*priya-svarupe dayita-svarupe prema-svarupe sahajabhirupe*  
*nijanurupe prabhur eka-rupe tatana rupe sva-vilasa-rupe*

**“Indeed, Srila Rupa Gosvami, whose dear friend was Svarupa Damodara, was the exact replica of Sri Caitanya Mahaprabhu, and he was very, very dear to the Lord. Being the embodiment of Sri Caitanya Mahaprabhu’s ecstatic love, Rupa Gosvami was naturally very beautiful. He very carefully followed the principles enunciated by the Lord, and he was a competent person to explain properly the pastimes of Lord Krsna. Sri Caitanya Mahaprabhu expanded His mercy to Srila Rupa Gosvami just so he could render service by writing transcendental literatures.”**

While recalling the attributes of Rupa Gosvami, Srinivasa repeatedly sighed,

**“O Sri Rupa! When will you give me the shelter of your lotus feet?”**

Srinivasa then thought, “Had Rupa Gosvami not been born in Kali-yuga who would be inspired to distribute Krsna consciousness throughout the world? Who would renounce all pleasures, worship Vrndavana, and discuss books on devotional service? Who could understand Krsna’s Vrndavana pastimes? And who could explain the loving exchanges between Radha and Krsna?”

Caitanya Mahaprabhu would also praise Rupa Gosvamis qualities, and the Lord’s associates accepted Sri Rupa as their very life. Indeed it is said that aside from Rupa Gosvami, only Sanatana knew all the technicalities of Vaisnava rituals and, more importantly, how to live according to the Vaisnava etiquette taught in Srimad-Bhagavatam.

Srinivasa meditated on how Rupa Gosvami was the foremost devotee, a reservoir of love and mercy, an ocean of kindness, the friend of the poor, a perfect gentleman, the dust of whose lotus feet cooled the earth from the scorching fire of Kali-yuga, and the scholar that continually worshipped Vyasadeva and Srimad Radharani.

Offering repeated obeisances, Srinivasa prayed,

**“All glories to Sri Rupa, the jewel of all ascetics, Lord Caitanya’s gilt to the world, that great soul whose devotion to Radha and Krsna was unflinching and who, upon giving up all worldly pleasures, sang the glories of the groves of Vrndavana. In his absence I have become an orphan.”**

Bhakti-ratnakara says that after completing worship of Sri Rupa’s samadhi, Srinivasa Acarya received Sri Rupa’s mercy. By following Srinivasa’s example we too may he the recipients of our acaryas mercy. That will certainly make our lives successful.

After offering praise and prayers, devotees may also offer some menial service, like sweeping or cleaning the samadhi and the surrounding area. And if they so desire, they may assist with the samadhis ongoing maintenance and beautification.

Although devoices in New Vraja-dhama are engaged in many services, thus remaining busy throughout the day, they should not forget the presence of the many samadhis that decorate their village. In Bhauma Vrndavana the residents often take vows to daily visit the main temples and samadhis there. Devotees in New Vraja-dhama may also take vows to visit the local samadhis as is practical for them, whether that be daily, weekly, or monthly. But at the very least, they should visit each samadhi at least once a year.

I shall conclude this appendix by reviewing a few duties of the samadhis’ custodians, who are so very fortunate to live in the association of great souls. As the words smrti-samadhi imply, these shrines are established primarily to help their custodians remember the exalted Vaisnavas they have been given the opportunity to serve.

Since the actual samadhis will generally be in devotees’ gardens, it is the duty of the custodians to keep the shrines and the surrounding areas neat and clean. Generally, service to the samadhis need not be elaborate. Offering a flower or a stick of incense once daily is sufficient. But as devotees come and go past the samadhi during the course of their other daily duties, they should always call out the name of the resident samadhi-acarya. For example, as the custodian of Narottama Dasa Thakura’s samadhi comes in and out of his house and walks past the samadhi he should always exclaim, “Thakura Mahasaya ki jaya!”

Over and above these activities, it is recommended that in their daily prayers devotees include a prayer to their samadhi-acarya. Also, the appearance and disappearance days of each acarya should be observed by inviting other devotees to his samadhi to enjoy kirtana, discussion of the acarya s glories, arati, and an offering of Radha-Syamasundara’s maha-prasada.

Of the two — appearance and disappearance days — Vaisnavas celebrate the latter more elaborately. Why? Because when a great soul leaves this world his accomplishments and purity have been made obvious by the passage of time. Therefore, in the Gaudiya Vaisnava calendar the appearance days of all acaryas may not be recorded, although their disappearance days always arc.

According to our means, we should observe these disappearance days with a festival. The following anecdote gives a practical example of how simple such a festival may be.

Once, Gaurakisora Dasa Babaji announced to his servant Bihari, “This afternoon we will observe a great festival to honour Sanatana Gosvami’s passing.”

Bihari replied, “But Babaji Maharaja, we have no ingredients with which to prepare a festival.”

Babaji Maharaja raised his hands and said, “We have the main ingredient for a festival, we don’t need anything else. We will simply chant Hare Krsna and fast and in that way enjoy a great celebration.”

Concluding on this note, let us remember that like Radha-Syama, our acaryas are most readily approached through the medium of nama-sankirtana. Without chanting Hare Krsna, the most elaborate festival remains incomplete. But whenever we chant Hare Krsna purely a dry fast becomes the greatest festival, for as Thakura Bhaktivinoda wrote:

**He reasons ill who says that Vaisnavas die,**  
**When thou art living still in sound!**  
**The Vaisnavas die to live, and living try**  
**To spread the holy name around.**

Dear devotees! With your tongues, always sing the names of the Vaisnavas, the friends of the fallen; within your minds, always remember the Vaisnavas, the personifications of kindness; and with your heads, always bow to the Vaisnavas, the embodiments of grace.

## About the Author

Sivarama Swami was born in Budapest, Hungary in 1949, and his family emigrated to Canada during the failed 1956 Hungarian revolution. He became a disciple of A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupada, the founder-acarya of ISKCON, in 1973 and entered the renounced order of life (sannyasa) in 1979. Sivarama Swami is well known for his deep knowledge of Vaisnava literature and has written many books about Gaudiya Vaisnavism, including the “Krsna in Vrndavana’’ series. He is a member of iskcon’s Governing Body Commission and is responsible for projects in Hungary, Turkey, and Romania. Although he travels and speaks internationally, he spends as much time as possible at New Vraja-dhama (Krishna Valley), a replica of Vrndavana and self-sufficient farming project in Hungary’s countryside.

If anyone, She finds a nice devotee of Krsna, She immediately recommends to Krsna, ‘How nice this devotee. He can render better service than Me. Please accept him.’ This is spiritualism. This is spiritualism. Who can give better service than Radharani? She is so great that She is captivating Krsna. Krsna captivates Madana, Cupid. But Radharani captivates Krsna. How great She is.”

Ramananda Raya describes what happens when Radha’s and Krsna’s hearts melt and mix together: “O my Lord, You live in the forest of Govardhana Hill, and, like the king of elephants, You are expert in the art of conjugal love. ? master of the universe, Your heart and Srimati Radharani’s heart are just like shellac and are now melted in Your spiritual perspiration. Therefore one can no longer distinguish between You and Srimati Radharani. Now You have mixed Your newly invoked affection, which is like vermilion, with Your melted hearts, and for the benefit of the whole world You have painted both Your hearts red within this great palace of the universe.” (Sri Caitanya-caritamrta, Madhya-lila 8.195)

Sri Caitanya-caritamrta (Madhya-lila 8.28-43) describes the companions of Ramananda Raya who accompanied him to the Godavari River where he met Lord Caitanya as “outsider brahmanas,” “nondevotees,” and “not pure devotees.” Despite their lack of devotional credentials, by the influence of Lord Caitanya’s presence these associates of Ramananda Raya began to shed tears and chant the names of Krsna.

Although Dig is a large place and can he found on Parikrama maps, it apparently has no special religious significance, for it lies outside Vraja-dhama and no pastimes were performed there. It is mentioned in the Skanda Purana as Dirghapura, a place Vajra was asked to repopulate. It is also known as Lathavana for the following reason: Once a king of Dig (possibly Jawahar Singh) lamented that pilgrims did not visit his town. He thus decreed that pilgrims who did not pass through Dig would be forcibly brought there by soldiers armed with canes (lathis).

According to the brahmanas in Badarikasrama, the sage Nara- Narayana Rsi appeared in Badari to show the world the necessity of performing penance (tapasya). The name Nara-Narayana Rsi is used in two ways: to denote the two sages Nara and Narayana, and as another name for Narayana Rsi. Nara is an expansion of the Pandava Arjuna and Narayana is an expansion of Krsna. Both sages reside eternally in Adi-badri.

In Satya-yuga the sages were visible to everyone, in Treta-yuga only those who had performed penance could see them, and in Dvapara-yuga, only the pure in heart. As it became progressively more difficult to sec the Lord, saints prayed that He make a concession for the fallen souls of Kali-yuga. The Lord agreed and manifested His Deity form, which is worshipped half of the year by humans and the other half by demigods.

Visvakarma constructed a temple for the Deity of Nara-Narayana Rsi, but some Buddhists later threw the Deity into nearby Narada kunda . Later, when Sahkaracarya visited the site, the Deity was recovered and reinstalled in the temple.

Tapta means hot or heated. The Tapta-kunda in the Himalayas is fed hy natural hot springs and so is warm all year round, even in winter.

A muhurta is forty-eight minutes, so half a muhurta is twenty-four minutes.

The sacred thread (upavita) is the sign of initiation (diksa). In ancient Vedic culture vaisyas wore one strand of threads, ksatriyas two, and brahmanas three. Each strand consists of three strings.

Sripada Sahkaracarya is an incarnation of Lord Siva, Lord Krsna’s best devotee (vaisnavanam yatha sambhuh). But, as shown in the following verses from the Padma Purana, he was requested by the Lord to preach Mayavada philosophy, thus making people averse to the Lord and to the Lord’s devotional service. For a great devotee like Lord Siva, that was certainly a thankless mission.

*mayavadam asac chastram pracchannam bauddham ucyate*  
*mayaiva kalpitam devi kalau brahmana-rupina*  
*brahmanas caparam rupam nirgunam vaksyate maya*  
*sarva-svam jagato ’py asya mohanartham kalau yuge*  
*vedante tu maha-sastre mayavadam avaidikam*  
*mayaiva vaksyate devi jagatam nasa-karanat*

**“‘The Mayavada philosophy,’ Lord Siva informed his wife Parvati, ‘is impious \[asac chastra\]. It is covered Buddhism. My dear Parvati, in Kali-yuga I assume the form of a brahmana and teach this imagined Mayavada philosophy. In order to cheat the atheists, I describe the Supreme Personality of Godhead to he without form and without qualities. Similarly, in explaining Vedanta, I describe the same Mayavada philosophy in order to mislead the entire population towards atheism by denying the personal form of the Lord.”’**

In the Siva Purana the Supreme Personality of Godhead tells Lord Siva, dvaparadau yuge bhutva kalaya manusadisu / svagamaih kalpitais tvam ca janan mad-vimukhan kuru: “In Kali-yuga, mislead the people in general by propounding imaginary meanings for the Vedas to bewilder them.” (Sri Caitanya-caritamrta, Adi-lila 7.110, purport)

Chapter twenty-six of Jaiva-dharma lists the eighteen material faults given in the Vaisnava-tantra: illusion, fatigue, error, crassness, lust, restlessness, pride, envy, violence, disgrace, exhaustion, untruth, anger, hankering, dependence, desire to lord over the universe, seeing duality, and cheating.

In Srimad-Bhagavatam (10.21.16) the gopis describe the friendship between Krsna and the clouds, praise further elaborated on by the commentaries of the acaryas, from which these two stanzas are derived. The verse is as follows, drstvatape vraja-pasun saha ramagopaih sancarayantam anu venum udirayantam / prema-pravrddha uditah kusumavalibhih sakhyur vyadhat sva-vapusambuda atapatram: “In the company of Balarama and the cowherd boys, Lord Krsna is continually vibrating His flute as He herds all the animals of Vraja, even under the full heat of the summer sun. Seeing this, the cloud in the sky has expanded himself out of love. He is rising high and constructing out of his own body, with its multitude of flower-like droplets of water, an umbrella for the sake of his friend.”

Sridhara Svami gives an interesting derivation for a bhara: “Four grains of rice are called one gunja; five gunjas one pana; eight panas one karsa; four karsas one pala; and one hundred palas, one tula. Twenty tulas make up one bhara.” (Bhavartha-dipika 10.56.11) Eight bharas, then, constitutes 10,240,000 grains of rice. Since there are about 13 grains of rice to a gram, the Syamantaka gem was producing about 780 kilograms of gold daily. At today’s prices of $1,200 per ounce, that would he roughly equivalent to 33 million dollars per day.

Pradyumna is in the category of visnu-tattva, one of the catur-vyuha. But by Krsna’s arrangement, Cupid, who according to Jiva Gosvami is jiva-tattva, also appeared in Pradyumna’s body. This is sometimes the case with the Lord’s eternal associates: two living entities are present in one form. When Krsna returned to Goloka, the portion of Pradyumna that was visnu-tattva returned to His own abode, while Cupid returned to heaven, where he continues to exercise his influence according to the time allotted to him by his karma. In his purport to Sri Caitanya-caritamrta, Adi-lila 1.57 Srila Prabhupada writes that Bilvamangala Thakura was horn in the eighth century Saka (roughly the ninth century AD). If he lived for 700 years at Brahma-kunda, he must have still been alive in the sixteenth century, meaning that he outlived the Gosvamis of Vrndavana. However, since Krsnadasa Kaviraja quoted Krsna-karnamrta in Sri Caitanya-caritamrta and wrote his own commentary on it, the book must have been composed by a much earlier date. This is also confirmed by Caitanya Mahaprabhu’s finding a copy of the text while He was in western India. The Lord brought it with Him to Puri, where devotees did not yet have the book. (Sri Caitanya-caritamrta, Madhya-lila 1.120) But the question then arises, if Bilvamangala Thakura was alive during the time of the Gosvamis, why is he not mentioned in their writings? Either the saint remained unrecognised, or otherwise hidden from view, or, like some other Vaisnavas, perhaps he requested that he not be mentioned in any texts.

It is said that Kasisvara was extremely reluctant to leave Lord Caitanya’s company in Puri, so the Lord gave a Deity of Himself to Kasisvara and explained that He was not different from His Deity form. To give Kasisvara further faith in His words, Caitanya Mahaprabhu sat down along with His Deity, and the two Caitanyas both ate together before Kasisvara, greatly satisfying Their devotee. Having personally witnessed that the Lord and His Deity were identical, Kasisvara left for Vrndavana along with the Deity of Gaura-Govinda, which is worshipped in Jaipur alongside Radha-Govinda to this very day.

The text and purport to Srimad-Bhagavatam 3.4.26 are reproduced here in their entirety:

Sri Uddhava said: You may take lessons from the great learned sage Maitreya, who is nearby and who is worshipable for reception of transcendental knowledge. He was directly instructed by the Personality of Godhead while He was about to quit this mortal world.

Purport: Although one may be well versed in the transcendental science, one should be careful about the offence of maryada-vyatikrama, or impertinently surpassing a greater personality. According to scriptural injunction one should he very careful of transgressing the law of maryada-vyatikrama because hy so doing one loses his duration of life, his opulence, fame, and piety and the blessings of all the world. To be well versed in the transcendental science necessitates awareness of the techniques of spiritual science. Uddhava, being well aware of all these technicalities of transcendental science, advised Vidura to approach Maitreya Rsi to receive transcendental knowledge. Vidura wanted to accept Uddhava as his spiritual master, but Uddhava did not accept the post because Vidura was as old as Uddhava’s father and therefore Uddhava could not accept him as his disciple, especially when Maitreya was present nearby. The rule is that in the presence of a higher personality one should not be very eager to impart instructions, even if one is competent and well versed. So Uddhava decided to send an elderly person like Vidura to Maitreya, another elderly person, but he was well versed also because he was directly instructed by the Lord while He was about to quit this mortal world. Since both Uddhava and Maitreya were directly instructed by the Lord, both had the authority to become the spiritual master of Vidura or anyone else, but Maitreya, being elderly, had the first claim to becoming the spiritual master, especially for Vidura, who was much older than Uddhava. One should not be eager to become a spiritual master cheaply for the sake of profit and fame, but should become a spiritual master only for the service of the Lord. The Lord never tolerates the impertinence of maryada-vyatikrama. One should never pass over the honour due to an elderly spiritual master in the interests of one’s own personal gain and fame. Impertinence on the part of the pseudo spiritual master is very risky to progressive spiritual realisation.

The babajis' approach to the worship of Radha and Krsna in the mood of spontaneous devotion is based on a spiritual tradition passed down through an unbroken succession of initiating spiritual masters who reveal the spiritual identity of their disciples at the time of initiation. Their understanding is rhat the mood of vraja-bhakti flows to practising devotees through this line, also known as siddha-pranali. According to this babaji line, for a practitioner’s meditation to be effective he or she must know the divine characteristics of the spiritual master and the spiritual lineage as far back as the associates of Lord Caitanya. This defines not only the babajis’ spiritual family tree but also the spiritual group or family in which they serve, because these identities are eternal. These spiritual families or groups have unique practices for their members and specific services and characteristics in their original transcendental services. Since Madhusudana Dasa BabajI did not know his guru’s lineage or characteristic services, other babajis could not and would not advise himin sadhana; rather, they advised him to depend on the holy name and Radha’s and Krsna’s mercy, which ultimately granted Madhusudana Dasa Babaji all perfection.

The Saints of Bengal, page 12.

The Brahma-vaivarta Purana (4.2) tells the story of Radha’s cursing Sridama. The curse resulted in a plenary portion of Sridama taking birth as the indestructible demon Sankhacuda, who, according to the curse, would return to his constitutional state when Krsna killed him. Nara-Narayana Rsi tells how, in the opulent portion of Goloka Vrndavana, Krsna was once confidentially enjoying with a gopi called Viraja, an indiscretion Radha quickly came to know of. To pacify His beloved, Krsna, accompanied by Sridama, went to appease Srimati Radharani. But She refused to allow the Lord to approach Her, speaking very critically of Him. Incensed hy Radha’s harsh words, Sridama scolded Her severely. Their heated argument ended in mutual curses: Radha cursed Sridama to become a demon on earth, and he in turn cursed Her to take birth as a woman on earth when Krsna appeared there. That woman, Sridama threatened, would he married to someone who was the form of Krsna’s shadow, and She would he separated from Krsna for one hundred years.

Devotees sometimes reject scriptures whose narrations of Krsna's pastimes differ from those found in Srimad-Bhagavatam. Such devotees may question the inclusion of stories from scriptures such as Sri Garga-samhita in this book. Bur Sanatana Gosvami, our previous acarya and senior disciple of Sri Caitanya Mahaprabhu, includes the Garga-samhita in a list of Vedic scriptures that discuss the confidential science of pure devotional service: “The confidential science of pure devotional service is taught explicitly only in a few Vedic scriptures little known to the public. Amongst these rare texts are the Gopalatapani Upanisad, the Narada Pancaratra, the Garga-samhita, the Visnu Purana, the Harivamsa Upapurana, the Uttara-khanda of the Padma Purana, and the Bhagavata Maha-purana \[Srimad-Bhagavatam\]." (Sri Brhad-bhagavatamrta 1.1.21-23, commentary)

Sri Caitanya-caritamrta, Adi-lila 2.117.

4\. The pastimes related to how Vrndavana forest got its name are adapted from the Brahma-vaivarta Purana 4.17.

The Vrnda in this pastime is not the eternal associate of Radha and Krsna but her expansion, just as Nanda and Yasoda have their own expansions named Drona and Dhara respectively.

The Brahma-vaivarta Purana says that the father of these two princesses was Kusadhvaja. But according to Srimad-Bhagavatam, Kusadhvaja was their brother and they were all children of Maharaja Janaka, who is also known as Siradhvaja: “From Hrasvaroma came a son named Siradhvaja \[also called Janaka\]. When Siradhvaja was ploughing a field, from the front of his plough \[sira\] appeared a daughter named Sitadevi, who later became the wife of Lord Ramacandra. Thus he was known as Siradhvaja. The son of Siradhvaja was Kusadhvaja, and the son of Kusadhvaja was King Dharmadhvaja, who had two sons, namely Krtadhvaja and Mitadhvaja.” (Srimad-Bhagavatam 9.13.18-19) I have here presented the genealogy as it is described in Srimad-Bhagavatam.

The pastime of Visvakarma constructing Vrndavana town is adapted from the Brahma-vaivarta Purana 4, chapters 17 and 18.

Vrndavana village in New Vraja-dhama is not mapped to replicate the arrangement of Bhauma Vrndavana’s Pancakrosi Vrndavana. In New Vraja-dhama the devotees’ homes correspond to samadhis and temples found in Pancakrosi Vrndavana. In some cases this means that the location of pastime places in the two dhamas does not correspond.

As mentioned in chapter nineteen, Srila Sanatana Gosvami was cremated and a samadhi mandira was built to house his ashes. This is the meaning of his being placed in samadhi in this stanza.

The tradition of mudiya is that male members of the family shave their heads upon the death of their father. From that day, the Guru- purnima festival became the most attended festival at Govardhana.

Even today more pilgrims come to do Parikrama of Govardhana on that day than any other — even Govardhana-puja to honour the memory of Srila Sanatana Gosvami.

These two stanzas are excerpted from Srila Prabhupada’s purport to Srimad-Bhagavatam 3.25.36. The entire purport is reproduced here, in which His Divine Grace thoroughly explains how even third-class devotees attain a liberated position by absorption in the Deity of the Lord: “There are three divisions of devotees — first-class, second- class, and third-class. Even the third-class devotees are liberated souls. It is explained in this verse that although they do not have knowledge, simply hy seeing the beautiful decoration of the Deity in the temple, the devotee is absorbed in thought of Him and loses all other consciousness. Simply hy fixing oneself in Krsna consciousness, engaging the senses in the service of the Lord, one is imperceptibly liberated. This is also confirmed in Bhagavad-gita. Simply by discharging uncontaminated devotional service as prescribed in the scriptures, one becomes equal to Brahman. In Bhagavad-gita it is said, hrahma-bhuyaya kalpate. This means that the living entity in his original state is Brahman because he is part and parcel of the Supreme Brahman. But simply because of his forgetfulness of his real nature as an eternal servitor of the Lord, he is overwhelmed and captured by maya. His forgetfulness of his real constitutional position is maya. Otherwise he is eternally Brahman.

“When one is trained to become conscious of his position, he understands that he is the servitor of the Lord. ‘Brahman’ refers to a state of self-realisation. Even the third-class devotee — who is not advanced in knowledge of the Absolute Truth but simply offers obeisances with great devotion, thinks of the Lord, sees the Lord in the temple and brings forth flowers and fruits to offer to the Deity — becomes imperceptibly liberated. Sraddhayanvitah: with great devotion the devotees offer worshipful respects and paraphernalia to the Deity. The Deities of Radha and Krsna, Laksmi and Narayana, and Rama and Sita are very attractive to devotees, so much so that when they see the statue decorated in the temple of the Lord they become fully absorbed in thought of the Lord. That is the state of liberation. In other words, it is confirmed herewith that even a third-class devotee is in the transcendental position, above those who are trying for liberation by speculation or by other methods. Even great impersonalists like Sukadeva Gosvami and the four Kumaras were attracted by the beauty of the Deities in the temple, by the decorations, and by the aroma of tulasi offered to the Lord, and they became devotees. Even though they were in the liberated state, instead of remaining impersonalists they were attracted by the beauty of the Lord and became devotees.

“Here the word vilasa is very important. Vilasa refers to the activities or pastimes of the Lord. It is a prescribed duty in temple worship that not only should one visit the temple to see the Deity nicely decorated, hut at the same rime he should hear the recitation of Srimad-Bhagavatam, Bhagavad-gita, or some similar literature, which is regularly recited in the temple. It is the system in Vrndavana that in every temple there is recitation of the sastras. Even third-class devotees who have no literary knowledge or no rime to read Srimad- Bhagavatam or Bhagavad-gita get the opportunity to bear about the pastimes of the Lord. In this way their minds may remain always absorbed in the thought of the Lord — His form, His activities, and His transcendental nature. This state of Krsna consciousness is a liberated stage. Lord Caitanya, therefore, recommended five important processes in the discharge of devotional service: (1) to chant the holy names of the Lord, Hare Krsna, Hare Krsna, Krsna Krsna, Hare Hare / Hare Rama, Hare Rama, Rama Rama, Hare Hare, (2) toassociate with devotees and serve them as tar as possible, (3) to hear Srimad-Bhagavatam, (4) to see the decorated temple and the Deity, and, if possible, (5) to live in a place like Vrndavana or Mathura. These five items alone can help a devotee achieve the highest perfectional stage. This is confirmed in Bhagavad-gita and here in the Srimad-Bhagavatam. That third-class devotees can also imperceptibly achieve liberation is accepted in all Vedic literatures.”

Srila Prabhupada writes that Gopala Bhatta Gosvami asked Krsnadasa Kaviraja Gosvami not to mention his name in Sri Caitanya-caritamrta. (Sri Caitanya-caritamrta, Adi-lila 10.105, purport) For this reason, Gopala Bhatta’s name is only mentioned a couple of times in that great work, despite Gopala Bhatta’s obvious significance. We also know that Prabodhananda Sarasvati is the guru of Gopala Bhatta. A possible explanation, then, for the lack of reference to Prabodhananda in the Gosvamis’ works is that Prabodhananda set the example for his disciple Gopala Bhatta and likewise asked not to be mentioned. After all, it is common for a disciple to take the mood of his guru.
