Ramananda-samvada was handwritten by Srila Prabhupada in 1959, and the manuscript was found amongst his belongings in Vrndavana in 1977, after his departure. Hari Sauri Prabhu recalls that Srila Prabhupada knew of the trunk where the manuscript was found, and simply gave the instruction to “destroy it;” he had already used the entire manuscript in his Sri Caitanya-caritamrta purports to Mahaprabhu’s conversations with Ramananda Raya. While a large portion of the original manuscript was never found, and the partial manuscript independently published some years later, Srila Prabhupada’s purports in Sri Caitanya-caritamrta expanded on his original manuscript considerably, making it a far more accurate and complete source of what he considered an important section of Sri Caitanya-caritamrta.

The following is a list of the literary works of Srila Visvanatha Cakravarti Thakura.

Original titles:

Sri Krsna-bhavanamrta-mahakavya (Nectar-meditations on Sri Krsna's Daily Sports) — 1,347 Sanskrit verses in twenty chapters that describe the eight periods of a day in the life of the divine couple and Their friends.

Sankalpa-kalpadruma (The Desire Tree of Resolute Determination) — One hundred and four Sanskrit prayers to Sri Radhika for the attainment of specific services during the eight periods of the day in Goloka Vrndavana. Though often published as a separate hook, this work is part of Visvanatha’s collection of prayers entitled Stavamrta-lahari.

Camatkara-candrika (A Moonbeam of Sheer Astonishment) —Two hundred and twenty-six Sanskrit verses in four chapters, each a short story depicting Sri Krsna’s mischievous pranks, conducted in various disguises: (a) Meeting in the Box, (b) Meeting in the Disguise of Abhimanyu, (c) Meeting in the Disguise of a Female Doctor, (d) Meeting in the Disguise of a Female Singer.

Prema-samputa (The Jewel Box of Love)—One hundred and forty-one Sanskrit verses narrating the story of Krsna, disguised as a demigoddess, approaching Sri Radha and extracting Her confidential confessions of Her selfless love for Him.

Vraja-riti-cintamani (The Touchstone of Life in Vraja)—Two hundred and thirty-four Sanskrit verses in three chapters describing the holy flora, fauna, hills, lakes, groves, temples, and towns of the eternal realm of Vraja.

Gauranga-lilamrta (The Nectar of Sri Gauranga's Daily Pastimes) — Eleven Sanskrit verses depicting Sri Mahaprabhu’s daily pastimes conducted in eight periods of the day. The descriptions of the pastimes in each verse are expanded by extensive Bengali verses composed by Sri Visvanatha’s direct disciple, the poet Krsnadasa.

Caitanya-rasayana (The Nectar-Tonic of Sri Caitanya)—an uncompleted work mentioned in chapter thirteen of Sri Narottama- vilasa hy Sri Narahari Cakravarti, the son of Visvanatha’s disciple Jagannatha Vipra.

Raga vartma candrika (A Moonbeam Revealing the Path of Spontaneous Devotion)—Twenty-two Sanskrit paragraphs in two chapters of prose and verse that elaborate on the proper behaviour and attitudes of one following the path of spontaneous devotional service.

Madhurya-kadambini (A Row of Clouds of Sweetness) —Eight chapters of Sanskrit prose that scientifically analyse the various stages of advancement that one ascends on the devotional path.

Aisvarya-kadambini (A Row of Clouds of Majesty) —A work mentioned hy Visvanatha in the second chapter of his Madhurya-kadambini. Not to be confused with the work hy Sri Baladeva Vidyabhusana of the same name. The book by Visvanatha is supposed to discuss the philosophy of “Dvaitadvaitavada,” but no copy of this work has ever been found.

Ujjvala-nilamani-kirana (One Ray of Ujjvala-nilamani) — Sixteen paragraphs of Sanskrit prose, a condensed summary study of Srila Rupa Gosvamis 1,453-verse Ujjvala-nilamani. It is a study of the psychology of the divine couple’s relationships with each other and with Their girlfriends.

Bhakti-rasamrta-sindhu-bindhu (A Drop From the Nectar-Ocean of Devotion)—Twenty-seven Sanskrit notes, a summary of Srila Rupa Gosvaml’s book Bhakti-rasamrta-sindhu, which outlines the process of devotional service.

Bhagavatamrta-kana (A Particle of Sri Rupa Gosvami’s Laghu-bhagavatamrta) — Fifteen Sanskrit notes outlining the contents of Sri Rupa’s book, which describes Sri Krsna’s various incarnations and plenary portions.

Gaura-gana-svarupa-tattva-candrika (A Moonbeam Revealing the Truth of the Identity of Gaura’s Associates)—A book that follows the earlier work of Sri Kavi-karnapura called Gaura-ganoddesa-dipika. It reveals the vraja lila identities of various descendants of the Gaudiya sampradaya, with updates to include various persons that appeared in the sampradaya after Kavi-karnapura time.

Rupa-cintamani (The Philosophers’ Stone of Gauranga's Bodily Beauty) — Sanskrit verses describing the exact locations of the sacred marks found on the palms and soles of Lord Caitanya, Sri Nityananda, and Sri Advaita. Visvanatha composed another work called Rupa-cintamani that describes the head-to-toc beauty of Sri Sri Radha-Krsna, as well as the marks on the soles of Their lotus feet; this work is included in his collection called Stavamrta-lahari.

Ksanada-gita-cintamani (The Philosophers’ Stone of Songs to be Sung at Night)—This is the first anthology of devotional songs written hy Gaudiya Vaisnava poets in the Bengali, Sanskrit, and Braja-bhuli languages. Visvanatha compiled the writings of forty-five authors, totalling three hundred and nine songs, amongst which are fifty-one of his own songs, signed with his pen name, Hari-vallabha. The songs are divided into groups that are to he sung each night of the month; thus there are thirty divisions — fifteen for the dark fortnight and fifteen for the light fortnight.

Mantrartha-dipika (A Torchlight Illuminating the Kama-gayatri Mantra)—Eighteen Sanskrit notes in prose and verse that give detailed explanations of each and every syllable of the kama-bija and Kama-gayatri mantras. It also contains a description of how Visvanatha had a doubt regarding the syllables of the mantra, and how Sri Radha Herself appeared to him in a dream in order to clear that doubt.

Stavamrta-lahari (Wave after Wave of Nectarean Prayers) —A collection of twenty-eight Sanskrit prayers, astakas, meditations, and glorifications.

Commentaries:

Sarartha-darsini (She Who Reveals the Inner, Essential Purports) —A commentary on Srimad-Bhagavatam.

Sarartha-varsini (She Who Showers Forth the Hidden Meanings)—A commentary on the Bhagavad-gita.

Bhakti-sara-pradarsini (She Who Demonstrates the Creamlike Essence of Devotion)—A commentary on Sri Rupa’s Bhakti-rasamrta-sindhu.

Ananda-candrika (Moonbeams of Pure Bliss) —A commentary on Sri Rupa’s Ujjvala-nilamani.

A commentary on Sri Rupa’s Lalita-madhava-nataka.

A commentary on Sri Rupa’s Vidagha-madhava-nataka.

Mahati (She Who Is Glorious) —A commentary on Sri Rupa’s Dana keli kaumudi.

A commentary on Sri Rupa’s Hamsaduta.

Subhodini (She Who Informs Very Nicely) —A commentary on Kavi-karnapura Alankara-kaustubha.

Sukha-vartini (She Who Establishes One in Happiness) — A commentary on Kavi-kanapura’s Ananda-vrndavana-campu.

An incomplete commentary on Krsnadasa Kaviraja’s Sri Caitanya-caritamrta.

A commentary on Narottama Dasa Thakura’s Prema-bhakti-candrika.

A commentary on Sri Brahma-Samhita.

Bhakta-harsini (She Who Gives Great Joy to the Devotees) —A commentary on Gopala-tapani Upanisad.

(Gaudiya 18.18, 1922)

Gaudiya Vaisnavism is traced through the Madhva sampradaya, and so it is called the Madhva-Gaudiya sampradaya.

All works written by Baladeva up to Govinda-bhasya were signed Damodara-vipra-racita, “written by Damodara Vipra.”

Baladeva Vidyabhusana disappeared in 1768. A partial list of his works follows: Govinda-bhasya — a commentary on Vedanta-sutra; Siddhanta-ratna — the “Jewel of Conclusions”; Sahitya-kaumudi — a summary of Vedanta philosophy also known as Vedanta-Syamantaka; Prameya-ratnavali—the “Jewel of Factual Principles” (an outline of the nine principles common to both the Madhva and Caitanya schools); Siddhanta-darsana — a summary of Gaudiya Vaisnava principles; Kalya-Kaustubha— a book of selected Sanskrit verses for Vaisnavas; Aisvarya-kadambini — a glorification of Vraja and Krsna’s pastimes; Vyakarana-kaumudi— a book on grammar; Pada-kaustubha — a book of selected prayers; Gita-bhusana-bhasya— a commentary on Bhagavad-gita. Baladeva also wrote commentaries on: Visnu sahasra nama; Srimad-Bhagavatam (known as Vaisnava-nandini-tika); Jiva Gosvaml’s Tattva-sandarbha; Rupa Gosvami’s Stavamala, Nataka-Candrika, and Laghu-bhagavatamrta; Jayadeva Gosvami’s Candraloka; his own Sahitya-kaumudi and Syamananda-sataka; and selected commentaries on important Upanisads, including Isopanisad and Sri Gopala-tapani Upanisad.

There are other versions of this pastime. One version tells that Holika had a shawl that kept her from being burned. When Prahlada began to pray to the Lord, a gust of wind blew the shawl off of Holika and onto Prahlada, exposing her to the flames and protecting him. Another version tells that Holika, who was actually Prahlada’s well- wisher, gave the shawl to him, thereby sacrificing her life for his.

Gaura-ganoddesa-dipika 133.

Srimad-Bhagavatam 9.3.27-36 and Sri Garga-samhlita 6.3.1-24 tell the following story of King Kakudmi and how he arranged for his daughter to wed Lord Balarama. Maharaja Saryati, whose daughter married the sage Cyavana, had three sons including Anarta, whose son Revata constructed the kingdom Kusasthali in the ocean. Revata’s eldest son was Kakudmi, who had one beautiful and qualified daughter named Revati. The king was very particular about who could marry his daughter. Revati also had high standards; she wanted someone who was handsome, possessed eternal life, and was better than all other men.

The king mounted his chariot along with his daughter and they wandered the earth in search of a suitable man, but could find none. Thus, by his mystic power, King Kakudmi took Revati to Brahmaloka to ask the creator for guidance.

But when Kakudmi arrived, Brahma was watching a dance performance. Only after the performance had ended could the king disclose his desire to Brahma. But once he had heard Maharaja Kakudmi’s appeal, Brahma laughed and said, “O King, all those whom you may have accepted as your son-in-law have passed away in the course of time. Twenty-seven catur-yugas have already passed since you came here. Those whom you could have chosen are now gone, and so are their sons, grandsons, and many, many generations of their descendants.”

Because of the difference in the passing of time between earth and Brahmaloka, what appeared about one hour in the creator’s court was twenty-seven maha-yugas (a period of the four yugas — Satya, Treta, Dvapara, and Kali) on earth. Thus all of Kakudmi’s relatives and their countless descendents had passed away. Thus there was no one left on earth whom Kakudmi had known.

The king was very sad to hear this, but Brahma said, “O King, leave here and offer your daughter to Lord Baladeva, who is still present on earth. He is most powerful. Indeed, He is the Supreme Personality of Godhead, whose plenary portion is Lord Visnu. Your daughter is fit to be given to Him in charity.”

The king and his daughter were both very happy to hear this advice. Offering obeisances to Brahma, they returned to earth using the same mystic power that had brought them to Satyaloka. Upon returning to his kingdom, Kakudmi saw that it was just as Brahma had said: his capital was in ruins, and his relatives were no more. Near to where his own residence had been, now stood Krsna’s capital, Dvaraka. After requesting Visvakarma to construct a special chariot as a wedding present, Kakudmi went to Dvaraka and offered his daughter’s hand to Lord Balarama.

The Yadus held a great wedding festival in which King Kakudmi offered the gift of the gilded, jewelled chariot along with a thousand white horses and celestial garments and jewels sent hy Brahma. Balarama and His new bride visited the home of every Yadu to receive their blessings and gifts. Seeing that he had completed his religious duty, Maharaja Kakudmi bade farewell to his daughter and new son-in-law and retired to Badarikasrama to please Nara-Narayana.

Therefore Virabhadra Gosani is non-different from Sri Krsna Caitanya Mahaprabhu.” (Sri Caitanya-caritamrta, Adi-lila 11.8, purport)

Readers may wonder why Madhavi Mahiti, an intimate associate of Lord Caitanya, is only counted as half an associate. It is due to her gender. Before commenting on this, let us hear the words of Kaviraja Gosvami: “Sikhi Mahiti’s sister was named Madhavi devi. She was an elderly lady who always performed austerities. She was very advanced in devotional service. Sri Caitanya Mahaprabhu accepted her as having formerly been an associate of Srimati Radharani. In the entire world, threc-and-a-half people were His intimate devotees. The three were Svarupa Damodara Gosvami, Ramananda Raya, and Sikhi Mahiti, and the half a person, ardha-jana, was Sikhi Mahiti’s sister.” (Sri Caitanya- caritamrta, Antya-lila 2.104-106) In a comment to an earlier text, Srila Prabhupada writes: “Madhavi-devi, being a woman, was considered the half.” (Sri Caitanya-caritamrta, Adi-lila 10.137, purport)

We can see that two considerations are in play in the descriptions of Lord Caitanya’s intimate associates: their internal spiritual stature and their external relative social status.

Of the hundreds of eternal associates of Lord Caitanya, four have been singled out as being the most confidential, meaning the closest to the Lord. This places them in a unique position, above all others, regardless of their gender. In other words, from the spiritual point of view, Sikhi Mahiti’s sister, Madhavi-devi, was considered as spiritually elevated as the other three, and more elevated than the rest of Lord Caitanya’s devotees, whether in male or female bodies.

When identifying her according to her external role as a woman in Vaisnava society, however, she is described as “half’ an associate. In Vedic culture, women are considered dependent members of society, their identities being defined in childhood in relation to the father’s family and at marriage in relation to the husband’s family. The social convention was that when counting a woman in her own right, she was considered half a member of society.

Another important point to consider is that Madhavi — devi's association with Caitanya Mahaprabhu could not have taken place on the physical plane. Caitanya Mahaprabhu was a strict sannyasi. How could He have had confidential interactions with a woman? It was not possible.

Kaviraja Gosvami writes of her: “She was an elderly lady who always performed austerities. She was very advanced in devotional service.. .Sri Caitanya Mahaprabhu accepted her as having formerly been an associate of Srimati Radharani.”

The conclusion must be that while Caitanya Mahaprabhu was absorbed in His inner identity as Srimati Radharani, He was intimately served by Madhavi devi in her inner identity as a maidservant. In other words, they associated only on the spiritual platform. And it should be emphasised that on that spiritual platform, all of these lour intimate associates are gopis, women.

Thus Vaisnava society distinguishes a person’s external social status from his or her internal spiritual identity, and we, as followers of Vedic culture, should respect the co-existence of these differences. Social values are meant to give the most suitable structure for conditioned souls to approach the liberated platform. Even the liberated souls respect this structure, as shown in this very example.

Another example was that of Haridasa Thakura, who, because he was born in a family of untouchables, continued to accept the restrictions placed upon him by his birth, despite his obviously exalted spiritual stature as the namacarya.

Does the example of Madhavi devi have any bearing on us today? It is interesting that Srila Prabhupada told Tamala Krsna Goswami that if some of his intelligent female disciples were placed on the GBC body, they could only have one half-vote each. This statement seems to reflect that, at least under certain circumstances, Srila Prabhupada applied the ardha-jana principle to his female disciples. In other circumstances, such as in preaching and Deity worship, he gave women equal rights.

In conclusion, the example of Madhavi devi may be applied when defining social roles for men and women. In other words, a woman’s spiritual advancement does not negate her social role and duty.

Lord Caitanya described ragatmika-bhakti to Sanatana Gosvami in this way: “The original inhabitants of Vrndavana are attached to Krsna spontaneously in devotional service. Nothing can compare to such spontaneous devotional service, which is called ragatmika-bhakti. When a devotee follows in the footsteps of the devotees of Vrndavana, his devotional service is called raganuga-bhakti. When one becomes attached to the Supreme Personality of Godhead, his natural inclination to love is fully absorbed in thoughts of the Lord. That is called transcendental attachment, and devotional service according to that attachment is called ragatmika, or spontaneous devotional service. The primary characteristic of spontaneous love is deep attachment for the Supreme Personality of Godhead. Absorption in thought of Him is a marginal characteristic. Thus devotional service which consists of raga \[deep attachment\] is called ragatmika, spontaneous loving service. If a devotee covets such a position, he is considered to he most fortunate.” (Sri Caitanya-caritamrta, Madhya-lila 22.149-152)

Sri Brahma-samhita 5.37.

In his commentary to Sri Brahma-Samhita 5.37, Bhaktisiddhanta Sarasvati Thakura lists these sixty-four arts: (1) gita — singing; (2) vadya — playing musical instruments; (3) nrtya — dancing; (4) natya — theatricals; (5) alekhya — painting; (6) visesakacchedya — painting the face and body with coloured unguents and cosmetics; (7) tandula-kusuma-bali-vikara — preparing offerings from rice and flowers; (8) puspastarana— making a covering of flowers for a bed; (9) dasana-vasananga-raga— applying preparations for cleansing teeth, cloths, and painting the body; (10) mani-bhumika-karma — making the groundwork of jewels; (11) sayyd-racana — covering the bed; (12) udaka-vadya — playing on music in water; (13) udaka-ghata — splashing with water; (14) citra-yoga — practically applying an admixture of colours; (15) malya-grathana-vikalpa— designing a preparation of wreaths; (16) sekharapida-yojana — practically setting the coronet on the head; (17) nepathyu-yogu— practically dressing in the retiring room; (18) karnapatra-bhanga— decorating the tragus of the ear; (19) sugandha-yukti— practical application of aromatics; (20) bhusana-yojana — applying or setting ornaments; (21) aindra-jala — jugglery; (22) kaucumara— a kind of art \[the art of disguise\]; (23) hasta-laghava — sleight of hand; (24) citra-sakapupa-bhaksya-vikara-kriya — preparing varieties of salad, bread, cake, and delicious food; (25) panaka-rasa-ragasava-yojana — preparing palatable drinks and tinging draughts with red colour; (26) suci-vaya-karma— needlework and weaving; (27) sutra-krida— playing with thread; (28) vina-damuraka-vadya — playing on the lute and small x-shaped drum; (29) prahelika — making and solving riddles; (29a) pratimala—reciting verse for verse as a trial for memory or skill; (30) durvacaka-yoga — practising language difficult to be answered by others; (31) pustaka-vacana — reciting books; (32) natikakhyayika-darsana— enacting short plays and anecdotes; (33) kavya-samasya-purana — solving enigmatic verses; (34) pattikd-vetra-bana-vikalpa— designing preparation of shield, cane, and arrows; (35) tarku-karma — spinning hy spindle; (36) taksana — carpentry; (37) vastu-vidya — engineering \[architecture\]; (38) raupya-ratna-pariksa — testing silver and jewels; (39) dhatu-vada— metallurgy; (40) mani raga-jnana— tinging jewels; (41) akara-jnana — mineralogy; (42) vrksayur-veda-yoga — practising medicine, or medical treatment, by herbs; (43) mesa-kukkuta-lavaka-yuddha-vidhi — knowing the mode of fighting of lambs, cocks, and birds; (44) suka-sarika-pralapana— maintaining or knowing conversation between male and female cockatoos \[teaching them how to speak\]; (45) utsadana— healing or cleaning a person with perfumes; (46) kesa-marjana-kausala— combing hair; (47) aksara-mustika-kathana— talking with letters and fingers; (48) mlecchita-kutarka-vikalpa — fabricating barbarous or foreign sophistry; (49) desa-bhasa-jnana—\[knowledge of\] provincial dialects; (50) puspa-sakatika-nirmiti jnana— knowing prediction hy heavenly voice or knowing preparation of toy carts hy flowers; (51) yantra-matrka — mechanics \[composing magic squares\]; (52) dharana-matrka— use of amulets; (53) samvacya— conversation; (54) mdnasi-kavya-kriya — composing verse mentally; (55) kriya-vikalpa — designing a literary work or a medical remedy; (56) chali-taka-yoga— practising as a builder of shrines called after him \[building shrines\]; (57) abhidhana-kosa-cchando-jnana— the use of lexicography and metres; (58) vastra-gopana— concealment of cloths; (59) dyuta-visesa— knowing specific gambling; (60) akarsa-krida— playing with dice or magnet; (61) balaka-kridanaka— using children’s toys; (62) vainayiki vidya — enforcing discipline \[by mystic power\]; (63) vaijayiki vidya — gaining victory; (64) vaitaliki vidya — awakening master with music at dawn. (Additional comments given in brackets are taken from Srimad-Bhagavatam 10.45.35-36, purport.)

Bhakti-rasamrta-sindhu 2.1.308.

The Vedic system for determining a person’s age differs from modern Western convention. In that system a newborn starts at one year old and each passing (lunar) year adds one year to his or her age. In other words, during the entire first year of life the newborn is considered one year old, making that person two years old from the beginning of his or her second year, three years old from the beginning of the third year, and so on. In the Vedic system, then, a person will be considered one year older than he or she would be in the modern, Western system. Thus when Krsna’s eternal age is given as fifteen years, nine month, seven days, by Western calculation that means fourteen years, nine months, seven days, or almost fifteen years old. Without understanding the difference between these two conventions, the calculations regarding the ages of Krsna and His eternal associates as given by scripture and acaryas will be misunderstood. Srila Prabhupada explains this system in relation to his own life: “...regarding the calculation of my birthday. The system for calculating is as follows: the first day of birth is the first birthday. The next year that day is the second birthday; i.e. the second birthday means that one year has been completed. So it is my 80th birthday, and I have completed 79 years.” (Letter to Radhavallabha, Vrndavana, 11 September 1975)

Sri Caitanya-caritamrta, Madhya-lila 20.384, purport. Why does Srila Prabhupada use the word “pre-youth” instead of just “youth,” as he does elsewhere and as do other commentators? Because the term “youth” is sometimes used to refer to sixteen years and beyond. For instance, Srila Prabhupada comments on the phrase navam vayah in his purport to Srimad-Bhagavatam 4.27.5: “The words navam vayah... indicate the period of youth from age sixteen to thirty.” To therefore differentiate between these two conventions regarding the use of the word “youth” — adolescence and post-adolescence or adulthood— Srila Prabhupada renders nava-yauvana, the mature stage ot kaisora, as “pre-youth.”

Jiva Gosvami cites niti-sastra to explain the six elements that constitute successful ruling over the kingdom of romantic love, sun- dhir navigraho ?anam asanam dvaidham asrayah: “Alliance (sandhi), battle (vigraha), going (yana), sitting (asana), division (dvaidha), and assistance (asrayu).” (Durgama-sangamani 2.1.332) According to Amara-kosa (2.7.968), battle is illustrated by picking quarrels; division, by dispatching letters; moving, by a desire for meeting; assistance, by resolving differences; alliance, by uniting with a gopi; and sitting, by being master of the groves.

Krsna, the Supreme Personality of Godhead, chapter 33.

Based on the accelerated rate (1.5 times faster) at which Krsna grows older and Visvanatha Cakravarti Thakura’s assertion that Krsna leaves Vrndavana after ten years, seven months, and eleven days, Krsna’s physical appearance would he fifteen years, eleven months, and three days.

Mature youth is called yauvana, which appears only after Krsna leaves Vrndavana. Nava-yauvana is distinguished by the special sweetness that Krsna displays only in Vraja, which is characteristic of His Syamasundara form. Yauvana is exhibited only hy Krsna’s Vasudeva expansion.

Krsna tells Uddhava, “A person who gives up all fruitive activities and offers himself entirely unto Me, eagerly desiring to render service unto Me, achieves liberation from birth and death and is promoted to the status of sharing My own opulences.” (Srimad-Bhagavatam 11.29.34)

Gopala-campu, Purva-campu 1.61.

The following is the calculation for Radha’s age: Childhood and girlhood are each three years and four months, which is equivalent to six years and eight months. Each sub-period of adolescence is one year, one month, and ten days. It takes two and a half of these sub-periods to reach the middle of sesa-kaisora, the final sub-period of adolescence. That span of time is therefore equal to two years, nine months, and ten days. Altogether, the total comes to nine years, five months, and ten days. As Radha, like Krsna, ages fifty percent faster than normal human beings, we can multiply that total by 1.5 to arrive at Sri Radha’s eternal age: fourteen years and four months.

Teachings of Lord Caitanya, chapter 31, and lecture, Boston, 25 April 1969, respectively.

The difference between Krsna’s eternal fifteen years, nine months, and seven days, and Radha’s fourteen years, two months, and fifteen days, is one year, six months, and twenty-two days. If we subtract the additional fifty percent that arises due to the rate at which They mature, the answer is exactly one year, and fifteen days. Therefore, by earthly calculation, Radha’s first birthday is fifteen days after Krsna’s, but one year later.

Radha-krsna-ganoddesa-dipika 2.167.

Srimad-Bhagavatam 10.2.9, purport; and Radha-krsna-ganoddesa-dipika 2.72, respectively.

Srimad-Bhagavatam 10.2.4-13.

Harivamsa Purana 1.4.12.

Another pertinent point is that Krsna and Balarama each acted as leaders of the younger and older groups of boys. This suggests that the age difference between the two brothers must have been more significant than a mere two weeks. The demarcation between the two age groups would lose its significance if the difference was counted in days. Thus one is led to conclude that at least a year, if not years, marked the distinction between the younger and older gopas.

Srila Prabhupada gives the following explanation in his purport to Srimad-Bhagavatam 10.3.47: “Srila Visvanatha Cakravarti Thakura discusses that Krsna appeared simultaneously as the son of Devaki and as the son of Yasoda, along with the spiritual energy Yogamaya. As the son of Devaki, He first appeared as Visnu, and because Vasudeva was not in the position of pure affection for Krsna, Vasudeva worshipped his son as Lord Visnu. Yasoda, however, pleased her son Krsna without understanding His godhood. This is the difference between Krsna as the son of Yasoda and as the son of Devaki. This is explained by Visvanatha Cakravarti on the authority of Hari-vamsa."

Brahma-vaivarta Purana 4.13.235.

Nectar of Devotion, chapter 42.

Brahma-vaivarta ?uran? 4.13.93-100

Ibid., 4.15. (Translation by Bhaktisiddhanta Sarasvati Thakura)

To understand the section that follows, readers would do well to recall the fundamentals of bhakti that underlie the topic of vibhava. That which causes one to taste devotion is called vibhava. That cause is Krsna, the object of devotion. This taste is stimulated by the dynamic between the two subjects of vibhava: Krsna’s devotee (the shelter) and the impetus (e.g. Krsna’s beauty, or some other personal characteristic belonging to Krsna). For more on this topic, devotees may study The Nectar of Devotion, chapters 20 through 26.

Durgama-sangamani 2.1.305.

When it is said that Subala or Gandharva “glow with the lustre of adolescence,” it means that, although they have not reached adolescence (nava-yauvana), they possess many of its qualities.

According to Patanjali, there are three stages of samadhi, from least to most intense, called savikalpa, nirvikalpa, and sahaja-nirvikalpa or maha-samadhi. Savikalpa-samadhi is fixed absorption through practice, and nirvikalpa-samadhi is spontaneous or perfected absorption, while maha-samadhi is the point at which one sheds the body and enjoys the same perfection as that enjoyed in nirvikalpa hut without the material body to anchor one to the world. Srila Prabhupada says, for instance, that the stage of perfection Bhismadeva attained in Krsna’s presence was nirvikalpa-samadhi. (Srimad-Bhagavatam 1.9.43, purport)

Srimad-Bhagavatam 1.9.42.

There is archaeological evidence of burial sites dating back 4000 years in Balathal, Rajasthan and 6000 years in Harappa. Thesewere not Vedic sites, however, but rather an affiliated tradition that eventually abandoned the system of burial in favour of cremation.

Lecture on Srimad-Bhagavatam 7.9.26, Mayapur, 4 March 1976.

Sri Caitanya-caritamrta, Antya-lila 11.66-70.

Most of the sixty-four samadhis in the garden before the Radha-Govinda temple are nama-samadhis. (Gaudiya Vaisnava Samadhis, page 9)

When Tamala Krsna Goswami was Srila Prabhupada’s servant, Prabhupada lost a tooth which Goswami Maharaja encased in gold and wore on a chain around his neck. He requested that at the time of his death it he placed with his body, so that those who came to pay their respects at his samadhi would ultimately be offering respects to Srila Prabhupada. Words to this effect are carved on the wall of Goswami Maharaja’s samadhi.

It is interesting to note that in Bhauma Vrndavana, in a garden outside the Govindadji temple, there are nama-samadhis of Caitanya Mahaprabhu’s sixty-four associates.

Sri Caitanya-caritamrta, Adi-lila 12.91-92.

Caitanya-bhagavata, Adi-khanda 16.247.

At the time of publication, the samadhi shrines of our previous acaryas are yet to be built at New Vraja-dhama. When they are, within those shrines will he various items from the original samadhi. For example, during the renovations to Srila Rupa Gosvami’s samadhi in Vrndavana in 2008, the original stone slab which was placed over his body by Jiva Gosvami was broken. The pieces were carefully stored by an old Goswami who lives at the site, who witnessed the renovations and identified each remnant as it was removed from the samadhi. He gave some of those pieces to Srimati Devi Dasi, a long-time resident of both Mayapur and Vrndavana; she in turn gave them to her friend, Braja Sevaki Devi Dasi who, knowing of my plans for New Vraja-dhama, gave a piece to be used in the construction of a samadhi shrine in honour of Srila Rupa Gosvami. Thus by visiting the marked holy sites in New Vraja-dhama, and the cottages and terraced houses as described herein, devotees are indeed visiting a site that is sacred in more ways than one.

Teachings of Lord Caitanya, as cited in Gaudiya Vaisnava Samadhis, introduction.
